# SÊRAT CÊNTHINI 1

1

## Sinom  1.

The illustrious and refined King, ruler of all Javanese lands in Surakarta Adiningrat, commanded his royal scribe Sutrasna to compose a vast chronicle—a treasury of all Javanese wisdom, woven into poetic verse, so that its legacy would never fade from the hearts of those who hear it.

### 2.
The tale unfolds with the deeds of Jayengresmi, known as the venerable Sheikh Amongraga, the scion of Sunan Giri. A man of surpassing virtue, a saint in the lineage of the elect, he walked the earth during the reign of Sultan Agung of Mataram—his story immortalized in the sacred text called Susuluk Tambangraras.

### 3.
By the will of the prince, the very essence of Javanese wisdom was cast into narrative form. The work was taken up at the appointed hour—on the twenty-first day of Saturday Paing, in the month of Mukaram, under the alignment of Hyang Surenggana and the god Bathara Yama, when the cosmic cycles all converged.

### 4.
It tells of the fivefold purification of the knight and the fiery authority of his path, marked by the Windu Adi and the seventh Mangsa—a chronogram of sacred word and soul. This song has its beginning in the era when the court of Majalengka was ruled by Sri Brawijaya, at the time a holy teacher arrived from the land of Jeddah.

### 5.
Now begins the journey of Sheikh Walilanang upon his arrival in Java. He made his way to Ampeldenta, where he met the great sage; together they spoke of deeper knowledge, of the Path and the Law of the Prophet. Yet his stay at Ampelgadhing was brief—he soon departed south and east, journeying at last to the realm of Balambangan.

### 6.
In the settlement of Purwasata, within the kingdom of Balambangan, there dwelt a king whose only daughter was of incomparable beauty. As she came of age and the fires of desire stirred within her, she was struck by a wasting illness. All remedies failed. The great king, heavy with sorrow, summoned Patih Samboja into his presence.

### 7.
"My lord," said the Patih, "your servant has learned that a holy man has come to Java—Sheikh Walilanang by name, from the city of Jeddah—and he now sojourns in Purwasata itself. With your leave, it would be wise to invite him here. Perhaps the yogi will consent to heal the princess of her affliction."

### 8.
Hearing the words of his chief minister, the king's heart was lightened with hope. He forthwith sent an envoy to invite the holy man. When Sheikh Walilanang arrived at the palace, the king hurried out to greet him. After they were seated and refreshments served, the sovereign laid bare the sorrow that weighed upon his soul.

### 9.
Sheikh Walilanang consented readily and at once undertook his healing. The noble princess recovered; joy bloomed anew in her breast. The king, overcome with gratitude, desired the Sheikh as his son-in-law. The wedding was celebrated—a harmony as perfect as the coupling of mimi and mintuna fish.

### 10.
After many seasons in Balambangan, Sheikh Wali spoke to the king, urging him to embrace Islam, to walk the path of the Prophet. But the king would not be moved. Disheartened, the Sheikh slipped away by night and journeyed to Malacca, leaving behind his wife, who was with child—a jewel of a woman abandoned to a grief beyond all telling.

### 11.
Hard upon the holy man's departure, a great plague descended upon the land; countless people perished. The king's wrath fell upon his Patih, whose rank was stripped away. Ki Samboja, burning with shame, departed the kingdom and journeyed to Majapait, where he entered the service of His Majesty King Brawijaya.

### 12.
There he was received with honor and settled in the precincts of Giri. Yet not long afterward Ki Samboja died, and was interred at Giri as well. All his possessions passed to his widow, who became known as Nyi Randha Sugih—a wealthy merchant woman whose fortune exceeded all others.

### 13.
Now the tale returns to Balambangan. The princess, the wife left behind, gave birth to a son—a boy whose skin was dark as the midnight sea. By the king's decree, the infant was cast into the vast ocean, sealed inside a coconut shell. Adrift on the currents, the child was soon rescued by a merchant captain who chanced to be sailing by.

### 14.
When the shell was opened, the dark-skinned babe was revealed. The captain brought him to Nyi Randha, who received him with boundless love. Having no child of her own, she cherished him as her own flesh and blood. She named him Santrigiri. And so he grew until he reached his twelfth year.

### 15.
Then Nyi Randha spoke gently: "My son, the time has come for you to leave my side. Do not cling to worldly comfort—go instead in search of knowledge, following the path of the Prophet. There is a great teacher here, a disciple of Sunan Ampelgadhing named Surapringga; it would be well for you to serve him and learn."

### 16.
Santrigiri entered the sage's tutelage and was trained in all things—outward and inward, visible and hidden. "Be neither careless nor negligent," his master warned. To shorten the tale: Santrigiri became the devoted attendant of the holy lord, never parting from him day or night, and likewise grew inseparable from the son of Ampelgadhing, whom his father had named Santribonang.

### 17.
The two became as brothers, of one heart, their desires never at odds. Their studies flourished; they mastered every branch of knowledge. At last the two companions resolved to journey afar, to seek the deepest wisdom in Mecca. They set out from Ampelgadhing, boarded a ship, and cast anchor at Malacca.

### 18.
In Malacca they encountered Sheikh Walilanang himself, who recognized them at once. They stayed and studied under the true Sheikh Wali. But after a year, the prince's son was determined to press on to Mecca. Walilanang was greatly displeased and would not consent.

### 19.
He commanded them to return to Java, to the mountain hermitage of Ampelgadhing, and gave them provisions—a coat and robe for each. To Santrigiri he bestowed the title Prabu Setmata; to Santribonang he gave the name Prabu Nyakrakusuma, who would later be known as Susunan Bonang.

### 20.
So they departed and sailed back to Java, returning at last to Ampelgadhing. There they met the great ascetic and recounted all that had befallen them. The sage spoke softly: "My sons, the knowledge you have gained is true. Sheikh Walilanang of Malacca is my friend and ally—we are of one mind in all knowledge outward and inward, without division."

### 21.
"So go now, return to your hermitage in Giri. Your mother is ill; she is gravely sick, close to her final hour. She is waiting only for you, my son. When she passes, bury her in Giri, beside your brother-in-arms."

### 22.
"And I appoint you to succeed as ruler of Giri—you shall bear the title Prabu Setmata. In time to come, after I am gone, you will be the one to lead. You will become the supreme saint, revered throughout Java, feared yet loved. You will be known as the king of holy men."

### 23.
"But take care that you understand rightly: you are not a king who rules a worldly realm. You will not be deceived—you will discern all movement, all stirring of this world and the next. I have spoken enough." Sunan Giri bowed low in gratitude, touched his master's feet, and departed from Ampelgadhing, without further delay the story swiftly brings him.

## Kinanthi  1.

### 1.
When he arrived in Giri, there lay his mother, stricken by illness. All the merchants and lords of Giri had gathered around her. Such was the sanctity of heaven's wali—the mother was brought near her son, and her spirit stirred.

### 2.
She awoke with a start and embraced him, kissing her child again and again. Her tears flowed in rivers as she wept with pity: "Ah, my child, the very core of my heart—I thought I would never see you again. You were gone so long, studying the holy word, and I had given up hope of ever being reunited with you."

### 3.
"Now your face shines like a flame—bright, radiant, luminous. Surely you have received a great blessing." The Sunan answered gently: "Mother, by your grace—all that has happened to me is through your prayers."

### 4.
The mother was overjoyed. "My son, complete my life for me—let me enter the faith of Islam." Swiftly he administered the holy creed, teaching her the two Testimonies. The mother received the light with a clear heart.

### 5.
Now a prose chronicle of the reign of Sunan Giri. King Brawijaya of Majapait was displeased by the rising spiritual power in Giri. He sent Patih Gajahmada with an army to subdue it. Majapait's forces were defeated when they were attacked by a sacred kris fashioned from a *kalam*—the very pen that Sunan Giri had been using to copy the Qur'an. That kris was named Kalam Munyeng. Sunan Giri died and was succeeded by his son (Sunan Giri Kadhaton). When the son died, his grandson succeeded, bearing the title Sunan Giri Prapen. King Brawijaya made a second attempt to subdue the spiritual power of Giri, but again failed: his army was put to flight by countless swarms of bees that issued from Sunan Giri's grave. In the end, Majapait was destroyed by Brawijaya's own son, Raden Patah. Sunan Giri Prapen conditionally reigned as king (of religion) for forty days, then surrendered the realm to Raden Patah, who became king of Demak.

### 6.
"My son, my heart is now clear; there is no more doubt within me. Remain here in peace, hold fast to your charge over the merchants and the people of the hermitage in Giri."

### 7.
"In time to come, after I am gone, distribute all my worldly goods in alms—to the poor, the destitute, the orphans—and use the rest to purchase a commission for the pilgrimage to Mecca."

### 8.
"One more charge, my son: Ni Samboja will soon breathe her last. When she has been laid out, bury her beside her husband Kyai Samboja, in the grounds of Giri."

### 9.
After the death of Nyi Samboja, the people of Giri all entered the faith of Islam, following the path of the Prophet, praying and reciting the Qur'an. Many built mosques.

### 10.
Prosperity reigned; there was none who did evil. Everyone had enough—clothing and food—and there were no poor among them. Their hearts were at peace, and people from foreign lands came in great numbers.

### 11.
Once they had come, none wished to leave; they settled permanently in Giri. King-Priest Setmata, bearing the title Susunan Giri—Gajah Kadhaton the exalted—was renowned in every land.

### 12.
Moreover, he was taken as a son-in-law by the lord of Ampelgadhing, who gave him his daughter in marriage—the lady Ageng Ratu, she was called. The marriage was harmonious, but it remained without issue.

### 13.
Eight children were born, both girls and boys, but Nyi Ageng predeceased them all, returning to the mercy of God. Her body was laid beside him. The aged mourned, both women and men; the spouse and children were deep in grief.

### 14.
Let the tale turn now to King Brawijaya. He heard the report: that the ruler of Giri was followed by multitudes who submitted without battle.

### 15.
The king sent word in haste to Gajahmada the Patih, commanding him to march in person and crush Giri and Gresik. We shall not dwell on the journey; soon he reached the borders of Giri.

### 16.
The drums of war thundered as the invaders fell upon the land, who fled toward the mountain palace of Prawata. At that moment Sunan Giri was engaged in copying the Qur'an, when he was startled by the clamor.

### 17.
"The enemy has come!" shouted the people. "They intend to destroy Giri!" The *kalam*—the pen—with which he was writing was instantly cast aside, and he prayed to the Lord God, and his prayer was swiftly granted.

### 18.
The *kalam* turned into a kris, flashing as it charged into battle on its own. The forces of Majalengka fell in great numbers. Those who survived the slaughter fled in panic.

### 19.
They returned to Majalangu, their enemy scattered. The kris returned of its own accord and settled before the sage, a length of dripping blood upon its blade.

### 20.
The king of the ascetics was startled to see the kris still wet with blood. Great was his repentance; he prayed to God Most High: "May Allah forgive me—what have I done, Your servant whose actions have gone astray?"

### 21.
The sage spoke to his people: "Listen, all of you, and bear witness. I give this weapon a name: Kalam Munyeng, the Whirling Pen."

### 22.
All the assembled soldiery answered with one voice; they were given leave to return to their homes. Peace settled upon the hermitage of Giri under the rule of His Holiness the Sunan, Prabu Setmata the Excellent.

### 23.
Prosperity was abundant, and the number of his followers increased; none suffered hardship. After a time, however, Sunan Giri fell ill, as fate would have it.

### 24.
He returned to the mercy of God. Loud weeping filled the palace—servants and nobles alike were stricken with grief. After the body had been purified, it was interred not far from his dwelling.

### 25.
He departed leaving ten children—sons and daughters. Two of them were by the senior queen: Pangeran Pasirbata and Siti Rohbayat. The remaining eight were by junior wives.

### 26.
The first-born was named Nyai Geng Ratu, known as Ratu Gedhe of Kukusan. Next came Sunan Dalem, and the third was titled Susuhunan Tegalwangi.

### 27.
The fourth was Nyi Geng Saluluhur, the fifth was Sunan Kidul, then Ratu Gedhe Saworasa, Sunan Kulon the gentle, and Sunan Waruju, the youngest—these are the ones recorded.

### 28.
After the burial, the people of Giri assembled to decide who should succeed as ruler of the Sunanate. They were of one accord: Sunan Dalem was to be the successor.

### 29.
He was installed with the title Sunan Giri the Second, Susunan Giri Kadhatyan. He had two wives, both junior wives. His reign was not renowned; soon the Sunan's appointed time had come.

### 30.
He returned to the mercy of God and was also buried in Giri, leaving ten children. His eldest son became Sunan Sedamargi; the one who succeeded him was called Sunan Giri Prapen Adi.

### 31.
The third was Nyi Geng Kuruganngurun, Nyi Geng Ngulakan the Pure, Pangeran Lor, Pangeran Dheket, Pangeran Bongkok, and then Nyai Ageng Waru, together with Pangeran Bulu the steadfast.

### 32.
The youngest was Pangeran Sedalaut, whose spirit had already ascended. It was agreed that the son should succeed. All his followers assembled; Sunan Prapen was installed, surpassing even his grandfather in glory. He ruled in Giri Kadhaton, his title enduring. Sunan Giri Prapen was illustrious—the people, both great and small, held him in awe, and his fame spread to other kingdoms without cease, as in the days of old.

## Mêgatruh 1.

### 1.
Now it is told that the king of Majalangu had heard the news: Sunan Giri, King Setmata, had died, and now his successor reigned in his stead.

### 2.
He heard that one called Sunan Prapen of Giri the Exalted conducted himself as before—the holy man refused to submit to Majapait. Therefore the king was filled with great anger.

### 3.
He commanded Ki Gajahmada the foremost, together with the king's sons, to lead a great army and crush Giri. When all was ready, they set forth.

### 4.
Sunan Prapen heard that he was to be destroyed by the king of Majapait. The king's emissaries had already departed—the Patih and the princes—with an army vast as the surging ocean.

### 5.
Sunan Prapen had prepared for battle; he resolved to withstand the forces of Majalangu. Not long after, the enemy came into view, and they met in close combat.

### 6.
Giri's forces were defeated in battle, many wounded and many slain. Sunan Prapen fled, escaping with his wife and children.

### 7.
He took refuge by the seashore. The city of Giri was destroyed and reduced to ashes; all its treasures were plundered. The prince moved on.

### 8.
He came to the hermitage of the holy man, the Sunan Giri who had already ascended. Two guardians were watching over the grave, rigid and silent at the approach of the young warrior. The troops were ordered to dig—all the servants set to work at once—the structure of the great holy man, the mark of a supreme wali, remained a lion crouching in defiance.

### 9.
They struggled and cried out for death; those who remained were terrified. Enraged, the prince himself stepped forward—though fearful of the shame should he fail.

### 10.
So he commanded the two guards to dig swiftly, threatening them with death if they refused—as certain as the blade of a kris.

### 11.
The two did not dare refuse; they began to dig. When they reached the teakwood plank that covered the grave, they opened it slightly—and something emerged.

### 12.
Countless swarms of beetles poured out, filling the sky, descending like a falling firmament upon the forces of Majapait—who struggled in vain against the onslaught.

### 13.
The beetles could not be withstood; the army of Majapait was in utter confusion, scrambling and trampling each other in a desperate flight for survival, throwing aside their weapons.

### 14.
All were shrouded in the swirling cloud of beetles, heads pressed against feet, howling in confusion. When they reached the city of Majapait, the beetles still clung to them, relentless.

### 15.
King Brawijaya was seized with panic, unable to withstand the onslaught. He abandoned his kingdom with all his forces—the city was emptied of people.

### 16.
He fled far away. When the beetles saw that the city was empty, they returned from whence they came. Not one remained behind. King Brawijaya of Majalangu and his forces returned to the palace. He vowed never again to persecute the holy man, resolved to let the matter rest, his heart now without malice.

### 17.
The two guardians of the grave were released from their paralysis; they set out swiftly to follow Sunan Giri, who had taken refuge in a remote place.

### 18.
In his desolation by the seashore, the two attendants approached and reported that the enemy had fled, routed by the beetles that had met them in battle.

### 19.
They had scattered in headlong flight, bruising and battering one another, abandoning their weapons, each striving only to survive, not one looking back—their cries a confused roar.

### 20.
When all had been reported—the beginning and the end, including the healing of the guardian's paralysis—the holy man gave thanks, praying to God Most High for continued peace and freedom from harm.

### 21.
Then he returned; the length of time is not told, for these events are long past. His servants' hearts were at ease, though some among them grew proud. Giri remained at peace, no enemy approaching.

### 22.
Sunan Giri understood clearly that the kingdom of Majapait was approaching its end, brought about by his own son—Raden Patah had arisen, as was known.

### 3
The sovereignty of Java passed from Demak to Pajang. When Sultan Adiwijaya reigned, he paid homage to Sunan Giri Prapen at a time when the regents of the eastern coast were assembled. Sultan Pajang was accompanied by Ki Ageng Pamanahan, whom Sunan Giri foresaw would one day give rise to a king who would rule all Java, including Giri. After the death of Sultan Pajang, the sovereignty of Java was held by Panembahan Senapati of Mataram. After his death, he was succeeded by his son Sultan Anyakrawati, who was in turn succeeded by his son, Sultan Agung Anyakrakusuma.

## Mijil 1.

### 1.
In accordance with the path of the Prophet Muhammad, the most honored, His Holiness Sunan Giri Prapen descended to the city of Demak, gathering with the saints, the faithful, and the multitude.

### 2.
After the fall of Majapait, when the king had departed and the palace stood empty, Sunan Giri served as regent, acting as the soul of governance—but only for forty days was his tenure.

### 3.
He ordained that the old Hindu-Buddhist kingship should come to an end, and after the forty days had passed, His Holiness Susunan Giri surrendered the sovereignty.

### 4.
He gave it to Raden Patah, who accepted it and was installed as king in Demak, ruling the land of Java. His Holiness Susunan Giri, his task completed, was given leave to return.

### 5.
After the kingdom of Demak fell, the court moved to Pajang. The Adipati of Pajang became the ruler; he married the daughter of the Sultan of Bintara, who had ended the line in Demak. He commanded a vast host.

### 6.
The honored Sultan of Pajang, after his enthronement, sought permission from Sunan Prapen. Kyai Ageng Pamanahan of Mataram accompanied him, together with all the regents—none was absent.

### 7.
When Sultan Pajang arrived in Giri, he was astonished to see the encampment spread before him, and the regents presenting themselves in homage to Sunan Giri—all their lodgings were in good order.

### 8.
When Sunan Prapen came forth to hold audience, Sultan Pajang hastened to pay homage, together with many regents. They sat in rows before the sage, attended by servants. When all the courtiers had assembled, the Sunan spoke softly: "Come here, Pajang, approach me." The Sultan seated himself near the sage, who spoke with a gentle voice, sweet as fragrance.

### 9.
"My children," the sage continued, "all of you who attend this audience, let there be no anxiety in your hearts—accept what has been ordained. As for the Adipati of Pajang, I have granted my consent to his reign."

### 10.
"The Sultan rules as sovereign of Pajang, illustrious and supreme, a chosen ruler without equal. He holds authority over all the land of Java, as God's vicegerent upon earth."

### 11.
"Sultan Prabu Adiwijaya the Exalted, by the will of God Almighty—bear witness, all my children, with the homage of every regent." And all rejoiced at the command of the holy man.

### 12.
Then a feast was brought forth from within the palace, arranged in the central pavilion. Sunan Prapen, the Sultan of Pajang, and all the regents partook until they were satisfied.

### 13.
The sage spoke again: "O my disciples—Sultan of Pajang and all the regents—you are all brothers. Let there be no discord among you. Live in harmony and unity."

### 15.
Raharjèng tyas sukur ing Hyang Widdhi|karsane Hyang Manon|ana kang tinitah dadi gêdhe|ana kang tinitah dadi cilik|papasthèning Widdhi|basane asnapun||

### 16.
Datan luwih sun têtèdhèng Widdhi|anak putuningong|donya akir salamêta kabèh|kang sinabdan samya saur paksi|tutug dènnya bukti|linorot mring wadu||Sunan Giri tinarbukèng Widdhi|waspadèng pangawroh|datan samar lalakon ing têmbe|ingkang tansah pinancêr ing liring|Ki Agêng Mantawis|cahyanya umancur||

### 18.
Angandika Jêng Susunan Giri|mring jêng sultan alon|jêbèng Pajang lah ta sapa kae|baturmu kang mangan angèrèni|sapa aranèki|kangjêng sultan matur||

### 19.
Rencang amba patinggi Mantawis|nami karan ênggon|angrèh siti sadhomas karyane|sang pandhita angandika malih|dhawuhana kaki|linggih jajar patut||

### 20.
Lawan para siswèngsun bupati|sandika kang kinon|Kyai Agêng Mantaram wus jèjèr|hèh sanggyaning siswèngsun bupati|wruhanamu kaki|turunne si kulup||

### 21.
Ki Mantaram besuk wus pinasthi|angrèh sakèhe wong|ing satanah Jawa iki kabèh|nadyan Giri kene têmbe ugi|ngidhèp ing Mantawis|kyagêng lajêng ngujung||

### 22.
Konjêm siti nênêdha Hyang Widdhi|widadaning dhawoh|saha matur mring Sunan Parapèn|sakalangkung sru nuwun kapundhi|lulusing sabda ji|têtêpe kang wahyu||

### 23.
Dhuh pukulun patikbra ngaturi|dhuwung wus mirantos|amung dadya pratandha yêktine|sungkêm amba timbalan sang yogi|sunan ngandika ris|sun tarima kulup||

### 24.
Nanging ingsun paringakên bali|apan kacariyos|samya rêsêp pra dipati kabèh|aningali Ki Agêng Mantawis|raja pandhita di|gya mucung dhadhawuh||

### 5
## Pocung  1.

### 5
Lah ta kulup para bupati sadarum|ingsun iyasakna|talaga kang luwih adi|tur sandika pra wadya nulya tumandang||

### 2.
Dhudhuk-dhudhuk tan pantara nuli rampung|dados kang talaga|luwih adi toya wêning|gilar-gilar lir pendah kaca brênggala||

### 3.
Wusing katur sinung ran Talagapatut|jêng sultan kalawan|sagunging para bupati|wus linilan mring prajane sowang-sowang||

### 4.
Tan cinatur nagri Pajang risakipun|pindhah ing Mantaram|ingkang jumênêng narpati|putranira nênggih Ki Agêng Mantaram||

### 5.
Risang Sutawijaya jujulukipun|Kangjêng Panêmbahan|Senapati ing Mantawis|lulus wirya gêmah arja prajanira||

### 6.
Panêmbahan Senapati ing Mantarum|ngyêktèkkên wirayat|dhawuhe Susunan Giri|ing nalika sowannya jêng sultan Pajang||

### 7.
Panêmbahan Senapati nulya ngutus|abdi kinèn sowan|maring Giri kanthi tulis|praptèng Giri sang wiku lagya sineba||

### 8.
Sêrat katur tamating pamaosipun|mèsêm angandika|hèh caraka ing Mantawis|pakênira matura Ki Senapatya||

### 9.
Lamun ayun nyatakkên wirayatingsun|gustimu turana|nglurug mring bang-wetan nuli|wus pinasthi karsane Allah Tangala||

### 10.
Lamun ratu Mantaram ing têmbenipun|ngrata tanah Jawa|sanadyan ing Giri iki|uga têluk marang ing nagri Mantaram||

### 11.
Lokilmakpul tan kêna owah sarambut|jamane walikan|kawula dumadya gusti|ingkang gusti sayêkti dadi kawula||

### 12.
Pratandhane wus katon Pajang Mantarum|iku dadya cihna|utusan wus nyuwun pamit|mantuk marang Mantaram agêgancangan||

### 13.
Wus sumiwi ing ngabyantara sang prabu|wus katur sadaya|wêlinge Jêng Sunan Giri|tan pantara lami sri nata Mantaram||

### 14.
Bidhal nglurug mring bang-wetan kang jinujug|ing nagari Japan|sagunging para bupati|ing bang-wetan wus kumpul anèng ing Japan||

### 15.
Kacariyos ing Giri sang maha wiku|wus miyarsa warta|lamun sang prabu Mantawis|ngêpung Japan para bupati bang-wetan||

### 16.
Pacak baris tutulung paguting pupuh|Jêng Sunan ing Arga|utusan akanthi tulis|tyang sadasa gêgancangan marang Japan||

### 17.
Praptanipun ing Japan nulya kinumpul|wong agung Mantaram|myang bang-wetan pra bupati|wusing pêpak duta Giri aturira||

### 18.
Dhuh wong agung Mantaram saha wong agung|bang-wetan sadaya|lampah kawula tinuding|ing Jêng Sunan Giri akanthi nawala||

### 19.
"Recite it aloud so all may hear clearly." The Giri envoy began to read: "The letter of His Holiness Sunan Giri to my son, Senapati of Mataram, and to my son, the Prince of Surabaya."

### 20.
"The matter of this letter: you are contending in battle, struggling for supremacy. I do not permit it, for there will be much bloodshed, and it is not good."

### 21.
"Hereafter, if such be the will of God Almighty, all stations—high and low—are already decreed. Their time will come, but that hour has not yet arrived."

### 22.
"Instead, pursue a path of harmony and remain as brothers. I give you a parable: the container and the contents—choose between them."

### 23.
"I will allow whichever one you select: the contents or the container." When they had made their choice, they pledged their loyalty, swearing it from the heart.

### 24.
"Then submit to the decree of Almighty God. Whichever you choose cannot be altered—its outcome will be known in time."

## Pangkur 1.

### 1.
"Now that you have made your choice, return to your respective lands. I grant you a safe journey, all of you." When the letter was finished, it was sealed. Senapati spoke: "Brother of Surabaya..."

### 2.
"What is your wish?" Thus said the Panembahan of Giri's command. "Only you and I are addressed in this letter—we are to choose between the container and the contents. Which do you desire? Choose first."

### 3.
"The contents, I accept," said the Prince of Surabaya when he heard this. Yet within himself he was troubled. At last he said: "Brother Senapati NgAlaga of Mataram—my heart is set. I choose the contents."

### 4.
"Brother, you take the container." Senapati NgAlaga answered softly: "Brother, I accept the container. It is well." The Giri envoy, having heard all this, took his leave of the Panembahan and of all the regents.

### 5.
Panembahan Senapati returned to his land of Mataram. The great lords of the east also returned each to his own realm. The tale passes over the envoy's journey until he came before the Sunan, made his obeisance, and reported all that had been done.

### 6.
"My lord, be it known: your son the Prince of Surabaya was bidden to choose first. He chose the contents. His Highness Senapati accepted the container."

### 7.
His Holiness the Sunan of the Mountain smiled. "Know, then, that destiny cannot be altered: the container is the land; the contents are its people. The day shall come when the eastern lands are ruled by Mataram. If the contents refuse to obey the one who holds the vessel..."

### 8.
"...truly they will not prosper. Should they rebel, death will surely follow. They must submit to the one who owns the container." Let the tale rest now in Giri. We turn to the kingdom of Mataram under Panembahan Senapati.

### 9.
His reign as king lasted only three years, and then his life came to an end. He was succeeded by his son, Prince Adipati Anom of Mataram, entitled His Highness Susuhunan Diningrat Anyakrawati.

### 10.
He reigned only twelve years, then the Sunan passed away. He was succeeded by his son, Prince Adipati Anom of Mataram, entitled His Highness Susunan Sultan Agung of Mataram.

### 11.
King Anyakrakusuma—at that time the Prince of Surabaya summoned all the Tumenggungs of the eastern lands. When all had assembled in Surabaya, they deliberated upon going to war against Mataram.

### 12.
All the regents were of one mind; no one disagreed. At dawn they set forth with a great roar of troops and horse, stopping first at the hermitage of Giri Kadhaton to pay respects before proceeding to Sunan Giri.

### 13.
Now a brief chronicle: Sultan Agung summoned Prince Pekik of Surabaya and wed him to Ratu Pandhansari, the Sultan's younger sister. Prince Pekik was commanded to subdue Giri, and he departed with his wife. Because Sunan Giri would not submit, war broke out between Surabaya and Giri. Sunan Giri relied upon a pupil and adopted son, a Chinese Muslim warrior named Endrasena. In the heat of battle, a son of Giri from a junior wife—Raden Jayengresmi by name—advised his father not to resist Sultan Agung. His counsel was ignored, and the son fled, followed by the santris Gathak and Gathuk.

### 14.
When the Sunan of the Mountain had grown old, the Prince of Surabaya and the Regent of Singasari came before him. He spoke softly: "Ah, my children, what is your purpose in coming before me? Is there some urgent matter?"

### 15.
The Prince of Surabaya and the Regent of Singasari bowed low: "Forgive us, holy father. Your servants and their comrades have resolved to march against Mataram. We seek your permission and your blessing."

### 16.
The Sunan answered gently: "The time is not yet come, my sons." The regents pressed him, seeking wholeheartedly his blessing. The Sunan spoke softly again: "It is impossible for Mataram to fall."

### 17.
"You men of the eastern lands—you would do better to take shelter. In time you will surely be brought to Mataram." Yet the Prince of Surabaya was not swayed, nor were all the regents. They bowed and withdrew from the sage.

### 18.
The holy king spoke: "Since that is your will, be cautious, my sons. I entrust you to God. Sweet and bitter shall you taste—all will be known in time." The regents bowed and withdrew.

### 19.
They departed with their armies. The tale does not dwell on their conduct in war. In the end they were defeated; the glory of Mataram was unmatched. The great lords of the east retreated in disorder.

### 20.
Some were slain, some wounded, some submitted peaceably. Mataram grew ever more renowned—the king's power, the courage of his officers. His court was honored, beloved of God.

### 21.
Prosperous and tranquil, orderly and peaceful were all the people, high and low. Gifts flowed from the king to all the regents. Their hearts were easy; no one harbored resentment. All feared and loved their sovereign, loyal in both body and soul.

### 22.
Such was the kingdom's felicity—if sung through the night, it could not be exhausted. Now the song begins of the King of Mataram, Sultan Agung Anyakrakusuma, as he held audience, surrounded by all who paid court.

### 23.
His troops and kin were present; Panembahan Purbaya was before him. Gently questioned, the Sultan said: "Uncle Purbaya, I have news: the Prince of Surabaya has died."

### 24.
"The only one left is his son, Ki Mas Pekik, the first-born. I wish to summon him to Mataram, to see his face for myself." Panembahan answered: "That would be most fitting."

### 25.
"I follow your will," said the king softly. "I shall send Alap-alap as my envoy to Surabaya. He is to summon Prince Pekik of Surengewuh." With these words the audience ended.

## Gambuh 1.

### 1.
The envoy set out and arrived at Surengewuh. He proceeded to the settlement of Ngararempah. Prince Pekik hurried to greet the king's messenger.

### 2.
After exchanging courtesies, Prince Pekik spoke: "Uncle, I offer you a welcome of blessing." Alap-alap gave thanks and conveyed the king's command.

### 3.
"The Prince—I am sent by your elder brother the king, His Highness Sultan Agung of Mataram, who conveys his blessing to you both."

### 4.
The Prince gave thanks. After receiving the blessing, he was told the king's order: he was summoned with his wife, children, and household.

### 5.
Prince Pekik answered: "Ah, uncle, I am truly astonished. The Sultan wishes to involve a poor wretch like me, a nobody in this village—I never dreamed of such a thing."

### 6.
"Yet I do not refuse my own body; I am like flotsam on the sea. Whatever the Sultan desires, I follow. I have no will but to obey his command."

### 7.
The Tumenggung who heard these gentle, moving words was touched to the heart. He said to Prince Pekik: "If it pleases you, my lord, we shall depart tomorrow morning."

### 8.
The Prince replied softly: "Excellent, my Tumenggung. I shall make my preparations now. You and your men should rest here in the lodgings."

### 9.
The Prince then commanded his wife and all his household to prepare themselves to go to Mataram. All busied themselves with their preparations.

### 10.
Ki Alap-alap quickly sent for the Tumenggung of Surabaya known as Ki Sapanjang. Not long afterward he arrived, and was told the king's command.

### 11.
"The Prince will depart for Mataram tomorrow morning. All his retinue are to accompany him and guard the rear. I will cover all expenses."

### 12.
Ki Sapanjang hurriedly gave orders to all his men: the troops of Surabaya were roused. All made ready, and at dawn they departed.

### 13.
Their journey was gradual; the length of time is not told. They arrived at the village of Butuh and rested for a night. At nightfall, the Prince visited the hermitage at Butuh.

### 14.
He sat in meditation, surrendering himself to God. At midnight, Prince Pekik heard a resonant voice, speaking to him: "Listen, Ki Pekik—the will of the All-Seeing One is this."

### 15.
"Know that in future you shall have a grandson, a boy of extraordinary beauty. He will become a great king ruling the land of Java, commanding a vast host. But he will move the palace."

### 16.
"To the land of Pajang, west of the city. The settlement of Wanakarta shall become the kingdom of Kartasura, renowned in time. And the title of the king shall be..."

### 17.
"His Highness Susuhunan..." There was a pause. "...Prabu Senapati in NgAlaga, Abdurrahman Sayidin Panatagama. The voice ceased. He awoke startled at the hour of dawn, at the call to Subuh prayer."

### 18.
When morning came, he met the caretaker who guarded the gate. Prince Pekik told the caretaker of the revelation he had received in the night. The caretaker was amazed.

### 19.
He bowed and said: "My heart overflows with joy. Thanks be to God—Alhamdulillahi Rabbil Alamin." The sun had risen above the rim. The Prince then departed.

### 20.
His journey is not described; he arrived at the city of Mataram. The Tumenggung Alap-alap went ahead to inform the king. The tale pauses to describe what followed.

### 21.
The King of Mataram was holding audience upon the high dais. Panembahan Purbaya was in attendance, surrounded by the full assembly of courtiers before the sovereign.

### 22.
Not long afterward, the Tumenggung Alap-alap appeared before the throne, bowing: "My lord, I was commanded to summon Prince Pekik of Surengewuh. He has now come before you."

### 23.
"With his wife and children; Ki Sapanjang and his men bring up the rear." The king was pleased and spoke gently: "Uncle Purbaya, what is to be done?"

### 24.
"Now that Prince Pekik has arrived, does he bear tribute, or will he himself be the tribute?" Purbaya bowed and replied in accordance with the meaning.

## Kinanthi 1.

### 1.
"In my humble opinion," said Purbaya, "the Prince of Surabaya should render tribute to Your Majesty's feet. None can surpass your exalted station."

### 2.
"You alone rule the realm of Mataram—only one in all the land of Java. Moreover, when summoned, he did not resist; he has already come before the king."

### 3.
"Surrendering body and soul." The Sultan, hearing his uncle's words, was greatly pleased. At that moment the Prince of Surabaya arrived with his wife to pay homage.

### 4.
He came to the south of the banyan grove, his bearing humble and touching. When the king saw him, he ordered him summoned. The prince approached, bowing, and was led up to the great hall.

### 5.
He advanced with bowed head. Those who saw him were dazzled, beholding the prince's radiance—the king himself was awed. His light outshone all, his presence eclipsed the court.

### 6.
When he came before the king, he paid homage close at hand, bowing low to the ground. After presenting his tribute—a kris laid at the right—he touched the king's feet.

### 7.
And he spoke, weeping, his words broken with sobs: "O lord, jewel of the world, gem of Java—you are rightly honored and revered, established as God's vicegerent."

### 8.
"Truly priest-like in bearing, calm in wisdom, generous in love, free in spirit. You show grace to your servants, seeking their welfare without end, bestowing kindness mingled with sweetness."

### 9.
"Your kin and subjects, low and high, none suffer harshness from you—all are guided by your will, revered in body and soul. The people of the Mountain Kingdom are enfolded in your supreme wisdom."

### 10.
"Righteous and virtuous, unequaled on earth, a fitting sovereign, clear-sighted in just rule—your fame wafts like fragrance. You are beloved of God."

### 11.
"Mighty and fearless in adversity, revered on all sides, embracing the noble faith, you are as the Prophet himself—Muhammad's teachings brought to Java, adorning the realm of Mataram."

### 12.
"O lord and king—your servant appears before you, surrendering life and death, aware of his great failings, indulging in pride, truly young and foolish."

### 13.
"Lest I be shrouded in forgetfulness of the king's grace—you granted me a noble life, the joy of food and rest. All my children, subjects, and kin are nothing but blessings from the king."

### 14.
"Endless be your peace. How could your servant ever offer fitting tribute? O lord, I am truly young and ignorant, far from knowing what is good."

### 15.
"If Your Highness does not forgive your servant, then truly I shall be forever wretched. But now I am wholly at your mercy, subject to the king's will."

### 16.
Thus the king spoke within his heart: "This man is handsome and honest, clear and precise in speech, steadfast, brave, and open-hearted. He deserves nothing false."

### 17.
"My heart is well pleased with him. He is fit to be my companion in authority, to propagate the royal line through Java, to sustain the prophetic light." At last he spoke gently.

### 18.
"Sit, my younger brother. Tell me, what is on your mind? The reason I summoned you before me is that it is already decreed by the will of God."

### 19.
"Think of me as the spout and you as the pool that receives—you are the vessel, and I am the contents. The meaning of what I say has already been written: you will never be parted. In time to come you will help engender the kings of Java—you will provide the maternal line."

### 20.
"And I will provide the paternal line. Now this is my will: you shall dwell in Mataram, near the palace, alongside the Kadipaten. As for the land of Surabaya—"

### 21.
"—I return it to you. You may rule it as before. Ki Sapanjang shall remain as its guardian, keeping order and peace in the land."

### 22.
"Remain here in Mataram—do not hesitate. Let us consider them as one: Mataram and Surabaya." Prince Pekik's heart was eased; his dream was fulfilled.

### 23.
He prostrated himself and spoke: "O lord and god-king, your servant accepts and honors the king's command—as though flooded with treasure, showered with honey and sweet nectar."

## Dhandhanggula 1.

### 1.
Then the king rose, and all who had been in attendance dispersed. The prince, with his wife and children, kin and retainers, was assigned a dwelling called the Surabayan quarter. The prince remained there, enjoying honor and prosperity in Mataram without any distress of heart. Thus passed a considerable time.

### 2.
The king's favor continued abundantly. Prince Pekik was married to the king's younger sister, of the same father and mother, named Ratu Pandhansari. Their marriage was harmonious—they loved each other day and night, like mimi and mintuna fish, inseparable.

### 3.
After forty days, Sultan Agung of the Mountain Kingdom was seated in the great hall. He spoke: "Servant—go quickly to the Surabayan quarter. Find my sister and tell her: if she is willing, I summon her husband. Tell her I am ill."

### 4.
The king's messenger departed and reached the Surabayan quarter. The prince and his wife came out and sat in the outer hall. The attendant bowed: "I am sent by the king, your elder brother. He summons the queen, your wife. I am to accompany her."

### 5.
"His Highness is ill. The king's order is this: if her husband permits, let her enter the palace to see my illness. But if her husband does not consent, she must not come. It is entirely in his hands, and he must not be disobeyed."

### 6.
She hastened and soon arrived before the Sultan. She bowed and touched his feet. Ratu Pandhansari spoke: "Elder brother, why so urgent a summons? My heart trembles, I am all aflutter—what illness afflicts you, brother?"

### 7.
The Sultan answered softly: "O my beloved, this is no ordinary sickness. It is lodged in my heart. All remedies fail; the more I dwell on it, the worse it grows. There is a holy man in Giri who still refuses to submit to me. That is the sickness in my heart—and no cure has yet been found."

### 8.
"Other than Giri, everyone across Java has submitted—none dares oppose me. Only Giri remains unconquered. I could take it by force, but from of old it has been ordained that the only one who can resolve this is your husband, by virtue of his noble descent from the line of Ampelgadhing."

### 9.
"Now go home, sister. Persuade your husband to undertake this task—do not delay." Ratu Pandhansari smiled and answered softly: "I obey your command, elder brother." She withdrew, followed by all her attendants, walking with a slow and measured step.

### 10.
Prince Pekik was waiting in the pavilion. When the queen arrived, he greeted her and led her inside. She touched his feet. The prince grew anxious: "Sister, what is the matter? You seem dispirited. What illness afflicts the king?" Ratu Pandhansari bowed her head and said nothing. The prince grew more troubled.

### 11.
"Perhaps the king is not truly ill," he thought. "Some other concern weighs upon him." He drew the queen into their private chamber—but what passed between them in their intimacy is not told. When they had rested, Ratu Pandhansari whispered the king's true intent.

### 12.
She explained the king's command. Prince Pekik stood firm in heart, stroking his mustache. He spoke slowly: "If only I had known the Sultan's wish from the first—he seeks Giri's destruction. In truth, I gave my pledge long ago. My submission to Mataram was already given."

### 13.
"Giri is already in my grasp. I am the one to break it open. Your brother is truly impatient. A thousand shames if I cannot subdue Giri—I would be too ashamed to face the sun. How can I repay the king's favor, but by offering my head as token?"

### 14.
"Come, sister, let us go to the palace and take our leave of the Sultan. We shall depart this very day." Together they went before the king. When the Sultan saw the prince and his wife approaching, he quickly invited them to sit beside him. The prince knelt.

### 15.
The Sultan spoke gently: "Younger brother of Surabaya, what is your intention? Giri has not yet submitted, has not paid homage to me. I entrust this matter to you." Prince Pekik bowed: "My lord, your servant will undertake it. I honor the king's command."

### 16.
"Even if Sunan Giri summons a thousand kings with all their hosts, my heart fears nothing. If I cannot break Giri, I will gladly die on the battlefield. I ask your leave to depart now."

### 17.
"I ask only your blessing, O king. But let me leave your younger sister here in Mataram. To go to war with a troubled heart would be my undoing. Remember your sister, my lord—let her stay."

### 18.
The king smiled: "It is my will that you take your wife. She is both your spouse and my sister. In life or death she must not be parted from you. It is the way of warriors: if you fall in battle, she would be taken anyway."

### 19.
Prince Pekik laughed softly: "Your sister is my burden, then—we shall die together as you command." Together they took leave, touching the king's feet. The Sultan said: "Go, sister—surrender yourself to your husband."

### 20.
The Sultan prayed to God for their safety and victory. The couple withdrew, provided with all manner of provisions. When they reached the Surabayan quarter, they assembled palanquins, litters, and all the accoutrements of war.

### 21.
When all was ready, they departed from Mataram. The journey is not described; they soon arrived in Surabaya. Ki Sapanjang came to meet them. After a brief rest, the prince took his place in the pavilion.

### 22.
Prince Pekik addressed his men: "Listen, all you people of Surabaya. By the king's command, I am sent to wage war against a great enemy."

### 23.
"The submission of Susuhunan Giri has been entrusted to me. My wish is this: if the lord of Giri submits peacefully, no one will die. But if he refuses, we fight—and many souls will be lost."

## Mêgatruh 1.

### 1.
The soldiers bowed and answered: "We welcome your command, Prince Pekik. We praise your desire for peace—let us not come to war."

### 2.
"But if Susuhunan Giri refuses to submit peacefully, it cannot be helped. He must be brought before the king, even if he persists in defiance."

### 3.
"Your servants are ready. If we cannot accomplish it, we will gladly perish in battle."

### 4.
"But I have heard that Sunan Giri, though weakened, still has strength to match Mataram."

### 5.
"He has a Chinese disciple, said to be of royal lineage, whom he has adopted. His name is Endrasena—a formidable warrior."

### 6.
"Tough and fierce in battle, with two hundred skilled men, he strengthens Giri's defiance."

### 7.
"And those who follow him do the work of devils, shrouding good hearts in darkness."

### 8.
"Even if the king of the rainbow came, your servants would not waver." Prince Pekik answered: "I accept your words with gratitude."

### 9.
"It may be that when I meet the son of Giri face to face, battle may be averted. We shall see."

### 10.
"Still, prepare for war. If it comes to battle, your lord is ready. Let the warriors of Surabaya prove themselves."

### 11.
Prince Pekik returned to the great hall. The audience dispersed. After the Isha prayer, the prince slipped away unseen.

### 12.
He went directly to Giri and entered the palace. Susunan Giri sat surrounded by attendants. Endrasena was always before him. The Sunan spoke softly.

### 13.
"Listen, my son. The Sultan of Mataram demands my submission. If I refuse, war will come—Giri will be destroyed."

### 14.
"The envoy is Prince Pekik of Surengewuh. But he is a son to me. His wife Ratu Pandhansari shares the burden."

### 15.
"What do you think, my son? I am old—I follow your conviction. Whether submission is good or not, you bear the weight."

### 16.
Endrasena's ears burned as if torn; his eyes blazed with smoke. He spoke hastily: "Father, pillar of Java, saint of God—whatever you wish will come to pass."

### 17.
"Has Sultan Agung not heard that Giri shelters a great warrior from China?"

### 18.
"Now he sends a mere boy, bringing his wife to war—let him come himself to face me!"

### 19.
"No, father, do not submit. Let the Sultan come to Giri and pay homage."

### 20.
Prince Pekik arrived with the greeting of peace. The sage replied and welcomed him. They seated themselves face to face.

### 21.
Sunan Giri welcomed him: "Father Prince Pekik, is all well?" The prince answered: "All is well."

### 22.
They exchanged courtesies. "Whatever there is, it is yours—no trouble at all."

### 23.
"O father, why does Your Holiness come so late, alone? What urgent matter brings you to Giri Kadhaton?"

## Pocung 1.

### 1.
Endrasena and all the attendants sat silent, not one daring to speak.

### 2.
The prince faced the sage with unflinching gaze. Susunan Giri remained calm, steadfast in heart.

### 3.
The Prince of Surabaya did not hesitate, yet to behold him was fearsome as a lion.

### 4.
His bearing was bold but his words were gentle: "Now, Sunan of the Mountain, hear me. Sultan Agung is sovereign of all Java."

### 5.
"Supreme in this world—noble, righteous, mighty, perfect in rule."

### 6.
"He crushes all who refuse to submit. I am sent by His Highness who seeks victory."

### 7.
"My lord Sultan Agung asks: what is your final answer?"

### 8.
"If you desire peace, that your kingdom may endure, pay homage to the Sultan."

### 9.
"Bring your wife, children, troops, and treasures."

### 10.
"Come with me to Mataram. If you follow my guidance, you shall live."

### 11.
"Refuse, and nothing remains—Giri will be turned to barren waste."

### 12.
"My lord the Conqueror of the Earth is wise, mighty, and just."

### 13.
"All your worldly glory is but a flash of lightning—do not let it be snatched away."

### 14.
"Speak plainly, without hesitation. A steadfast heart is what matters."

### 15.
Sunan Giri sighed: "Wa Allahu a'lam. God knows best. I submit to His will."

### 16.
The Prince of Surengewuh, hearing this, withdrew from the sage's presence.

### 17.
After he left, they prepared for war. Endrasena was made commander.

### 18.
The sage issued his commands to all disciples and troops throughout Giri.

### 19.
At dawn the prince returned to Surabaya. The certainty of war was discussed.

### 20.
Sunan Giri refused to submit. The troops assembled on the square; the queen rode in a palanquin.

### 21.
She circled the ranks, rewarding those eager for battle with money and clothing of every kind.

### 22.
The war-drums boomed, signalling the advance. The vanguard marched forth like the flowing of a river.

### 23.
Then came the queen's palanquin, and the prince behind on horseback, not far, surrounded by the mass of troops.

### 24.
Beyond count and not described, they arrived at the Giri palace, surrounding it with ranks arrayed—the press of hearts like those yearning for love.

## Asmaradana 1.

### 1.
The lines were drawn; the Surabaya troops besieged the palace of Giri, ready for war. The tale now turns: the holy man of Giri had been told.

### 2.
That His Highness Prince Pekik and his wife had surrounded the city with a sea of troops, filling every space. The people of the Mountain Realm, men and women alike, were in turmoil like grain tossed by the winnowing wind.

### 3.
Sunan Giri held court, surrounded by his forces arrayed before him, all ready for battle, awaiting his command. There was a son of the sage, born of a junior wife.

### 4.
His name was Raden Jayengresmi. He knelt and spoke to his father: "O father, my pillar of faith, what will become of your resolve? My heart is full of doubt. Do you truly intend to meet the Sultan of the Mountain Kingdom in battle?"

### 5.
"Your child speaks—forgive my presumption, I am young and ignorant of what is fitting. But if it may please you, it would be better for us all to submit to Sultan Agung of Mataram."

### 6.
"Do not take the wrong course. I have heard the king of the Mountain Kingdom is noble in heart and gentle in conduct—his spirit is complete and pure. Truly he is exalted, his influence the radiance of a clear mind."

### 7.
"He is virtuous and good—it is not good to oppose him. While the conflict has not yet erupted, it would be wise to follow his will. This is your servant's thought: to resist is to invite war."

### 8.
"But if my counsel is rejected, the envoy's purpose is fixed. It will not be Your Holiness who begins the war—it will be his own doing, and he will bear it well."

### 9.
Sunan Giri persisted; his son's words could not move him. The young hero withdrew, weeping along the way, his heart wrung. Such was the decree of God Almighty—destiny cannot be avoided.

### 10.
After his son had withdrawn, the king-priest spoke: "Endrasena, what do you think? The Surabaya forces have arrived and are besieging us." Endrasena answered: "Giri's troops stand ready."

### 11.
"We await your command, father." "My son Endrasena, what is there to think? Sound the signal. Lead your troops to crush the Surengewuh lines. You shall be commander."

### 12.
"As you command," said Endrasena, bowing to touch his father's feet. "O teacher, my guide, give your blessing. Trust me—the retreat of Surengewuh is already in my hands."

### 13.
He beat his war-banner, blowing upon it again and again, crying "Victory! Victory!" He sounded the signal. The troops were roused, and Endrasena's men set out first.

### 14.
Now a prose account: The Surabaya forces were initially driven back, but then rallied under the command of Ratu Pandhansari. It is told that three of Sunan Giri's children fled during the heat of battle. Raden Jayengresmi was accompanied by a servant; Raden Jayengresmi together with his younger sister Niken Rancangkapti were also accompanied by santris. They went their separate ways, constantly searching for one another. In the end, the Surabaya soldiers prevailed. Giri's champion Endrasena was captured. Sunan Giri submitted and was brought to Mataram with his entire family and all his treasures.

### 15.
Two hundred men were counted, all bearing weapons, their muskets slung from shoulder straps, dressed all in white. They had all converted to Islam—"Sabilillah!" was their cry—following behind Endrasena.

### 16.
His weapons were prized: two pistols tucked at his belt, a sabre from Karalok at his side, krises left and right, and a banner on his back inscribed with the Lord's name.

### 17.
He murmured incantations, invoking the Lord's name, crying "Sabilillah!" Then all the clerics, scholars, officials, and troops of Giri dressed in white.

### 18.
United in the spirit of death, they vied with one another. The Giri forces and the Surengewuh troops faced the signals of war—drums, gongs, tambourines—while the soldiers' roar thundered like a splitting mountain.

### 19.
The battle raged—friend and foe indistinguishable. The Giri men fought mightily, pressing wave after wave. The Surabaya troops could not hold, and their lines broke.

### 20.
Endrasena rampaged through the ranks like the hero Raden Seta, scattered from his own men. Whoever he struck fled in all directions; those nearby he cut down, none daring to rally.

### 21.
The Surabaya soldiers could not wield their weapons; they stumbled, overwhelmed. The Giri warriors fought like wounded bulls, resolved to die to a man.

### 22.
Then the enemy grew terrified, unable to withstand Giri's assault. Many Surabaya men were wounded, many slain. The Giri warriors were jubilant.

### 23.
Only a few of Giri's men fell. The Surabaya troops, weary, withdrew slowly, trying not to be noticed. But the Giri forces pressed the attack fiercely, like a suitor storming a maiden's heart.

## Sinom 1.

### 1.
They fought an entire day, the Surabaya forces defeated, retreating as Giri pursued. When night fell, the pursuing Giri troops turned back, shouting in triumph, playing angklung and dancing.

### 2.
Endrasena and his men, together with all Giri's forces, appeared before the king-priest, bowing to report that the Surengewuh troops had retreated in disarray, none daring to rally. Many leaders had fallen.

### 3.
The king-priest was greatly pleased, but his joy was clouded by pride. He felt favored by God, believing he could rule all Java. Vainglory took hold—his holiness was lost to desire.

### 4.
At last he spoke: "Thanks be to God—Alhamdulillah! Listen, my son Endrasena—this is a sign. The Prince of Surabaya has been thwarted. The people of Mataram will now feel afraid."

### 5.
Endrasena answered eagerly: "The fighting was overtaken by night. Your troops are weary and the Maghrib prayer has come. Tomorrow I will surely bind him and drag him to Mataram. Thanks be to God—in war there is no double effort."

### 6.
"I will take them all at once—Prince Pekik and the Sultan himself will bow before you." The sage answered softly: "Then I will pray for you, my son. While I still hold my office, you have nothing to fear. Now I give Giri a new name—"

### 7.
"—it shall be called the realm of Sokaraja." The servants bore witness. "Tonight, celebrate—recite the Maulud, sing dhikr and singir. Reward the common soldiers who have just come from battle, so their courage may grow. But do not neglect caution."

### 8.
"Tomorrow your enemy may return in force." Endrasena and his men answered: "They have lost heart, too afraid to return. Their leader is nowhere to be found. Tomorrow we shall see them submitting to the Sultan."

### 9.
That night the Giri troops feasted. Now the tale turns to those who fled the battle—the Prince of Surabaya and the queen his wife. Their troops had reassembled. The prince was downcast, his heart heavy at the devastation of his forces.

### 10.
Ki Sapanjang, commander of the war, spoke to Prince Pekik: "My lord, your servants report truly: Endrasena's fighting is terrifying. The Mountain warriors are ferocious, resolved to die, rampaging like wounded bulls."

### 11.
All the Surabaya soldiers said the same: they felt powerless, no longer eager to fight. The prince, hearing his officers, grew more despondent and said nothing.

### 12.
Then Ratu Pandhansari, seeing her husband in distress, could not speak. At last she addressed him gently: "My lord husband—if the war goes so badly, I beg your leave—"

### 13.
"—allow me to take command." The prince was startled by her words. He asked gently: "O my heart, jewel of the world—what do you intend?"

### 14.
"Your troops are many dead; those who remain are afraid. If you would allow me, perhaps I might turn their hearts." The prince embraced her: "Truly, sister, you are the soul of Surabaya."

### 15.
"Whatever you wish—I will not oppose you. I am but a shadow following you. I have surrendered my life and body to you." What passed that night is not told. At dawn Ratu Pandhansari sat with her husband.

### 16.
In a decorated pavilion, surrounded by the troops, the queen spoke: "Hear me, all my soldiers. The king my brother commanded me to wage this war. He gave me over eight thousand ryals and many fine garments."

### 17.
"All this I give to you—share it among the steadfast." The troops received their rewards; their spirits lifted. The queen spoke again: "My soldiers, all who have received—"

### 18.
"—your devotion is clear: following your lord to war, steadfast in hardship. I will repay you a hundredfold—this is not yet the full measure."

### 19.
"It is as if you seek glory—but my repayment is only this. The people of Surabaya, accustomed to honor, have been brought low by me to misery."

### 20.
"Bound together in suffering, serving a compassionate lord—yet I have caused this. What will become of me? I once enjoyed prosperity, but now I have dragged everyone into misery. Countless dead, driven by the urge to repay my lord. My husband and I are at fault. We pile misfortune upon heroism."

### 21.
"As for your reply, it has come to pass. I am the one indebted—shown clearly in battle, fighting against wild santris aided by Chinese converts whose power surpasses our troops."

### 22.
"Perhaps it is God's will that Surabaya is now made different from before, when all the eastern lands took shelter under Surabaya's protection."

### 23.
"In ancient times their hearts were faithful—the very bulls of Java, who knew no retreat. But now many turn away from the loyal service of old."

## Pangkur 1.

### 1.
"Now, all my soldiers—let us take the better course. Do not fear death by weapons. Though we be shamed, we shall endure, as long as loyalty remains."

### 2.
"Go now, disperse—I forgive you all. Return each to your home. Take these rewards as provisions, and care for your families."

### 3.
"I will return to Mataram, to report to my brother the king, surrendering my life. Tell him your brother has fallen against an old holy man aided by Chinese converts."

### 4.
"Their weapons are ordinary—santris with tambourines—yet they steal the heart's resolve. Much blood has been spilled; many bear wounds unto death."

### 5.
"Those who remain are broken in spirit. It is I who am at fault. Let my husband and I alone face this. Go now—may fortune be with you."

### 6.
The officers answered, weeping: "O lady, do not withdraw so soon. Behold your troops—the devotion of Surabaya."

### 7.
"We await the moment when the enemy's power is spent. Even unto death, your troops will not waver."

### 8.
"If your wish is unfulfilled, better to fall in war. What purpose is life without serving the lord's will?"

### 9.
"All who witness will say the Surabaya people are base. Yet we have a king who is God's vicegerent—even so we do not serve him fully."

### 10.
"Ah, what worthless men!" The officers sobbed. Ki Tumenggung Sapanjang cried: "Curse us! Enslave us all!"

### 11.
"In life and death we do not part from the prince. Let us perish first. The Surabaya warriors will serve in war unto death."

### 12.
"Let not one remain—let us all be slain by Sunan Giri. If you reach the afterlife first, await news: we met the defiant Sunan in the midst of death."

### 13.
"Let the enemy remain our enemy. Until we have avenged the wrong done to you, our lord." The queen and her husband heard with joyful hearts.

### 14.
The officers' words raised their spirits. Their vow came from the heart, with no falsehood. They begged leave to join battle, to destroy Prawata palace this very day.

### 15.
"If they become captives, their wealth, wives and children taken—let us all die together in this war," the queen said softly.

### 16.
The soldiers bowed: "Trust in the destruction of Giri. Remain in camp and rest yourself, my lord." The queen answered: "Your kindness touches me—"

### 17.
"—I am deeply grateful, and may God repay you. But my heart is set and cannot be swayed." The soldiers bowed. The queen continued: "When we reach Giri—"

### 18.
"—let this be the order of battle: my men of Surabaya, fierce and ready, take up your weapons before me. Let my husband and I be your vanguard. All of you, conceal yourselves until the fight is joined."

### 19.
"I will give the signal: when my pistol has fired three times, at the height of the fray, charge swiftly. Heed my words." The soldiers answered "As you command," and withdrew to arrange the ranks.

### 20.
The soldiers rejoiced, shouting with glee, their spirits restored. None were faint of heart. All were resolved to die, and the Surabaya men were filled with joy.

### 21.
The signal was sounded, and the Surabaya troops departed. Porters led the way in garments of many colors—red, yellow, black, white, green, violet—bearing poles and spears, like scattered blossoms.

### 22.
Ratu Pandhan brought up the rear in her palanquin; the prince was not far behind on horseback, surrounded by troops. The soldiers pressed on, their demeanor like fierce lions, with no thought of retreat.

## Durma 1.

### 1.
The journey is not described. The Surabaya troops had entered Giri's territory, and the people of Giri were alarmed. The soldiers in the rear veered off and hid themselves, unseen.

### 2.
Now is told of Susunan of Prawata, who sat comfortably in audience with Endrasena, attended by his troops. The only topic was victory in war. The Sunan spoke softly.

### 3.
"Endrasena, what of your enemy? Will they not return?" Endrasena answered: "My lord, I think they have continued their retreat, unable to withstand my assault."

### 4.
"If they dare to return, they will be like tinder to fire. Even if the Sultan of Mataram hears of my deeds in war, he will tremble at my power."

### 5.
Hardly had they finished speaking when a commotion arose outside—cries that the enemy had arrived, wearing five-colored garments, their commander riding in a palanquin at the rear. Susunan Giri was startled.

### 6.
"My sons, sound the signal! The enemy has come suddenly. Be on guard—I will pray for your safety." Endrasena withdrew quickly, sounded the signal, and roused the Giri troops.

### 7.
Swiftly they donned their war regalia and issued from the city, from the fortress. The signal for battle was given, drums beaten, shouts rising to the heavens.

### 8.
The forces of Giri and Surapringga clashed together, exchanging thrusts, friend and foe indistinguishable. Weapons fired in volleys. The Giri troops trampled boldly, their assault like wounded bulls.

### 9.
The Surapringga forces were like fierce lions, ferocious and terrifying, all resolved to die. The dead were trampled underfoot. The battle was a tangled chaos, and none sought to flee.

### 10.
They felt the favor of their lady and were determined to repay her kindness with their lives. Locked in battle, they traded dagger-thrusts, spear-thrusts, and club-blows in turn.

### 11.
Some fought with bare hands, grappling and striking. Others used spells and incantations, blowing charms at one another, whispering mantras under their breath.

### 12.
Endrasena's fury grew fiercer, his sword flashing as he struck. Those he charged scattered, unable to withstand his assault. Many were wounded; the dead beyond count.

### 13.
They scattered and fled to their lady's side. Ratu Pandhan and Prince Pekik rebuked their troops: "Hey, Surabaya men, where are you going?" The soldiers answered: "We need to rest awhile."

### 14.
After resting briefly they attacked again. Endrasena and his troops were wildly jubilant, truly ferocious in their assault. The queen watched with pity.

### 15.
She spoke to her husband: "My brother, the Chinese commander is drunk with battle—ripe for a bullet." The prince nodded, and the queen quickly took aim.

### 16.
She aimed her pistol and fired. Endrasena was hit—his right hand was wounded, his sword fell broken. He fought on with his dagger in his left hand. She fired again, wounding both hands.

### 17.
Still he fought on, leaping, stamping, biting ears. Ratu Pandhan quickly aimed her pistol and hit Endrasena's leg. He fell at once.

### 18.
Hearing the three pistol shots, the Surabaya soldiers swiftly rushed into battle from left and right. Those attacked were thrown into confusion; the Giri men did not realize the Surabaya forces had been reinforced.

### 19.
They thought only the visible troops remained, their hearts swollen with pride, so they were off guard. The Mataram trick worked—the Chinese troops were overwhelmed, attacked from right and left.

### 20.
Endrasena stood dazed in the midst of battle. The Giri and Chinese troops had lost heart; their fighting spirit flagged. The Surabaya men were all resolved to die.

### 21.
They destroyed their enemies, who lay scattered like felled banana stalks. Endrasena was pierced by spears, crushed to dust, mingled with the earth. All his troops were wiped out.

### 22.
They scattered and fled, throwing away their weapons, all seeking to save their lives. Some rolled into ravines, others hid in caves, some plunged into the sea, others climbed mountains, pursued by shouts that grew ever more terrifying.

### 23.
Not a single Giri soldier was to be seen—the area was clean as if swept. The Surabaya troops cheered again and again, a sign of victory in war. Giri was besieged, surrounded by ranks.

### 24.
Now is told of Sunan Giri's children from a junior wife—three daughters of the same father and mother. Their hearts were greatly troubled, like gold tossed upon the waters.

## Maskumambang 1.

### 1.
The eldest was named Raden Jayengresmi. In the heat of battle he searched for his two younger siblings, Jayengsari and Rancangkapti.

### 2.
He wished to take them both away to safety, but within the palace they were lost in the turmoil and could not be found. The prince was deeply grieved.

### 3.
He fled from Giri seeking his younger siblings, uncertain where to go. No one could give him news. The prince went his way.

### 4.
Now is told of Raden Jayengsari, who was not parted from the weeping Niken Rancangkapti, as they searched for their elder brother.

### 5.
They did not find him. The santri Buras reported: "O my master, Giri has lost the war. Without doubt it will be plundered."

### 6.
"Your elder brother Raden Jayengresmi has already been taken to Mataram, bereft of his servants who once followed him."

### 7.
Hearing the words of the santri Buras, Raden Jayengsari spoke, his tears streaming: "Little sister, what do you wish to do?"

### 8.
Niken Rancangkapti answered through her tears: "Brother, I cannot bear to be parted from you. Wherever you go, I will follow."

### 9.
Raden Jayengsari's heart grew even more troubled: "Sister, I intend to follow our elder brother Jayengresmi—in life and death, not to be parted."

### 10.
Buras said: "Allow me, your servant, to follow wherever you go." So they set out, crossing steep and rugged terrain.

### 11.
"Come, sister, let us leave now," he said. His sister answered: "Brother, let me dress first and gather my playthings."

### 12.
"Buras, bring my toys later—the woven basket, the little bed, the small shield, the sack of old coins."

### 13.
"Also the mortar, frying pan, brazier, hearth, pot, and kettle; the ladle, the scrapers, the spatula, the slats, the little pins; the small sack of rice."

### 14.
"The small basket of spices—don't let any spill—and the brick fragments, the jamblang flowers, the coconut dregs, the burnt turmeric, the sooty pan."

### 15.
Buras asked: "How am I to carry all this? Put some in bags. I almost forgot—the little nyamplung seeds in the garden!"

### 16.
"My little treasures I'd better carry myself, lest you lose them along the way. And my kitten—take it too."

### 17.
"If you get tired, I'll carry you instead." Buras sighed: "O master, what next? Just go to bed properly!"

### 18.
"Come, Buras, help me fetch—" Raden Jayengsari heard terrifying shouts and quickly seized his younger sister.

### 19.
Quickly hoisted by the santri Buras from behind: "Brother, get down! My gear is still here. If I leave it, it might be lost."

### 20.
Buras said everything was accounted for, already packed and carried. "Where is my little treasure? And my kitten? If they're lost..."

### 21.
Raden Jayengsari had gone his way, entering the forest, descending into a steep ravine. Now the story turns to another.

### 22.
The queen and her husband the prince ascended the mountain, surrounded by soldiers and retainers.

### 23.
The wife of the great sage is told of—she came out of the palace, running and weeping at the sight of the battle lines before her.

### 24.
Arriving before them, she bowed low, kissed their feet, and spoke piteously through her tears: "O my lord, my pillar—"

### 25.
"—you have come to the settlement of Giri—a thousand blessings, like a shower of flowers, the emergence of a noble king."

## Mijil 1.

### 1.
"The jewel of the earth does not tarry—he comes with all his company. Forgive this devotion of your servant, the priest of Giri. Let it not end in death—this is my earnest prayer."

### 2.
"Your child in Giri was truly swayed by words, seduced by sweet speech that concealed the sage's virtue, and so became dangerous—his very life forfeited."

### 3.
"Endrasena is a devil from abroad, a prince in his own house; he would rival the lord who rules Java. In the end he died and brought the sage with him."

### 4.
"Your child is truly ignorant, defiant of his king. What does he rely upon? My lord, this is my faithful report—believe it in your heart."

### 5.
The Prince of Surabaya spoke softly: "Do not worry. If his heart comes to its senses and he is willing to submit to Mataram, surrendering his life to Sultan Agung—"

### 6.
"—if he obeys, I will not kill him. But if he persists in defiance, then he and his wife and children will be utterly destroyed in Giri—leveled with the earth, not a sprout remaining."

### 7.
"I never imagined it would come to all-out war. I advised him many times before, but he paid no heed—now I am silent."

### 8.
"Tell your husband quickly—if his heart still doubts, his purpose still fixed, he is too ashamed to retreat after serving that devil. He does not value his life, and is content to be destroyed."

### 9.
"Perhaps it is only a flash of lightning. Giri is already exhausted. Yet if he now feels his fault and is willing to submit to Mataram, tell him to come out—without his dagger."

### 10.
The queen bowed and withdrew. The ruler waited outside with her husband. Then the queen of Giri touched the feet of the king-priest.

### 11.
Her words were full of pity: "O my lord, your father together with his wife—Lady Ratu Pandhansari has arrived and is outside, bringing a great army."

### 12.
"Her command to me, your servant: 'His lordship awaits. Do not cause him to forget—if he wishes to submit to Mataram, Prince Pekik guarantees his safety.'"

### 13.
"'You, your wife, and your children may continue to reign in Giri for generations to come. But if not, then Giri's kingdom will surely be destroyed, all wiped out.'"

### 14.
"You are summoned to come out—you have been awaited long. If you delay, perhaps he will be angered, thinking you still shelter that devil. Come, my lord, and do not wear your dagger."

### 15.
Sunan Giri was mindful; his heart was anxious. Hastily he dressed, and without wearing his dagger he went out, staff in hand, bowing as he walked.

### 16.
When he arrived before Prince Pekik, they exchanged hurried greetings. After the greeting, he touched the prince's feet, crouching low before him, like a blossom fallen to earth, his fear so great.

### 17.
Prince Pekik addressed him: "This is the end of the road. What is your wish now? Be resolute, do not waver. Which will you choose—death or submission?"

### 18.
"If you submit, I will take you now to my country—you, your wife, and all your children, the treasures and all within Giri, and all your troops—not one left behind."

### 19.
"Afterward, you will worship the exalted Sultan—I myself will intercede for you." The sage answered softly: "I do not refuse, my lord. I will follow your will."

### 20.
"Let there be no thought of pain—even unto death, your servant will not resist. I only await the king's pleasure. I remember the teaching of old."

### 21.
"The will of God has turned—the high is brought low, yet the thread is not cut. How then can I stand aside?" The prince heard this with a heavy heart.

### 22.
He spoke softly: "Do not be anxious. I will bear the burden. Trust in the Lord of all creation. Come now, let us go quickly." Sunan Giri withdrew—all was made ready.

### 23.
All was ready, nothing lacking. The prince departed; his younger sister rode in a palanquin. Sunan Giri was carried in front with his wife and children, guarded by a great host.

### 24.
The Surabaya soldiers whispered among themselves and to the young prince: "It would be best if Sunan Giri is now made to meet the lord's will—a matter of death, of Gambuh-style pitched battle."

## Gambuh 1.

### 1.
"To engage in pitched battle brings great loss, causes suffering to the common people, tears the kingdom apart with many dead."

### 2.
"Clearly it has been spent—increased unto death. Destroyed, no longer of benefit, faith has been scattered, lost in surrender to the All-Seeing."

### 3.
"They fill their purpose—outside and within, their intent ensnared, not feeling overshadowed by the king's throne. The sage's insight is like an anvil, solid—only its base is mere exterior."

### 4.
Now that they were gathered, some in front pulled from behind, Prince Pekik laughed and spoke softly: "All you say is true. It has been spent and destroyed."

### 5.
"But by the command of His Majesty Sultan Agung, I am not authorized to kill—it is not my task. The king's will is greater."

### 6.
"The tradition of the great ones: the time is not yet now. In the future, someone will seize the palace of Giri—the blood of Sultan Agung mingled with my blood."

### 7.
All the soldiers bowed, their hearts deeply stirred, their spirits glad that their intent would be fulfilled. Then they continued their march and soon arrived at Surengewuh.

### 8.
Sunan Giri was quartered, his troops gathered and guarded by Surabaya soldiers. The queen intended to depart the following morning.

### 9.
The spoils and plunder had been counted. Sunan Giri said to Prince Pekik: "Know this, my lord—three of my children are missing."

### 10.
"Jayengresmi the eldest, and Jayengsari his younger brother; the youngest is Niken Rancangkapti. In the heat of battle they slipped away and fled the palace."

### 11.
"I do not know where they have gone, nor whether you will be angered by their flight. They are of one mother, all three from the same womb." The prince then commanded his Surengewuh troops.

### 12.
"Hey, men, be quick! Search for the sage's children—Jayengresmi, Jayengsari, Rancangkapti. When they are found, treat them gently, and do not let them escape."

### 13.
"Bring them to Mataram. If they resist and will not come, do not wait. If the burden is heavy, report to me in person."

### 14.
Ki Sapanjang had already sent out Surengewuh troops to search. They were scattered in all directions. The next day they were to depart.

### 15.
They were to present themselves at Mataram. The officers were ready, each with his own men. The plunder and spoils were prepared. The signal gongs were struck—a great clamor of drums and gongs.

### 16.
At dawn they set out with a clamor, shouts of victory in war. The queen rode ahead in a palanquin; the prince followed behind on horseback, flanked by attendants.

### 17.
Surrounded by his troops, accompanied and encouraged, Sunan Giri brought up the rear with his wife and children carried along, the plunder not far behind.

### 18.
The ministers were guarded, accompanied and urged on, pressed together by the Surabaya troops, all vigilant against trouble, watched by crowds along the way.

### 19.
The journey is not described. They arrived in the land of Mataram. The people were stirred, eager to see the queen's return with the spoils of war.

### 20.
Sunan Giri was taken captive with his wife and children. All the treasures of Giri were plundered. The onlookers were packed tight, all murmuring in amazement.

### 21.
"Which one is Sunan Giri, the defiant king?" His companions answered, pointing: "There, there, in the litter—the one who is already old, toothless and wrinkled."

### 22.
"Oh, is that so? What did he rely on? Ah, a holy man—perhaps his prayers misfired. And he had help from those worthless Chinese converts."

### 23.
"Now he has been captured. The queen wears the sambel wandu, placed upon all the Giri people. In the end they were helpless—their king could be carried off."

### 24.
