So it went. The queen arrived at the alun-alun. Prince Pekik with his troops and Sunan Giri halted at the great pavilion. The queen proceeded to the palace.

## Dhandhanggula 1.

### 1.
Now is told of the Sultan of Mataram, who had been informed of Ratu Pandhan's return with victory in war—Sunan Giri had been captured and brought to Mataram. The king came out into the middle of the pendapa, surrounded by all his courtiers and attendants.

### 2.
Soon his sister was seen. The Sultan hurried to meet her, embraced her neck, leading her by the hand. After they sat near the king, he spoke gently: "O my sister—your journey, and your husband—I sent you to humble the priest of Giri. Are you both well?"

### 3.
"Where is your husband now?" The queen answered with a bow: "By your grace, my lord king, carrying out your command: I and my husband the prince, and all the Surabaya troops, are all well. Sent on your errand, we have accomplished our task. Sunan Giri has been defeated in war, and now I bring him."

### 4.
"My husband awaits outside, ready for the king's command." The Sultan sent a messenger at once to summon his brother-in-law. Prince Pekik soon arrived at the courtyard, bowing low, sitting humbly on the ground. Quickly beckoned, the prince approached to pay homage, touching the king's feet.

### 5.
"It is well that you have come safely, sister, after only a few days. Have you succeeded in your task?" The prince bowed and answered: "O my lord, God's vicegerent, jewel of the world, favored by the Almighty—by the grace of Your Majesty, I and all the Surabaya troops are safe and well."

### 6.
"We were sent forth to deliver Your Majesty's command to Sunan Giri, that he should not be stubborn—that his erring intent should submit at Your Majesty's feet. I alone went to meet him, without escort, and found him in assembly."

### 7.
"The clerics were gathered before the sage, together with Chinese converts—two hundred troops, all already Muslim. Their leader had been adopted as a son, named Endrasena, truly excellent. It was he who swayed the great sage's heart, supported by the Chinese."

### 8.
"I entreated him gently. For nearly a full night he still would not yield, not a word of response—his heart was exceedingly stubborn. I returned without taking leave. The next day I joined battle, nearly the whole day long. Your Majesty's Surabaya troops were driven back, terrified by his ferocity—Endrasena was a mighty warrior."

### 9.
"Do what I could, he still refused, his troops fierce in their assault. My heart was deeply troubled, blocked and confounded in my thoughts. When Your Majesty's sister saw that I was at an impasse, with no other recourse, she spoke gently to me with compassion, asking leave, entreating—"

### 10.
"—she wished to restore the spirits of the troops. As soon as your sister spoke, I entrusted myself wholly to her will, merely following as her servant. She then took eight hundred ryals and garments of many colors and distributed them to the officers and ministers of Surabaya."

### 11.
Their hearts were all gladdened; their courage sprouted bit by bit. The Sultan laughed heartily: "Continue, my sister!" "As you wish, my lord." Many were the words spoken, sweet and fragrant, like the teaching of true knowledge—yet the servants were not weary, though the discourse was long.

### 12.
The Surabaya servants, hearing these words, all felt their courage rise. They spoke between sobs, vowing again and again, all resolved to die—even if crushed to dust, they would not waver in their service. The king was all the more pleased and spoke gently: "Come, sister, continue."

### 13.
"The next morning the signal was sounded. Your sister took command as general, riding in a palanquin with her pistol in her lap. I followed behind on horseback, not far off. The battle formation was like a Garuda spreading its wings. The porters wore brilliant colors. Your sister was the spearhead, and I the shaft, with the Surapringga troops—"

### 14.
"—arranged as the left and right flanks, invisible, hidden from sight. Only the vanguard was visible, shouts rising wave upon wave. The Giri troops then issued forth, without danger, without hindrance, charging hastily. Commander Endrasena and his Chinese warriors fought fiercely—the battle was at its height."

### 15.
"The Surabaya troops were overwhelmed and fled to their commander. Your sister commanded: 'Why do you flee?' They answered that they could not advance, that Endrasena's assault pressed hard—his fighting like Raden Seta himself. Your sister shot him with her pistol, hitting his right hand—his sword was broken."

### 16.
"He still fought with his dagger in his left hand. She shot again, wounding his left hand. Both hands were wounded. Only his feet and head remained—he jumped and stamped like a bleating goat. The people laughed. At the third shot, his leg was struck and he fell, together with the crushing of his wings. The Surabaya troops—"

### 17.
"—attacked from right and left. The Surabaya soldiers were enraged, their fury unleashed, trampling boldly. The Giri troops offered no resistance. They died without number, piled in heaps. Only a few remained alive. They scattered, fleeing into steep ravines; some climbed mountains."

### 18.
"Endrasena was crushed to dust, mingled with the earth. All the Chinese troops were wiped out. Your sister then ascended to the palace. When she arrived outside, the sage's wife rushed to embrace her feet, weeping loudly, begging forgiveness for her husband's life—the priest of Giri surrendered unconditionally."

### 19.
"He did not resist the king's will. All the treasures within the palace were surrendered, together with his wives and children—except for the children by a junior wife, three daughters only. Jayengresmi the eldest, who had assumed the life of a holy and virtuous sage; then Jayengsari, a handsome youth; and Rancangkapti, a maiden."

### 20.
"All three fled in the heat of battle. I have been unable to find them, searching for all three. Now, my lord, Sunan Giri with his wife and children, together with all the plunder, is at the alun-alun. I present them to you."

### 21.
"I am deeply grateful," said the Sultan. "The priest of Giri deserves forgiveness. For now, all authority in Giri I give to you. All the plunder shall be yours." The prince bowed and answered: "I am deeply honored by your grace, my lord, toward your servant."

### Prose summary
The Surabaya troops were initially driven back, but then rallied under the command of Ratu Pandhansari. It is told that three of Sunan Giri's children fled during the heat of battle. Raden Jayengresmi was accompanied by a servant; Raden Jayengresmi together with his younger sister Niken Rancangkapti were also accompanied by santris. They went their separate ways, constantly searching for one another. In the end, the Surabaya soldiers prevailed. Giri's champion Endrasena was captured. Sunan Giri submitted and was brought to Mataram with his entire family and all his treasures. (pages 31–50)

### 22.
The Sultan spoke again: "At another time I will meet with the priest of Giri. For now, let him be taken home. As for the defiant Jayengresmi—his intention is to contend with me. Yet his purpose is safe; in time he will leave a legacy to the realm, perfect in sainthood."

### 23.
The prince bowed and took his leave, together with his wife, to return to Surabaya. Permission was granted, and they withdrew. The king returned to the palace. The queen departed, led by the hand. When they arrived in Surabaya, the sage was not separated from them—he and his wife and children were lodged together in one place, not mingled with others.

### 24.
The king's gifts came in a steady stream. All those who had been taken were given new garments. Their hearts were joyful, like guests at a wedding feast—all worry forgotten. They exalted the Sultan's name as truly supreme. The queen distributed wealth and plunder to the troops great and small—they were delighted to receive their share.

### 20
## Asmaradana 1.

### 1.
Let Mataram be still. The destruction of Giri is finished. Now the story turns to another: Raden Jayengresmi is told of, arriving in the midst of the forest. His two santris caught up with him—Gathak and Gathuk by name, native sons of Cirebon.

### 2.
They bowed and spoke, their tears streaming: "We cannot part from you, master. We will follow wherever you go." The prince spoke softly: "I am grateful that your love is so steadfast."

### 3.
They journeyed on until they came upon the remnants of the ancient palace of Majapait. The prince gazed upon the beauty of the gate—red brickwork set so tightly that the joints seemed seamless, hard as stone.

### 4.
The mortar was laid without lime, using only interlocking joints and brick against brick. So perfect was the masonry that no crack was visible, like a single whole—each brick carved with floral patterns.

### 5.
Above the gate, layer upon layer rose in terraces, the carving intricate and delicate. At the very peak of the gateway grew a mandira tree, its branches drooping lushly on either side of the gate.

### Prologue — The Wanderings of Raden Jayengresmi (pages 52–65)
Raden Jayengresmi's journey continues, accompanied by the santris Gathak and Gathuk. They arrived at the former Majapait palace, then viewed the Brahu and Bajangratu temples, proceeded to Blitar to see the Panataran temple, then to the village of Gaprang and the Lodhaya forest, where they saw the Kyai Pradhah gong. They continued to the village of Pakel, meeting Ki Carita, who explained the contents hidden within the tajug in the middle of the Lodhaya forest. In Lodhaya, Jayengresmi encountered a tiger with a gem. They arrived at Tuban and continued to Bojanagara. In the Bagor forest, they met the spirit Ratu Mas Trengganawulan, who gave knowledge of the omens of crows, prênjak birds, woodpeckers, and the virtues of the platukbawang bird.

### 6.
The brickwork ran in sweeping arcs around the palace compound. The prince and his two santris gazed their fill, then entered the inner court, arriving at a great pool whose waters gleamed clear and bright.

### 7.
The pool was bordered by red stone, its edges lined with every kind of flower—pandanus, orchids, argulobang, noja, nala flowers, nagasari, fragrant cempaka, clakêt, and rêjasa blossoms.

### 8.
Rukêm, ragaina, klurak, kalak, kanigara, kamuning, kananga—all surpassing—dlima and dilêm wafting their scent, taluki, sruni, wungyan, wratsari piercing deep, pacarcina and pandanus filling the air.

### 9.
Many things are described in writing, enchanting the prince's heart.

### 10.
He thought of his sister Rancangkapti: "Where are you, sister? In former times you delighted in gathering blossoms in the garden."

### 11.
"Where have you gone, sister?" The prince's heart was heavy with longing, his spirit low. He turned northward and saw a cemetery. The prince called out a greeting—an answer came: "Yes, young master, alaikumsalam."

### 12.
Jayengresmi and his two servants together performed the ratib prayer. Afterward they withdrew. Not far off, they saw a statue with a dog's face. Gathak and Gathuk were startled, thinking it was a living man.

### 13.
The dog statue hurled a stone. The prince explored within the palace—all was empty, no one there. He returned to the pool and took water for ablution. Gathak gave the call to prayer and Gathuk the iqamah; it was time for Asr.

### 14.
After prayer they emerged and sat at the front of the gate. Then a man arrived and sat near the prince. He spoke gently: "Young sir, you who have just arrived, peace be upon you."

### 15.
"What name do you bear, and what is your purpose?" The prince answered softly: "Uncle, I thank you for your welcome. I am homeless, an orphan seeking my kin."

### 16.
"My name is Bagus Santri. Uncle, let me ask—what is your name?" "I am called Ki Purwa, entrusted with the care of this great ruined palace of Majapait."

### 17.
"If you wish, young sir, stop at my hut." The prince answered sweetly: "I am deeply grateful. I will stay here awhile, fulfilling my intent, seeking the blessing of the holy."

### 18.
"That would be most fitting, for this is a place of meditation. On the night of Garakasih many foreign visitors come, seeking various goals—paying homage at the shrine of Princess Campa."

### 19.
"Have you seen the interior of the palace yet? Uncle, I have wandered within—I saw the cemetery, the statues, and the great pool. What is their ancient significance?"

### 20.
Ki Purwa answered softly: "That gate is called Waringin Lawang. The pool was a pleasure garden of King Brawijaya. The Princess of Campa is buried there—she who converted to Islam."

### 21.
"The statue in the likeness of the king of Balambangan, Minakjingga, serves as a great meditation object. That is the lore of old. As for the prayer shrine, it is called Candhi Brahu, not far from the palace."

### 22.
"Have you not yet seen it? Tonight I will accompany you." The hour of Maghrib was approaching. Gathak and Gathuk gave the call to prayer, and they performed the Maghrib and Isha prayers.

### 23.
The full moon shone bright, the night aglow with scattered light. The prince walked slowly, Ki Purwa leading, Gathak and Gathuk close behind. They arrived at Candhi Brahu—the meditation shrine of King Brawijaya.

### 24.
The temple was like a mosque, its roof of layered brick rising to a point, with an opening below. Ki Purwa said: "Enter, young sir, into this place of prayer."

### 25.
The prince and his two santris entered the shrine. Their hearts were filled with wonder at the beautiful ornaments and the fragrance of incense—rising heaped like mounds. When they had seen enough, they emerged.

### 26.
Ki Purwa spoke again: "There is another beautiful temple called Bajang Ratu. But it is best avoided—if anyone sees it, they will not achieve their goal, and misfortune will befall them."

### 27.
"It would be better not to look at it." The prince answered: "Uncle, I ask to stay here. I will take my leave at dawn tomorrow, to continue my journey, seeking news of my kin."

### 28.
Ki Purwa answered: "As you wish, my lord." What passed that night is not told. At daybreak they took water for ablution and performed the dawn prayer. The prince spoke gently: "Uncle, farewell."

### 29.
Ki Purwa answered softly. The prince departed, weeping along the way, praying to God for the safety of his siblings, his father, and all his family.

### 30.
His journey brought him to the Panataran temple in Blitar, on the slopes of Mount Kelud, built of black stone—a single great monument, from base to summit carved with wayang figures.

### 31.
The prince climbed the temple by seven terraces to the top, descending carefully. Gathak and Gathuk were terrified, their hearts pounding. When they reached the bottom, Gathuk asked: "Gathak, what was that picture?"

### 32.
"Carved on the temple walls were vines, roundels, and flowers, resembling wayang figures of giants and monkeys." Gathuk said: "In my opinion, it's a picture of the Rama battle—see the hunchbacked monkey!"

### 33.
"A small structure like a cungkup above the doorway, inscribed with ancient Buddhist script." Gathuk said: "Indeed, sir—what is the meaning of that script? It's all squiggles, not clear."

### 34.
The prince answered softly: "It is Buddhist script, a chronogram. One thousand two hundred and ninety-one years—that is the date when this meditation shrine was built." Gathak and Gathuk nodded.

### 35.
When they had finished viewing, they departed from Panataran and continued on their way, arriving at the village of Gaprang. There they saw two stone statues, one male and one female, by the roadside.

### 36.
Perfume and flowers covered them; incense offerings burned without cease, tended by a guardian. Gathak and Gathuk asked: "Kyai, what is this?" The guardian answered slowly: "Young sirs, this is a sacred shrine."

### 37.
"The male is called Kyai, the female Nyai Gaprang—they serve as the object of devotion for those who seek blessings, especially those desiring children. The requirement is to sit between them, facing away from the statues."

### 21
## Pangkur  1.

## Pangkur 1. (Jayengresmi's Wanderings)

### 1.
"This is the sacred place," explained the guardian. "The one who stands here is the kyai's representative. Those who come to pray offer incense, then speak their wish. If they ask for children, they must sit together here."

### 2.
At the head of the shrine sat one who had already received a child. Gathak and Gathuk snickered: "Good heavens—this one sitting so primly has become a matchmaker!" The prince continued his journey, entering the Lodhaya forest.

### 3.
He saw a small bamboo hut with a thatched roof inside the enclosure. A single gong hung there. The prince stopped. Soon the caretaker arrived and asked their purpose. Gathak and Gathuk answered quietly.

### 4.
"We are just passing through. Why is there a gong in the forest?" The caretaker answered: "It has been here since ancient times. It has become the object of devotion for all the villagers. Its name is Kyai Pradhah. No one knows who made it."

### 5.
"Whenever there is a gamelan performance, they must use the Kyai Pradhah gong. If not, misfortune will follow. Every day it is offered incense, decorated with flowers and cosmetics, until its surface has turned yellow."

### 6.
After hearing the caretaker's story, the prince continued on into the village. He saw a small tajug-shaped building and said to Gathuk and Gathak: "Come, let us rest at the mosque."

### 7.
They found a clear spring. The prince and his servants took water for ablution and entered the prayer house. Gathak and Gathuk gave the call to prayer softly—their hearts were pounding. They performed the Maghrib prayers.

### 8.
They continued with Isha. As they finished praying, they heard the murmuring of voices. Gathuk grew excited. Gathak whispered: "Come, let us go out and see who is speaking—it must be the guardian."

### 9.
They went out slowly. At the door they were startled to see tigers sleeping all around the prayer house, piled together head to head, snoring loudly. Gathak and Gathuk rushed back inside.

### 10.
Trembling, they reported to the prince: "Master, we thought we heard people coming to the mosque, but when we went out, we saw many tigers lying about."

### 11.
"They are countless, scattered like felled banana stalks. If they wake, they will surely attack us and devour us." The prince smiled and said: "Do not be afraid."

### 12.
"It is the will of God. What is fated cannot be changed. Surrender instead to the Almighty. I am but a servant, like a leaf upon the ocean."

### 13.
"Gathak, Gathuk, sleep now. I will keep watch until dawn." The two lay down and slept, curled up without moving, not breathing. The prince prayed all night, beseeching safety. When morning came—

### 14.
—he performed the Subuh prayer alone. Afterward, at the first light of dawn, he woke the sleepers. They rose, still drowsy, listening: all was silent. They sat up. The sky was nearly bright, tinged red as the sun was about to rise.

### 15.
Gathak and Gathuk peered out and saw three men sitting as if they had just woken. When they saw guests inside the prayer house, they hurried off to report to their village head.

### 16.
Arriving before the lurah, they reported: "There are three men in the prayer house, handsome, radiant with light. They have stayed all night." The lurah was amazed and concluded they must be men of excellence.

### 17.
He ordered his wife: "Rubiyah, prepare quickly—the best betel set, venison jerky, spinach, liver, pickled vegetables, cucumber, young siwalan fruit, and sweet palm syrup."

### 18.
Rubiyah obeyed. When all was ready, the lurah and his wife hurried to the prayer house. Approaching the prince, he said: "Young sir, forgive my intrusion. What is your name and lineage?"

### 19.
"Uncle, I am a wandering santri, seeking my kin who have gone away. I know not where. And who, uncle, are you?" The lurah answered: "I am called Ki Carita, lurah of the village of Pakel."

### 20.
"The reason I have come so eagerly is that until now, no one has dared to enter this prayer house—only you, young sir. You must truly be a holy man, of ascetic lineage, born of noble blood."

### 21.
"Your humble servant dares to offer a modest meal. May it bring blessing and reward." The food was laid out. The noble one graciously accepted and ate—a morsel of venison jerky the size of a palm seed.

### 22.
"Tabé tabé," he said again and again. When the meal was finished, the dishes were cleared. Fresh siwalan and palm syrup were brought. The prince ate siwalan and drank—the sweet palm drink was refreshing to his body.

### 23.
Gathak and Gathuk were invited: "Come, young men, do not be shy. Eat what is provided." They ate hungrily, heads bowed, until both were full and loosened their belts.

### 24.
The prince asked gently: "Ki Carita, tell me the story of old. This house and the kepék hanging inside—what are they?" Ki Carita answered: "It comes from the tradition of my ancestors. This house is called a sanggar, a place of prayer from ancient times."

### 25.
"These are two kepék bundles. One contains lurik cloth of many colors and patterns, along with cloth for prayer, headcloths with lace borders. The other kepék contains a kampuh gadhung mlati jacket."

### 26.
"It is said to belong to Her Majesty Nyai Roro Kidul. Once a year I am entrusted to bring it here, to check the completeness of the tally, and also to patrol the coastline."

### 27.
"After the tally is complete, it returns to the shrine. When I collect or return the items, there are always tigers following, though at a distance—those tigers are the guardians."

### 28.
"Every night there are three of them; by day they take the form of men. The pakel tree stands before the shrine, and two durian trees flank the prayer house—planted by the Lord Senapati himself."

### 29.
"Therefore, every fruit is offered to Mataram." Gathak and Gathuk whispered: "What is this 'tiger-with-a-gem' of which they speak? These creatures have no heels..."

### 30.
The prince said sweetly: "Ki Carita, I am deeply grateful for your hospitality. May God reward you. The day is advancing—I must continue my journey." Ki Carita answered: "As you wish, young lord."

### 31.
He passed beyond the village of Pakel, traveling north then east, until at the appointed time he arrived in the land of Tuban, resting beneath a great banyan tree in a vast forest. Gathak and Gathuk marveled at the tree's immense size.

### 32.
They measured its girth—seventy-two cubits. The prince sent Gathuk to fetch water. Not far off, he found a well of black stone with clear water within.

### 33.
He returned and reported to the prince. They approached the stone well—it was full to overflowing. Such was the miracle of a future saint. Gathak and Gathuk were amazed and took water for prayer.

### 34.
When they had finished their ablutions, the water receded to its former level. They returned to the banyan tree and performed the Asr prayer. Then they continued, filled with longing and uncertainty—

### 35.
—thinking of his two siblings: "Where are you, sister?" He saw a spring-fed pool of clear water, named Bêkti. "Rancangkapti, my sister, in former times you loved to bathe in the tiled pool." The prince rested awhile, then continued on.

### 36.
He went south, then west, entering a dense forest as the hour of Maghrib approached. He stopped beneath a great mandira tree at the edge of a pond, bordered by carved black stone. After taking water for ablution, he performed the Maghrib prayers.

## Mijil 1. (Meeting with Ratu Mas Trengganawulan)

### 1.
After completing Isha and Maghrib, the young prince meditated, glorifying the name of the Lord of all creation. Gathak and Gathuk had long been asleep when a sound was heard—like a cannon booming.

### 2.
The earth seemed to tremble and shake. Gathak and Gathuk screamed, scrambling to cling to the prince. The sound grew louder, then faded. Gathak and Gathuk grew drowsy again.

### 3.
Before long the prince saw a woman of great beauty, who knelt and sat before him, bowing low as she spoke without formality: "O exalted one, deliver me, I pray."

### 4.
"In my devotion I am truly afflicted with sickness among my people." The prince smiled and answered gently: "O lady, what is your name, and where is your home, that you come before me?"

### 5.
"Know, O exalted one," she said, "in former times I was a princess, the last of Brawijaya's line who ruled the kingdom of Majapait at its fall, when the realm changed to the Prophet's faith."

### 6.
"I could not embrace the new religion, so I came to the Bagor forest, stubborn against God's will, and was commanded to rule over all the spirits."

### 7.
"I dwell in the Bagor forest. I am called Ratu Mas Trengganawulan. This spring I name Sugihwaras—it is my bathing place."

### 8.
"Only on Friday Kliwon eve do I wander. Whoever is steadfast of heart and wishes to meet me may do so by keeping a night vigil."

### 9.
"On Friday Kliwon night I will surely come and grant whatever is rightly desired. The cannon sound you heard each night is my sign."

### 10.
"It is a sign of a prosperous kingdom. Yet I am resigned—it is already fated, God's decree cannot be changed. I merely submit to His will."

### 11.
"What is your purpose in coming here? Tell me truly." Jayengresmi answered softly: "I am seeking my kin who have gone away—a brother and a sister. It has been long and I have not found them."

### 12.
Ratu Mas answered gently: "O noble one, be patient—the time is not yet. You will meet them when you have been banished from the realm, cast into the sea."

### 13.
"In Tunjungbang you will dwell, and there you will meet them. Do not waver in your journey, persevere in becoming a saint." Raden Jayengresmi was satisfied with this.

### 14.
"Ratu Mas, what are those creatures crouching on the branches, their faces unseen, like monkeys with no tails?" Ratu Mas answered softly: "They are called tukang (woodpeckers)."

### 15.
"Ratu Mas, who told you of my coming?" She replied gently: "The crow gave the omen by its call—this is what the elders say."

### 16.
"If a crow calls from east to west, it is a good omen—a guest is coming, a priest or a holy man. If a crow calls from east to south—"

### 17.
"—that is also a good omen—any undertaking will succeed. If a crow calls from due south, it is an omen of fortune coming."

### 18.
"There is one bird I must still ask about—the one that perches on the house pole and chirps in the morning. What omen does it bring?" "That is the prênjak bird. If it chirps at dawn and again at dusk, expect news from far away."

### 19.
"The platukbawang bird—what is its virtue? It is said to have qualities—if it pecks at a coconut, you can tell the character of a person by noting the direction of the beak."

### 20.
"If it pecks on the east side, the person is trustworthy; if on the west, there is deceit within. And so also with the other directions."

### 21.
"And the dove—what does it signify?" "The dove's call brings news of people's well-being. If you hear one at dawn, it foretells prosperity."

### 22.
Ratu Mas answered all his questions with patience and wisdom. When the sky began to lighten, she took her leave and vanished.

### 23
## Kinanthi  1.

### 23
Lah pirêngna radyan ulun|amarna sarahing pêksi|saking jêng Nabi Suleman|nênggih ingkang anjarwani|kang pêksi pêlatukbawang|kèh manpangatnya winilis||

### 2.
Yèn kang rumiyin dinunung|cucukipun ingkang nginggil|karya sêpuhing gagaman|gagaman sakalir-kalir|sawabipun datan ana|wong têguh tan pasah titis||

### 3.
Yèn tan pasah kang tinanduk|dadya sakit gêng (n)dhatêngi|tan waras prapta ing pêjah|ilating pêksi winarni|dhinahar ingkang paedah|ing pamicara patitis||

### 4.
Netranira kang ginantung|munggèng saluhuring kori|têbih saking duratmaka|wuluning murda kinardi|sumping rarywa lit sawabnya|atêbih saking panyakit||

### 5.
Polo pan kinarya pupuh|ing netra pan datan kêni|ing lalamur sawabira|jajantungipun binukti|sawabira sarwa gampang|sabarang ingkang kinapti||

### 6.
Lan kabuka ngelmunipun|apêruninpun upami|winor lan minum-minuman|miwah winor lawan jampi|sawabe rikat lumajar|pringsilanipun binukti||

### 7.
Kinasiyan sawabipun|mring wanodya lawan malih|kinasiyan mring pandhita|miwah maring para wali|dhadhaning pêksi dhinahar|sawabipun lamun sakit||

### 8.
Enggal ing waluyanipun|brotol binukti pawèstri|sawabipun kinasiyan|dyah ika maring ing laki|buntut winor lan gulunya|binasmi nulya binukti||

### 9.
Ing pawèstri sawabipun|linuwih samining èstri|laripun kinarya gêlang|elingan sabarang kardi|lawan malih ingkang êlar|winor ing sabuk prayogi||

### 10.
Kinasiyan sawabipun|lamun suwitèng narpati|yèn sinèlèh soring bantal|patilêman sawabnèki|tiningalan sarwa endah|tur rinêksa ing Hyang Widdhi||

### 11.
Sawabe ingkang babalung|akuwat yèn dènsimpêni|myang balung suwiwinira|sinèlèhakên ngisoring|pasareyan datan liyan|sawabe akuwat malih||

### 12.
Atinipun pan ginantung|luhure dènnira guling|sawabipun bêtah sahwat|yèn winor lisah kalêntik|myang wawêdhak ingkang sahwat|sirna sagunging sasakit||

### 13.
Suku kalih sawabipun|lamun pinêndhêm ing sabin|myang sakèhing tatanêman|tulus wohipun andadi|lamun malih ingkang manah|ginêlangakên ngastèki||

### 14.
Wisa tawa sawabipun|isining wisma sakalir|têbih sangking ing lalara|nênggih atinipun malih|ingusapakên ing dakar|bêtah sahwat sawabnèki||

### 15.
Ingkang ilat kinaryèku|jimat sawabe tan kenging|ing luwe bêtah alapa|lawan kulitipun malih|tinalèkakên padharan|yèku bêtah luwe malih||

### 16.
Kêpalanira kang manuk|kinarya jimat ngajurit|kinajriyan marang mêngsah|yèn wontên wisma upami|panas tan kêna kanggonan|pinêndhêman êlarnèki||

### 17.
Panjawat kang têngênipun|wolung lêmbar aja luwih|pêpadonipun lor wetan|dadya tawa panasnèki|gêtih dènakingkên ika|winor lawan bawang abrit||

### 18.
Lawan adas nulya kinum|ing toya nèng pinggan putih|karya jampi lara netra|pinupuhakên tumuli|insa Allah dadya waras|yèn wontên sasakit kuping||

### 19.
Pinupukakên pan mantun|lamun arsa dènkasihi|lan wong akèh myang yèn arsa|sugih lan yèn arsa dadi|barang ingkang tinanêman|puwasaa tigang ari||

### 20.
Dèn kadya puwasa agung|winatêka donganèki|puniki mèle kang donga|Allahuma barkat saking|Gusti Bagendha Suleman|mung iku tan ana malih||

### 21.
Kang pinangan atinipun|ingolah sakalir-kalir|yèn wus dènnira puwasa|matêk donga sarwi bukti|yèn adagang myang ngawula|dèn têtêp hya walanggalih||

### 22.
Kalamun arsa sirèku|siniyan wong kathah malih|tyas lan ilat winor barang|olah-olahan binukti|lamun arsa matènana|ing wong durjana mamaling||Ingkang kiwa tapakipun|sinundêp ing cucuk nênggih|insa Allah pêsthi pêjah|lamun arsa sugih ngèlmi|tyas binukti lawan uyah|gorèng wus titi kang paksi||

### 24.
Rahadyan ngandika arum|Ratu Mas Trêngganasasi|bangêt ing panrimaningwang|sira sung pitutur jati|ngalamatèng paksi dhandhang|miwah pêksi prênjak muni||

### 8
Lajênging lampah nglangkungi rêdi Pandhan dumugi ing Gambiralaya, ningali tilas pêrtapanipun Dèwi Gêndrasari. Angsal katrangan saking Ki Padhang bab rêca Ki Drêpa ingkang dongèngipun kasmaran dhatêng Dèwi Gêndrasari kanthi sumpah manawi botên kalaksanan, pajalêranipun badhe katigas. Kalampahan Ki Drêpa pêjah dados rêca mangku pajalêranipun.

### 8
Sarahing tukang linuhung|myang palatukbawang paksi|apa kang dak walêsêna|Ratu Mas turira aris|luwih karsaning Hyang Suksma|ingsun iki mung sadarmi||

### 26.
Kabèh kang wus ingsunwuwus|iku darbèkmu pribadi|amung kang mêdharkên ingwang|sarèhne wus lingsir akir|sun pamitan lilanana|mantuk mring kahyangan mami||

### 27.
Wus tan katon kangjêng ratu|Gathak Gathuk nuli tangi|sarya matur mring rahadyan|kawula satêngah ngimpi|mirêng swara tanpa rupa|amung ganda mamrik minging||

### 28.
Lir tindhihên raosipun|sruwing-sruwing kapiyarsi|tukang platuk lunjak-lunjak|sarah-sarah dènadhangi|punika ginêm punapa|sintên rencang paduka ngling||

### 29.
Rahadyan ngandika arum|kawruhanamu kang prapti|anêmoni jênêngingwang|iku ratuning dhadhêmit|ngadhaton ing Bagor wana|ing nguni putri narpati||

### 30.
Sang Aprabu Majalangu|Brawijaya kang sisiwi|anggêntur amatiraga|tan sarju salin agami|katrima panêdhanira|bisa jumênêng narpati||

### 31.
Ngratoni sagung lêlêmbut|sajroning Bagor wanadri|sabên bêngi mariyêman|Gathak Gathuk duk miyarsi|marinding githok mangkarag|wus awanci pajar sidik||

### 32.
Samya wulu waktu Subuh|paragat dènnya ngabêkti|linggar sangking Sugihwaras|ngancik suku Pandhan wukir|manjat manginggil wus prapta|ing dhusun Kadhaton nami||

### 33.
Nyabrang lèpèn mili ngidul|toyanya nyarong awêning|miyat balung langkung kathah|agêng-agênge nglangkungi|lajêng lampahira radyan|wus praptèng sukuning ardi||

### 34.
Ing Gambiralaya gunung|nulya umanjat manginggil|praptèng pucak non pratapan|tanggul siti angubêngi|tinanêman sasêkaran|argulo gandanya amrik||

### 35.
### 34.
He measured its circumference—thirty-two cubits. Outside the southern wall stood a black stone statue, like a man holding his own genitals.

### 35.
Detached from his body, the size of a cubit. Gathak and Gathuk snickered: "This one beats Ki Gaprang's statue—his is smaller and shorter!"

### 36.
The prince descended northward along the mountain slope and saw huge long bones, four times the previous ones. He spoke, his words like gold rising on water.

## Maskumambang 1. (Gambiralaya)

### 1.
"Gathak, go quickly to the village and find a local to get me some young coconut."

### 2.
Gathak went straight to the headman's house. The headman hurried to receive him: "What is your wish, young sir?" "I wish to buy young coconut."

### 3.
"My child is at the foot of the mountain," said the headman. "No need to buy—" and he took out young coconuts.

### 4.
Four were prepared. "Please, young sir, accept these as my gift." Gathak hurried back.

### 5.
Arriving before the prince, the headman bowed. Jayengresmi asked: "Elder, tell me—what is this place and what is your name?"

### 6.
"The village I dwell in is called Padhangan. I am commonly called Ki Padhang."

### 7.
"Allow me to offer you refreshment—a modest meal and cool young coconut water."

### 8.
"Yes, elder, I am most grateful for your kindness. May God reward the sincerity of your heart."

### 9.
The prince drank the cool coconut water; it refreshed his throat and felt good to his body. He said, "Alhamdulillah."

### 10.
He passed the remaining four coconuts to his two santris. They cracked them open and finished them all.

### 11.
The prince spoke: "Elder, I wish to know—I passed through the village on the mountain slope and saw many huge long bones."

### 12.
"Then I climbed to the mountain peak and saw an old hermitage. To the south stood a statue holding what seemed to be its genitals."

### 13.
"Descending the slope, I saw more bones, even larger than before. What is the story behind these?"

### 14.
Ki Padhang knelt and answered: "The legend of old—this mountain is called Pandhan Peak, and the village is named Kadhaton."

### 15.
"In ancient times there was a kingdom of giants here—King Arimba, when he waged war against the enemies of the Pandawas."

### 16.
"Arjuna prevailed in battle; many giants were slain. These are their bones. King Arimba the giant—"

### 17.
"—died on the slopes of Gambiralaya mountain. Those bones you saw are extraordinarily large and long."

### 18.
"A hundred men could not lift them. The small river flowing south is called the Jerowan River."

### 19.
"After Arimba died, his entrails were torn out by Arjuna's claws, cast into the river."

### 20.
"Hence the river is called Jerowan to this day. The hermitage on the peak is called Gambiralaya."

### 21.
"In ancient times there was a beautiful princess who performed asceticism here, named Dewi Gendrasari. She attained her prayers."

### 22.
"A man named Ki Drepa passionately desired her. As he set out, he vowed—"

### 23.
"—if rejected, he would castrate himself. The princess refused him, and Ki Drepa immediately cut off his own genitals."

### 24.
"He sat back down and died, turned to stone, remaining to this day. Truth or tale—believe as you will."

### 25.
Gathak nudged Gathuk: "That Ki Drepa was a man of his word—he kept his vow. Not like people today, whose loyalty is only on the lips."

### 26.
Raden Jayengresmi spoke gently: "Elder, I thank you. May you remain in peace."

### Prologue
His journey continued to the village of Dhandher, where he met the village head who told him of the fire pool (Pakayangan), the measurements of keris and spear shafts. He arrived at Kasanga and met Ki Jatipitutur, who told the story of King Jaka of Medhangkamulan killing a crocodile and later having a son who was also a crocodile named Jaka Nginglung, explaining the origin of the oil springs.

### 27.
"I continue my journey," said the prince, bowing to Ki Padhang. He set off, accompanied by Gathak and Gathuk.

### 28.
Traveling north then west, he arrived at Bojanagara and saw smoke rising in a column. The prince approached—what burned was a pool of fire.

### 29.
Its circumference was twenty-four cubits, its length thirty-two, its depth a thousand handspans.

### 30.
Waterless, the prince gazed long. Suddenly it blazed like a house on fire, flames leaping to the clouds.

### 31.
The fire seemed to pay homage to the newcomer. Gathak and Gathuk were anxious, their hearts pierced with fear.

### 32.
The headman of Dhandher village hurried over to inspect the fire. He was startled to see three people sitting by the pool.

### 33.
His heart raced: "Strange are these people. Unless they are of noble lineage, they would not dare approach the Pakayangan fire pool."

### 34.
The headman drew near the prince. The prince asked: "Are you the guardian of this pool of fire?"

### 35.
The headman replied: "That is correct, young lord. I hurried to inspect the fire pool, for it is a portent."

### 36.
"When it blazes, many calamities and sicknesses befall the nearby villages. Those who understand the fire take it as an omen of ill fortune, like playing hide-and-seek with doom."

### 25
## Pocung 1. (Pakayangan Forge)

### 1.
"It blazes greatly, yet no one sees it—only I myself know the fire's flame. It is a sure sign that a noble person has arrived."

### 2.
"Its demeanor is like paying homage. This is called the Pakayangan fire, so the story goes—the forge of the Empu of old."

### 3.
Gathuk asked the headman quickly: "Are you a descendant of the ancient Empu who owned the Pakayangan forge?"

### 4.
"Can you forge keris?" Ki Kapalang answered: "Oh, young sir, I am not his descendant, but truly I can forge them."

### 5.
"Is my guess right? When crafting a keris, is the length measured arbitrarily? And the spear shaft—is it measured too, or just by the material?"

### 6.
Ki Kapalang answered: "Keris and spear shafts have their measurements."

### 7.
"The rule when crafting a keris: the length of the ganja (ferrule) is measured first, then used to measure the length of the blade."

### 8.
"From the base beyond the ferrule to the tip: six measurements. The first is cakra, the second gundhala—"

### 9.
"—third gunung, fourth guntur, fifth sagara, sixth madu. The best is when the tip falls upon arga (mountain)."

### 10.
"If it falls on sagara or madu, those three are good. Or if it falls on jaladri, then reverse the measurement from the tip."

### 11.
"From the base beyond the ferrule—the most excellent is when it ends falling on ardi (mountain). That is the one fit to be kept."

### 12.
"When making a spear shaft, measure by the fist. The count is four: sangga, runggi, sarah, watang—the most suitable."

### 13.
"Let it end on sangga." Gathuk bowed and said: "Your teaching, elder, is most valued." Ki Kapalang then turned and addressed the prince.

### 14.
"O exalted one, if it pleases you, stop at my home in Dhandher—it is not far, that village visible ahead."

### 15.
"Forgive me, uncle, I must continue my journey." The headman bowed: "As you wish. I can only pray for your safe travels."

### 16.
The Pakayangan fire of Dhandher was left behind. The prince continued across open plains, then turned west and saw water flowing from a spring.

### 17.
Gathuk unhesitatingly scooped up the flowing water. But when it reached his throat, he spat it out, choking.

### 18.
Gathak asked: "What's wrong, Gathuk? Why are you spitting?" Gathuk said: "That's not water—it's pure oil!"

### 19.
"Oh yes, Gathuk, I've heard of this—they say it's usable for bathing. The villagers just put up with the fumes, God forbid."

### 20.
The prince smiled and said to Gathuk: "That comes from meeting people who are hasty in spirit—they are sure to meet trouble."

### 21.
The prince continued on, wading through tall grass and thick undergrowth, crossing rivers five times.

### 22.
He saw a settlement in the midst of the vast forest. The prince said: "Come, Gathak and Gathuk, let us stop and ask what land this is."

### 23.
Soon they met a man carrying a bamboo container of palm wine. The two santris approached him, asking to buy some.

### 24.
"Drink your fill, young sirs—no need to pay. Where are you headed? You seem to have wandered into my village."

### 25.
"I follow a noble man who roams far, delighting in solitary places, exploring what is hidden and untrodden by men."

### 26.
"That fragrant pandanus grove ahead—young sirs, if you wish, stop at my home. Let me go ahead and prepare."

### 27.
Gathak and Gathuk whispered: "This man is insistent—you should accept his invitation. It is already late in the journey."

### 28.
Soon the man came out to meet them. The prince arrived. Gathak went to look for water. The man asked: "Child, what are you looking for?"

### 29.
"I am looking for water for ablution." "Oh, child, there is no clean water here—it is all wallowing holes of boars."

### 30.
"Let me find you good water." The man struck a bamboo stump—water gushed out like a fountain, clear and pleasant, just enough.

### 31.
Gathak said: "This water from the stump is clear as a fountain—good for washing." The prince and Gathak and Gathuk performed their ablutions.

### 32.
When they finished, the water stopped flowing. Gathak marveled: "Truly this man is blessed! I never imagined such a miracle."

### 33.
At the house, Gathak gave the call to prayer, lingering in his chant. They performed the sunat prayers, then the iqamah. Maghrib and Isha were completed.

### 34.
They sat down to a meal—young coconut wine, boiled taro, ripe bananas. The man spoke sweetly.

### 35.
"O noble young sir, forgive this modest offering—whatever a poor man living in the forest without companions can provide."

### 36.
"I am deeply grateful—my heart is much pleased." The prince ate, finishing the meal, and drank about three gulps of palm wine.

### 37.
Feeling full, he belched twenty-four times. Gathak and Gathuk ate and drank their fill of coconut wine as well, leaving nothing.

### 38.
The prince spoke gently to the man: "Uncle, I wish to ask—what is this place called, and what is your name?"

### 39.
He answered: "I am Jatipitutur, originally from Sela, now the caretaker of Kasanga. This region is the Grobogan area."

### 40.
"Kasanga has many springs: Bledhug, Kuwu, Crewek, Mandhikil, and Sendhang Ramesan with colored waters."

### 41.
"Brother Jatipitutur, I wish to know the story behind all these names and places."

### 42.
He answered slowly: "I remember, though not in order. It serves as a pastime against drowsiness. In ancient times there was a king—"

### 43.
"—named King Jaka of Medhangkamulan. He delighted in hunting in the forest, chasing wild bulls, sambhur deer, muntjak, and mouse-deer."

### 44.
### 44.
Without attendants, only a single companion, he came upon a huge crocodile with five-colored scales, blocking his way.

### 45.
The king was furious: "What do you want, crocodile, that you dare to oppose me? I will surely finish you!"

### 46.
The king drew his bow and loosed an arrow. It struck the crocodile dead. Then a voice was heard by the king.

### 47.
"O king, truly you have done violence. You will surely receive God's punishment—you cannot overcome your own desires."

### 48.
The voice vanished, the crocodile gone. Then calamity came—rain, whirlwind, lightning, thunder without end.

## Megatruh 1. (Rarasati)

### 1.
In the midst of the storm, the king sought shelter in a thicket. He recalled what he had seen long ago.

### 2.
The daughter of the widow Kasiyan of Sangkèh village—Rarasati, still young but now of age, fully grown.

### 3.
He arrived at the village and waited at the gate. Rarasati came out. The widow was busy with the women, pounding rice to the rhythm of their songs.

### 4.
Rarasati emerged from her house, wearing a red patola cloth, yellow powder, a kawung sash edged with gold leaf—radiant in her beauty.

### 5.
Behind her came her beloved pet—a fine white hen with a red comb, like a garden flower. The maiden approached.

### 6.
At the rice-pounding, she joined in, one foot stepping on the rice stalk as she winnowed her cloth, her calf gleaming.

### 7.
Flashing like lightning, the king saw her and was filled with longing. He thought: "She is fit to be my queen."

### 8.
He descended and approached the women, reciting love charms. The women paid him no heed, intent on their singing.

### 9.
So intense was his concentration that he inadvertently dropped his semen. The princess did the same—both fell to the ground. The white hen quickly pecked at them.

### 10.
It pecked up both drops, mingling them as one. King Jaka was astonished and deeply ashamed. He returned to the palace.

### 11.
Gathak and Gathuk snickered. The prince smiled and said softly: "Brother Jatipitutur, continue the story of that hen."

### 12.
Ki Jatipitutur continued: After some days, the hen laid a mottled egg. The widow noticed and quickly took it.

### 13.
She placed it in the rice storage, setting it with three ordinary eggs mixed with selected rice. She checked it daily, but it did not diminish.

### 14.
The rice did not decrease—it grew! The widow thought: "This egg must bring good fortune." Each day she offered rice, yet it increased.

### 15.
She moved it to the granary. When the rice ran out, she went to fetch more. Opening the granary door, she took one portion and carried it away.

### 16.
The egg stored in the granary had hatched—but what emerged was an enormous cobra, coiled and sleeping. Startled, she cried out.

### 17.
"What are you doing here, child?" The widow turned, saw the snake, and fled in panic, stumbling as she ran.

### 18.
She went to report to the king. The cobra watched, then left the granary and went to Rarasati, saying: "Mother!"

### 19.
Rarasati fainted. The widow rushed after her. They both arrived before the patih. The snake followed behind, rearing up.

### 20.
Seeing it, both women collapsed. The patih tried to flee. The snake laughed and said: "Elder, do not be afraid of me."

### 21.
"Come, let us sit together." The patih, trembling, asked softly: "What manner of snake are you, appearing from nowhere?"

### 22.
"I wish to be presented before the king." The patih nodded and walked ahead, the snake following behind. They arrived before the king.

### 23.
Before the patih could speak, the snake was already there. King Jaka asked: "What are you, snake, that you can speak like a human?"

### 24.
The snake claimed to be the king's child. The king was angered: "Snake, know this—I have never married. How dare you lie!"

### 25.
"Leave, or you will die!" The snake wept and explained the events of the king's hunt in the forest and his return.

### 26.
He told of the mottled hen's egg and its hatching. The king heard this and was deeply ashamed. He returned to the palace.

### 27.
The patih was summoned. The king commanded that the snake be sent to find the king's destined bride and to slay the king's enemy.

### 28.
"Find the white crocodile of the Southern Sea. If successful, I will adopt the snake as my son." The patih conveyed this to the snake, who wept.

### 29.
"If I fail to fulfill the king's will, I am content to die." The patih pitied him. The snake departed swiftly.

### 30.
Southward he went, destroying all in his path, plunging into the earth and emerging in the depths of the sea, roaring a challenge.

### 31.
He proclaimed himself the son of King Jaka of Medhangkamulan—the divine king Dewatacengkara, in the form of a white crocodile—and emerged from the palace.

### 32.
They joined in battle—many days it raged. The princess of the Southern Sea watched, pleased.

### 33.
She prayed for the white crocodile's destruction. The sea churned, waters surged, countless fish died. The white crocodile was defeated.

### 34.
It was cast ashore at the edge of the sea. Its body became the Argalima mountain range. The snake's joy was complete—he was acknowledged as king.

## Gambuh 1. (Tunggulwulung)

### 1.
The tale tells of the Princess of the Southern Sea, rejoicing at the death of the white crocodile. The snake was summoned quickly.

### 2.
He entered the palace and appeared before the princess. She spoke: "O snake, victor in battle—I once vowed: whoever defeats the white crocodile—"

### 3.
"—shall take my throne for a while and fulfill my command." The snake answered softly: "I do not refuse, O king. But I am on a mission—"

### 4.
"—to find my father's destined bride. I must first seek her." The princess answered: "I am that bride. Tell the king—"

### 5.
"—that his bride is I. But let him know: his reign will not last—only three years, then another shall take the throne."

### 6.
"On Wednesday, at the full moon of Galungan, let him come to my palace. I will wed him to the maiden Balorong."

### 7.
The wedding is not described—the celebration was grand. After the wedding, he ruled for seven days, then swiftly took his leave.

### 8.
He was given leave and told what he would encounter—of body and kingdom, beginning, middle, and end—warned again and again.

### 9.
The princess spoke: "Your name shall be Linglung Tunggulwulung. In time, by God's will, you shall rule the spirits of the mountains."

### 10.
"At our meeting you shall be a true omen—of the turning of ages and the prosperity of the realm. Go now, my son. Your bride is the maiden Balorong."

### 11.
"Do not linger on the road. Leave her here for now. Travel through the depths of the ocean, then plunge into the earth."

### 12.
"He emerged in Pasundhan, plunged again, emerged at Kuwu, then at Carewek and Mandhikil—now close to the kingdom. He traveled by land, crawling."

### 13.
Arriving at the kingdom, he entered the patih's residence. Before the king he bowed low. Tunggulwulung prostrated himself.

### 14.
His mission was reported from beginning to end. King Jaka was overjoyed in his heart. He acknowledged the snake as his son and bestowed a royal crown.

### 15.
The king's garments—tail, teeth, and fangs—were sheathed in gold inlaid with jewels. His scales were layered with gleaming gold, his necklace of radiant gems.

### 16.
He was given a cup of asthagina oil. Its power: if a snake died, a drop of this oil would revive it, writhing again.

### 17.
A serpent's gem was placed in his tail—all snakes of the earth were subject to it, blazing on his chest. So the king commanded.

### 18.
"Hear me, all my subjects—I elevate Linglung to the rank of Prince Adipati, dwelling at Tunggulwulung. Go now, my son."

### 19.
"Do not fail in your duty. Go forth with the blessings of the people." The prince withdrew and his journey is not described.

### 20.
He ruled at Tunggulwulung. After a year, he sent tribute to the king. Then he was instructed by his father the king in all knowledge—both coarse and subtle.

### 21.
Tunggulwulung meditated in the forest for so long that his body grew overgrown with moss.

### 21.
Nglênggirik kadya gumuk|ical sipatira taksaka gung|tutuk kadya wiwaraning guwa wingit|wus matêng ing tapanipun|manungsa tan ana wêroh||

### 22.
Karsane jawata gung|nuju dêrês jawah gumarubug|wontên rare angon sasanga kèhneki|kajawahan samya ngaub|nèng tutuk naga kinaot||

### 23.
Kang wolu wus umangsuk|kang satunggal siningkang tan antuk|gya minggah ring klêrês gigir nyangking kudhi|mêmêrang siti marêntul|guwa mingkêm kadya angob||

### 24.
Rare wolu kang ngaub|sarêng samya pêjah tanpa muwus|kang satunggal wontên jawi mêrang gigir|inggal lumajêng umantuk|mring sudarma awawartos||

### 25.
Tandya kunjuk sang prabu|sri narendra sakalangkung bêndu|gya adhawuh kinèn mantèk wêsi gilik|cangkêmira Jaka Linglung|supadi pêjah ing ngênggon||

### 26.
Wus pinantèk kang tutuk|Jaka Linglung tumêka ing lampus|lah punika cariyosipun ing nguni|atatanya Gathak Gathuk|athik ula kaya uwong||

### 27.
Le nyêmbah niku prèhpun|Rara Blorong kacariyos ayu|pinaèsan saya wimbuh amrakati|Jaka Linglung sigit bagus|winêwahan mawi topong||

### 28.
Wong gèpèng sirahipun|kados pundi kêkahe puniku|ah wong criyos wikana nyatane nguni|tamating cariyos bangun|Gathak Gathuk adan Suboh||

### 29.
Wus samya wêktu Subuh|sabakdanya Subuh ngandika rum|lah kiraka wawi ulun ayun uning|patilasan Tunggulwulung|inggih sumangga sang anom||

### 30.
Kula gadhah panyuwun|mrih lêstantun tan sangsayèng laku|kaparênga miturut sacaranèki|kiraka hywa walang kalbu|datan nyuwalani ingong||

### 31.
Wus kêrit lampahipun|prapta ing Kasanga têpinipun|siti lêbu kadi awu warnanèki|tan wontên taru kang thukul|ngilak-ilak lir sinapon||

### 32.
Ambanon ingkang wangun|nanging mawi malengkong mangidul|kathah bolong kadya lènge yuyu nênggih|katutub ing lêbu lêmbut|tiniyub maruta katon||

### 33.
Sadaya sami ngêmpus|lir tutuban dene têlêngipun|wontên êlèng sadandang wiyarirèki|akumrangsang ungêlipun|tinindakan alon-alon||

### 34.
Wus cêlak mêndhak sampun|lampah bocong ngambah krikil alus|warna abrit pêthak jêne anêlasih|kèndêl sila dènnya lungguh|rahadyan anulya jongkok||

### 35.
Mariksa lêbêtipun|kilêng-kilêng kinclong warninipun|kadya lingsah ingkang sakalangkung wêning|swara kumrangsang saya sru|wusing anon ngandika lon||

### 36.
Punapi marmanipun|lampah bocong Ki Jatipitutur|matur kurmat rèh wus cêlak dunungnèki|Prabu Anom Tunggulwulung|kadinanan ngèlmi kaot||

### 28
## Asmaradana  1.

### 28
Kang kilêng-kilêng kaèksi|gih punika netranira|lêbu lir awu warnane|tilas Linglung duk samana|pinantèk tutukira|badan polah siti (m)blêdug|nanging botên sagêt kesah||

### 2.
Krikil warangan puniki|kinarya nyêmêng gêgaman|toyane jram pêcêl winor|paedah ampuhe mindhak|rahadyan sampun linggar|tan pati doh nulya (n)dulu|sumbêr alit isi toya||Nyêmêk-nyêmêk raos asin|kidul têlêng kawistara|wontên gumuk mujur ngilèn|kilèn gumuk datan têbah|rawa lit kêbak toya|ugi asin raosipun|lèr kilèn têlêng Kasanga||

### 4.
Ara-ara tinon asri|sitinipun bulak pêthak|barênjul samya inggile|turut pinggir pupundhungan|dumugi ing lèr wetan|mathuthuk lir wisma pingul|bênggang ajêg lir tinata||

### 5.
Wetan têlêng tinon asri|waradin pênuh thukulan|rumput mêrakan myang mendhong|têkèng kidul notok arga|tinon asri kawuryan|nglilipur ing tyas margiyuh|Ki Jati malih turira||

### 6.
Ingriki bilih marêngi|mangsa katiga akathah|sima sawêr samya manggon|asring-asring ing Kasanga|sawiyaring têlêngnya|

(

### 6.
m)blêdhos swara kadya gludhug|inggilnya ngungkuli arga||

### 7.
Tinon yêktos (ng)gigirisi|gih punika kruranira|Tunggulwulung ingkang manggèn|ing sangandhaping Kasanga|ri wusnya mangun krura|kula gya mariksa gupuh|punapa kawontênannya||

### 8.
Yèn katingal sanepaning|maryêm badhe wontên pêrang|yèn katingal kados layon|badhe kathah tiyang pêjah|sampuning amariksa|nuntên lapur lurah ulun|dhatêng Mêthakan ing Sela||

### 9.
Kang nglajêngkên mring Mantawis|dene lèpèn cacah gangsal|kalamun rêndhêng mangsane|punika dados satunggal|tinon wus kadya rawa|ila-ilane rumuhun|para priyantun ing praja||

### 10.
Saèstu yèn botên kenging|anon têlênge Kasanga|kilap punika wadose|yèn tinrajang kadrawasan|linungsur kang darajat|apês nandhang ing papa gung|kinêbat kang sandhang pangan||

### 11.
Wus tapis dènnya ningali|Ki Jatipitutur lingnya|suwawi angalèr ngilèn|punika tilas kadhatyan|nagri Mêndhangkamulan|ing Kasanga wus kapungkur|prapta tilasing kadhatyan||

### 12.
Wujud kantun siti mênggir|wus dumadya wanawasa|linajêngkên ing lampahe|prapta ing sêndhang Ramêsan|wawêngkoning Kradenan|cakêt satêpining ranu|tan patya lêbêt kang toya||

### 13.
Kalangkung gawoking galih|de toya sasêndhang wrata|kadi dene wedang umob|lir umbul modal ing toya|gumlêdhêg kang suwara|mancawarni toyanipun|pindha kukuwung wangkawa||

### 14.
Ijêm jêne wungu abrit|dadu jambon cêmêng pêthak|inginum anta raose|sêmune kaworan lirang|bêntère sawatara|tan na ilèn-ilènipun|pindhane kadya balumbang||

### 15.
Ki Jati said softly: "This is Sendhang Ramesan." They continued to Carewek, seeing a rocky hill. Above the hill to the north and west were springs.

### 16.
The water tasted salty. It could be used for something. The quality was fine, reddish, less salty. After inspecting, the prince continued north to Mendhikil.

### 17.
They arrived at a low hill and climbed it. At the top was a well, round and level, but with no water—only liquid mud filling it to the brim, overflowing.

### 18.
Like boiling water, its sound rumbled. The current was slow. There was an outflow—salty. He saw three wells, hot as boiling water, and many smaller ones.

### 19.
Toyanipun ugi asin|sadaya kenging kinarya|sarêm kadi ing Carèwèk|kawratan wus pinariksan|ing Blêdhug tinindakan|saking mandrawa kadulu|anon ara-ara wiyar||

### 20.
Ki Jati turira aris|punika kang katingalan|ing Blêdhug Kuwu wastane|kalangkung agêng tinimbang|lawan kang kathah-kathah|mawi suwara jumêgur|tan kêndhat dalu raina||

### 21.
### 19.
"Uncle, let us stop here—please guide me." Ki Jati agreed. They left Mandhikil and soon arrived at the Kuwu region, where the glow of molten lava was visible.

### 20.
The plain was square. The prince stopped at the edge, choosing firm ground. The soft earth was impassable. He marveled at the center of Bledhug—bubbling like boiling water.

### 21.
The bubbles rose high, then burst with a sound like cannon fire, releasing white steam. After erupting, it subsided, then rose again as before.

### 22.
Continuous, never ceasing—its sound never stopped. Ki Jati said softly: "Bubbling and sounding, day and night, in both dry and rainy seasons—always like this."

### 23.
"The smaller ones are quieter, but behave the same. If someone steps there, they sink and vanish—it is so deep they disappear."

### Prologue
He arrived at the village of Sela and met Kyai Pariwara, who explained: 1. The prohibitions of Kiageng Sela; 2. The story of Kiageng Sela catching lightning; 3. The weaving of lurik cloth. His journey continued to Gubug, meeting Dhatuk Bahni, who explained: 1. The four days for wedding tents; 2. Choosing auspicious wedding days; 3. Taboo days for intercourse; 4. Knowledge of intercourse; 5. The prophetic days of misfortune.

### 24.
Ki Jati searched for a bamboo stake, thrusting it into the middle. It sank, unseen. "That bamboo has vanished. After three days, if the ground shifts, the bamboo surfaces with the mud, landing nearby."

### 25.
"Sometimes it never emerges. All that you see here are the remains of Tunggulwulung Jaka Linglung. That is why it is called Kuwu—when he emerged, he stayed here long."

### 26.
"He ruled over all the snakes of the earth. It is said he married and took the princess Balorong." The prince was moved, seeing God's wonders—that a snake could leave such legacy.

### 27.
Longing for his kin, he yearned for the All-Seeing God, whose mercy was manifest. He spoke gently: "Brother Jatipitutur, how can I repay you?"

### 28.
"Your kindness to me is measureless—you who have given me stories and shown me the relics of ancient kings and the age of the gods."

### 29.
"I offer my deepest gratitude, with joy and great forgiveness. Now I take my leave to continue my journey. May you remain well and prospered in all your wishes."

### 30.
Ki Jati replied: "I am deeply happy that you have wished to know all this. May your journey be blessed." They shook hands. Ki Jatipitutur returned home. The prince continued on.

### 31.
After some distance, he stopped at the village of Sela, making for the caretaker's house. The caretaker received him, saying gently: "Since you have come to me—"

### 32.
"—do not be anxious. I am not bound by formalities with you, child. Now I ask your name: where are you from, what is your name, and where are you headed?"

### 33.
The prince answered softly: "Sir, what you command is truly proper—the elder speaking to the young, using familiar speech. But if you ask, I am a santri from the eastern hamlet."

### 10
Kula mangkya nyuwun uning|ingriki namaning dhêkah|jêng kyai lon andikane|

è

### 10
"Ah, child, now you understand. This is the realm of Sela, under Mataram. I am the one who keeps it."

### 36.
"Our ancestor was the king, styled Kiageng Sela, who lies buried here. My name is Ki Pariwara, great-grandson of Kyageng Getaspandhawa, the ornament of Java."

### 29
## Dhandhanggula  1.

### 29
"Now, child, do not worry. Truly I am not ignorant of your journey—for it is foretold that you are a chosen person. Be calm in heart, stay in my home. If you wish to build a house, I will support your plan." Hearing this, the prince felt at ease and replied softly:

### 2.
"Oh my lord, I submit wholly, not refusing your love." So the handsome prince stayed long in Sela, visiting for a time. One morning after Subuh, Kyai Ageng Pariwara sat on the porch, the prince not far from him. The elder said:

### 3.
"Come, child, be seated before me. Your knowledge is not insignificant—the teaching I profess is simply the instruction of Grandfather Kyageng Sela, the peerless one. Truly he realized reality, his sign was sublime. He is the one who watches over Java—none other than the descendant of Kyai Sela himself. Hear this."

### 4.
"Treasure these prohibitions, revere them for blessing, safety, health, and well-being. These are the prohibitions: Do not be arrogant, do not be haughty, do not be deceitful. Do not be greedy of heart, do not be crooked. Do not be a show-off, do not be harmful—for harmful people die quickly. Do not harbor leftward intentions."

### 5.
"Do no evil, fear shame. To live is not to exalt oneself. Do not forget true beauty—beauty is not gold or coin, not fine clothes, not lovely form. True beauty is hard to find. Among all living people, all love one another; its color is the heart's silver."

### 6.
"Do not boast of gold or money, do not boast of fine garments, do not boast of your abilities, do not boast of knowledge, do not boast of your endurance, do not boast of your mantras, do not exalt your practices—all these are vain. Do not cling to your own learning—what does it avail you?"

### 7.
"Practice honesty, child. Do not practice deceit. Be mindful of everyone's feelings. Human desire—do not follow it. The desires of humanity must be curbed. Treat others as yourself. That is what you must shun."

### 8.
"Do not equate humans with animals—buffalo, cattle, chickens. Do not try to teach animals as you would people—it cannot be done. Do not be like Samerta, beating his buffalo, trying to teach it to read. Buffalo and cattle simply cannot—so stop."

### 9.
"Chickens driven up to roost, or eating rice—they are just fed. That is people working deception. As for neighbors: if it is proper, join them in their work; if not, leave them be. They eat their own rice—they are not your subjects."

### 10.
"If they are your own dependents, treat them well. Do not cause them heartache. Do not teach them when they have not erred. If they have already suffered loss, do not blame them—loss added to transgression. Do not revile them, child—what does that gain you?"

### 11.
"Strike a proper balance in living. Do not do things to excess. Life has many meanings. Be aware of difficulties—there are three kinds: difficulty in speech, difficulty in sight, difficulty in the heart. If the evil within the heart becomes apparent, it becomes a warning."

### 12.
"Be taught by shame. Shame is of two kinds: first, shame before God; second, shame before others. If you are disgraced, shame follows. Get along with your family and relatives; never cease being careful. If there is a rift, evil results."

### 13.
"Do not be bold toward your family and kin. Do not be presumptuous. Do not be arrogant. Do not be intrusive. Do not oppose. Whoever has an evil heart will surely find evil in the end—an evil person meets evil."

### 14.
"Whoever plants goodness will surely find prosperity, and so will their descendants. If a descendant becomes great, ruling the common people, do not be excessive, or it will not endure. When you are in a position of authority, do not oppress the little people, do not act arbitrarily."

### 15.
"Do not be bold, do not be quarrelsome, do not rely on your knowledge, do not be obstinate, do not be haughty, do not be deceitful, do not be careless—or you will be blamed. Ruin will come to you. When you see someone suffering a misfortune, fear it—it can strike you too."

### 16.
"On the contrary, follow this: a person of good character, love them. Give them their reward. Bless that person—they must not be measured. Follow their example—their demeanor is refined. When they speak, they consider their words. When they sit, they are ever careful, never arbitrary."

### 17.
"Do not be overbearing, child. Do not crave power and authority. Do not be a shaman, do not be a puppeteer, do not be a farmer. Do not be a merchant, do not be a religious official—the duty of zakat and tithe is to reduce quarrels over chickens and goats. That is what a true religious leader does."

### 18.
"Laundering, greed, swindling, butchery, peddling—these do not make one great. Avoid such talk. In life, be diligent. When speech is met, when expression is met, when glance is met—the heart of a person, good or evil; the honest, calm one shines."

### 19.
"Know the true person—that is the guru of the wise. His conduct is unseen—he neither fasts nor feasts, does no asceticism, does not wear white, but is ever mindful of himself. His only fear is his kin—and his kin is the world."

### 20.
"Water, earth, wind, fire, sky, sun, and moon—these are his kin. The person who is wrong—that is his enemy, truly. Therefore there is illness, and calamity comes. The person who is honest, man or woman, that one is his kin. Therefore there is blessing."

### 21.
"Those who are halfway, not yet certain, pursuing worldly wisdom, always lost in distraction, drifting north and south, bowing to the rich—they make knowledge their trade, they brag, they covet what belongs to others. They do not see that they are entered by Satan—they become windbags."

### 22.
"There is a prohibition called 'knowledge'—those who visit the homes of captains and officials, unwilling to accept correction, seeking only lasting profit, profit in this world, unshaken in their efforts. The efforts of such scholars are drawn to every glitter—they end up low."

### 23.
"If knowledge is defeated, such a scholar is called wicked. His body is already a giant—confused, adrift—he does not know the right means. He constantly creates discord in his begging, not counting body and soul, thinking only of gain—his conduct is ruined."

### 24.
"He pursues his own interests—this is someone who does not pity his own body, does not love his own flesh—so he becomes lost, never receiving the blessing of knowledge. His conduct is aimless, always distracted—he pursues only what glitters, not yet knowing the conduct and posture of knowledge—doing everything excessively."

### 25.
"He thinks his knowledge is correct, not realizing he is despised by superior beings. Such a person is deaf and blind, not knowing the right means, for his heart is already Satan's. His conduct is confused, his intention unbridled. His heart is swept along, entered by Satan—he no longer knows shame or disgrace."

### 26.
"There are many ways in life to pursue bodily gain, to make one's knowledge famous—staying awake every night, constantly fasting, being extremely humble, with a dagger smile, sweet expression, patient heart—making smile and expression a tool of enchantment, but with wrong intention."

### 27.
"It is not so for the one who is true. This body is like a raft on the ocean. What is the wave of water? The raft follows the current of water. That is called 'servant'—having no will of one's own, only the will of God, without desire, submerged in the ocean, with nothing left to approach."

### 28.
"The heart no longer approaches anything—it is empty, having no Lord, empty in true knowledge. It neither sees nor is seen, neither meets nor is met, neither approaches nor is approached—it has reached emptiness. All its actions are hidden—nothing can grasp it. It is empty, like a madman."

### 29.
When the teaching was finished, the prince kissed Kyageng Pariwara's feet, tears streaming, and said softly: "Oh revered ascetic, you have given me true guidance. My deepest gratitude—I treasure it in my heart. Only your blessing and the reward of your words, O exalted one, may they be fixed in my soul."

### 30.
Kyai Ageng spoke further: "The legacy of Kyai Sela's brilliance remains to this day. Here is his story from long ago: his work was farming—tending wet rice fields and dry fields. One afternoon after Asar prayer, he went to the fields, hoe in hand. When he arrived at the rice fields—"

### 31.
"—he set to work as was his habit. Then clouds gathered, rain fell gently, lightning flashed. Kyai Ageng quickly recited Subhanallah, and suddenly he saw an old man approaching. Kyai did not waver, undaunted by what came. The old man was drenched as he walked. The old man was seized."

### 32.
"He was caught—rumbling was his voice. The one who seemed an old man was in truth lightning. He was bound with a gandri rope. Kyai returned to his work. The lightning trembled, unable to escape. Later it was brought before the Sultan in Bintara and imprisoned in iron. Not long after, someone came—"

### 33.
"—an old woman carrying a water jar, coming to the prison. She sprinkled water—and both vanished, leaving only a terrifying sound. The iron prison shattered into dust. Throughout the land it became known: Kyageng Sela was truly peerless—he could catch lightning."

### 34.
"The gandri vine used to forge it—after the lightning was presented to the king, it blazed brilliantly. Kyai Ageng commanded his children, grandchildren, and servants: 'Take this lightning fire, use it as a household lamp. God willing, lightning will fear you, truly. Therefore do not die needlessly.'"

### 35.
"To this day, the lightning fire remains, unextinguished, in the burial grounds. Every year I pass the fire to His Majesty in Mataram. The rice field he worked is called 'heaven'—only a span of four 'kedhok' they call it. The cloud was its name."

### 36.
"Because every time the revered Kyai worked, a cloud came—two 'kedhok' they call it; third, 'Subhanallah'; 'Pandhapan' is its name. The kedhok of lightning is named. So it remains to this day. The gandri that was burned is gone—only its soil remains, bare, with no trees or grass growing. That is the story."

### 37.
"Besides farming fields and dry paddies, he taught his family, made indigo and salt, made dye, planted cotton and sesame, planted fiber plants, spun fine thread, wove silk for cindhe and gedhog cloth, and built the frames of joglo and limasan houses."

### 38.
"He composed the patterns of lurik cloth: tuwuhsela, three-five, badra and the rest. He fitted out the equipment, added structures, revived calculations from the true reckoning. In ancient times, Sri Manuhun began the counting, but no one heeded it—so it became the origin of tradition."

### 39.
"From Eyang Sela until now, the land serves as people's measure—called 'sabau.' The work of two people is called 'sakikil.' The work of four is 'sak-jung.' The butcher's reckoning: one sampil is sabau, two sampil is sakikil, four sampil is sak-jung, and so on."

### 40.
"Indeed, Ki Ngabdurakhman Kyageng Sela was rich in skill and sharpness. It is fitting to be your example—be instructed in heart, thankful if you can match him. If not, it is merely a slight. Your task is to continue his legacy, to carry on the testament of the king, Sri Nata of Ngèksiganda."

## Sinom 1.

### 1.
Thus the prince's heart was moved by Kyai Ageng Pariwara's words, forgetting food and sleep. His mind dwelled only on the love of the Almighty. He spoke softly to Kyai Ageng: "If it pleases you, I wish to continue my journey."

### 2.
"May you remain in well-being. I take my leave." "Go, child, do not be careless—I send you off with blessings." After the blessing, they shook hands. The prince withdrew. Gathuk and Gathak took their leave, and were permitted. The companions never left their master's side.

### 3.
They travelled northwest. As Maghrib approached, they arrived at the village of Gubug, where they saw a fire burning in the middle of a rice field, surrounded by a large screen. Nearby stood a small building in the shape of a mosque, close to the fire. Dhatuk Bahni of Marapi was its caretaker.

### 4.
Knowing visitors had come, he emerged from the building. He met the prince and the three shook hands. Ki Dhatuk said softly: "I am deeply moved by your arrival. What great fortune that someone wishes to visit this hermitage. Grandchild, where are you from?"

### 5.
"And what is your name, and what do you seek?" The prince answered simply: "My name is Jayengresmi. These are my companions, Gathak and Gathuk. I was born in Giri. I seek nothing in particular—I wander searching for my two brothers."

### 6.
"Now I only ask for shelter, if I may rest a while on your floor." Ki Dhatuk eagerly replied: "Oh, what a fine young man! Stay not just one night, but as long as you like—you are welcome to lodge here. My heart rejoices greatly, Grandchild."

### 7.
He brought water for ablution—it was time for Maghrib. After washing, Gathak gave the call to prayer. They completed Maghrib prayer and continued with Isya. After the prayers and greetings, they all sat together. The host offered a meal.

### 8.
Glutinous rice and roast chicken dipped in coconut milk, served on a panjang leaf, with windu chili sauce made from peanuts, pickled bamboo shoots, siwalan sugar in an emplik container, and sridenta flower tea. Ki Dhatuk spoke sweetly: "Come, Grandchild, please eat."

### 9.
"Take what there is—it is humble, but do not go hungry." The leftovers were given to the servants. The prince said softly: "Just now I saw clean, level ground, from which fire emerged steadily, close to the earth."

### 10.
"The flames were black, blue, yellow—like flowers at the edges, rising straight up, of moderate width, close to Marapi. There was fine, beautiful stone resembling a house pillar." Ki Dhatuk replied: "Marapi that burns with flickering flames—"

### 11.
"Come, child, let us go." He left the mosque, Ki Dhatuk leading the way. They arrived close to Marapi. Ki Dhatuk entered the fire, thrust his foot into it, and died—darkness descended. But in a moment he blazed up again. Dhatuk Bahni said: "Now, Gathuk, Gathak—"

### 12.
"Thrust your foot in like I did—when you die, just light something and you will surely blaze again." Gathak and Gathuk replied: "This is not a game, Grandfather Dhatuk. Fire is not a toy. Truly, if we did that, we would surely burn to ashes."

### 13.
"Go on, do it!" Gathuk was shoved quickly by Gathak, fell flat, scrambled to get up—no reaction. He thrust his foot in and died. He was lit and came back to life. He then took irrigation water and plunged knee-deep into it.

### 14.
The fire still flickered on the water's surface. The prince was deeply amazed by God's wonders. "How is this, Kyai—what is the origin of this?" Ki Dhatuk Bahni said: "I do not truly know. That pillar there dates from the Demak era."

### 15.
"When the Demak mosque was built, the Walis undertook it. Sunan Kalijaga was allotted one pillar-base. He carried it here, but by the time of post-Subuh, the mosque could not be completed—the pillars could not be set. So it was left here, and now it is a landmark."

### 16.
"Whoever is strong enough to lift it and sit it in place, his wishes will be fulfilled. Try if you wish." The prince replied: "I only seek the blessing of the exalted Walis of God." He was amazed by the sight of Marapi, and did not return to the house.

### 17.
The prince asked: "I have heard that in the realm of Ngèksiganda, the heart of the Javanese kingdom, if one wishes to undertake a wedding for a child, the tent erection is calculated and the month must be chosen. During the rainy season, one seeks an auspicious day."

### 18.
"Indeed, child, truly—even here in this village, whenever anyone has an undertaking, they ask me: the auspicious months for marrying off a child, drawn from Arab sources and Javanese reckoning, are combined so that one may find well-being."

### 19.
"The erection of the wedding tent is calculated over seven days. Starting from the first date, it is called 'king's tent'; the second date is called 'male ghost'; the third, 'female ghost'; the fourth, 'prosperity and well-being'; the fifth, 'prosperity and perfect safety'."

### 20.
"The sixth is 'male death'; the seventh, 'female death'. The eighth is the same as the first—so the sequence repeats. Counting: the first, eighth, fifteenth, twenty-second, and twenty-ninth are called the king's tent."

### 21.
"The second, ninth, sixteenth, twenty-third, and thirtieth are called 'male ghost'. The third, tenth, seventeenth, twenty-fourth, and thirty-first are called 'female ghost'. The fourth, eleventh, eighteenth, and twenty-fifth—these all bring safety and well-being."

### 22.
"The fifth, twelfth, nineteenth, and twenty-sixth—all bring prosperity and safety. The sixth, thirteenth, twentieth, and twenty-seventh are called 'male death'. The seventh, fourteenth, twenty-first, and twenty-eighth are called 'female death'—these are not auspicious."

### 23.
"Avoid those. As for the dangerous and ill-omened ones, and the restraints—the corpse, the leg-corpse, the 'ladder-hand' and others—their influence is strong. The main posts: it is required to use a dhadhap srêp timber for one. Use whichever is most auspicious."

### 24.
"One is the king's tent, worn by His Majesty the king. Two is 'male ghost'. Three is 'female ghost'—all these are not auspicious. The fourth brings well-being. The fifth brings prosperity—these are all auspicious. The sixth is 'male death'—not auspicious."

### 25.
Kaping sapta patining-dyah|ugi tan sae pinanggih|petang sapta yèn sri nata|kang kagêm petang satunggil|sanèse narapati|petang sakawan puniku|kalawan etang gangsal|punika salah satunggil|kang kinarsan dene wulane kawinan||

### 25.
"There is a saying of the Messenger commanding the faithful: know that those who marry find misery if the month is bad, but find joy and well-being if the month is good. It is proper to understand this."

### 26.
"Avoid Muharram for marriage—it foretells debt. The month of Safar is not auspicious—know what follows. Rabiul Awal brings the death of one spouse. In Rabiul Akhir, there is constant resentment and quarrelling."

### 27.
"In Jumadil Awal, one earns sin before God, often suffers miscarriage or stillbirth. If known, one bears great disgrace. Jumadil Akhir is a month rich in gold and money. Rajab brings children and wealth."

### 28.
"Sya'ban brings well-being—all endeavors find good. Ramadan brings sin and many whisperings of Satan. Syawal is not good—sickness is its reward. Dzulqaidah brings frequent sorrow. Dzulhijjah brings well-being in all actions."

### 29.
