"Now the matter of intercourse is described, from the words of the Prophet (peace be upon him) when he taught his exalted daughter Siti Fatimah, and commanded his son-in-law Sayid Ali (may God honor him) on these matters."

### 30.
"Ali, my teaching to you: do not have intercourse on the first of the month, nor at the end of the month—both are not good. If it happens, the child will be small. And do not have intercourse without a lamp."

### 31.
"If a child is conceived, it will be foolish, lacking in intelligence. And do not have intercourse on Sunday or Sunday night—if it happens, the child will be wicked. And do not have intercourse on Wednesday or Wednesday night—it is not good; the child will be unlucky."

### 32.
"And do not have intercourse at dawn—it is not good. If a child is conceived, it will be deficient. And do not have intercourse when the sun is rising—if a child is conceived, it will become a sorcerer. And do not have intercourse on the eve of a holy day."

### 33.
"For if a child is conceived then, it will be disobedient to father and mother. Do not have intercourse on the eve of the great holiday of Besar—if a child is conceived, it will have a cleft palate. And do not have intercourse during a solar eclipse—if a child is conceived, it will be far from fortune."

### 34.
"And do not have intercourse while standing—it is not good. If a child is conceived, it will have some illness later. And do not have intercourse on Saturday or Saturday night—if a child is conceived, its misfortune is to fall into water."

### 35.
"And do not have intercourse while wiping the genitals—and do not wipe with coarse cloth. If a child is conceived, it will be of weak intelligence. And do not have intercourse while chatting—it is not good, for the child may be talkative."

### 36.
"And do not have intercourse while looking at the genitals—if a child is conceived, it will be blind. And do not have intercourse under a fruit tree whose fruit is eaten—if a child is conceived, its deeds will be harmful."

### 37.
"And do not have intercourse on the night of Bara'at—it is not good. If a child is conceived, it will suffer incurable illness. Also, do not have intercourse while staying at an inn—if a child is conceived, it will bring great misfortune."

### 38.
"And do not have intercourse while a woman is menstruating—if a child is conceived, it will suffer from skin disease. Remember this well, Ali—believe in my teaching. Ali, you may have intercourse on Monday night—that is good, and Monday daytime is also good."

### 39.
"If a child is conceived then, it will be diligent in studying the Qur'an. And have intercourse on Tuesday night—it is good, and Tuesday daytime too. If a child is conceived, many people will love it. And have intercourse, Ali, on Thursday night and Thursday daytime."

### 40.
"If a child is conceived, it will have much fortune. And have intercourse on Friday night—it is good, and Friday daytime too. If a child is conceived, it will be rich in skills. And have intercourse before the setting of the sun on Friday."

### 41.
"Or on Thursday—both are good. If a child is conceived, it will become a religious leader. And it is also sunnah for those having intercourse to recite Bismillahirrahmanirrahim, and to recite the ta'awudz—that too is sunnah."

### 42.
"If one does not recite Bismillah when about to have intercourse, and a child is conceived, it will be foolish and deficient in intelligence. Therefore, when about to have intercourse, recite the ta'awudz first—lest the child take on the form of a known enemy. Let all follow this hadith of love."

## Asmaradana 1.

### 1.
"There is also a saying to remember: when about to have intercourse with a wife or concubine, avoid Saturday Legi—do not have intercourse on that day. It is bad if it happens."

### 2.
"The child will have a sickness, madness, that has occurred. The avoidance is only one day and night. And the next day too, do not have intercourse during the three garêbêg nights."

### 3.
"That is not good. If a child is conceived, it may have epilepsy. And also, do not have intercourse with a woman during her menstrual period—if a child is conceived, it may have a skin disease."

### 4.
"Beware and avoid it. When a woman is pregnant, do not be rough. Before she has bathed after her period, do not invite intercourse. If the desire is very strong, better seek another."

### 5.
"On Saturday Legi and the three garêbêg nights, refrain—do not forget. If the desire is very strong, endure it—it is only one night. Afterward, you may have intercourse."

### 6.
"As for Saturday Legi, one day and one night—endure it, do not forget. It is only one day and one night—what are you waiting for? Consider it a fast, abstaining from women."

### 7.
"Remember well, do not forget, be not careless. The words of the elders are the foundation—they must be obeyed. Do not contradict them. And there is an excellence when about to have intercourse—"

### 8.
"One must choose—time it for Friday, which is not dangerous, has no evil-omened days, and avoid the naas days. Have intercourse after the midday Friday prayer. After intercourse, bathe, then attend Friday prayer."

### 9.
"If a child is conceived, so say the elders, it may become a scholar, a religious leader, and have much good fortune. Here ends the written reminder—it is good to follow it."

### 10.
Gathak and Gathuk chuckled, whispering to each other. Gathuk spoke to Ki Dhatuk about the talk of cows—half in jest. Gathuk said softly: "Ah, old people are like that."

### 11.
"Let me ask, Kyai—besides what has already been laid out, are there any other prohibitions regarding intercourse?" Gathak stepped forward, bowing, and said softly: "Oh Kyai, forgive me—"

### 12.
"—my humble self asks to know: besides what has been revealed, what are the prohibitions of intercourse?" Ki Dhatuk smiled and said: "You rascal, you are clever—there are more prohibitions."

### 13.
"Elders who give instruction command us to refrain from intercourse with prostitutes and with female dancers—do not engage with them. For the consequences are bad—many suffer from venereal disease."

### 14.
"And secondly, often one suffers from syphilis—skin eruptions, rashes. If it reaches the nose, it often becomes blocked. If it reaches the eyes, many go blind."

### 15.
"There is more to avoid: intercourse with a woman of bad character. Even if she is beautiful, she is not fit to be visited. Once you are ill, many will blame you—no one will praise you."

### 16.
"Even if she is slightly flawed, if she is a virgin, you strain your muscles. The effect of a widow or divorcée is different—it does not increase your strength, only drains you. Once you are united, it takes no practice."

### 17.
"Nevertheless, it is not according to each person's preference—everyone has their own taste. Some desire widows, some desire divorcées, and some people enjoy marrying many times."

### 18.
"Their thoughts within their hearts, day and night they pursue it—yet when they go home, they work all day without rest. They go to a dancer for just a moment and spend three and a half."

### 19.
"And when evening comes, they need no capital—only flowers, powder, and cosmetics, a sarong and sash to change. Yet many buy—it does not take a moment, they earn only five coins."

### 20.
"That is why some people do it, casting aside shame and disgrace—marrying dancers and prostitutes. It cannot be equated—everyone's desires are ordained, varied and different."

### 21.
"Various are their forms, different are their wishes, they cannot be merged—different bodies, different souls. If they could be merged, those of one body and soul would still not agree in desire."

### 22.
"Like the eyes and the bladder, the stomach and the body—each has its own wish. The eyes want to sleep, the stomach wants to eat, the body is very tired, the bladder urges intercourse."

### 23.
"There is another teaching: when you have intercourse with a wife or concubine, do not overdo the foreplay—it is not good. If a child is conceived, it will be nervous, as is known by experience."

### 24.
"And secondly: during intercourse, do not chat or make inappropriate jokes—it is not good. If a child is conceived, it will be noisy, so say the elders."

### 25.
"Therefore, when having intercourse, do not overdo joking. Instead, keep your body and clothing clean. Use fragrance. If a child is conceived, it will be clean and fine in appearance."

### 26.
"And further, during intercourse, if you have a bad smell, the child will be ugly. Therefore, during intercourse, if you need to pass wind, release it first. Once the wind has passed, quickly resume."

### 27.
"So do not have intercourse until your body is clean. Getting up from sleep and doing it immediately, without going to the latrine—it is not good. If a child is conceived, its appearance will be coarse, with a rough complexion."

### 28.
"And there is another teaching: after eating, do not immediately have intercourse—until the food has gone down. It is harmful to the body, causing cough. That is well known."

### 29.
"The easier rule: after eating, while still feeling full, do not be bold to have intercourse until the stomach is relieved. If the desire is strong, endure a little while."

### 30.
Gathak and Gathuk, hearing this, burst out laughing uncontrollably, holding their aching stomachs. The prince merely smiled. Dhatuk Bahni said: "Rascals, that is indeed crude talk—it is joking about intimate matters."

### 31.
The prince said softly: "This is most beneficial—plain and without cover, easy to follow. Please continue, for in life surely..." Ki Dhatuk bowed and spoke.

### 32.
"Foremost is avoiding the Prophet's ill-omened days. In the month of Sura, the thirteenth date—that was when Prophet Ibrahim was thrown into the fire. The third of Mulud—Prophet Adam was sent down."

### 33.
"The eighteenth of Rabingulakir—that was when Prophet Joseph was lowered into the well. The fifth of Jumadilawal—Prophet Noah was cursed by his world. The twenty-first of the fasting month—"

### Prologue
He arrived at the village of Sela and met Kyai Pariwara, who explained: 1. The prohibitions of Kiageng Sela; 2. The story of Kiageng Sela catching lightning; 3. The weaving of lurik cloth. His journey continued to Gubug, meeting Dhatuk Bahni, who explained: 1. The calculation of days for erecting wedding tents; 2. The choice of auspicious wedding days; 3. Days forbidden for intercourse; 4. A treatise on intercourse; 5. The calculation of the Prophet's ill-omened days.

### 34.
"Prophet Moses when he was cast into the sea. The twenty-fourth of Sela—Prophet Jonah when he was swallowed by the whale. The twenty-fifth of Besar coincides with the Prophet beloved of God."

### 35.
"Muhammad the chosen, when he was stoned by the unbelievers—both faces were bruised. Those who are meticulous in counting every month: the base dates that coincide are the third and the fifth."

### 36.
"The thirteenth, the eighteenth, the twenty-first, the twenty-fourth, and the twenty-fifth—all are prohibited, along with the dangerous months. The year is divided into four: the Fast, Syawal, Dzulqaidah—"

### 37.
"Friday is dangerous. Besar, Sura, and Sapar—Saturday and Sunday are dangerous. Mulud, Rabingulakir, and Jumadilawal—Monday and Tuesday are dangerous. Madilakir, Rajab, and Ruwah—"

### 38.
"Wednesday and Thursday are dangerous." The prince said softly: "So many are the calculations. If this is the case, one must be very careful—it cannot be forced." "Yes, Grandchild, indeed—so rest this discussion."

### 39.
As dawn approached, they took water for ablution, then performed Subuh prayer. After the prayer time, the prince touched his head to the ground: "Oh Ki Dhatuk Bahni, truly you have given me a guide for living."

## Kinanthi 1.

### 1.
"Overwhelming is my gratitude—you have given me true guidance, the right and wrong of actions, the fitting method of intercourse, what is proper to do, what is worthy to be remembered."

### 2.
"Now I ask your leave, O exalted one—permission to continue my journey. May you remain well. Only your blessing ensures the safety of my path."

### 3.
Dhatuk Bahni said gently: "Oh grandchild, fine young man—do not lack forgiveness. I offer prayers for your well-being. May your journey know no hardship, and may you find what you seek."

### Prologue
His journey continued northward to Mount Prawata, viewing the remains of the Prawata palace. Then on to Demak, then to Mount Murya, making a pilgrimage to the grave of Sunan Muryapada. He met Buyut Sidasêdya, who expounded on: 1. Calculations for travel; 2. The journeys of the Prophets; 3. The dangerous influences on travel; 4. The Prophetic dangerous days; 5. Astrology; 6. Dangerous influences at the birth of an infant and at death; 7. Measurement of grave plots; 8. Measurement of pillars and rafters of a house; 9. The slope of a pendhapa and house; 10. Calculations for making a stable; 11. Stabling animals... pages 100-121

### 4.
"May you find well-being, and may your father in Mataram receive the king's favor, return and restore Giri, be honored by the family, established as of old."

### 5.
The prince bowed in thanks. After the blessing, he conveyed his greetings to the ascetic. Gathuk and Gathak paid homage. After receiving blessings, the three withdrew from his presence.

### 6.
Kyai Dhatuk who remained behind felt tears welling—as if he would follow them, such was his deep love. Unspoken was the parting—the depth of affection for his guests.

### 7.
The prince continued on his way, heading north-east for a time. The sun had climbed the mountain, emerging to illuminate all creation, bringing cheer to the heart.

### 8.
Past the open fields, entering the dense forest, crossing many marshes, his feet trod the hills—the region of the Undhakan kingdom. The hamlet of Prawata came into view.

### 9.
The prince said to Gathuk: "Come, let us approach that village we see." The three continued their journey. They arrived at the outskirts, stopping at the edge of a river.

### 10.
Shaded by nagasantun trees, bordered by wratsari foliage, enclosed by black stone—the prince took water for cleansing. Asar prayer was completed, and he sat by the edge of the river.

### 11.
Gathak and Gathuk nodded, examining their master's legs, peering left and right—nothing troubled their hearts. Their bodies felt calm. Such was the scene described.

### 12.
The headman of Prawata village was named Ki Darmajati. Darmawati was his wife. They had one daughter, named Wara Surendra, truly peerless in beauty.

### 13.
Her expression was awe-inspiring—golden-skinned, glowing, calm, gentle, and refined, sharp in discernment, thorough, virtuous of heart, radiant, with a brilliant face.

### 14.
Her hair hung in thick waves, dark and luxuriant. Her neck rose gracefully, shapely. Her bosom was full—like two perfect coconuts barely contained.

### 15.
Her shoulders were elegant, her arms like ivory bows. Her figure stirred desire. When she walked, she was careful—slow, soft, measured, her hips swaying like the waves.

### 16.
Like a lotus blooming on the water, swayed by the wind. She was skilled in all women's work. The girl followed her father to the fields to gather produce.

### 17.
Chili, eggplant, bitter melon, cucumber, ceme beans, and winged beans. She walked ahead, illuminated by the sun. The sun itself hesitated to enter the mountain.

### 18.
Enchanted, she paused and turned. The maiden stopped at a spring by the water's edge, breathless, trembling, with sighs and soft sobs.

### 19.
She met her father and spoke sweetly: "Father, let me tell you—over there by the riverbank, there is a newcomer in sight, sitting with legs outstretched, staring at me."

### 20.
"The one who stares at me is singing a Kinanthi tune, sitting cross-legged." Ki Darmajati asked: "Girl, is it a man or a woman?" "I don't know, Father—I didn't see clearly." The village head chuckled and said softly:

### 21.
"Hey, wife, go quickly to the fields at once with our daughter. After you have gathered the produce, whatever is in the fields, then prepare it well."

### 22.
"Pick jambu, dersana, and mangosteen, kepel, kokosan, rambutan—get duku too, don't leave any out—and old pomegranate, kaffir lime, salak, medhi. Mango, santok, sengir, honey—choose the ripe ones from the tree. Make clear vegetable soup, corn sambal, mênir vegetable, roast chicken with peanut sauce, and a salad of bean sprouts and basil."

### 23.
"Roast chicken—don't forget—and fried hen, smoked and grilled, onion sambal with celery salad, a sour-spicy mix of galangal and garlic, with sliced cucumber."

### 24.
"Coffee with sugarcane sugar, strained through a clean filter. Snacks: puthu tegal, carabikang, mendut koci, semar mendem, rolled omelettes, drizzled with coconut milk."

### 24.
"Fried sambal with shrimp—don't forget—mixed with liver, chicken skin crackling, petis that you have seasoned. Soft rice, hard rice, chicken and goat stew."

### 25.
"Sour starfruit leaf tea, cooled by cold water. Snack treats: cassava chips, linjik chips, fried banana with sugar, crispy fried cassava."

### 26.
"Don't be foolish—think it over with your wife, Nyai. Let no one be disappointed." Ki Darma then went to the river. Meanwhile, the prince was still being stared at by his two servants.

### 27.
The prince said to Gathak and Gathuk: "Earlier you were singing a tune, then you stood gaping and blinking." Gathuk replied: "There was a person—a woman, beautiful and lovely—about to go to the river."

### 28.
"Not long after, she hurried back—the earlier plan abandoned. Only the trace of her steps remained, flickering like a moving star. I think she is a dancer—or perhaps a daughter of a spirit."

### 29.
They stopped speaking as Ki Darmajati arrived. He drew close to where the prince sat and said softly: "Oh, Sir, I have only now seen you—forgive my familiarity, Grandchild."

### 30.
"Where are you from, handsome one? What is your name?" The prince replied sweetly: "I am called Jayengresmi, a santri from Surapringga, having visited Marapi."

### 31.
"And you, Uncle—what is your name and title?" "Darmajati is my name, headman of Prawata here. If it pleases you, I invite you to stop at my home."

### 32.
"At my humble dwelling, Uncle—that would be most welcome." "But that gate you see, Grandson—that is not my house. It is the remains of the palace of the Lord."

### 33.
"The ancient Sultan of Demak. Uncle, I wish to see the relics of the great." "Come, let me accompany you." They left the bathing place. Ki Darma led the way.

### 34.
They climbed and arrived at the alun-alun—a wide plain where only two banyan trees remained side by side. Then they ascended to the sitinggil. To the south, a gate was visible, arching at the edge of the hill.

### 35.
It was made of fine limestone, carved and fitted with intricate ornament. The path rose by steps of limestone. Arriving inside the gate, Ki Darma spoke:

### 33
## Mijil 1.

### 1.
"This is the form of the Demak palace, of the king—now two, the sultan's cities. When it rains, he moves here, for Demak city is flooded."

### 2.
"In the dry season, he returns to the city. So this place is called Susuhunan Prawata—so it is known to this day. That is the origin of the name of this mountain, Prawata."

### 3.
The prince marveled at the northern cliff—a wide marsh below the slope, like a sea crowded with birds: pelicans and ducks, herons, egrets, and white storks.

### 4.
"Storks, cuckoos, snipes, trinil birds—in flocks they shimmer, following the season for fish and shrimp. Spread like flowers, beautiful to see." After gazing long, the prince descended.

### 5.
He went west along the edge of the marsh and saw a shaded spring, walled with white limestone blocks arranged to form four corners within a pond.

### 6.
The floor was laid with polished black stone, built like brickwork—never submerged, though the water came close. In the pond, turtles great and small, all with white bellies, floated in orderly rows.

### 7.
They welcomed the newcomer. Gathak and Gathuk were amazed: "So many turtles, big and small, all with white bellies! And their leader—its head is the size of a gênuk jar!"

### 8.
Darmajati said softly: "Young lord, this is named Grudha—the former bathing place of the noble Sultan of Demak. The polished black stone was his seat."

### 9.
"And whenever he came here, even for midday prayer, it was never hot—shaded by gayam, nyamplung, waringin trees. Every Friday eve, on the night of Garakasih—"

### 10.
### 10.
"Many come here to perform ascetic practices—burning incense, flowers, and ointment under the kajeng tree. Come, my lord, inspect the bathing pool called Jibing—the royal bathing place."

### 11.
The royal entourage arrived at the edge of Jibing—clean, cool, and shaded by a gentle breeze. After the prince had seen it, Darmajati said:

### 12.
"Enough, my lord—you have seen all. Come, rest at your uncle's home, to restore your body." The prince agreed and they continued on their way.

### 13.
In the pendhapa, lamps blazed. The prince sat beneath the rainspout. Gathak and Gathuk were on the pendhapa's veranda. Ki Darma went to the inner quarters to speak with his wife.

### 14.
"You yourself serve them, Nyai, with our daughter. Prepare hot water, tea, and food. After Isya, arrange a proper meal. Your guest is of high rank."

### 15.
Darmawati agreed—everything was prepared. Darmajati emerged before the prince. Before long, the meal was brought. Nyai stood in front, with the girl Menok behind.

### 16.
He spoke quickly, making introductions: "This is my wife, Nyai Darmawati. Behind her is my daughter—the first-born and also the youngest, she has no siblings."

### 17.
"Surendra is her true name, but she is called Menok—a nickname, for she is plain and poor in looks, simple from living far from the capital, her foolishness extreme, nothing but a simpleton."

### 18.
"Nyai, present the offering to the young lord. May your coming here, Grandchild, bring blessing—your aunt's prayer is for your well-being."

### 19.
Nyai Darma turned to the back: "You there, Menok—bow at his feet, Grandchild. Ask for blessing that you may soon find a husband, obtain a proper match, and have a long, safe life."

### 20.
The girl Menok without hesitation bowed at the young prince's feet—composed, steady, her heart untroubled. Prince Jayengresmi thought within his heart—

"This girl—truly beautiful and charming."

### 21.
"There is not a single flaw. She is fittingly composed. Her face is the equal of Rancangkapti's, only her expression is gentler." After bowing, the girl Menok withdrew.

### 22.
Darmajati said softly: "Please, young lord, partake of what there is—and these snacks from the mountains." Jayengresmi bowed. The tea was drunk.

### 23.
While eating cassava chips, he finished three baskets. Gathak and Gathuk were no different in their eating—the lads there ate ravenously, both stuffing their mouths, slurping noisily.

### 24.
They devoured four plates of snacks in an instant, as if swept clean. When Maghrib came, the prince went to the langgar, the host accompanying him. They arrived at the mosque. Kyai Darma returned home.

### 25.
The meal was laid on a decorated bench, covered with cloth. A bright lamp with wick blazed. The girl Menok arranged it all. When finished, she stood close to the bench.

### 26.
Darmajati asked his child: "Is it done, Menok?" "Yes, Father—only the stew remains, and the fatty meats. Later, when it is ready, I will sit in my usual place."

### 27.
Now the story turns to the mosque. Gathuk gave the call to prayer. After the sunnah prayer and the qamat, they performed the obligatory Maghrib prayer, followed by supplication until Isya.

### 28.
After Isya, the prince said softly: "Gathuk, the girl they brought up earlier—is she as beautiful as the one at the river?" Gathuk replied: "They are the same—not a hair's difference."

### 29.
"I am amazed at the girl's composure—she showed no hesitation. And the child was so steady and confident." "Yes, Gathuk, in my observation—here and there it is the same, though different in seeming."

### 30.
Prince Jayengresmi said softly: "That is no lie—such is life. It is a sign of one thing: in this life, there is no difference."

### 31.
Kyai Darma came to the langgar to invite the young warrior: "If you have finished your prayers, your meal is prepared—the food for the young lord is now ready."

### 32.
"Please proceed to the inner quarters." The prince walked slowly, then sat close to the table. Hot stew and fatty meats were placed before him—all steaming.

### 33.
Niken Darmawati said softly: "Grandchild, your aunt offers this humble meal." The prince bowed and replied gently: "Please, Uncle and Aunt—it is best we eat together."

### 34.
The three ate together. The girl Menok served, offering vegetables, sambal, and meat without pause. Prince Jayengresmi ate heartily.

### 35.
The rice and stew—just one luwak portion, no more. The grilled meat—only a thumb-sized piece. Ginger salad—two slices, no more. After eating, he refused repeatedly. Ki Darma was astonished.

### 36.
Fruit was then brought forward. The prince quickly took two white duku fruits, peeled them, and ate one. Gathak and Gathuk ate, loosening their belts.

### 37.
After the meal was finished, they shifted their seats westward. Kyai Darma said to the prince: "If you wish to rest, please go to the inner room—for you seem tired, like gold washed away." The prince replied:

## Maskumambang 1.

### 1.
"I am quite comfortable here, Uncle." Kyai Darma bowed and spoke to his child: "Prepare hot water and serve it."

### 2.
Tea, food, whatever was prepared—the girl Menok was quick, serving before him, a cloth draped over her shoulder.

### 3.
When all was ready, Ki Darma introduced: "Please eat—Menok prepared it herself, the product of her study of cooking."

### 4.
The prince smiled and said softly: "It is a woman's duty to be skilled in all cooking—not to be lazy and always asking."

### 5.
"Skilled in all women's work: making medicinal porridge and powder, cosmetics, paste, green powder, salves, potions and remedies."

### 6.
"Knowing all the ingredients—roots, barks, leaves that are beneficial—ready for whatever work is needed."

### 7.
"Spinning, weaving, embroidering, crocheting, sewing, stringing flowers, batik-making, dyeing, weaving fine cloth, adorning the contents of the house."

### 8.
"Careful, thorough, keeping secrets, never lacking in example—the highest duty of a woman is to follow the right and avoid the left."

### 9.
"She who is accomplished in discernment and propriety, who considers before acting, whose day and night are directed by the guidance of the household."

### 10.
"If you can be as I have said, begin now: learn household management as if serving a husband."

### 11.
"Perhaps you will be blessed in this world and the next—that is the foundation. Later, when you serve a husband, you will seek no further excellence."

### 12.
Ki Darma and Nyai nodded. "Menok, take to heart what the prince has said—plant it in your heart."

### 13.
"Yes, Father—may I receive true blessing. The words of the exalted one bring benefit to body and soul, that I may receive grace."

### 14.
The night passed without sleep—they sat comfortably, eating snacks from time to time. Until the hour of dawn arrived.

### 15.
"The pot of the drum—Brahim's iron-opponent—from the drum-pot at Asar time. Joseph is safe in double measure. Asar, one full night. In the morning, the sun emerges. Izrail provides for it. The second is the same as the seventh, twelfth, seventeenth, twenty-second, and twenty-seventh."

### 16.
"At the full moon of the sun-god—the limit of the twenty-first opponent—Jibrail provides for it, from the twenty-first opponent limit. The eleven limit—Ibrahim's teaching, his double: the eleven pot of the drum. Joseph's double encompasses it. The drum pot of Izrail—iron-opponent. Asar, one full night."

### 17.
"The pot up to the full moon of the sun. Ahmad is safe on the third, eighth, thirteenth, eighteenth, twenty-third, and twenty-eighth. From the full moon of the sun, the opponent's twenty-one limit. Ibrahim is safe in double measure. The opponent's twenty-one pot, the eleven pot—it falls upon Joseph's provision."

### 18.
"The eleven opponent pot of the drum—at the time Izrail's teaching comes double. The drum up to Asar's pot. Ahmad is encompassed. From the market, the pot arrives at the full moon of the sun. Jibrail—his double is iron-opponent. Then the fourth, ninth, fourteenth—"

### 19.
"Nineteenth, twenty-fourth, and twenty-ninth—these are counted from the full moon of the sun. The twenty-first opponent pot—Joseph's iron-opponent falls. From the twenty-first opponent, the eleven pot. Izrail is safe at opponent eleven. The drum-pot is Ahmad's provision, from the drum-pot at Asar."

### 20.
"Jibrail's teaching comes double. Asar arrives at the full moon of the sun. Ibrahim is encompassed now. The fifth, tenth, fifteenth, and twentieth, twenty-fifth, thirtieth—these are all the same. From the full moon of the sun to the opponent's twenty-one pot-limit, Izrail is encompassed."

### 21.
"The opponent of twenty-one, the eleven pot—Ahmad's iron-opponent in double measure. The opponent of eleven is its origin. The pot up to the drum—Jibrail is safe, doubling. The drum's pot at Asar is Brahmin's provision. Asar, one full night, the pot—the full moon of the sun concludes. Joseph's teaching is Ahmad."
### 22.
He stopped at the river to perform Subuh prayer. After prayer, the prince continued north-west, crossing obstacles.

### 23.
His heart was drawn to see the Demak mosque—the work of the Walis. The story does not linger on the journey.

### 24.
He entered the mosque courtyard. No one suspected this was the prince of Giri—he was taken for an ordinary santri.

### 25.
He ascended the veranda—the eight pillars were carved and ornamented, brought from Majapahit. The chronogram was visible.

### 26.
The double doors were the work of ancient craftsmen. The openings to the mosque were carved like royal motifs, lightning designs painted. On the door's step was written a chronogram reading 'four kingdoms made as one, the form of lightning unified.' The prince entered quickly.

### 27.
Upon arriving inside the mosque, there was profound blessedness—the sense of spiritual benefit filled his being, strengthening his submission to God.

### 28.
The four main pillars were of equal size, their height fitting, round and smooth-grained, precisely shaped—each two arm-spans in girth.

### 29.
The east-north pillar was different from the other three—it was made of wood shavings compressed into one, round and evenly smooth.

### 30.
The work of His Holiness Sunan Kalijaga, the venerated—a legacy for the land, a sign of the blessing of the Walis of God.

### 31.
The prince's devotion grew as he witnessed the wonders of God from beneath the pulpit.

### 32.
Clusters of fragrant glagah grass grew there—the prince's sign, hidden from all eyes, a mark of future grace.

### 33.
He left the mosque interior and continued his journey. The tale does not dwell on the time—he set foot in the Jepara region.

### 34.
He arrived at the foot of Mount Murya, arching over the Java Sea. He climbed, wishing to see the peak of Mount Murya.

### 35.
The path was steep and rough, flanked by deep ravines—the walking was hard. Gathak and Gathuk were exhausted, stopping to rest every moment.

### 36.
They rested at night; in the morning they set off again. After three days they reached the summit, gazing at the vast ocean.

### 37.
On the sea, sailing ships could be seen—some at anchor, others weighing anchor and sailing—a beautiful sight to behold.

### 38.
In the kingdom below the mountain, the village was a joyful sight. The prince was startled to see a grave radiating a bright aura.

### 39.
He offered a greeting; it was answered. They recited the tahlil together—the prince and the two santri. After the tahlil, someone arrived.

### 40.
An old man approached Prince Jayengresmi. He spoke softly: "Kyai, forgive my boldness—who is it that you guard here?"

### 41.
"And who is buried here?" The reply came gently: "I am the caretaker, named Buyut Sidasêdya."

### 42.
"He who is buried here is Sunan Murya by name. The name of this grave is Muryapada, and the mountain is called Murya."

### 43.
"So, do you wish to perform ascetic practices here?" "Oh, Buyut, I only wish to see the peak of Mount Murya."

### 44.
"Since I have seen it, I now take my leave to descend the mountain." Buyut Sidasêdya said: "Take note of the day for your journey—"

### 45.
"—if it pleases you, young lord, stop at my dwelling. It would be well for the future—the day's influence is most auspicious."

### 46.
"The fulfillment of your wishes and the attainment of blessing—" Hearing this, the prince's heart was gladdened.

### 47.
They proceeded and arrived at the dwelling. Ki Buyut ordered: "Gathak and Gathuk, santris—do not delay!"

### 48.
"Fetch my food yourselves—intellect, offering-neck, jicama, taro, sweet potato, yam, cassava, and potato."

### 49.
Gathak and Gathuk did not refuse—they went to the fields, happy to gather produce. When done, they returned to the house.

### 50.
He roasted corn, potato, and sweet yam. Tea was prepared with kahwa leaves and served. Ki Buyut was pleased to see.

### 51.
"Come, young sir, let us eat together." They snacked. "This is all a mountain dweller has—alone for thirty years."

### 52.
Jayengresmi smiled after eating the yam, then asked: "After gathering all this, what is your purpose?"

### 53.
"I use it as alms for the ascetics and the poor. Those who ask for food come here, bringing their own provisions."

### 54.
"I only save some for seed. That is the way of it—I cannot give money. My sweetness is only in root crops."

## Dhandhanggula 1.

### 1.
Prince Jayengresmi spoke softly: "Which days are auspicious? Are there any others—times to choose and avoid?" Ki Buyut replied: "Grandchild, this is a calculation as preparation, following the counsel of ancient times, originating from Arabia."

### 2.
"This is a matter of knowledge—the words of the Prophet (peace be upon him): 'All my community, know what is above: the conduct of travel. Choose what is proper. Do not travel on the first—there is no profit. Travel on the second—you will gain profit.'"

### 3.
"The third date is not good—no profit. The fourth—hardship on the road. The fifth brings profit. The sixth brings no profit—loss of one's work. The seventh is auspicious. The eighth brings God's mercy but illness in the destination."

### 4.
"The ninth brings death and difficulty, all standing ready. The tenth brings profit and well-being. The eleventh is fitting. The twelfth brings no profit. The thirteenth brings debt. The fourteenth is good—profit in the destination."

### 5.
"The fifteenth brings fine gold and silver. The sixteenth brings no profit. The seventeenth brings well-being. The eighteenth brings profit. The nineteenth is fitting and well. The twentieth brings illness on the road. The twenty-first brings restlessness at the destination."

### 6.
"The twenty-second is fine and good. The twenty-third brings profit in work. The twenty-fourth is fitting. The twenty-fifth is bad. The twenty-sixth brings life and death—that is what is met. The twenty-seventh is bad—quarrels on the road. The twenty-eighth—"

### 7.
"The twenty-ninth brings profit—mercy from God. The thirtieth brings debt on the journey. Here ends this matter of travel. The words of the Messenger (peace be upon him)—be meticulous as this. And God knows best."

### 8.
"The journeys of the venerable Prophets: when about to leave home, there are signs to use. On Friday, wear a veil and follow the journey of Prophet Muhammad the Messenger of God. On Saturday—this contains earth—press it to the navel, following the journey of Prophet Adam, the venerated."

### 9.
"On Sunday, wear earrings—the journey of Lord Joseph. On Monday, sharpen the blade—the journey of Lord Umar. On Tuesday, hold the hand in fire—the journey of Lord Abu Bakr. On Wednesday, wear a head covering and sarong—the journey of Prophet Job. On Thursday, gaze at the sky."

### 10.
"And bowing to the earth—the journey of Lord Ali. Additional prohibitions: when about to set out on campaign, the departure—you already know—the step count of the opponent. The dangerous influence: if the opponent has taken ten steps, the power of Garuda is defeated by any serpent."

### 11.
"Monday, after six steps, the power of the fierce lion is defeated—as is the deer. Tuesday, after two steps, the power of the charging lion is defeated by the elephant. Wednesday, after seven steps, the power—"

### 12.
"Of the sun, defeated by the moon. Thursday, after two more steps. Friday, after thirteen steps, the power of the dog is defeated by the mouse-deer. Saturday, after twelve steps, the power—"

### 13.
"Of the serpent, defeated by the hunchbacked frog. Here ends the prohibitions of Lord Ali. There are also prohibitions used for marrying off a child and for setting up any structure. These follow the date: the first is the beginning; the second, third, fourth, fifth—then it returns again. The sixth is like the first."

### 14.
"The first date—the same as the sixth, eleventh, eighteenth, twenty-first, and twenty-sixth—from the sun's emergence to the opponent's twenty-one limits. Ahmad spoke truly: from the opponent's twenty-one limit—the opponent's eleven limit—Jibrail has a double portion, from the opponent's eleven limit."

### 15.
Ê

Purnamaning Hyang Pradanggapati|

### 16.
pot pêcak salikur punika|Jabarail rêjêkine|wiwit pêcak salikur|

ê

### 16.
pot pêcak sawlas Ibrahim|pitutur rangkêpannya|sawlas êpot bêdhug|Yusup kalangan rangkêpnya|bêdhug potnya Ijrail apacak-wêsi|Asar sadalu pisan||

### 17.
Pote têkèng purnamaning rawi|Ahmad slamêt tanggal kaping tiga|ping wolu ping triwêlase|wolulas tigalikur|wolulikur punika sami|wit purnamaning surya|pot pêcak salikur|Ibrahim slamêt rangkêpan|pêcak slikur pot pêcak sawlas marêngi|Yusup rijêkinira||

Pêcak sawlas êpot bêdhug wanci|Ngijrail pitutur rangkêpan|bêdhug têkèng Asar pote|Ahmad kalanganipun|awit pasar potira prapti|purnamaning Hyang Surya|Jabarail iku|pacak-wêsi rangkêpannya|gantya tanggal ping sakawan saminèki|ping sanga ping patbêlas||

Ping sangalas patlikur myang kaping|sangalikur punika ingetang|wit Hyang Surya purnamane|

ê

pot pêcak salikur|Yusup pacakwêsi marêngi|awit salikur pêcak|sawêlas potipun|Ngijrail salamêt pêcak|sawlas êpot bêdhug Ahmade rêjêki|wit bêdhug êpot Asar||

Jabarail pitutur ngrangkêpi|Asar prapta purnamaning surya|Ibrahim kalangan mangke|ping gangsal sadasèku|gangsalwêlas myang kalihdèsi|salawe tigang dasa|pan sami puniku|awit purnamaning surya|têkèng pêcak salikur êpotirèki|Ngijrail akalangan||

Pêcak slikur sawêlas potnèki|Ahmad pacakwêsi rangkêpannya|pêcak sawêlas awite|

ê

### 21.
pote têkèng bêdhug|Jabarail slamêt ngrangkêpi|bêdhug êpotnya Asar|Brahim rijêkiku|Asar sadalu êpotnya|purnamane Sanghyang Surya amungkasi|Yusup pitutur Ahmad||

### 22.
Prince Jayengresmi asked further: "What about the dangerous influences of the stars? Are there any others?" Ki Buyut nodded: "It depends on the day of travel. There are seven stars, each day and night having their own benefits—good, fitting, and the birth of an infant, all determined by the stars."

### 23.
"The star Samsu: everything is auspicious—approaching the king, great people, and the like. Marriage is especially favorable. What is bad: illness, stopping travel, war, medicine, employment, making clothes, summoning relatives—gatherings are not auspicious."

### 24.
"A child born during the daytime, male or female, will have long life, a bright heart, but little fortune and a worried heart. If born at night, their nature is dark of heart, with a gloomy spirit."

### 25.
"The star Juhrah, number two: everything is auspicious—discussions of knowledge, excellence, marriage, prosperity, blessing children, considering all matters, suitable work. What is bad: dividing work, confronting enemies in war, any confrontation."

### 26.
"A child born male or female during the day has a broad countenance. If born at night, they are very sharp in speech, firm, not fumbling. The third is spoken of: the star Natarit—praised."

### 27.
"Everything is auspicious for teaching and instructing. Approaching the king and seeking medicine are all good. What is not good: adornment, divorce, haircutting for birth. If a woman, good with much fortune, but excessive in speech, hot-tempered of heart. Only capable in work, not disappointing. If a man is born by day—"

### 28.
"It is bad—great anxiety of heart. If a woman is born at night, her nature is narrow-minded. If a man is born at night, his nature is broad-minded, patient, generous in the world, rich in fortune, compassionate to all. Now the star Kamar is described: everything is auspicious."

### 29.
"Also approaching the king, speaking with benefit, storing up good fortune. Trading brings profit. Confrontation brings favorable acceptance. Visiting brings sincere welcome. What is bad is spoken of: lawsuits, quarrels, proposing marriage, or wedding arrangements—these are not auspicious."

### 30.
"A female child born during the day—wasteful in nature. If male, born by day—excessively deficient. If female, born at night—good, capable in work, devoted to husband, careful in all things. If male, born at night—wasteful with women."

### 31.
"The star Pancajuhkal is described. What is good: beginning intense asceticism, devotion to God, from beginning to end. Other than that is not auspicious. If a child is born by day or night, male or female—all the same—they will constantly suffer rheumatism without relief, but have much fortune."

### 32.
"They are patient and generous in the world. Their only flaw is being somewhat forgetful. Now the sixth: the star Mustari—the king of the seven stars. Everything is exceedingly good—all actions bring well-being. What is bad is only one: contemplating confronting war. If a child is born by day—"

### 33.
"Male or female—both are auspicious. Their nature is truly grateful. If a female is born at night—fearful of her husband, skillful in both coarse and fine work, proficient and wise. If a male child is born at night—very extravagant, not content to ask from the garden, but liking to steal in his heart."

### 34.
"Now the seventh and last: the star Mirah, which is auspicious for—rejecting slander, form, adornment—also lawful gain, or confronting war, and making weapons. Other than that, all work is bad. If a female child is born, her nature is malicious of heart."

### 35.
"If a male is born by day—his nature is unreliable. If born at night—"### 36.
"Sunday evening at Maghrib time—the star Samsu receives. At Isya, Juhrah receives. At the time when people sleep, the star Tarit receives. Midnight—Kamar. The waning of night is received by the star Juhkal. At dawn, the star Mustari receives. At Subuh, Mirah takes over."

### 37.
"Sunday morning—Samsu receives. The busy market is received by Juhrah. After the market—Tarit. The drum-hour—Kamar sits. Luhur—Juhkal receives. Asar is received by Mustari. Evening is received by the star Mirah. Complete—one night and one day—the course of the seven stars."

### 38.
"Monday evening at Maghrib time—the star Kamar receives. Isya—Juhkal receives. At the time when people sleep, the star Mustari receives. Midnight—Mirah. The waning of night—the star Samsu sits. At dawn, the star Juhrah receives. At Subuh, the star Natarit."

### 39.
"Monday morning—the star Kamar receives. The busy market is received by Juhkal. After the market, Mustari receives. At the drum-hour, Mirah receives. At Luhur time—Samsu. Until Asar is complete, received by the star Juhrah. At evening, Natarit receives. Complete—the seven stars."

### 40.
"Tuesday evening at Maghrib time—the star Mirah settles. At Isya time—Samsu receives. When people sleep, the star Juhrah sits. Midnight—Natarit sits. The waning of night is received by the star Kamar. At dawn, the star Juhkal receives. At Subuh, the star Mustari."

### 41.
"Tuesday morning—Mirah receives. The busy market—Samsu sits. After the market, Juhrah receives. The drum-hour is quickly received by Natarit at Luhur. Kamar receives. Asar—Juhkal sits. Evening is received by Mustari—the pot at Maghrib time. Complete—the seven stars."

### 42.
"Wednesday evening at Maghrib time—the star Natarit's pot. At Isya time, received by the star Kamar. When people sleep, the star Juhkal receives. Midnight—Mustari sits. The waning of night is received by the star Mirah. Dawn has already received. At Subuh time—Juhrah."

### 43.
"Wednesday morning—Natarit receives. The busy market is received by Kamar. After the market, Juhkal receives. The drum-hour is then taken by Mustari at Luhur. The star Mirah at Asar time. Samsu sits at evening. The star Juhrah receives. Complete—the seven stars."

### 44.
"Thursday evening at Maghrib time—Mustari arrives. Isya is received by Mirah. When people sleep—Samsu. Midnight is received by the star Juhrah. At the waning of night, Natarit receives. At dawn, the star Kamar receives. At Subuh time—Juhkal."

### 45.
"Thursday morning—Mustari receives. The busy market—the star Mirah sits. After the market, Samsu receives. The drum-hour is quickly received. The star Juhrah receives at Luhur. Natarit sits at Asar time. Received by the star Kamar. At evening, the star Juhkal receives. Complete—the seven stars."

### 46.
"Friday evening at Maghrib time—the star Juhrah sits. At Isya time, received by Natarit. When people sleep—Kamar receives. Midnight—Juhkal. The waning of night—the star Mustari. At dawn, the star Mirah receives. At Subuh—Samsu."

### 47.
"Friday morning—the star Juhrah receives. The busy market—Natarit sits. After the market, Kamar resides. The drum-hour is quickly taken by the star Juhkal. Luhur is immediately received by Mustari. At Asar time, received by the star Mirah. At evening—Samsu receives. Complete—the seven stars."
### 48.
"Saturday evening at Maghrib—Natarit. At Isya time, received by Kamar. When people sleep, the star Juhkal—its pot. Midnight—Mustari receives. The pot of the waning night is received by Mirah. At dawn, the star Samsu receives. At Subuh—the star Juhrah."

### 49.
"Saturday morning—the star Juhkal receives. The busy market—Mustari sits. After the market, the star Mirah. The drum-hour is received by Samsu. At Luhur time, Juhrah receives. The pot of Asar is received by Natarit. At evening, received by Kamar. Complete—the course of the seven stars, night and day."

### 44.
Malêm Kêmis ing waktu Mahribi|taranggana Mustari praptanya|Ngisa tinampènan Mirèh|sirêp ing janma Samsu|têngah ratri ingkang nampèni|kanang tranggana Juhrah|sèrèn lingsir dalu|tinampèn Ngatarit lintang|bangun enjing lintang Kamar ingkang tampi|ing waktu Subuh Juhkal||

### 45.
Kêmis enjing Mustari kang tampi|rame pasar lintang Mirah lênggah|bar pasar Samsu tampine|bêdhug tinampèn gupuh|lintang Juhrah Luhur tinampi|Ngatarit ingkang lênggah|sèrèn Ngasri wêktu|tinampènan lintang Kamar|wanci sontên lintang Juhkal ingkang tampi|jangkêp kasapta lintang||

### 46.
Malêm Jumngah ing waktu Mahribi|lintang Juhrah ingkang apilênggah|ing waktu Ngisa êpote|tinampèn Ngataritu|sirêp janma Kamar nampèni|têngah ratrinya Juhkal|lingsir ratrinipun|lintang Mustari punika|bangun enjing lintang Mirah kang nampèni|Subuh Samsu tranggana||

### 47.
Jumngah enjing lintang Juhrah tampi|rame pasar Ngatarit kang lênggah|lêbar pasar Kamar manggèn|bêdhug ginantyan gupuh|lintang Juhkal ingkang nyitèni|luhur gya tinampènan|Mustari sitèngsu|wêktu Ngasri tinampènan|lintang Mirah sontêne Samsu kang tampi|jangkêp lintang kasapta||

### 48.
Malêm Saptu Mahribi Ngatarit|waktu Ngisa tinampèn ing Kamar|sirêp janma gya tinampèn|lintang Juhkal potipun|têngah ratri ingkang nampèni|Mustari taranggana|

ê

### 48.
pot lingsir dalu|tinampèn Mirah tranggana|bangun enjing lintang Samsu kang nampèni|Subuh pan lintang Juhrah||

### 49.
Saptu enjing lintang Juhkal tampi|rame pasar Mustari kang lênggah|lêbar pasar lintang Mirèh|bêdhug tinampèn Samsu|waktu Luhur Juhrah nampèni|pot Ngasri tinampènan|Ngatarit sitèngsu|sontêne tinampèn Kamar|titi sampun lampahing lintang saptari|dalu praptaning siyang||

### 50.
Kang minangka naasing lintang dwi|(ng)gih punika Mirah lawan Juhkal|ngagêsang winênangake|sêtiyar mrih rahayu|yèn wus atut etanganèki|gya sumendhe ing Suksma|widadaning kayun|amung aywa kinêkahan|pêpetangan tan sagêd ngewahkên takdir|siningkura kainan||

### 50.
"A teaching from friends and associates, brahmins of the Upper Wind: when a person is about to have a child, or someone approaches the appointed time of birth or death, it is based on the seven days. Day and night are divided into twelve parts."

### 51.
"Starting from sunrise: the divisions are six, seven, eight, nine, ten, eleven—the noon hour. Twelve o'clock is the drum. Then the division returns to one, two, three, four, five, and back to six at sunset. Then counting continues as in daytime. So there are only twelve periods. On Sunday—"

### 52.
"The divisions: six, seven, eleven—the hour one and five. Monday: eight and ten, one and three. Tuesday: seven, ten, and eleven, two and five. Wednesday: seven, nine, eleven, two and four. Thursday: eight, eleven, and one, three, four."

### 53.
"Friday: eight, ten, twelve, three, and four. Saturday: seven and nine, twelve, two, and four. If someone is ill or in death throes on Sunday—whether birth or death—the divisions are six, seven, eleven, one, or five—one of these."

### 54.
Prince Jayengresmi said softly: "So many are your explanations, to ensure well-being and blessing. It is best to remember them and not be careless with your words. Is there a consensus—is it true—about making grave plots and houses, measuring pillars and rafters, the good and bad of the calculation?"

### 55.
"Indeed, there is. When making a grave plot, it must be observed. When working on a wall, its length and width—the owner's intention. When beginning, measure north, west, south—changing from the fathom-measure. The calculation of earth, wealth, time, river—it falls on wealth."

### 56.
"If it goes east, it falls on earth. If making a garden, aligning with the seasons—the width and narrowness of the plot—the heart's desire. In the courtyard, if it goes north, it meets the wall's measure—the owner's prosperity. The state of one's house."

### 57.
"Align with the wall, do not forget. If there is a wing on the left side of the house, the right side is the path to the house. Connect it to the outer wall. The east—the left arm from the house—must connect with your house. The span of the pendhapa—quickly measure it, and your sitting place."

### 58.
"The structure of the house and the pendhapa. The pendhapa and the lodra. The taper of the footmark. Both pendhapa and lodra—if making a gate, the wall's length and its measurement. Do not be careless with this instruction—it is good not to neglect it, if your fortune permits."## Pangkur 1.

### 1.
"When building a house, the measure is the owner's own fathom. The length is measured in five calculations: sri, adi, naga, gold, silver. If the house falls on gold, the pendhapa falling on silver is auspicious."

### 2.
"If the kitchen falls on naga, the roof and mortar falling on adi is auspicious. If making a granary, falling on sri in its measure is good. The pillars are measured accordingly, besides the joining at the bottom and the tenon."

### 3.
"To measure the width of a pillar, the same measurement is used for its length—also five calculations: earth, water, mountain, time, fire—these five complete. Falling on earth is good—earth's nature is firm."

### 4.
"Falling on water—its nature is dampness—that is also good. If falling on mountain, its nature is often overgrown. If falling on time, it is very bad—the owner will be ill. Falling on fire is not good."

### 5.
"Its nature is to be burned. Now the rafters: count each one, also five calculations. Sri, kakitri, gana, kliyu, pokah. If a house is calculated by rafters, falling on sri is good. The pendhapa should fall on kitri."

### 6.
"Both of these are good. For a mosque, gana is what is good. For anything other than a mosque, if it falls on gana, it becomes haunted or terrifying. Kliyu is not good—illness for the house owner."

### 7.
"If counting falls on pokah, it is not good—often suffering sorrow, death, calamity. Enough of this counting. Now the fence: its measure is the height of the owner and the width of his stride."

### 8.
"For the width of a grave plot, one's satisfaction is not to be forced. Five are the numbers: opening, sri, tapa, lungguh, sedana, pugeran. Falling on sri—receptive nature. Tapa—hungry nature."

### 9.
"Lungguh—firm nature. Sedana—wealthy nature. Pugeran—believing and steadfast nature. Enough of this. The distance between the pendhapa and the house also has its measurement."

### 10.
"The owner's fathom: four calculations, starting from the hall. The house, the yard, the kitchen—the hall is where they fall. For making a stable or a buffalo/cattle shed—"

### 11.
"Its crossbeam is measured by fathom, besides the tenon that enters left and right. Four are its calculations: candhi, karta, rogoh, sampoyong. What is good is falling on karta, and falling on candhi."

### 12.
"For the stable or shed posts, aside from the tenons and buried parts, they are measured with the crossbeam as above. Falling on candhi is good. For the distance from the post base, it is measured by fathom—five calculations."

### 13.
"The counting is: karta, baya, tura, samaya, angemasi. The best is what falls on the number karta. So much for the counting of Ki Buyut. Now dawn approaches—time for Subuh prayer."

### 14.
"It is best to be a little early. They all bathed and completed Subuh. Prayers were completed. After the prayer, Gathuk said: 'Kyai Buyut, I ask for your advice—the words for taming animals.'"

### 15.
"'For training any animal—perhaps someday I will keep goats—so that they become attached to me.' Ki Buyut said: 'Yes, lad, there is indeed a charm for it—without ritual and short. Here are the words:'"

### 16.
"'Seka leka, seka leka, singa buntut—close your eyes, close your eyes to me, tut, according to my word—that is all, nothing else. If you happen to stroke any animal, whatever your heart desires—'"

### 17.
"'And with firm intention, it is also short—here are the words: Singa wulu, singa buntut, ingusapan pandhawa—the five—tut, according to my word. That is all. If used at the right time, perhaps it will truly be tamed.'" Gathuk gave thanks, bowing deeply, treasuring the ascetic's kindness. Ki Buyut gazed at the prince, seeing his radiant aura—thinking in his heart: "This is a chosen person, a brilliant sign of sainthood." Finally the prince spoke.

### 18.
"Kyai Buyut, I take my leave. Rest well." Ki Buyut blessed him: "May your journey meet safety, may you find your two brothers—do not worry." They shook hands. The three descended the mountain. Departure is not described. Arriving below, passing Gubug, passing Demak, passing Prawata—

### 19.
"Now where do you wish to go?" "A thousand thanks if I am comfortable here," the prince replied softly. "Forgive me greatly—now I wish to follow my heart, wandering aimlessly, seeking those who have disappeared."

### 20.
"Only your blessing for the safety of my journey—perhaps later I may return. Remain in well-being." They shook hands. Ki Buyut said gently: "Grandchild, my ascetic offering to you—may we mutually pray for each other."

### 21.
He descended from Murya, went south-west, reaching the coast. Unspoken is the journey. He arrived at Pekalongan harbor, stopping at the seashore, watching captains descend from ships from other lands.

### Prologue
Arriving at Pekalongan, continuing to Panegaran, he studied with Wasi Kawiswara, receiving the contents of the Nitisruti text. Arriving at Mount Slamet, he met Seh Sekardalima, who taught: 1. The Suluk of the Nine Walis; 2. The story (symbolism) of the Waringin Sungsang; 3. The five kinds of asceticism; 4. The Suluk of the Seven Heavens; 6. Divine gifts, the nature of humans, and the four desires. His journey continued to Mount Sawal, meeting Resi Narawita—they debated knowledge until finally they competed in supernatural arts. The sage was defeated and vanished... pages 122-141

### 22.
"Perhaps on the second day, taken by a captain from Gresik, as a passenger or found by relatives." When the captains descended from the sea, his brother was not seen—his heart grew heavier.

### 23.
"Oh, my younger siblings—Jayengsari and Niken Rancangkapti! Where have you gone, my beloveds? If only I knew your whereabouts—but now it is too late, like a ring without a gem." So Prince Jayengresmi grieved.

### 24.
He prayed to God for the safety of his two siblings—that they might be found. His heart was steadfast. He walked on, his intention unclear. He stopped only at night; after Subuh he continued.

### 25.
He arrived at Mount Panegaran. The ascetic who dwelt there was Wasi Kawiswara, clairvoyant of heart—he knew a wandering knight had stopped at the mountain's foot. The sage quickly sent a messenger.

### 26.
His disciple named Waskitha was sent to descend the mountain. Waskitha descended and met the prince. He greeted him: "I am sent by the great sage of Panegaran, Kawiswara the ascetic."

### 27.
"Please ascend to my humble dwelling." The prince replied: "Yes, Uncle, that is good." They proceeded, Gathak and Gathuk following behind. The sage waited at the entrance—they bowed and were quickly embraced.

### 28.
"Grandchild, you who have just arrived—tell me your name, child, and where you are from. It is fitting that you rest here, though lacking food and sleep, so that you may attain what you desire, child."

### 29.
The prince was deeply reluctant. He studied long with the sage Kawiswara, his asceticism continuous. The sage spoke: "There is a sublime volume—you should use it as a companion for a virtuous person:

## Kinanthi 1.

### 1.
Truly the head—asceticism that pleases the heart, foremost like the rays of the sanctuary, all kinds of calamities—since the One who causes them is loving compassion that renews.

### 2.
Generosity in the hermitage, the peace of the kingdom's teachings, asking for the love of the Merciful, compassion for the world, dwelling in contemplation, the heart fixed on God.

### 3.
"It is not yet realized if one has not yet persevered in hardship. Whatever is willed by fate, as destiny falls, the division is according to the power of one's deeds, the force of the fabric of existence."

### 4.
"Dwell in stillness, cultivating the peace of samadhi, affirming the will of the One who is the True Vision. See with the eye of the heart—may you be safe and not forget."

### 5.
"The way of one who is carried along by desire: let what is changing be examined. Be still, do not be swept away, do not be negligent, do not become lost, wandering excessively—without shelter, exposed to calamity."

### 6.
"Trust the state of the body. Be still, do not be agitated. Do not be flustered in the moment; let your reason be gentle. Seek your direction—steer, do not diminish."

### 7.
"Lessen what you hear; in your affairs do not be irritable. If you are at odds, it brings shame—dishonoring the path of wisdom. Let every point be examined carefully—do not reject what follows after."

### 8.
"It is good to hold your practice firm, capable in both great and small. Observe the subtleties; do not rely on mere appearance. Look at the art of elegance—its root is nothing else."

### 9.
"From kakawin and kidung, as if a sibling interprets them—the secret of beauty, the loveliness of one who watches. Let go, do not be troubled; hold on if there is excellence."

### 10.
"And also the nature of refined feeling—a certain shyness before the poet, the hidden code of one who is moved, the poet's craft inspired by God. The divine inspiration in bestowing gifts—that is why one is honored."

### 11.
"The gift of the Word accompanies the perfection of existence. Charity first is to be sought. Sanctity is left behind. Compassion—a tender heart—let the heart be accustomed to sweetness."

### 12.
"Let the heart not be narrow; you set the charge. The problem of blocked desire—desire is scattered, unmoving. Do not look at what is whispered—as if keeping hidden the pure will."

### 13.
"In speech do not be boastful, like lightning concealed. You do not know the way to observe—clear in answering. If you do not speak, be silent—be firm in restraint."

### 14.
"Do not be gullible, naive, like a cowherd caught in the rain—startled, panicked, stumbling on rough ground, flailing, thrashing about—idle, the heart unsettled."

### 15.
"The fruit of a scowling man—his expression is bitter, his speech the blindness of the eye. The evil eye goes unnoticed; he is led, weary in countenance, containing evil, gaining nothing."

### 16.
"In speech, when it happens in gatherings and company, let it be offered in song; perhaps the song will return—let it be grasped in utterance. Every point is to be noted."

### 17.
"Perfection when one has attained mastery—it is already told in the Sruti. The clear truth of the origin—that is what may be held. In contemplation it becomes fitting—in song, perhaps one arrives."

### 18.
"There is a secret that is hidden, concealed—the essence of essences, the cause of the heart's burden. Manifest in the heart that receives it. The reality of the heart is in the way; the way, the eye that receives."

### 19.
"The secret of my heart: in the eye is its meaning, in speech is the place of revelation. The power to give is the power of the secret. Skillful in the illusion of creation—the illusion of intention."

### 20.
"The heart desires earnestly: love one another. The origin of the body is to be loved—it comes from the body itself. Each self loves the body—the path is seen, followed narrowly."

### 21.
"The adornment of my body weighs heavy on what is seen. Without a trace of many things—focus on your own self. The self—what is its difference? Bear it and be borne."

### 22.
"One such as I, where do I begin? In service it is thus: there is a likeness to the earth. By gathering all intentions—it is already the nature of a king."

### 23.
"Loved in gracious conduct, serene in one's self-acceptance, following, bearing the world. The world clings to mutual love, bound to the people's affection, established in mutual refinement."

### 24.
"Conditions made prosperous in the heart—teaching one another in gentle ways, without insistence, to be maintained. Who first initiates and makes things prosper? He follows after what is wretched—wretchedness and misery are shunned."

### 25.
"What is oppressive, the oppression of harshness—its causes are truly real. The truth of the cause of oppression is a torment like a sea of fire. The fire's content is vast—the body cannot refuse to speak."

### 26.
"The boast of the world is vainglory—the content of asceticism on earth. The earth is true with interpretation—words are put into practice. The bee sips the flower—the radiance of fragrant pollen."

### 27.
"All the burden of what is given in the secret—skilled in that—tending the world, vigilant. The grace of the world is not sought. The reality of the eight-fold skill is trained through recognition."

### 28.
"Turned about exceedingly, interspersed, shifted, exchanged—tasted, felt, worn as armor. What is coarse is shunned. Oppression is not tended. Strength has a fine-spun root."

### 29.
"The beginning from about eight—restoration again and again. Lessons of relinquishment and calamity—do not be negligent, lest you forget and then choose ruin—violent, destructive, overwhelming."

### 30.
"Overwhelming, swaying—revelations of everything in turn. Successive manifestations—the emergence of inherited grace. Soundness—he causes pain. Pain—he drives out the life-force."

### 31.
"Not delighting, diligent, following—follow the world's destiny. Directing, aiming, shooting—pierced, blocked, not parted. The stain comes from oppression. Difficulty, hardship is shunned."

### 32.
"Good deeds and actions are remembered. Steady, moderate, clear—sharp in the art of song, unceasingly pursuing intention. The intent of one who is moved—trust so that you may be trusted."

### 33.
"In the heart do not be uncertain—it is the foundation of human life. Strive for works—the works of each individual. Pursue the seed of it—the eight-fold skill made manifest."

### 34.
"The quality of Lord Indra's conduct—scattering fragrances, delighting the world—endowed with sweetness, filling the heart, expanding—only stillness reaches the mark."

### 35.
"The quality of Lord Yama's conduct—imposing his will unjustly. It is like death—the wicked are the earth's treasure. Even kin dare to approach—they are destroyed, sought to be killed."

### 36.
"The quality of Lord Surya's conduct—absorbing water immeasurably. Seeking gently, without hardship—its secret does not oppress. Clean, continuous, orderly—always vigilant."

### 37.
"The quality of Lord Candra's conduct—withdrawing into the silence of the earth. Lovely and soft to behold—its laughter sweet, like drops of holy water—pleasing to the sages."

### 38.
"The quality of Lord Bayu's conduct—observing all deeds. The world's mind is known—it is known through discernment. Yes, excellent skill and artistry—practiced day by day."

### 39.
"The quality of Lord Kuwera's conduct—enjoying food and pleasure, adorning with gold garments. The ordering of servants is diligent. Trust is given to those who are trusted—not complaining, not stirring."

### 40.
"The quality of Lord Baruna's conduct—bearing weapons, subduing. All who practice evil are investigated, examined, searched out, seized—he is firm in his observation."

### 41.
"The quality of Lord Brama's conduct—burning enemies to ash. Fierce toward foes like a lion—those attacked vanish to dust. The blaze spreads evenly—all are consumed in fire."

### 42.
"Such are the praised virtues—my valor guarding the earth. The earth is made a covering. Endurance is made still and contemplated. This is the state of those who practice—the practice of offering—only sound remains."

### 43.
"Do not be hasty in imitation, seizing what is impure. The Sruti is the sign of what is proper—the flash of lightning observes. Islam is the mark of a noble person; the infidel is the work of the asthagina."

### 44.
"The Asthabrata is what is practiced—the way of its cultivation. What is gained if it is abandoned, along with the Buddha's way of old? Different are the minds of men—some are celebrated as excellent."

### 45.
"May you be safe, progressing onward—continuing in deeds. Jayengrana in his priesthood, in the battlefield, in the seat of formation. Nothing else but the end of asceticism brings exaltedness."

### 46.
"Careless of death before foes—the asceticism of warriors. The other is the asceticism of priests. Asceticism is the footprint of victory. The final offering is foremost in asceticism—asceticism on the iron mountain."

### 47.
"Worshipped by the great hosts—yes, if you wish to go to war, prepare the steps—the opening, the origin, the clear point. Let each point be examined—follow, bound together."

### 48.
"Religion is the intention for good—firm in the brightness of death. Do not be interrupted by foreign matters—lest you meet calamity. Even if there are a million arrows, the good intention gives shelter."

### 49.
"Religion is the shelter—guarded, far from calamity. The banner follows, moving—the opening, do not delay. Take precedence—if already displaced, apply soothing, if done."

### 50.
"There is a rule of conduct: in speech do not be loud; let no other hear—only the king himself—when he joins battle, clothed in his position."

### 51.
"Those placed in front—the clash of battle is already upon them—they trust the king. They will not be shaken in war. The king arranges the rear—not too many following."

### 52.
"Only to the right, left, and rear—be ready, vigilant against calamity—in case there is a breakthrough, the horses' bodies stumble. Help comes, swift, replaced by troops pledged to death."

### 53.
"That is the objective of one who practices asceticism on the iron mountain—not astonished, not seen—in the becoming of the earth, the sky, and observation—that is true primal sovereignty."

### 54.
"There is no pain nor death—life comes from the True God—therefore fearless in battle—low, middle, excellent—the minister according to the ancients is now commanded."

### 55.
"The lowest minister is spoken of as one who is negligent—negligent, leaving his troops behind. The middling minister dies together with his troops. The highest minister—such is the minister."

### 56.
"The perfect ascetic on the iron mountain—the burnt offering of the body—all visible form is not regarded—luminous in the world. He is skilled in all—the work of the world is not difficult."

### 56.
Yèn ing bala ngarsa gêmpur|tulung ngadêgkên jêmparing|mangsa mangke dadya warta|yèn amangun jayèng jurit|nistha madya utamanta|mantri ngêmasi rumiyin||



### 38
## Asmaradana 1.

### 1.
The prince was greatly moved, hearing the words of the renowned Wasi Kawiswara. After a long time at Panegaran, he exhausted his teachings—being sharp-witted, the prince grasped all the lessons fully.

### 2.
He drew further from food and sleep; Gathak and Gathuk were at ease. Kawiswara said gently: "Child, do not let your heart be troubled. I am not at all reluctant to keep you—only that you may increase your knowledge of the excellence of life."

### 3.
"'Knowledge of the excellence of life, reaching to the farthest bounds—go straight, child, to Mount Slamet. There you will find a knight, a prince of Majalangu, named Seh Sekardalima."

### 4.
"His hermitage is on Mount Slamet; he has embraced Islam. Sunan Tembayat is his teacher; he is the elder, the Lord Sunan Adi of Kalijaga. Rarely can one meet him, if not by grace."

### 5.
"Depart now, at a fitting time." The prince kissed the sage's feet, saying softly: "Only your blessing. I am but an instrument, walking by your direction—truly I have no power."

### 6.
The sage smiled. "Child, my blessing—" Gathak and Gathuk bowed, saying: "We ask for blessing, that we be given strength to accompany the prince; may we receive permission and prayer."

### 7.
"Yes, I grant it. May God strengthen you." The prince departed, leaving Panegaran, continuing his journey—Gathak and Gathuk following behind. They reached the foot of Mount Slamet.

### 8.
The fragrance of flowers spread, sweet scent wafting. What is now told: at the hermitage on Mount Slamet, the mosque was visible rising—beautiful was the mountain's adornment; the trees were arranged in order.

### 9.
Flowers were arranged all around; clear water flowed in channels, streaming gently—a splendid lake sparkled, rippling. The master of the mountain sat at the edge of the lake.

### 10.
Attended by two disciples, Ki Maklum and Ki Sabar. Seh Sekar spoke softly: "Sabar, Maklum—go attend to the prayer house; make it clean. A guest is coming—a noble one, a candidate for sainthood."

### 11.
Sabar and Maklum withdrew quickly. The prayer house was spread with mats. Seh Sekar slowly returned, seated himself in the prayer house. The prince was now in sight—the sage waved in haste. The prince kissed his feet.

### 12.
He was embraced, clasped warmly. Sweet words issued forth: "Welcome, Prince—make yourself at home in my hermitage. Great is my joy." The prince bowed and said: "Blessings from you, O exalted one."

### 13.
Seh Sekar spoke again: "Child, do not be impatient—be calm in your seeking. Only do not cease in your asking; let your striving be continuous. If the time is right, perhaps you will receive."

### 14.
"The Lord of Bayat formerly gave me a story—your grandfather and great-grandfather, when gathered with the Nine Walis, all brought forth their jewels. 'Deep learning, let it be assembled—let there be no contention.'"

### 15.
"It was made into a song of poetry. Giri Gajah was the place when the Nine Walis held their discourse. Argapura was the venue—the palace of the king of religion."

### 16.
All the Walis were questioned by Prabu Satmata. Sunan Benang was asked, also Sunan Kalijaga, Sunan Ngampel Denta, Sunan Kudus, and Seh Siti Jenar.

### 17.
"Seh Benthong was also asked, together with Pangeran Palembang and Panembahan Madura. They appeared before Giri Liman. Prabu Satmata spoke: 'Thankfully, my children are complete—all five of you are united.'"

### 18.
"'Let all be of one will; let there be no disagreement. Let your words be harmonious; let all bring forth your jewels, all open the veil. Let there be no concealment—be open and clear.'"

### 19.
"Sunan Benang spoke, revealing his knowledge: 'The meaning of my body is Essence, Attribute, Act of God—truly in the heart, I am the Divine Essence, the Omnipotent, who rules over His own self.'"

### 20.
"Sunan Adiluwih (Kalijaga) spoke, revealing his knowledge: 'The meaning of my body is the Shahada—I am named as the name of the Beginning. I am Allah, the Eternal Spirit—the name of Life and the World.'"

### 21.
"Sunan Giriwesthi spoke, revealing his knowledge: 'The meaning of my body is faith, life, and grace—mind, the life of the Spirit—Life and Allah are one. Such is my knowledge.'"

### 22.
"Sunan Kudus spoke, revealing his knowledge: 'The Spirit is obligatory in my faith—a light shining like the sun, issuing from power, giving vitality to its course, bringing life to all the world.'"

### 23.
"Panembahan Madura spoke, revealing his knowledge: 'There is a gift called the "Container of Allah." The meaning of "container" is the Prophet of God in truth—made manifest in the name of Allah.'"

### 24.
"Pangeran Palembang spoke, revealing his knowledge: 'The meaning of my body—the meaning of Allah is this: Allah who rules, who gives life, the Most High, who rules over His own self.'"

### 25.
"Prabu Satmata then spoke, revealing his knowledge: 'It is the same as Allah who rules. Who knows? It is Allah. Second: the Light of the body. Third: the Prophet. Fourth: the Divine Essence.'"

### 26.
"Seh Siti Jenar then spoke, revealing his knowledge: 'I worship Allah—my bowing and prostration are Allah. The worshipper and the worshipped are Allah. I am the one who rules; I also am the one who has power.'"

### 27.
All the Walis then spoke: "Siti Jenar—Kadariyah—is too extreme in his words." Siti Jenar quickly retorted: "Far from it, if it is true. That silver—who knows?—this is Allah, so be it."

### 28.
Prabu Satmata then spoke: "That is the body, Siti Jenar." Seh Lemah Bang then said: "Body and soul are to be examined. Leave your village—Allah is the one who is open, omnipotent according to His will."

### 29.
All the Walis spoke as one: "You are wrong, Siti Jenar, claiming your body is Allah. The body of Siti Jenar is not eternal in this world. Siti Jenar is mistaken—later he will claim to be the Spirit."

### 30.
"There was a way of old—all brought forth their jewels. Let there be no veiling—all open the covering. Let there be no wrong thinking. It is permissible to teach what is other, but Siti Jenar persists."

### 31.
Prabu Satmata then spoke: "Seh Lemah Bang—humanity is your kin, all of you—no different from yourselves. But do not be too open, lest it become a trend—many people take it lightly."

### 32.
"Many people are without truth, unwilling to study with a teacher. Many take it lightly—because of news passed down. Knowing the truth, they then refuse to study—ashamed to ask questions."

### 33.
The Molanas all arrived—all the nobles of Campa—gathered in the great mosque. Only seven Walis remained. They all brought forth their jewels—no different from before. Siti Jenar was addressed.

### 34.
Seh Molana then spoke: "Siti Jenar—is that your name?" Siti Jenar replied: "Yes, Allah is my name. There is no Allah—only Siti Jenar vanishes, and Allah is."

### 35.
Molana Ngaribbi spoke: "Siti Jenar has become an infidel." Aji Campa said softly: "A thorough infidel is Siti Jenar. His Islam is lost—a thorough infidel is that man—that is perfect infidelity."

### 36.
Molana Mahribi spoke: "The Lord must be taught. The mosque is entirely empty. Some worship, some do not—it ends up destroying religion, making light of it. Whoever is wrong must be executed."

### 37.
Seh Siti Jenar then spoke: "It must not be delayed—the gates of heaven are all open." Siti Jenar was executed by the four executioners. Seh Lemah Bang was likewise judged—his neck was severed by the sword.

### 38.
His three disciples likewise intended to be killed—all were brave in judgment. The three of them resolved, crying "Subhanallah!" There was a shepherd boy who heard the news.

### 39.
"Siti Jenar dares to die—boldly claiming to be Allah." The shepherd boy ran, boasting, approaching the assembly: "There is still another Allah—the one preoccupied with herding goats."

### 40.
Prabu Satmata then spoke: "That boy must die—judge him quickly. Place him beside Siti Jenar." Ki Siti Jenar said: "Let him be beside me—do not let him be far; I will take him."

### 41.
The boy spoke calmly: "Do not delay—" the small child wept loudly. "Quickly, follow me to destruction—the gates of heaven are already open." His neck was severed. The goat-herding boy smiled.

### 42.
Sunan Ratu Giri marveled—Siti Jenar's body remained intact for three days, shining. Then he spoke a greeting: "Farewell, O King." Siti Jenar vanished.

### 43.
None of his disciples remained—all had vanished. Also the shepherd boy did not remain—he vanished too. All were gone—all who witnessed marveled at Seh Siti Jenar.

### 44.
"My tale that has been uttered—Seh Lemah Bang has proven himself. His steadfastness is evident, but few are capable like Seh Siti Jenar. Many stop at words—when pressed, they retreat and yield."

### 39
## Durma 1.

### 1.
"Moreover, Prince—may you know, there is further knowledge: the story of the Waringin Sungsang. It is your duty to know it. Contemplate it with sincerity in your heart—come, child, I will explain it."

### 2.
"The Waringin Sungsang—as a young tree—rises without knowing—its taproot is one, its secondary roots are four, its shoot is only one. Its branches are four, its fruit is excellent jewels."

### 3.
"The skin of that waringin is noble gold, the flesh is excellent gems, the veins are jewels, the bones are rings, the marrow is the finest pearl. The tree's root is faith, the certain Form."

### 4.
"And moreover, child—know the center: the five are in the center, called the 'bird of the gods.' As for what flanks it—four birds—that you must know."

### 5.
"Pay heed, child—you must know the names of the waringin: its taproot, and the four secondary roots, its shoot and its fruit—what are they called? You must know them."

### 6.
"And moreover, Prince—you must know: what was your origin? That you must know. From what did you come? And later, when you die—where will you go? And what is the path of death?"

### 7.
"When you die, the soul—where is its path? Does it exit through the ear? That is difficult. Does it exit through the eye? That is unlikely, child. If it exits through the head, the skull would certainly shatter."

### 8.
"If it exits through the nostrils, it would surely be liquid. If it exits through the mouth—that is unclean. Where then is the path, when you die?"

### 9.
"If it exits through the anus—that is most unclean. If through the navel—it would certainly burst. Where is the path? If through the body hair—that is impossible, a path too narrow."

### 10.
