"Come then, Prince, ask me—everything else ask of me. Ask everything: beginning, middle, end—ask precisely. Surrender your body, offer up your death and life."

### 11.
"When you die, is there truly an afterlife? Where is the place of the afterlife? Ask so that you may receive—do not be uncertain. Know the truth of the place of the afterlife—the abode of the great one's forgiveness."

### 12.
"As for the abode of the perfect one—not in the world, not in the hereafter—it is in Darussalam, the abode of the great and noble. Seek that truly—Darussalam is the pure heaven."

### 13.
Têgêsipun kang Darussalam punika|wujud mukal kang pasthi|iku gurokêna|kang aran wujud mukal|yèn tan wêruha sayêkti|angur si kopar|karuwan mati garing||

### 13.
"So, Prince—seek it out. Pay heed that you may find a teacher who instructs. Dive into the ocean—that is your likeness—do not turn back for wife or child."

### 14.
"If you are negligent in the teacher's instruction, you will end up dying an infidel. Then fulfill the teacher's teachings—four kinds of practice—do not lag behind—four kinds of asceticism."

### 40
## Pangkur 1.

### 1.
"First, what is called asceticism: acceptance of the love of the True Spirit. Whatever the will of the Almighty—one simply follows, like a raft in the middle of the ocean. Whatever His command—the servant simply undergoes it."

### 2.
"Second, what is called asceticism: fire-asceticism—that is to be practiced. Its meaning: entering into fire. If burned by words, be steadfast. If spoken of by neighbors—do not intend to move away."

### 3.
"Third, water-asceticism: its meaning is asceticism of flowing—following the advice, the counsel of family and relatives, and the good name of others. That is the foremost practice—one who is aware of the True Spirit."

### 4.
"Fourth, earth-asceticism: burying—hiding in the earth. That is its meaning: do not show your own goodness, your own practice—conceal it, keep it secret."

### 5.
"Moreover: be humble, calm in character—yield in disputes. Put practice foremost. That is one who devotes himself to the Almighty. That is called a true man—one who has won the holy war."

### 6.
"That is the reward hereafter: prepared, dwelling in the highest heaven—the supremely exalted, called Darussalam—all luxury, infinite beauty. Such is the will of Allah, who owns those who wage holy war."

### 7.
"Those who wage holy war—not against the infidel and the unbeliever—but within the assembly there is a Bharata War—more turbulent, a clash of battle—against one's own self. That is the true holy war."

### 8.
"After winning the battle, he then enters the kingdom of Maespati, bringing Kencanawungu—then becomes king, ruling over all the lords—none oppose—he becomes the sovereign."

### 9.
"So heed this—do not doubt in your heart. Be faithful to this word, the earlier teaching. Be steadfast, contemplating in the heart. This is the foremost practice—there is none to equal it."

### 10.
"And further, ask about the perfection of intention and the takbir, and the Shahada, and when in the throes of death. Pay heed, Prince—attain this. And the perfection of death—ask precisely."

### 11.
"There are five—seek them out, understand them—that is the perfection of knowledge. Pay heed, attain it—seek it, child. If you do not study thus, he is not truly your teacher."

### 12.
"If you wish to know the seven earths—come, Prince, I will explain. The first is called the 'earth of gems'; the second is called 'the earth of the heart'; the third is 'the earth of the core'; the fourth is 'the earth of intellect.'"

### 13.
"The fifth is called the 'earth of the subtle'; the sixth is called the 'earth of spirit'; the seventh is the 'earth of rasa'—that is its name. You must know the meaning of each."

### 14.
"What is called the 'earth of gems'—its meaning is the chest itself—the earth of noble humanity—that is the glorious treasury, called the abode of Islam. And the second is called the 'earth of the heart.'"

### 15.
"The earth of the heart—its meaning is the abode of true faith. Third, the earth of the core—that is called the abode of all knowledge. And fourth is called the earth of intellect."

### 16.
"The earth of intellect—its meaning is the abode of praise and dhikr. And the fifth, the earth of the subtle—its meaning is called the abode of true love. And the sixth is called the earth of spirit."

### 17.
"The earth of spirit—that is the abode of knowledge. And the seventh is called the earth of rasa. The earth of rasa—its meaning is the abode of vision—asmaradana in return."

## Asmaradana 1.

### 1.
"Know further, Prince—the matter of the seven heavens that dwell within you. Pay heed, behold them—the heavens that are in you. Come, I will explain, child—listen carefully."

### 2.
"First, the heaven of the physical spirit; second, the heaven of the prophetic spirit; third, the heaven of the passionate spirit; fourth, the heaven of the rational spirit; fifth—"

### 3.
"Is called the heaven of the luminous spirit; the sixth heaven is called the heaven of the lordly spirit; the seventh heaven is called the heaven of the transcendent spirit—that is the meaning: the heavens of each single spirit."

### 4.
"The meaning of the physical heaven: created by Allah, encompassing the life of the whole body. The heaven of the prophetic spirit encompasses the life of the entire body."

### 5.
"The heaven of the passionate spirit encompasses desire—the wishes of the whole body. The heaven of the rational spirit encompasses your knowledge—all of the body. The heaven of the luminous spirit—"

### 6.
"Encompasses the light of all bodies. The heaven of the lordly spirit, Prince, encompasses your devotion—all of the body. And the heaven of the transcendent spirit—it is the complete surrender of the body."

### 42
## Mijil 1.

### 1.
"How could it be fitting to be born forth—what is spoken of: grace and gift. And who is the giver? True grace belongs to the Almighty."

### 2.
"Grace in you is the gift of the All-Seeing God—like a drum beaten, struck. The striking is true grace. Its sound is truly called grace."

### 3.
"One who accepts the love of the All-Seeing God—that is an excellent person. One who accepts the love of God—its form is also a gift."

### 4.
"Every action, every utterance—these are gifts of the All-Seeing God. Scent, sound, and sight—accepting them all as one—that makes one excellent in the hereafter, when dead."

### 5.
"As for the container of knowledge—know truly: knowledge should not be excessive. If too fine-spun, it is not good. When the sail of death comes, do not let much be seen."

### 6.
"Do not be obscured by sights—that are of the species of spirit. The spirit of the high is the veil. What darkens is the physical body—that is truly the source of straying."

### 7.
"That is the path hereafter for those who go astray—if one cannot attain union. If one can unite with the single Form—that Form is also the Form of the Almighty."

### 8.
"That is called the container of knowledge—your true Form is acknowledged as the Divine Essence itself. Your hair is the hair of God. Your skin is truly the skin of the Almighty."

### 9.
"Marrow, bone, sinew, flesh, blood—all belong to the All-Seeing God. Everything within this body comes from the Most Exalted God. That too is the container of knowledge."

### 10.
"The knowledge that is contained is precious before the All-Seeing God. Pay heed—this is my counsel, child—cherish it and be thorough. The meaning of 'cherish'—let it not be forgotten."

### 11.
"And moreover, the meaning of 'thorough': in every command of the All-Seeing God, be vigilant in all your knowledge. Let it penetrate outer and inner—do not dispute knowledge with others, child."

### 12.
"If you do not know the knowledge of God—much could be said. Its likeness: many rivers—great rivers and small rivers—their intention is the same: they reach the great ocean."

### 13.
"Each follows their preferred path—some go east, some west, south, north—each as they like. But their goal is the ocean. Do not deny another's knowledge—none is wrong."

### 14.
"Do not be complacent, you bald santri, always disputing—who is right, who makes it right? Who is wrong, who declares it wrong, child? Just follow—what harm can it do?"

### 15.
"Bald santri who are always disputing—like an egg that is complacent: white only on the outside, but inside it is red—like a bird that looks black-and-white from afar."

### 16.
"The great crane stands in the water—if there is a frog, it preys on it—that is its nature. The white heron—clothed all in white—looks pure from a distance."

### 17.
"But its food is filthy. Prince, my final word is only this: accept it all. My knowledge, child, is complete. But I remind you—pay heed."

### 18.
"My final counsel is but one: see that you do it. Do not be arrogant, Prince, and do not be conceited in your practice. Be careful—do not forget, child."

### 19.
"The meaning of arrogance: insisting that good and bad are predetermined. As for conceit—its true meaning—making oneself out to be good, stubborn in one's own opinion."

### 20.
Thus the story continues: the prince remained in attendance until the time of Subuh, not moving from the sage's presence. The great Seh Sekar spoke gently: "Child, what has been uttered is all my teaching."

### 21.
"Add to your knowledge, child, and with my final counsel, go now to the west, Prince—to Mount Sawal. There is a sage there, a relative of mine, moreover an elder."

### 22.
"He does not wish to follow the Sharia of the honored Prophet; he still clings to his Buddhist ways. If anyone defeats him in his supernatural powers—the crane submits willingly."

### 43.
## Dhandhanggula 1.

### 1.
"His name is Wasi Narawita, child—I am anxious about you. Now go, Prince." Seh Sekar finished speaking—his words were no longer seen. Only his disciples remained: Ki Sabar and Ki Maklum. The prince lingered, then spoke softly: "Uncle, one of you—accompany me."

### 2.
Ki Sabar led the way, walking before him—the journey was not difficult through the blessing of Seh Sekardalima. They arrived at Mount Sawal. The prince spoke: "My thanks are great. Ki Sabar, return. Gathak, Gathuk—stay at the foot of the mountain. I will ascend alone."

### 3.
Prince Jayengresmi ascended. Resi Narawita was not unaware that a visitor had come—a person of excellent knowledge. The sage quickly prepared—the prince was showered with rocks without cease. His body surrendered to the Spirit. Reaching the mountain, he stopped on a flat rock, like a step of the mountain.

### 4.
The rock was ascended step by step. He soon stood at the mountain's flank. The sage's heart trembled. The great Lord spoke: "You daring one—what is your purpose in coming to this hermitage-mountain?" The prince replied: "It is the command of the Lord. My purpose with you, O Sage, is to counsel you to embrace Islam."

### 5.
"So become Muslim, Ki Ajar, quickly—along with your pendhapa and courtyard—all the western regions must submit." The teacher responded stubbornly: "I am reluctant to become Muslim unless my knowledge and sanctity are surpassed."

He quickly took eggs and placed them, stacked before him.

### 6.
Before the prince, he stacked the eggs—the bottom ones did not fall, only the top one remained. Ki Ajar quickly took a cup containing water, hung it in the sky. The prince blew on it—it burst, scattering—the water did not fall. Ki Ajar was confounded.

### 7.
Kyai Ajar quickly threw his hat into the sky—it flew like a hawk. The prince swiftly pulled off his sock and threw it into the sky, riding the hat. Thus mounted, Ki Ajar leaped in meditation, landing on a banana leaf.

### 8.
Just as he was about to ride it, the prince was already there—standing on its tip. Ki Ajar quickly returned and lit his fire—the flames billowed. Ki Ajar entered—the prince was already there, standing opposite him in the midst of the fire. Ki Ajar was confounded.

### 9.
Everything on the mountain was in chaos—only a golden bell remained, whose location he could not find. The prince, his body enduring hardship for love, resolved to destroy the bell—crushing it to dust. The prince said to Ki Ajar: "I will restore all your idols intact." Ki Ajar could not.

### 10.
Kyai Ajar was vexed and pleaded with the prince: "Come, restore that bell-idol quickly!" The prince immediately prayed to God—his prayer was granted—the idol was whole again. The prince told him to become Muslim. Ki Ajar said: "If you can bring the dead to life, I will believe your words."

### 11.
"If you can truly bring the dead to life, and make the sun and moon vanish—if the dead revive—then all your words are true. Otherwise I am unwilling to follow." The prince descended and approached the dead. Finding them, he commanded them to live.

### 12.
"Live, you who are dead—by the command of the Most Holy God!" Still they lay silent. The prince spoke again: "Arise, by the will of God and my command!" They stirred and sat up—alive, not defying the will—before the prince. Ki Ajar demanded his promise. The prince spoke gently:

### 13.
"Embrace Islam." Ki Ajar did not answer—ashamed, he was struck ill—paralyzed, receiving his punishment. He begged forgiveness from the prince, asking for a cure. The prince spoke: "Islam is the cure. Recite the Shahada." Kyai Ajar did not refuse—he received it. After the Shahada, he ascended.

### 14.
Samengkrade ri sang maharêsi|kukuwunge nêlahi saarga|sumunar katon tejane|sru ascaryaning kalbu|ngunguning tyas datan winarni|tumurun sangking arga|panggya Gathak Gathuk|gupuh matur ing rahadyan|kadipundi dene ngantos kalih ratri|tyas amba kêlap-kêlap||

### 14.
The prince smiled as he turned away—the sage was defeated in battle, overcome by the prince's radiance. Gathak and Gathuk nodded—their hearts joyful, thanking God. The victor was startled, looking at Mount Cerme. The prince's steps were difficult—yet his journey continued.

### 15.
The path was very rugged, swarming with wasps—the bees hummed, following the mountain slopes. He strengthened his heart toward his lost siblings. He came upon a lake—its water warm, surrounded by trees and vines—abundant with fruit, all excellent, all ripe.

### Prologue
Arriving at Mount Cerme, he met Resi Singgunkara, who taught: "1. Wariga-Gemet—the good and bad of days according to the 30 Wuku cycles; 2. Nagajatingarang calculations for those moving house." His journey continued to Mount Tampomas, meeting Seh Trenggana with his wife Sitaresmi and daughter Ruhkanti, taught the knowledge of perfection through riddles. Before long, Seh Trenggana vanished. Jayengresmi taught Ruhkanti about the Seven Grades of Being. Ruhkanti was taken as wife by the Sultan of Cirebon and brought away with her mother. Jayengresmi continued his journey.

### 16.
Like a host welcoming a newly arrived guest—offering medicine to the victor in battle—Gathuk offered fruit, fragrant. But the prince did not wish to eat—only smelling it, his heart troubled at the sight of the lake surrounded by mountains. In the middle of the lake there were seven small islands.

### 17.
The lake water glittered under the sun—the seven islands shimmered in reflection, colorful. Vines hung dangling over the water like a bride's adornment—a noble maiden being wed, shimmering, the rustling of bamboo leaves in the wind like the sigh of a newly-wed girl.

### 18.
Prince Jayengresmi ascended to the hermitage at the mountain peak—the garden was beautiful, fragrant breezes wafted, mixing with the glow of fine trees, flanked by parijatha blossoms spreading before him. The hut stood high—the prince was deeply enamored, yet pressed on in his quest.

### 19.
The ascetic on the mountain was named Wasi Singgunkara, child of the lord of Majalangu's fall—who fled by boat across the sea, stopping at Cirebon harbor, then pressing on to Mount Cerme, surpassing all in asceticism. When the prince saw him—

### 20.
The ascetic was exhausted, weary in life—but when he saw the prince, forgetfulness and fatigue vanished. He came before him. The sage sat high. Jayengresmi spoke sweetly: "O master of asceticism, most deeply still—your power protects a servant who is pitiful, whose heart knows no malice."

### 21.
"Indeed, my resolve in asceticism—I yearn greatly to be like you, awesome in countenance, fulfilling worship in stillness. I remember my teacher, Seh Sekardalima of Mount Slamet." Singgunkara was startled and spoke: "It is not only you who has come before me."

### 22.
"All obtain what they desire—whatever is sought is attained—but in accordance with the source. Ah, child, I will tell you my story—my asceticism on this mountain." The sage recounted his journey from beginning to end. The prince asked about wisdom in the physical and the knowledge of divination.

### 23.
The great sage spoke gently: "That, child, follows from calculation. The wuku number thirty. The Wariga-Gemet determines good and bad, fixed within seven days—the reception of each wuku. Sunday receives Sinta; Watugunung ends them. Know this, child."

### 25.
### 24.
"Sunday Paing Sinta begins—for any enterprise it is good and prosperous. Monday Pon its goodness is for hunting—you will catch the game you pursue. Tuesday Wage is bad for travel—you will be defeated on the road, lose your good teacher, be reduced to weeding fields."

### 25.
"Wednesday Kliwon is supremely good—travel is safe wherever you go. Thursday Manis its nature: both good and bad—soothe a sick eye, it will quickly heal; serve others, you will be trusted and beloved. Friday Paing is bad—only good for setting bird traps."

### 26.
"Saturday Pon is bad for undertakings—do not travel, you will encounter misfortune. Having received Sinta, now Landep is received. Sunday Wage landep: truly good for seeking a woman—you will quickly obtain one. Monday Kliwon is prosperous for trading—you will have quick, easy profit; good for digging a well."

### 27.
"Tuesday Manis is mixed good and bad—digging a well: abundant water flows. Travel yields quick fortune in the forest. Wednesday Paing is all bad—do not go anywhere; only setting traps and nets is good. Thursday Pon is good for all tasks—good for digging a well."

### 28.
"Friday Wage is all good—setting snares and traps quickly catch game. Saturday Kliwon is bad—do not travel, child. Landep received, now Wukir is received. Sunday Madu—good for bringing rice into the barn. Monday Paing is prosperous."

### 29.
"Tuesday Pon for any task is good and prosperous—travel brings fortune on the road quickly. Wednesday Wage—auspicious, all work is favorable. Thursday Kliwon is good—travel brings success. If a child is born, food and clothing will come."

### 30.
"Friday Legi is good—'memarangi' means intending to seek a betrothal. Saturday Paing is bad—do not go anywhere; you will encounter misfortune and quarrels on the road. However, a child born is favorable. Wukir received, Kurantil takes over."

### 31.
"Sunday Pon—undertakings are bad, but what is good is seeking a woman: quickly obtained. Monday Wage is bad for everything—travel does not reach its goal, but hunting is good. Tuesday Kliwon is bad for travel—you will be struck by great illness; all tasks end in suffering."

### 32.
"Wednesday Legi is thoroughly good—all work succeeds; seeking food and clothing is quickly and easily obtained, as desired. Thursday Paing is bad—the enemy's ambush brings death—do not travel."

### 33.
"Friday Pon is also bad—do not travel, you will surely meet misfortune and suffering on the road. Saturday Wage is good—fishing is good; seeking fortune—all work succeeds. Kurantil received, now Tolu takes over."

### 34.
"Sunday Kliwon is bad—do not travel. Planting tubers succeeds well. Monday Manis is good for planting—tubers bear fruit. Tuesday Paing—all tasks are unfavorable—the influence of spirits, witchcraft, sorcery—do not go anywhere."

### 35.
"Wednesday Pon is good for all tasks. Thursday Wage is fortunate—making a loan brings mutual joy and safety on the road. Friday Kliwon is truly good—all who live prosper. Saturday Legi is good—healing the sick with God's help. Tolu received, now Gumbreg takes over."

## Sinom 1.

### 1.
"Gumbreg: Sunday Paing is bad—all work is unfavorable; travel is obstructed on the road. Monday Pon is also bad—half of the neck is stiff. Tuesday Wage is bad, but buying poultry is good, and healing the insane may cure them."

### 2.
"Wednesday Kliwon is prosperous—seeking a betrothal is good; mutual joy, abundant family. Thursday Manis is good and true—all undertakings succeed; travel is safe. Friday Paing for any task is good for travel—joy on the road."

### 3.
"Saturday Pon is prosperous—all work is excellent; fortune comes—food increases. Gumbreg received, now Warigalit takes over. Sunday Wage is bad, but hunting yields what is sought. Monday Kliwon is good for borrowing—mutual joy in the offering."

### 4.
"Mutual joy in the offering—a broken house brings forth a teacher—still the house is good. Tuesday Legi is good for work—a well filled with water. Wednesday Paing is good—all work is favorable; food comes continuously. Mutual joy—Thursday Pon is bad—do not travel, do not be negligent—the enemy's ambush is deadly."

### 5.
"Friday Wage is prosperous—safety is found in life; travel is good. Saturday Kliwon is mixed good and bad, but fasting brings light that overwhelms—all desires are fulfilled. Warigalit received, now Warigagung takes over."

### 6.
"Warigagung: Sunday Legi is good for making protective charms, exorcising all pests—charms and protective tools are good. Monday Paing is prosperous—borrowing brings lasting joy; on the road and in house-building it is good."

### 7.
"Tuesday Pon is prosperous—setting snares for all poisons and all deadly thieves—they will not approach you; making protective charms is good. Wednesday Wage is prosperous—digging a well is good, and all work is also good."

### 8.
"Thursday Kliwon is bad—the enemy's deadly ambush—be vigilant, do not be negligent. Friday Legi is truly good—all work is good; travel brings joy on the path. Saturday Paing is prosperous—serving a great one is good—you gain love and honor."

### 9.
"Warigagung received, now Julungwangi takes over. Sunday Pon is all bad—do not travel, child. Monday Wage is fortunate—sharing worldly goods; the reward is well-received. Tuesday Kliwon is not good—do not travel, lest you meet calamity and illness on the road."

### 10.
"All that pertains to life becomes an obstacle. Wednesday Legi is good for planting—bananas and other crops bear fruit; setting offerings is good. Thursday Paing is good—setting protective charms; working the fields is good. Friday Pon is good in all conduct."

### 11.
"Travel to collect a debt—before long, you will receive it; those you meet are happy. Saturday Wage is unfavorable—all work is not good—that is the assessment. Julungsari received, now Sungsang takes over."

### 12.
"You will suffer on the road; on the path you will meet shame. Monday Manis is good for making protective charms—for the house and fields it is good; lack that seeks is not good—for weapons—remove fish—all creatures become stiff. Wednesday Pon is good for all work."

### 13.
### 13.
"Travel brings joy on the road—what you desire is obtained. Thursday Wage is good—setting out is also good; relatives trust you. Friday Kliwon is good—if a child is born, there will be sustenance. Saturday Legi is unfavorable—the worst for all work."

### 14.
"Sungsang received, now Galungan takes over. Sunday Paing is bad for all work, but setting protective charms to ward off misfortune is good. Monday Pon: hunting yields much game. Tuesday Wage: giving to the poor is good for prayers."

### 15.
"Wednesday Kliwon is prosperous—begin fasting, receive acceptance; setting out is also good. Thursday Legi is mixed—travel is not good—hindered on the path. Bringing rice into the barn is favorable. Friday Paing is most good and excellent."

### 16.
"Travel brings joy on the road—all desires are achieved. Saturday Pon: search for a runaway—soon found. Galungan received, now Kuningan takes over. Sunday Wage is good—raining gold—if a child is born, they will be wealthy and know their kin."

### 17.
"Monday Kliwon is prosperous—healing the sick makes them well and strong. Seeking food is good. Tuesday Legi: collecting debts—you will receive. Wednesday Paing: seeking a runaway—soon found. Thursday Pon is truly bad. Friday Wage is good for making protective charms."

### 18.
"In war, Kerigan is good—all else is not good. Saturday Kliwon is prosperous—collecting debts yields results. Kuningan received, now Langkir takes over. Sunday Legi is good—planting anything—the fruit will provide food. Monday Paing is good if a child is born—there will be sustenance."

### 19.
"Travel brings fortune. Tuesday Pon is prosperous—travel brings fortune to you and provides food. If a child is born, clothing will come later. Wednesday Wage is fortunate—begin fasting, receive glorious light."

### 20.
"Receive acceptance. Thursday Kliwon is fortunate—sprouting seeds, beginning, is good and succeeds in the end. Friday Manis is fortunate—seeking betrothal is achieved; planting tubers. Saturday Paing is fortunate—mutual joy in betrothal."

### 21.
"Langkir is passed to Mandhasiya. Sunday Pon is bad—do not [travel]—loss occurs that day—many enemies lie in wait. Monday Wage is good for making fish traps—nets, scoop nets, seines, spears, and baskets are receptive."

### 22.
"Tuesday Kliwon is bad—the enemy's deadly ambush—be careful, do not travel. Wednesday Manis is good—borrowing yields results; building a house is good. Thursday Paing—borrowing yields results. Friday Pon is good for building a dam."

### 23.
"It can block and hold—rice and offerings are good. Saturday Wage is good—be firm and in awe—seeking marriage is good—mutual joy in the heart. If the enemy surrenders... Mandhasiya passes to Julungpujud."

### 24.
"Julungpujud: Sunday Kliwon is prosperous—making friends and studying with a teacher are good and loving. Monday Manis is prosperous—seeking a wife quickly obtained. Tuesday Paing is fortunate—travel brings fortune—they wait joyfully—all work yields food. Wednesday Pon is good for all work."

### 25.
"Thursday Wage is bad—all work is unfavorable—do not travel—meet calamity. Friday Kliwon is not good—all intentions are bad—travel is extremely bad. Saturday Legi is good—bringing rice to the barn as a blessing. Julungpujud passes to Pahang."

### 26.
"Pahang: Sunday Paing is bad—do not travel while performing it—journey befalls misfortune. Monday Pon is also bad—do not travel. Tuesday Wage is said to be bad—do not go anywhere—you will suffer on the road. Wednesday Kliwon is good for all work."

### 27.
"Beginning in the fields is good. Thursday Manis is fortunate—seeking birds is easy. Friday Paing is excessive—bad—do not travel—you will be bewitched on the road—but building a house is good. Saturday Pon is fortunate—healing the sick makes them well."

### 28.
Pahang sèrèn wus tinampan|Kruwêlut Dite Wage-ki|uwas-pangantèn ing paran|nanging masang tutumballing|wurungan blai bêcik|Soma Kaliwon raharja|mamarangana yogya|mêtu dunya-palanèki|(ng)Gara Manis ala aja lunga-lunga||

### 28.
"Great suffering befalls. Wednesday Paing is also bad—do not go anywhere all day. Thursday Pon is good—borrowing brings joy; building a house is pleasing. Friday Wage—set protective charms to ward off evil—those who intend harm will not come near."

### 29.
"Saturday Kliwon is bad, but working the fields is good—do it quickly. Kruwelut received, now Marakeh takes over. Sunday Legi is good for planting tubers and vegetables. Monday Paing is also good—all work, building a house is favorable."

### 30.
"Tuesday Pon—do not travel—you will be struck by illness on the road. Wednesday Wage is good—all work is fine—only travel is bad—you'll be frustrated on the path. Thursday Kliwon is prosperous—borrowing brings joy; healing the sick makes them well."

### 31.
"Friday Legi is good for setting tools—hooks, spades, scoop nets, fishing nets—all are good. Saturday Paing is fortunate—travel is safe on the path. Marakeh passes to Tambir. Sunday Pon is bad—do not travel—you will meet misfortune, such is its nature."

### 32.
"Monday Wage is fortunate—travel brings you luck this very day. Tuesday Kliwon is fortunate—planting gourds—they bear fruit. Wednesday Legi is bad—do not travel—it brings all kinds of illness."

### 33.
"Thursday Paing is good—beginning to sow melon seeds is fine. Friday Pon is good for travel—full fortune is given. Saturday Wage is excellent—auspicious—all work is favorable. Tambir passes to Madhangkungan."

### 34.
"Madhangkungan: Sunday Kliwon receives. Set protective charms against misfortune—sorcery is not good for those with evil intent. Monday Legi is truly good—bringing rice into the barn as a great blessing. Tuesday Paing is good—setting protective charms and spears is fine. Wednesday Pon is bad for all work."

### 35.
"Slander comes causing division—words are to be proven true. Thursday Wage is good—travel is favorable; wait on the road. Friday Kliwon is only bad—administering poison—that can neutralize it. Saturday Legi—goodness without end."

## Megatruh 1.

### 1.
"Borrowing brings mutual joy; building a house is most favorable. Madhangkungan passes quickly to Maktal. Sunday Paing: bad for travel—legs become weak."

### 2.
"Monday Pon is mixed good and bad—middling. Tuesday Wage is bad—do not travel—an enemy lies in wait. Wednesday Kliwon is good."

### 3.
"Seeking a betrothal—mutual joy—many blessings. Thursday Manis is good—all work—but travel ends badly."

### 4.
"Friday Paing is bad—do not travel—quarreling on the road. Saturday Pon is good—seeking a betrothal is excellent—mutual joy penetrating the heart."

### 5.
"Maktal received, now Wuye wuku takes over. Sunday Wage—work brings joy in the heart; the seventh-month ritual for a child is good. Monday Kliwon is most bad—do not travel—you will meet shame on the road."

### 6.
"Tuesday Manis is good—all work, planting root crops, quickly bears fruit. Wednesday Paing is good."

### 7.
"Making scoop nets and fishing nets—receptive, they yield. Thursday Pon is good—travel brings fortune. Friday Wage is truly good—administering poison is reliable."

### 8.
"Saturday Kliwon is good—work at it—sweetness flows. Wuye passes to Manail. Sunday Manis—the wedding assessment is bad—but setting bird nets quickly yields catch."

### 9.
"Monday Paing is fortunate—travel yields results—joy where you go—planting cotton bears fruit."

### 10.
"Tuesday Pon—travel yields nothing. Wednesday Wage is fortunate—joy, wealth in seeking clothing; planting coconut is good. Thursday Kliwon is bad—do not travel."

### 11.
"Friday Manis is good—fortune awaits you on the road before long. Saturday Paing is good for work—truly joyful in all things."

### 12.
"Planting is good and quickly grows. Manail received, now Prangbakat takes over. Sunday Pon—bad for travel—you will meet illness. Monday Wage is good—prosperity comes."

### 13.
"A child born will truly be wealthy. Tuesday Kliwon is not good—do not travel—blocked on the path; beware of illness, but harvesting fruit is good."

### 14.
"Wednesday Manis is good—bringing rice into the barn as a blessing. Thursday Paing is truly bad—do not travel—the enemy waits—be vigilant."

### 15.
"Friday Pon—travel is bad—weariness on the road. Saturday Wage is fortunate—great luck arrives—travel brings meeting with the beloved. Prangbakat passes quickly to Bala."

### 16.
"Bala: Sunday Kliwon is good—great blessing comes; seeking a person is soon found. Monday Legi is also good—travel brings quick fortune."

### 17.
"Tuesday Paing—work with traps yields results. Wednesday Pon—travel is good—fortune awaits on the path. Thursday Wage—digging a well is good, without difficulty."

### 18.
"Seeking a betrothal—mutual joy—all work is good. Friday Kliwon is truly bad—do not travel—the enemy waits—be cautious."

### 19.
"Saturday Legi—travel on the road is safe. Bala passes to Wugu. Sunday Paing is truly bad—do not travel—you will be ill on the road and struck by terrible slander."

### 20.
"Monday Pon is good for travel—pleasant—fortune awaits on the road and quickly reaches the destination. Tuesday Wage is good for beginning to sow seeds."

### 21.
"The planted fruit is quickly eaten. Wednesday Kliwon is truly bad—the enemy's ambush cannot be escaped. Thursday Manis is reliable."

### 22.
"Planting—it surely thrives. Friday Paing is good—seeking a betrothal is pleasant—digging a well fills with water—travel is good, joy in the place."

### 23.
"Saturday Pon is good for borrowing; building a house, travel—all good. Wugu passed to Wayang. Sunday Wage—all work is good."

### 24.
"But the next day, do not trust it—false. Monday Kliwon is bad—do not go—you will become ill on the road—calamity waits on the path. Tuesday Manis is good."

### 25.
"Travel brings food awaiting on the path, and you will meet prosperity, but be careful of deception. Wednesday Paing—all work is good, but travel is bad."

### 26.
"Thursday Pon is good for all work—travel brings food; waiting on the road before long. Friday Wage is truly bad—travel brings suffering at the inn."

### 27.
"Saturday Kliwon is bad for all work, but setting protective charms is good. Wayang passes to Kulawu. Sunday Manis is good—buying poultry is fine."

### 28.
"Healing the sick makes them well. Monday Paing is good—treating all ailments—all sickness returns to health—truly answered."

### 29.
"Tuesday Pon—do not travel at all—you will be stricken with illness. Wednesday Wage is bad for travel—an enemy lies in wait, but gaining a friend is truly good."

### 30.
"Joy and wealth—Thursday Kliwon is truly bad—an enemy lies in wait, unable to retaliate, they fall. Friday Legi is supremely good—planting yields results."

### 31.
"Saturday Paing is good for all work—travel is also safe. Kulawu passes to Dhukut. Sunday Pon is good—buy cattle and buffalo."

### 32.
"Monday Wage is good—fortune comes—provision and food are born. Tuesday Kliwon is not good—do not leave home. Wednesday Manis is good."

### 33.
"Borrowing—mutual joy with results—building a house is fortunate. Thursday Paing is good—all work is fine—seeking a betrothal succeeds."

### 34.
"Friday Pon—farmers are good—together beginning to sow brings much rice. Saturday Wage is bad for work—do not travel."

### 35.
"Dhukut passes to Watugunung. Sunday Kliwon concludes—do not buy four-legged creatures—it is fitting and good. Monday Legi is truly good."

### 36.
"Beginning to sow rice surely thrives. Tuesday Paing is truly bad—do not travel—illness on the road. Wednesday Pon is also bad—do not travel—misfortune on the path."

### 37.
"The 'Seven Native Earths'—that is the name. Thursday Wage is fortunate—travel is safe on the road. A child born is favorable—food and clothing will come."

### 38.
"Friday Kliwon is bad—do not travel—you will fall ill on the road. A child born receives nothing—sick and ailing. Saturday Manis is good."

### 39.
"All work is good; planting tubers yields a harvest. That is my standard for children and grandchildren who wish to ask me in Pocung."

### 46.
## Pocung 1.

### 1.
Jayengresmi was deeply perplexed—perfect in calculation, nothing was hidden from him. The sage no longer needed reminders.

### 2.
"The place is situated within the heart; the writing is memory, contemplated day and night, weighed, offered, asked of God. Thus the word is certain, undiminished." Finally he spoke softly: "O truly excellent one, my heart is most joyful."

### 4.
Hearing the discourse on wuku and their pairings for all tasks—good and ill are recorded—the excellence of a noble person does not burst forth.

### 5.
The sage, his heart most joyful, spoke gently: "The abode of the silent sage—clairvoyant, intent on whatever is desired.

### 6.
"There is more to become the instrument of that. Know the ways of the Dragon and the Jatingarang—the legs of the giant dwelling in the four directions."

### 7.
"Every three months it changes place; after a full year it returns to the starting place, matched with the months of the Islamic calendar."

### 8.
"Beginning from Sura, Sapar to Mulut—the Jatingarang is in the east—the dragon's head is there—northeast—the pethit is in the center."

### 9.
"The belly is in the northwest; the back is in the southeast; it gazes eastward. The leg is in the north—a full three months received."

### 10.
"Rabiulakir, Madilawal, and Jumadilakir months—the Jatingarang dwells quickly in the true south—the dragon's head in the southeast."

### 11.
"The pethit in the center, the belly in the northeast—the back in the southwest—it gazes westward—the leg in the true east—the transfer is received."

### 12.
"Rejeb, Ruwah, and Fast month—the Jatingarang dwells in the west—the dragon's head—southwest—the pethit dwells in the center."

### 13.
"The belly in the southeast—the back dwells in the northwest—it gazes westward—the leg is in the south—true—the transfer passes on."

### 14.
"Sawal, Dzulkaidah, Besar month—the Jatingarang dwells in the north—the dragon's head—northwest—the pethit dwells in the center."

### 15.
"The belly is in the southwest; the back in the northeast—it gazes eastward. The leg is in the west—at the conclusion."

### 16.
"For moving house, avoid the Dragon's dwelling—the Jatingarang. That is all, nothing else—my knowledge. But as it has been long—"

### 17.
"It seems I am already weary, grown old in the matter of perfection. I submit to God's will. Not long after, there was a divine revelation."

### 18.
"Singgunkara's words: you will become a great scholar, a candidate for sainthood. That is the guidance to a perfect death."

### 19.
Sira manjing agamane Kangjêng Rasul|Islam tur ginanjar|kamulyan tan owah gingsir|tikêl sèwu kamulyan nikmating dunya||

### 20.
Hèh ta bagus muga asiya maring sun|asunga pitêdah|mungguh sampurnaning pati|anut marang agama ingkang minulya||

### 21.
Dyan tumanduk radyan nyêpêng astanipun|rêsi Singgunkara|winêjang Sahadat kalih|purna jati ingikêt têlênging driya||

### 22.
Mahawiku alon pangandikanipun|Jayèngrêsmi sira|asung sampurnaning lalis|apranyata cacaloning aoliya||

### 23.
Ingsun tutur kulup marang ing sirèku|ing têmbe katêkan|kang dadya sêdyanirèki|adoh neya katêmu lan kadangira||

### 24.
Pancaddane saka ing Karang argagung|kang dhêdhepok Karang|Sèh Ibrahim Ibnu Bakrim|ya Ki Agêng Karang satuhu utama||

### 25.
Sarwa putus patakon tan ana wangsul|lah pruhitanana|gugunên satuturnèki|wusing tampa samoaning wulangira||

### 19.
"You will go to a great kingdom called Wanamarta—a settlement most devout. It has a daughter of peerless beauty."

### 20.
"If you are wed to that lady—see that you do it—after you marry, there you will be close to your goal."

### 21.
Thus the sage spoke—then vanished from sight, perfected in body. The prince remained alone.

### 22.
Restless, his heart stirred by the words, the prince spoke: "Gathak, Gathuk—we have been long at Cerme. Let us depart."

### 23.
He descended to the road; flowers spread, aligned with the mountain breeze—the wind fanned, fragrance wafting.

### 24.
It touched the heart of the lovelorn prince. From Mount Cerme he went northwest along the mountain range, reaching the foot of Mount Tampomas.

### 25.
"The borderlands of Priangan, Pasundan, Bandung. He climbed Tampomas, saw a mosque—a beautiful banner—surrounded by a pond, topped with stone."

### 26.
"Year-old trees circled the enclosure—dark green foliage flourishing, just maturing—some in flower, some in bud, some fruiting, some aging."

### 27.
"The hermitage of Seh Trenggana—already exalted—middle-aged, just at his prime, still passionate for knowledge. His wife was Ni Ken Sitaresmi, beautiful."

### 28.
"Exalted in form, modest in bearing—when adorned, splendid—just naturally gracious. They had a daughter, already grown."

### 29.
"Named Rara Ruhkanti, most excellent—graceful in speech, slender of stature like a queen. Her beauty was supreme—like gold untarnished."

### 47.
## Pangkur 1.

### 1.
So exquisite was her complexion—her body radiant, glowing like a keris. Hence the beautiful one was called Rara Emas by common accord. Merchants and village elders—many sought Rara Emas on the mountain.

### 2.
But the lady did not desire it—she was still passionate, devoted to the Prophet's way—never ceasing in her recitation of the Qur'an and holy books—fervent in worship, never neglecting devotion to the Almighty—observing the Sunnah fasts on Monday and Thursday.

### 3.
Seh Trenggana and his wife, Rara Emas, after Asr prayer, were in the courtyard picking fruits—mango, melinjo, mangosteen, oranges, langsat, durian—when a disciple noticed visitors to the southeast at the mountain's foot.

### 4.
Three had come—one was exceedingly handsome, radiant light streaming forth, calm with a noble bearing. The other two seemed to be his companions. Seh Trenggana heard the disciple's report.

### 5.
He spoke gently to his wife: "Sitaresmi, prepare the finest food, whatever there is, and spread the house. Make it as fitting as possible—perhaps the new arrival will wish to stop by."

### 6.
"Rara, you serve." "Yes, Father." She withdrew to the house quickly, arranging the offerings. Before long all was ready. Seh Trenggana descended to the mountain's foot.

### 7.
He greeted the prince from afar. The prince responded promptly: "Wa'alaikum salam." They bowed, shaking hands. Seh Trenggana spoke softly: "So glad in my heart—child, do not be shy with me."

### 8.
"Call me 'Father'—to be close without hesitation. Welcome to you. What is your name, and where do you come from, handsome one? Where are you going? Let me know."

### 9.
The prince thought to himself: "This man is rather forward, too sweet—simple and graceful in speech." Finally he spoke: "My name, Father, is Jayengresmi, from the kingdom of Sokaraja, Mount Giri."

### 10.
"Gresik, the land of Surapringga—child of Sunan Prapen of Giri. I am currently wandering, by the will of the Almighty—greatly afflicted, with no desire in my heart—finding joy only in wild solitude, in places of stillness."

### 11.
Seh Trenggana then said: "Yes, child—do not dwell long in thought. Settle your heart—stop at my house. Meet your mother and your sister—my only daughter, named Rara Ruhkanti."

### 12.
The prince was long silent. Seh Trenggana smiled and spoke gently: "Do not be hesitant in your heart. I give you knowledge—my daughter's heart, if I suspect you, your wish to stop arises because you are drawn to the maiden."

### 13.
"That, child, is not your nature." The prince replied, deeply reluctant in his intention. He continued on his way—Gathak and Gathuk behind, nodding as they heard the sage's words: "This man is simple, clear, firm, precise."

### 14.
"As if catching the wind"—he entered the house, seated on a fine stone mat. The host spoke: "Be comfortable wherever you sit, child. Consider this house your own." Prince Jayengresmi was silent.

### 15.
The wife and child were summoned. The sage spoke softly: "Sitaresmi, know that this is the child of Sunan Giri Prapen—Jayengresmi by name. I am taking him as a son—to become Ruhkanti's brother."

### 16.
"Come, give your welcome." Sitaresmi spoke sweetly: "Welcome, child, to my presence." The prince replied: "Aunt, your blessing." Rara Ruhkanti offered welcome: "Brother, may you be well."

### 17.
"Yes, sister, I accept your welcome." Seh Trenggana spoke softly: "Sitaresmi, bring out the refreshments—betel and its accompaniments. Do not forget the snacks. It is almost Maghrib time."

### 18.
Refreshments were placed before them—deliciously enjoyed. Maghrib time came. Seh Trenggana said: "Come, child, to the mosque for prayer." They left the house and performed ablution.

### 19.
The time is not described. After Isha they returned to the house. A meal of rice was prepared, complete with vegetables and fish. Ni Sitaresmi said softly: "Husband, I have prepared the meal—it is ready."

### 20.
They ate heartily; Ken Ruhkanti served. When they finished, the remains were promptly passed to Gathak and Gathuk, who ate ravenously—having long gone without such food.

### 21.
Fruits were also served. The prince's heart was pleased, acknowledged as a son. Seh Trenggana spoke softly: "Child, what do you wish? Will you rest or still desire to stay awhile in the house?" The prince replied softly:

### 22.
"Except as Father wills—I am comfortable staying. Let us talk of what I have obtained—I ask for blessing, that my heart may not be consumed by longing." Seh Trenggana was greatly pleased, speaking gently:

### 23.
"Whatever your questions, I will answer precisely—using fitting riddles, that you may not be dull. Come, child, do not doubt me. Father: what is life's purpose? Answer precisely."

### 24.
"'Lae-lae, gudhe pandhak'—'Ah, child, the kingdom of Kuranji.' Your life, child—has no 'wherefore.' 'Flower of the lake'—the name of the great snake. Your life is like the lotus flower—not of the pond, child."

### 25.
"'Where is your Lord?' 'The great curled fern, petis mixed with turmeric.' 'Who speaks to you? Who created the world?' 'The bird of the chariot—prince of Ngayodya—destined to become one with the self that is concealed.'"

### 26.
"'Who moves the world?' 'As the minangkara, always weaving songs.' 'Lae-lae—my thought—power without cause. Where do you come from?' 'I come from Sawosempal—the dwelling of Lord Bagaspati.'"

### 27.
"'From nothingness I originated—thus I became created in this world. When Father reaches death—where does he go?' 'Palm-fiber, palm-sugar, like my bundle—returning to the beginning—as when you were not.'"

### 28.
"'In death—what does one meet?' 'Yellow turmeric—child of the glorious king of Janggala.' 'In death, child—one meets nothing.' 'What of Father's Shahada?' 'Gula-drawa—the ogre-king—my Shahada is Purubaya.'"

### 29.
"'Deaf, mute, and blind—yet never ceasing to chatter.' 'Where is the True Spirit?' 'The water of life in the underworld—the voice of God's existence without cover—within thought—many are preoccupied with worldly courtesy.'"

### 30.
"'Ah, child—the essence of the day—contemplating, ruling a noble kingdom—forgetful of the soul, veiled in the excess of love—love ever causes my confusion.' 'Ah, child—the rocky face—set it aflame.'"

### 31.
"'Lae-lae—your prayer—is a vain pursuit, uncertain of its goal.' 'Heed—do not be absorbed in your worship of the true—' 'Leather and silk, the royal hunt—true death—groping, uncertain is your worship—child, that is not precise.'"

### 32.
"'Where is the place that does not emerge? Show the way—answer quickly.' 'Wisangkara—my child—the shelter of the forest.' 'One who does not know—much talk is heard. If that is revealed—later, Father, I will serve.'"

### 33.
"'Child—a jest of feeling—heed, do not misinterpret.' 'The lovely woman's life-breath—fragrant, touching the soul.' 'If one utters words without purpose—' 'Ah, ah—wild guava—the palanquin—only the lady is the goal.'"

### 34.
"'Regretting friendship with one not lasting—' 'The flower clinging to Mount Weléri—love cut off, not true.' 'Child, your words—is Father lacking in offerings? Be faithful—if false, the words already spoken.'"

### 35.
"'How to make known?' 'White bamboo-shoot—the battlefield of the king—Arjuna Sasrabahu.' 'If it is revealed—come, you yourself will be bathed.' After speaking thus, Seh Trenggana passed away.

### 36.
The prince embraced him, bowing, tears streaming: "Father—why have you suddenly passed away without cause?" Seh Trenggana vanished, leaving only a sweet voice: "I do not die—merely change my dwelling to the origin."

### 48.
## Asmaradana 1.

### 1.
Those left behind grieved—the prince and the wife and child—in the depth of sorrow. Yet they surrendered to God's will—destiny cannot be avoided—finally accepting in their hearts: "Thanks be to Allahu Akbar."

### 2.
Rara Ruhkanti grieved—her love for the Spirit deepened, her devotion strengthened—fasting sunnah without ceasing each month. Thus the lady greatly increased—radiant, brilliant in her splendor.

### 3.
Ni Sitaresmi and the prince were deeply compassionate—only praying to the All-Seeing God for her to draw near her true spouse, to obtain an excellent match. Thus the handsome prince remained long on Mount Tampomas.

### 4.
One day, Ni Sitaresmi sat on the west veranda, her two children before her. Rara Ruhkanti spoke: "Brother, what is the nature of the Seven Grades?"

### 5.
"It is fitting that you know, sister—one who loves the Spirit: the Grades—the seven Grades altogether—are the stages of ascent. One sees with penetrating vision—the sight toward the Spirit."

### 6.
"First: Akadiyat. Second: the Grade of Wahdat. Third: Wakidiyat. Fourth: the World of Spirits. Fifth: the World of Similitudes. Sixth: the World of Bodies. Seventh: Insan Kamil."

### 7.
"The Grade of Insan Kamil is placed at the end—the gathering place of the six Grades that are spoken of. The inner three are the first mentioned; the outer four—"

### 8.
"Now, Akadiyat first—called the Absolute Being. The meaning of 'absolute'—not yet having attributes, nor yet having names. Therefore—it is not yet manifest."

### 9.
"It is called 'Kun-Ijati'—the meaning of 'Kun'—the highest of the high. The mind becomes bewildered—no one can know the 'Kun' of the Most High God—all intellect is confounded."

### 10.
"The Grade of Wahdat—called the First Determination—because the Almighty willed to manifest Himself and His knowledge. Thus His existence became manifest, with half of His attributes."

### 11.
Ingaranan suku Dat-ti|khakekat Mukamadiyah|têgêse suku Dat rêke|iku kalakuaning Dat|

ê

### 11.
"...the place of gathering—where knowledge gathers—and all the attributes of the Essence."

### 12.
"Now Wakidiyat—called the Second Determination—because the All-Seeing God manifested Himself and all creatures, along with the attribute of knowledge—now separated."

### 13.
"It is called 'Ayan Sabit'—that is the reality of man. The meaning of 'ayan'—his being is manifest—the seven earths and heavens, and the animals already formed—in the knowledge of the Lord."

### 14.
"The meaning: this man is forgetful of his physical existence—already encompassed by the Wakidiya of the Spirit. Thus he does not know joy—only the one who knows, knows the reality of the Spirit."

### 15.
"It is called 'Insan Hakiki'—because it is not separate from the Essence of the All-Seeing God. His existence is Spirit—does not issue from 'yes'—fixed in knowledge forever."

### 16.
"Now manifest are his existence and attributes together—called Insan Hakiki—with what is more manifest. Because that Man is the manifestation of the Almighty—the true manifestation."

### 17.
"Now the World of Spirits—the manifestation of the Spirit—because the All-Seeing God brought forth what exists within His knowledge—the place of manifesting the existence of the World of Spirits."

### 18.
"Now manifest are his existence and all attributes and all names—with what is more manifest. Because the World of Spirits becomes the substitute for the Almighty, the Most Holy."

### 19.
"It is called the True Spirit—named Nur Wilayah—because the name comes from His perfection—the simile of a child emerging from the mother's womb—the entire form is born."### 20.
"Now the World of Similitudes—called thus because it becomes the likeness of what is fixed in His knowledge—and manifest in existence—and also His attributes—then clearly the World of Bodies."

### 21.
"The World of Bodies—the manifestation of God—because the All-Seeing God manifests His existence—clearly the World of Bodies. The Almighty brings about the existence of the World of Bodies."

### 22.
"Thus the existence of God—with what is more manifest—clearly the physical form—that is the adornment of the Spirit—the outer adornment—that is the existence of the Almighty—the adornment of the manifestation of the Real."

### 23.
"Now Insan Kamil—the place of the seven Grades—the most exalted. The three inner ones: La-Takyun is its name, second: the Essence, third: Ayan Sabitah."

### 24.
"The three outer Grades: World of Spirits, World of Similitudes, World of Bodies. The meaning of 'bodies': the density and subtlety of existence that receives portion—Jawhar Awwal—its attribute—and Act and Mukdas."

### 25.
"It is called 'Insan Kamil'—called thus because its existence is not separate from the existence of God—manifest is the existence of the Beginning—and the attribute of the Most Holy—manifest in that Man."

### 26.
"It is also called 'Insan Basari'—called thus because it is of the species of physical form—its density and subtlety—its existence changes—because its existence comes from the Elements as its origin."

### 27.
"The Elements: wind, fire, water, earth—these four. Therefore they are distinguished—the nature of humans—none gathers together—the four are not united."

### 28.
"The nature of the earth element: base in its character. Water: cool in character, and also base. The wind element: cool-warm in character. Fire: hot in character."

### 29.
"All these natures gather in humans—all are present—so that one may feel coolness and heat, pleasure and pain, low and high—the nature of humans."

### 30.
"It is the duty of every believer to mirror what has been spoken—for the mirror of all believers is that which manifests the True Spirit—called 'Man'—with what is more manifest."

### 31.
"The chosen Prophet said: whoever sees anything at all, if he does not see the True Spirit, his sight is void—his looking is in vain—he gains nothing from seeing."

### 32.
"The sight of the gnostic—the gnostic's sight penetrates—like watching a wayang performance—the dalang is seen—but the dalang has vanished—the dalang's words are what remain—truly the dalang speaks."

### 33.
"There is an adornment—the Form 'Kak' is manifest—with the Essence—and Ayan Sabitah—the existence of the World of Spirits—and the World of Bodies—with the Essence and Its attributes all together."

### 34.
"May you be able to receive all these mirrors—for the path of perfection—all the saints are commanded to affirm the words of the Almighty—all mirror the Adornment."

### 35.
"As for the Grades from the World of Jabarud—because many are confounded by all these truths—manifesting what belongs to the Most High—thus they become the colors of the Grades."

### 36.
Thus Rara Ruhkanti was stirred in hearing her brother's words on the arrangement of the Seven Grades. The sun set—the bustle of tasks—Maghrib time as usual.

### 37.
Now the tale turns: the young Sultan of Cirebon had heard news of the death of the hermit of Mount Tampomas, who left a noble daughter—exceedingly beautiful, perfect, supreme.

### 38.
His minister was sent to Tampomas to bring the wife and child. To cut the story short, they departed and arrived at Tampomas. They met the lovely one. Sitaresmi welcomed them.

### 39.
The envoy was entertained; then the Sultan of Cirebon's wish was conveyed. Ni Sitaresmi spoke: "What is your wish, child? Do not let there be blame—be open with your mother."

### 40.
Ni Ruhkanti spoke softly: "My life and death are yours—I follow your will, Mother—and also whatever Brother says. I am not reluctant. The elder sibling's guidance is truly of the highest."

### 41.
Hearing her daughter's words, Ni Sitaresmi wept, remembering her husband who had gone to heaven. Then she spoke sweetly to her son, Prince Jayengresmi:

### 42.
"Ah, what is your counsel, my child—regarding the Sultan's wish for me and your sister? Do not be hesitant in your heart—whatever you say, I will follow."

### 49.
### 43.
The prince bowed and said: "Aunt, my refuge—whatever you wish should be done. It is the Sultan's desire—truly a grace from God."

### 44.
"If fortune is favorable, perhaps it will bring good—the great man of Cirebon." Ni Sita heard this—her heart was gladdened. She gave answer to the minister:

### 45.
"I follow his will—let there be no dispute." After they prepared, the prince spoke again: "Aunt, I ask your forgiveness—I cannot accompany you."

### 46.
"I wish to follow my own heart—only offering prayer for the welfare of you all in this world and the next. May Allah grant faith and keep far from calamity."

### 47.
Ni Sita spoke softly, tears flowing: "Child, my prayer—may God accept it." The prince said to the lady: "Ah, my dear sister—"

### 48.
"You are grown now—follow your mother. When you serve your husband, let your heart be true. Do not be haughty—be gentle and careful in all things."

### 8.
"I provide you with blessing, accompanied by all prayers—day and night unceasing, like flowing water. May all wishes be granted, fulfilled in supreme excellence."

### 9.
"When you are wedded, do not abandon the old ways—the examples of old, like the ways of the Prophet's wives, which bring the lady excellence, to attain the highest heaven."

### 10.
"As provision for all your life—lasting, never changing nor shifting—perfect your conduct, shun all that is called fault in the bearing and action of a woman."

### 11.
"Nothing is lacking in noble examples—the conduct of women. Faithfulness is to be practiced, as mentioned in the scriptures. Those who do so are lasting—if they fall to degradation—"

### 12.
"But you must know the right—the nature of truth, child—do not rely on being 'right' alone—that is the balance of existence. Consider the place—estimate—waking and sleeping, do not forget."

### 13.
The lady bowed and said: "Brother, your commands I treasure. Your blessing is supreme—may I be able to practice all that has been set forth, may it penetrate my heart."

### 14.
"If Brother is truly wandering—I follow you in well-being." "Yes, sister, I accept." The prince spoke to the Sultan's envoy, requesting the noble minister—

### 15.
Humbly he spoke: "I entrust my mother and sister to you—do not fail in kindness." The envoy bowed: "Yes, child, do not worry." They quickly shook hands; Rara Ruhkanti paid homage. Then they departed each way. Mount Tampomas lost its serenity—like a setting without its jewel.

### 16.
The story tells of the noble lady's arrival in Cirebon. Before long she was taken as the Sultan of Cirebon's wife. The mother cared for her child—abundant honor and glory.

### 17.
Now the tale turns: Jayengresmi, Gathuk, and Gathak descended from the mountain, always traveling along the range, walking day and night.

### Prologue
At Mount Mandalawangi he met Ajar Suganda, who taught: 1. The daily divination of good and bad days; 2. Pranatamangsa (seasonal reckoning); 3. Candrasangkala (chronograms) with the character of each word and how to compose them; 4. The nature of dates 1 through 30. Arriving in Bogor, he met Ki Wargapati, who taught the practice of daily prayer for long life. Jayengresmi gave instruction on the age of turmoil and the Great Resurrection. Finally he continued his journey, viewing the ruins of the Pajajaran kingdom...

### 18.
At one point he rested, then went south up a mountain called Atunggul. He did not stay long there, then went northwest along the ridge to Mount Tangkuban Perahu.

### 19.
Only three days—he descended to Mount Burangrang, then to Mount Wayang. Seven days he departed, going northwest to Mount Sanggabuana.

### 20.
Krawang to the south—there only three days. He descended southward to the Priangan Pasundan region, always traveling on the mountains, delighted by the sight—

### 21.
The beauty of the mountains—the region of Bandung—steep ravines charming to behold—hilltops seen green—gave pleasure to the journey—delighting one accustomed to the mountains.

### 22.
His journey grew more distant—peaceful as he walked. He climbed the foot of Mount Gedhe Mandalawangi, his intention set on the peak of Mandalawangi.

### 23.
No obstacle was encountered—the prince's journey was smooth, Gathak and Gathuk following behind. He reached the mountain's flank and saw a village—beautiful. The prince climbed further.

### 24.
Arriving at the dry field, the prince met people weeding and tending their fields. He spoke gently: "Uncle, I ask—what village is this?"

### 25.
"And who is the elder here?" The weeder stopped and spoke: "Handsome one—this village is named after the mountain—Mandalawangi. As for the hermit of the mountain—"

### 26.
"Ajar Suganda is his name. If you wish to meet him, I will announce you first." The prince spoke sweetly: "Go on, announce me."

### 27.
The weeder stopped his work—shouldering his hoe, carrying his mattock—striding home to the hermitage. He met Ajar Suganda and told him three people had come, one of noble bearing.

### 28.
Ajar Suganda said: "Go, summon him quickly—leave your hoe." The messenger went swiftly, carrying his mattock at the ready. He reached the dry field and met them.

### 29.
He said: "Please, Sir—you are called—I will lead." The prince went, Gathak and Gathuk behind. Ajar Suganda came to meet them, welcoming with smiles and greeting.

### 30.
He scattered flowers of speech—danced like a peacock. They bowed, hands clasped; the prince was led. Gathak and Gathuk watched happily. They arrived at the hermitage.

### 31.
Seated on a mat, Ajar Suganda spoke gently: "Your grace and excellent sign—the radiance of a newcomer is seen. Where do you come from, and what is your name, my love?"

### 32.
The prince spoke sweetly: "My name is Jayengresmi, from Giri Prapen. I seek my lost siblings—the brother named Jayengsari, the sister named Rancangkapti."

### 33.
Ajar Suganda was long silent—then nodded and spoke softly: "You are received, child, as I perceive you. Soon they will be found—they too have been given excellence."

### 34.
"As for your goal—where are you headed, child?" The prince bowed: "My goal is toward Kawis, but I do not know the continuation of the story."

### 50.
## Gambuh 1.

### 1.
"At Karang, child—the land of Banten, the westernmost tip of Java—there is no other kingdom beyond. There dwells a noble man, excellent in knowledge, extraordinary."

### 2.
"A truly saintly scholar, master of all knowledge—nothing is beyond his power. He has a son, whose mother died in childbirth."

### 3.
"The child, after his birth, has always devoted himself to ascetic wandering—wandering until now—no word yet of his return to Karang."

### 4.
"His name, I recall—Kyageng Karang, Seh Ibrahim Ibnu Abubakar, originally from the land of Aib. He has long dwelt in Karang."

### 5.
"Your intention, child, is toward Mount Karang. Study there—truly supreme. But, child, do not yet reveal my words."

### 6.
"Sidik the Exalted—meditate first on Mount Salak. Later you will be destined—the noble one will fetch you, taking you to the hermitage at Karang."

### 7.
The prince replied softly: "If it is your will, sage—I will depart now for Mount Salak." Ajar Suganda chuckled: "That is the way of youth—"

### 8.
"Not thinking of difficulty—truly you do not yet know. But do not be anxious, child—I will escort you later. First stop by the ruins of the palace—"

### 9.
"Of Pajajaran, child—but we must go on a favorable day—according to the calculation of Iladuni." The prince spoke sweetly: "I follow your will."

### 10.
Ajar Suganda then calculated the day—a suitable Iladuni was found. They departed for the ruined palace. Their journey is not described—they arrived at the remains of Pajajaran.

### 11.
The remnants of the palace were in ruins—collapsed, overgrown with trees, entangled with vines. The prince felt deep pity. Ajar Suganda said:

### 12.
"Such is the fate of the world—nothing is lasting. Once grand, now fallen—collapsed, ruined. Have you not heard the example of old? This and Malacca—famous kingdoms—now only traces remain."

### 13.
"As for the calculation—the Iladuni according to the standard—" Kyai Ajar Suganda spoke gently: "Within one month there are seven days referred to."

### 14.
"For example, starting on Friday—Friday the 1st: its character is good. Friday the 2nd: it meets the enemy of the All-Seeing God."

### 15.
"Friday the 3rd: meets death. Friday the 4th: many are pleased—observe carefully. Friday the 5th: good. Then Saturday:"

### 16.
"Saturday the 1st: the enemy of God. Saturday the 2nd: loss and error. Saturday the 3rd: meets sorrow. Saturday the 4th: good. Saturday the 5th: meets—"

### 17.
"Sunday is stated: Sunday the 1st: loss and error. Sunday the 2nd: good. Sunday the 3rd: meets sorrow. Sunday the 4th: good in all conduct."

### 18.
"Sunday the 5th: enemy of God. Then Monday: Monday the 1st: loss—not to be done. Monday the 2nd: good. Monday the 3rd: meets sorrow."

### 19.
"Monday the 4th: good. Monday the 5th: meets death. Then Tuesday is related: Tuesday the 1st: the enemy of God truly."

### 20.
"Tuesday the 2nd: good. Tuesday the 3rd: separation. Tuesday the 4th: good. Tuesday the 5th: meets a corpse."

### 21.
"Then Wednesday: Wednesday the 1st: meets sin. Wednesday the 2nd: good. Wednesday the 3rd: meets death. Wednesday the 4th: good—nothing to be said."

### 22.
"Wednesday the 5th: enemy of God. Then Thursday: Thursday the 1st: meets loss. Thursday the 2nd: enemy of God truly."

### 23.
"Thursday the 3rd: good. Thursday the 4th: meets calamity. Thursday the 5th: enemy of God. Thus complete are the seven days, in consideration of the date."

### 24.
"For a wedding, child—it is most important to choose what is favorable—meeting goodness and prosperity—many will visit. That is one choice, child—truly without worry."

### 25.
The prince asked again: "There are people who delay building their house, waiting for the right season. What is the intention—what season do they await?"

### 26.
The sage spoke softly: "The character of the seasons for building a house—from Kasa to Sadha—each has its own nature. Listen—I will tell the story."

### 27.
"The season Kasa: 41 days—its omen: good, suitable for all work—safe and pleasing. The season Karo is related:"

### 28.
"Its age: 23 days. Its omen: quarrel and conflict—great difficulty. Season Katiga: 24 days—omen: fire—bad."

### 29.
"Theft and loss. Season Kapat: 25 days—omen: very good—blessed by Sri and fortune, good—increase of children, male and female."

### 30.
"Season Kalima: 27 days—omen of many children and safety. Season Kanem: 43 days—"

### 31.
"Omen of many relatives. Season Kapitu: also 43 days—omen of much work—many people traveling."

### 32.
"Those at home are not attained—because many affairs accompany. Season Kawolu: 26 days—omen: there is no wife—divorce or death."

### 33.
"Season Kasanga: 25 days—omen of sorrow and lack of everything. Season Kasadasa: 24 days—omen most good—all desires arrive, safe with children and family—plantings thrive. Season Dhesta is related:"

### 34.
"Season Dhesta: 23 days—omen very dangerous—illness, and if the body is sick, no cure is found—even death may come. Season Sadha is told:"

### 35.
"Its age: 41 days—omen: sorrow of heart, separation from family. Wherever one desires—good or ill has been told."

### 36.
The prince spoke softly: "What is fitting should be remembered for prosperity—it is proper to choose. If one transgresses what is unfavorable, one is ruined and becomes a cautionary tale."

### 37.
"When I once saw the Demak mosque and its veranda, besides the ornaments finely carved, there was an inscription: 'Kori Roro Gawening Wong.'"

### 38.
"The doorsteps also had an inscription: 'Patra Kinarya Rupa Gelapi.' What is the meaning?" And the sage thus spoke:

### 36.
Umpak kori lumêbu|ugi wontên sasêratanipun|mungêl Patra Kinarya Rupa Gêlapi|kadi paran kajêngipun|Ki Ajar mijil kang raos||

### 51.
## Mijil 1.

### 1.
"That, child, is the chronogram of the year—my calculation: 'Kori Sanga Loro Lugu Bae Gawe Papat Njupuk Saka Karti Wong'—each character is one—becoming the year number."

### 2.
"One thousand, one hundred, twenty-nine, child. The lower doorstep: 'Patra Siji Kinarya Papate Rupa Papat Njupuk Warna Ugi'—becoming four again—'Gelapi' is one."

### 3.
"My calculation of the upper doorstep—when made—is the year one thousand four hundred and forty-one, child—that is my chronogram reading."

### 4.
The prince bowed, speaking softly: "Explain to me the nature of the chronogram." The sage spoke again: "Know, child, my chronogram—"

### 5.
"The character of each (1) to ten is fixed. When ten is written with 'edas' or 'nul'—I will explain, child, along with their meanings."

### 6.
"Character 'one': 'rupa' (form), 'candra' (moon), 'wulan' (month), 'lek' (full moon), 'sasa' (star), 'lintang' (star), 'dhara' (dot), 'weteng' (belly), 'bumi' (earth), 'lemah' (ground), 'Buddha' (Buddha)—all are one."

### 7.
"'Roning godhong' (leaf), 'medi' (buttocks), 'buntut' (tail), 'dara' (dove), 'janma' (person), 'uwong' (person), 'eka' (one), 'tuduh' (pointer), 'siji' (one), 'wak' (body), 'badan' (body), 'sadhemi' (corpse), 'suta' (child), 'anak' (child)—all meaning one."

### 8.
"'Siti' (earth), 'lemah' (ground), 'wedhen' (sand), 'akendel' (strong), 'wungkulan' (one), 'wutuh' (whole), 'wulan' (moon), 'niyat' (intention)—all are one—all like the character 'one' entirely."

### 9.
"Now character 'two' is described: 'netra' (eye), 'mripat' (eye), 'caksu' (eye), 'nayana' (eye)—'sikara' (hand), 'tangan' (hand), 'dwi' (two), 'buja' (arm), 'bau' (shoulder)."

### 10.
"'Paksa' (wing), 'uwang' (palm), 'drasthi' (eyebrow), 'ama' (eyelid), 'locana' (eye), 'carana' (heel), 'karna' (ear), 'karni' (horn)."

### 11.
"And the meaning of 'ambaning kang kuping' (ear-width), 'anebah' (earlobe), 'talapukaning netra' (eyelid), 'talingan' (ear), 'pangrunguning kuping' (hearing), 'mata' (eye), 'netra' (eye), and 'tangan' (hand)."

### 12.
"'Lar' means wing-feather, 'anembah wot sinom' means paying homage, 'suku loro' means two feet. Now character 'three' is described in words."

### 13.
"'Bahni' (fire), 'gêni' (fire), 'pandhe' (smith), 'pawaka' (fire), 'gêni pucaking arga' (fire on the mountain peak), 'siking upet' (wick), 'tekên' (staff), 'guna' (virtue) means fire, 'agaran' (hearth) truly."

### 14.
"'Dahana' (fire without source), 'trining rana' (three in battle), also fire of war—'lintah' (leech), 'ujel' (eel), 'welut' (eel), 'cacing' (worm), 'jatha' means fang."

### 15.
"And fire in a vessel—'weddha' (hearth-fire), 'geni pawon' (stove-fire), and 'serat papakem' (writing), 'nalagni' (fire of the heart's heat), 'wong manggang' (one grilling)."

### 16.
"'Kayalenna' (ember), 'geni diyan' (lamp fire)—three in one—'geni obor' (torch fire). Now character 'four' is described."

### 17.
"'Wedang' (hot water), 'banyu panas' (hot water), 'sagara' (ocean), 'banyu kang ngideri jagad' (water surrounding the world), 'karti' (water), 'banyu sumur' (well water), 'we padasan' (water vessel), 'jaladri' (sea)."

### 18.
"'Banyu rawa' (swamp water), 'nadi' (river), 'banyu kali' (river water), 'her' (water), 'udaka' (water), 'nawa' (water), 'banyu adhem' (cold water), 'samudra' (ocean), 'banyu teleng' (deep water)."

### 19.
"'Jalanidhi' (ocean), 'waruna' (god of waters)—all meaning water. 'We jembangan' (water pot), 'o ana toya truh' (dew water)."

### 20.
"'Waudadi' (two waters), 'we pancuran' (two spouts), 'we deresan' (coconut and palm water), 'sindu' (water-milk), 'banyu krambil' (coconut water)—and 'catur' means four."

### 21.
"'Tasik' (sea), 'sagara' (ocean)—water. 'Catur' (four), 'yuda' (war), 'keblat' (direction)—'pat' means four. Now character 'five' is described—know it, child."

### 22.
"'Buta' (giant), 'wil lanang' (male), 'siyung' (tusk)—'Pandhawa' (five Pandawas), 'atmajendra' (son of Pandu), 'getih' (blood), 'otot' (sinew), 'ati' (liver), 'keteg' (pulse), 'pramana' (life-force)—'mangsa kalima' (the fifth season)."

### 23.
"'Wisaya' (object), 'panggawe' (action), 'indri' (sense), 'bayuning kang panon' (strength of sight), 'yaksa' (giantess), 'wil wadon' (female), 'siyung' (tusk), 'sara' (arrow), 'landhep' (sharp), 'maruta' (wind), 'angin' (wind), 'ngirit ganda wangi' (wafting fragrance), 'pawana' (wind), 'ribut' (storm)."

### 24.
"'Gana' (forest), 'wana' (forest), 'margana' (wind), 'samirana' (breeze), 'warayang' (weapon)—'panca' (five)—'bayu' (wind)—all meaning five."

### 25.
"Wind that enters and exits—'wisikan' (whisper), 'wuruking bapa' (father's teaching), 'gulingan' (rustling)—five. Now character 'six' is spoken."

### 26.
"'Masa' (season), 'mangsa' (season), 'nem' (six), 'rasa' (taste), 'winaya' (teaching), 'ganggang' (dragonfly), 'gana' (forest), 'tawon' (bee)—all six."

### 27.
"'Awor' (mixed), 'yek-uyekan' (squirming), 'anggas' (insect), 'ber-iberan' (flying things), 'kayu glinggang' (rolling wood), 'oyag obah' (shaking), 'rinegga' (adorned), 'kaupakara' (decorated)."

### 28.
"'Pangrarasing nem' (six music) is the character of six. 'Tahan' (teak), 'wreksa kayu' (tree), 'prabatang' (trunk), 'kayu rubuh' (fallen tree)."

### 29.
"'Malang ana ing dalan gumuling' (lying crosswise on the road), 'kilating nem' (flash of six), 'lilidhahing mangsa kanem' (sixth season), 'lona pedhes mla kecut' (sweet, spicy, sour), 'tikta' (bitter), 'kyasa gurih' (salty-savory)."

### 30.
"'Dura' (salty), 'sarkara' (sweet). Now character 'seven': 'ardi' (mountain), 'gunung' (mountain), 'prawata' (mountain), 'turangga' (horse), 'wajik' (diamond), 'giri' (mountain)."

### 31.
"'Resi' (sage), 'pandhita' (priest), 'angsa' (swan), 'banyak' (swan), 'biksu' (monk), 'sapi' (bull), 'cala' (mountain), 'sikil gunung' (mountain foot), 'imawan' (peak), 'imasri' (beautiful peak)—'sapta' means seven."

### 32.
"'Pandhita putus' (perfect sage), 'swara wiku' (monk's voice), 'gora agung' (great voice), 'pandhita amumuruk' (teaching sage), 'swa kuda kabiri' (gelding horse), 'tungganganing gunung' (mountain steed)."

### 33.
"'Iku tengah-tengahaning wukir' (the middle of the mountain), 'wiku' (monk), 'pandhita ing gunung' (mountain sage)—all seven. Now character 'eight' is described."

### 34.
"'Naga' (dragon), 'ula' (snake), 'panagan' (python), 'gyan ula geng' (great snake), 'salira' (body), 'manyawak' (lizard), 'basu tekek' (gecko), 'kumareng nagari' (coiling in the land), also called the 'eighth season.'"

### 34.
Naga ula gêng panagan kaki|gyan ula gêng kaot|salira manyawak basu têkèk|myang atêgês kumarèng nagari|tanapi araning|kang mangsa kawolu||

### 35.
"'Tanu' (chameleon), 'bunglon' (chameleon), 'murti' (form), 'cecak' (lizard), 'kunjara' (elephant stable), 'kandhanging gajah' (elephant stable), 'gajah wantilan' (fighting elephant), 'dipangga' (elephant), 'tinitihan ratu' (king's mount)."

### 36.
"'Asthi' (elephant), 'gajah kang denpalani' (trained elephant), 'gajah ana tengahing wana' (elephant in the forest), 'manggala' (war elephant), 'dwiraddha' (elephant), 'gajah meta' (raging elephant)."

### 37.
"'Bujangga' (male snake), 'brahmastha' (brahmin), 'pandhita sabrang' (eight overseas priests), 'dwipara' (elephant), 'gajah binakta maring pacangkraman' (elephant brought to the paddock), 'liman' (elephant)."

### 38.
"'Gajah binakta midhang' (elephant brought to the court), 'dening sang akatong' (by the ruler), 'ula lanang' (male snake)—all 'eight.' Now character 'nine' is described."

### 39.
"'Trustha' (gun barrel), 'trusthi' (cannon barrel), 'muka' (face), 'rai' (face), 'gapura' (gate), 'lawan' (door), 'ratu wiwara' (king's opening), 'leng saketheking' (fortress opening), 'dwara' (doorway), 'nanda' (large frog hole)."

### 40.
"'Wilasita' (beetle hole), 'guwa' (cave), 'leng patapan' (hermitage cave), 'lodra' (gully), 'gatra lengan gangsir' (cricket hole), 'leng lenging semut' (anthole)."

### 41.
"'Rong leng ula' (snake hole), 'song leng landhak' (porcupine hole), 'trusan' (passage), 'bubutulan' (piercing)—all meaning 'nine'—'babahan leng maling' (thief's hole), 'hawa sasanga' (nine airs)."

### 42.
"Now character 'ten' is described. 'Boma' (earth), 'suket mati' (dead grass), 'sunya' (emptiness), 'sepi' (silence), 'gagana' (sky), 'langit' (sky), 'kesisan ing mendhung' (cloud-height)."

### 43.
"'Barakan' (firmament) color unseen—'adoh tebih' (far distant)—'ing langit' (in the sky)—exists but not seen—'windu' (cycle), 'tumbuking taun' (year's conjunction)."

### 44.
"'In the sky'—'langit kalimputing mendhung' (sky veiled by clouds containing rain)—'widik-widik' (glimpse) appears then vanishes—'maletik satengahing wiyati' (flashing in mid-heaven)—vanishing in the sky."

### 45.
"'Das'—all that is nothing—'walang mesat' (grasshopper springing)—all meaning character 'ten'—everything signifying nothingness."

### 46.
"When making a chronogram of the year, the placement is fixed: the units first, then the tens follow. But the units' pronunciation falls behind."

### 47.
Prince Jayengresmi asked again: "O excellent one—how is it that 'welut' (eel) and 'agni' (fire) are the same? Explain clearly."

## Jurudemung 1.

### 1.
The sage smiled and spoke: "According to my interpretation—the book Candrageni is what explains the chronogram—that which employs the poetry of the meter Kusumawicitra."

### 2.
"The calculation is of twelve courses—told long ago—the composition of the Great Sage, the Aged Mpu Wilasaya—a poet perfect in knowledge—in the kingdom of Purwacarita—by the will of the King."

### 3.
"Widhayaka by name—called Aji Isaka—composed from the beginning to become the chronogram. But its interpretation still lacked purpose—the reason thereof."

### 4.
"The characters were not yet laid out—lacking clarity. Therefore the sequence was harmonized from one to ten—those of the same meaning—only gathered together—those of the same secret."

### 5.
"All was explained, along with the words that carry intention—arranged completely—I will follow as before—from character one to character ten."

### 6.
"The character 'one' is described; its form means appearance, that is the glow of my inner light. Therefore it is also the essential character, for this light becomes the mark of life in all that is created."

### 7.
"The moon, meaning the full moon on the fifteenth day—that becomes the character 'one' from ancient times, the place where Dewi Sri descended, sowing the seed that became the origin of humankind."

### 8.
"The month, meaning a full cycle, counting an entire month—therefore it becomes that character 'one' because it is noble, giving rise to one measure. Once more, like the foremost, now it means 'prophet'."

### 9.
"The navel therefore becomes the character 'one', for the navel is made as its support. The name is 'once' at birth—when the umbilical stump has fallen off, the child is given a name according to what is fitting, and then 'sasa' is its meaning."

### 10.
"A star therefore becomes the character 'one', for its form is solitary, each one separate—visible from the middle world, all are called stars. 'Dhara' means pregnant—thus it came to be."

### 11.
"The character 'one' is real, receiving its essence. The secret meaning of 'one' is earth, meaning soil. Therefore soil becomes the single character that nurtures everything into growth."

### 12.
"The seed of planted things that grow—now 'buddha' means the male principle, or 'more'—therefore it also becomes a character. For 'more' represents the seed of humanity, which is the foliage of its meaning."

### 13.
"The radiance of leaves is the origin of the character 'one', for it depends on life—plants, growth, all vegetation. If the leaves are visible, that is a sign of living seed; if the leaves are not seen, it is dead."

### 14.
"'Medi' means the anus, used as its passageway—refuse from the body. That means 'tail'—the tip of the tail, which is certainly only one. Now 'dara' is its meaning."

### 15.
"Indeed the dove bird—thus it came to be the character 'one', for it is willing to take shelter, familiar with humans. 'Janma' means 'person'. Therefore it also becomes a character—alone among the creations of the Great God."

### 16.
