"'Eka' means 'one', or that which is pointed out. 'Wak suta' means 'child and body are one'—from the seed of its becoming. 'Siti' means 'soil', its sense 'earth'—thus it came to be."

### 17.
"The character 'one' is able—its form emerges. 'Gya awani' means 'brave, the noble sun'—therefore the character 'one' is a beam reaching out toward all forms."

### 18.
"If 'wungkulan' is its meaning, 'wholeness' is its origin—it becomes the character 'one', for its form is still whole, truly singular. That meaning is all complete. The character 'two' is now spoken."

### 19.
"Eyes mean sight; thus they came to be. The character's duty is to close and to open. 'Caksu' truly means illumination. Therefore there are two characters, for there is impurity."

### 20.
"Two forms—the stain of tears. 'Nayana' means the true gaze. The stirring of the gaze is two matters: good and bad. 'Sikara' means the two hands—their duty is two matters: to open or to close."

### 21.
"'Buja bau' is its meaning—therefore its character is two: strengthening those two hands. 'Paksa uwang' is its meaning—thus it came to be, the character 'two', for its function is the two matters aforementioned."

### 22.
"Closing the jaw's clasp, opening the mouth's gap—'drasthi' means the eyebrow. Therefore it becomes the double character, for it truly adds quickness to two matters: closing both eyes."

### 23.
"Adding to the radiance of the face—now 'ama' is its meaning, the area around the mouth, the lower part of the countenance. Thus it came to be the character 'two', for it represents the flank of the two eyes."

### 24.
"Now 'locana' means the shimmering of the eyes, that is the corner of the eye, the direction right and left. Thus it came to be the character 'two', for it can move the eyelid."

### 25.
"The closing of the lid once becomes its watchfulness; the second is its shutting—serving as a shield against all impurities. Now 'carana' is its meaning, 'carefulness' its origin."

### 26.
"Therefore it becomes the double character, for that carefulness works to adorn the face: two matters—first, causing the radiance of the countenance; second, becoming the strength of its light."

### 27.
"'Karna' means the ornament of the ear—thus its character is two, for the ornament of the ear in ancient times was earrings and plugs. 'Karni' means the scent of the ear. Thus it becomes the character 'two'."

### 28.
"Able to receive taste and feeling—'anebah' is its meaning—the eyelid located beneath the lashes, above the shimmering. Therefore its character is two."

### 29.
"Because it mingles, opposing its movement—the brightness and darkness of the eyes: first, it governs the closing, the sight when waking; second, it governs the dimness of the eyes when wanting to sleep."

### 30.
"'De talingan' means the hearing of the ear—therefore its character is two, for it hears two matters: good and bad. 'Maha' is its meaning—the sense of sight."

### 31.
"Therefore it becomes the two characters, for it supports the senses—two matters aforementioned: 'kedhap' is the circling of the gaze, that which is green in color; the second is 'kilat', the jewel of the gaze in its rolling."

### 32.
"'Lèn tanganna' is its meaning—applause is its desire—joy and sorrow. 'Lar wulu suwiwinya' (the structure of feathers and wings)—hence two characters with two tasks: to open or to close."

### 33.
"Used as a paddle for cooling the force of flight—now 'suku karo' (two legs), 'angabekti dalamakan' (paying homage)—making a cup of the two hands. The character 'two' is finished. The prince bowed and asked."

### 34.
"Character 'one' and 'two'—their conduct and bearing—their inclination and secret meaning." The sage spoke in unison. "Now character 'three' is revealed. Sinom is the meter that changes."

## Sinom 1.

### 1.
"'Bahni' (fire), 'geni' (fire), 'pande' (smith)—hence they become character 'three.' Arising from three things: first, fire from friction; second, from means—charcoal; third, the manipulation of wind through bellows."

### 2.
"'Pawaka' meaning 'fire at the mountain peak'—hence character 'three,' from three things: 'agni sasalat' (lightning fire) from sulfur; second, fire from incense—prayer-offering meditation; three, the 'bedhiyan' (flint) as the means of ignition."

### 3.
"'Siking geni upet' (fire-striking steel)—hence character 'three.' The form of the striker is three-colored: 'mancung' (pointed), 'sepet' (flat), 'ontel-kluwih' (round). Its use is to kindle fire from three sources: from the heat of sparks, from the friction of the tool, from the tinder—complete with three colors."

### 4.
"'Dahana pan geni salat' (lightning fire)—hence character 'three,' from three things: first from the heat of the earth's air; second from the heat of the fire-stone; third from the heat of sulfur, combining to make lightning."

### 5.
"'Trining brana pyu paprangan' (three treasures of warfare)—hence character 'three.' Their existence from three colors: first from sharpness; second from the meeting of warriors; third from the burning of the enemy. 'Uta' means leech—hence character 'three'—its teeth: two below, one above."

### 6.
"Its functions are three: 'napel' (adhering), 'molah' (moving), 'ganda bangkit' (powerful scent). 'Ujel welut' (eel-squirming) means character 'three' because it possesses three powers: slipperiness, suppleness, strength—seizing, growing, writhing. Hence character 'three' because it has these three."

### 7.
"Its three functions: coiling, stretching, crawling. 'Jatha' means 'fire contained'—hence character 'three': first charcoal-fire; second contained fire; third the fire of purpose—burning, roasting, and so forth."

### 8.
"'Wedha geni pawon' (fire-hearth)—there are three colors: fire, wood, and the hearth-ring, third, the 'laweyan geni.' 'Nalakni' means 'the heat of passion'—hence arising from three things."

### 9.
"First from kindling; second from burning through anger; third from unleashing the airs. 'Geni manggang' (roasting fire) gives character 'three': fire, wood, charcoal, skewer—complete with three. 'Kayalena' means 'ember-fire.'"

### 10.
"Hence character 'three'—because the ember-fire is often used for three things: warming, straightening, igniting. 'Geni diyan' (lamp-fire)—hence character 'three'—because its light is three colors: blue, yellow, red—complete as three."

### 11.
"Finally, know 'three': 'geni thithikan' (spark-fire) is three: fire-stone, tinder, steel. Now character 'four' is described. 'Wedang' (hot water) first—'banyu panas' (hot water) means 'four'—its four powers: ripening, bubbling, moving, surging."

### 12.
"'Sagara' (sea) 'we pakumpulan' (gathering water)—water from four sources: river-springs, river-torrents, spring-fountains, and raindrops. 'Katri' described: well-water used for four things: watering, bathing, washing, rinsing."

### 13.
"'Sukci wedi we padasan' (holy water basin)—used for four purifications: cupping, rinsing hands, washing feet. 'Jaladri' means 'contained water'—holding four waters: rain, river, stream, torrent."

### 14.
"Hence river-water has character 'four'—becoming four purifications: bathing, washing, cooking, preparing, watering animals. 'Her banyu pucuk arga' (mountain-spring water)—character 'four' because the mountain peak has four kinds of water."

### 15.
"'We kawah' (crater water), 'banyu pamuja' (offering water), 'banyu pedhut' (fog water), 'banyu riris' (drizzle). 'Nawa' means 'sweet water'—wellspring and fountain water. 'Samodra' means 'encircling water'—the four cardinal directions are surrounded by ocean water."

### 16.
"'Jalanidhi' is water from drizzle, rivers, streams, and torrents. 'Banyu sawangan' (pond water) from rivers, seepage, springs—four reservoirs. 'Toya' means 'pond water used for body purification.'"

### 17.
"Bathing, washing wholly—'wahana' is rainwater—acquired from four things: from steam of fire, from steam of plants, from steam of water, fourth from steam of earth. Thus 'we deresan' (dripping water)."

### 18.
"From excessive heat, from constriction of wind, from the force of the sun, from the heat of the rays. 'Sindu' means 'milk-water'—from four things: first from medicinal means, second from wind-oil, third from coconut water."

### 19.
"'Warih' means 'coconut water'—called 'warih' in four stages: from 'bluluk' (young coconut), then 'cengkir,' 'dawegan,' and 'krambil' (mature coconut). 'Tasik' means 'root-water'—first drawn and pulled from four kinds of shoots."

### 20.
"Thus dripping becomes water—from fire, wind, water, and earth. 'Catur we keblat' (four directional waters)—there are truly four oceans. 'Yogya pat'—from four things: the yoga age, the water age, the gateway age, and the age of works."

### 21.
"Character 'five' is described. 'Buta' (spirit) as already told—character 'five' because it can unite five species: the unseen, the flying, the animals, the water-creatures—and the fifth can unite with humans."

### 22.
"'Pandhawa' means the five sons of King Pandu Dewanata: first Yudhisthira, second Bima, third Arjuna—same father, same mother. The two from a different mother: fourth Nakula, fifth Sadewa. Now 'getih otot' (blood and sinew) is defined."

### 23.
"The eyes manage the senses, the tongue manages speech, the hands manage grasping, the feet manage sitting or walking, the genitals manage passion. Now 'gati' is its meaning—steadfastness in its place, in the neck, chest, joints, and arms."

### 24.
"The ankle joints of the feet and the stride of the elephant—the sense-power that is the eye—thus gathered from the nose and ears, the face and from the heart. 'Yaksa' means 'giant', 'danawa' means 'demon', with fangs indeed. 'Ditya' means 'ogre'—thus they become character 'five'."

### 25.
"Thus they become the foremost—four demon-chiefs, the fifth is oneself. 'Sara landhep' is its meaning—in ancient times, the weapon called 'sara' was sharp with five faces. 'Maruta' means 'wind'—that which carries the scent of the five flowers."

### 26.
"Flowers from the rain from the gods, flowers from the garden, flowers of incense-offering, flowers of the coiffure, and flower of the essence—the flowers of the delicate. 'Pawana' means 'wind', the strong wind from the four directions and from above. 'Bana' means 'great forest'."

### 27.
"Thus it becomes character 'five'—five kinds of danger: snake, tiger, dog, jackal, demon, and other things. 'Margana' is its meaning—wind in the sky, that is the breath emerging from the five senses. 'Samirana' means the wind that casts away."

### 28.
"Sweat in five places: face, arms, chest, back, feet—complete as the fifth. 'Warayang' is its meaning—the weapon of sharpness—the starfruit has four, the fifth is its tip. 'Panca bayu' truly—they are considered the five sons of Lord Bayu."

### 29.
"The first is Jajahwreka, the second is the monkey Anoman, the third is Mount Maenaka, the fourth is Setubanda, the fifth is Bratasena. 'Wisikan' is its meaning—the guru's instruction on five matters, showing how things come to be—their dwelling is that of the five qualities."

### 30.
"'Gulingan' means 'bed', indeed 'guling' is a sleeping person—restraining the five senses—the five that end the count. The count of five is finished: one, two, four, five. Character 'five' is complete. Character 'six' is now described—the meter changes for the sweetness of words."

### 54.
## Dhandhanggula  1.

### 54.
"'Masa' and 'rasa'—the relished meaning that is proven—are the six matters: the taste of rice and fish, the taste of seeds, the taste of leaves and herbs, the taste of dripping saps and exudations, and the taste of the six fruits. The wayang-figure Gang-Anggang—because it has six legs, that is six—becomes character 'six', the meaning of poor quality."

### 2.
"'Mangsa kanêm' truly means the sixth season—honey offered to its child, with 'rêtu' mixed in meaning, combined with the heart's desire. The first is the passion of the self, the second is the excellence of the heart, the third is the excellence of the male organ, the fourth is the excellence of the eye, the fifth is the excellence of the hand, and the sixth is the excellence of the foot."

### 3.
"'Hoyag' means its meaning is truly in motion—the trembling of the stance and the vital spirit, the hearing and the scent, the feeling of the body. Thus the meaning is quickly heard—indeed six matters—the secret whisper of the ancients, the foremost teaching, and the hearing of sweet words of advice, the praising that enchants."

### 4.
"'Namaning nênêm' means the designation of 'six'—the six fruits scattered in the garden, hanging as garden-produce, and the kucila and kirnêk fruits, the timber of mature trees, felled tree-wood, and other logs fallen crosswise on the road—all these become character 'six' for wood, such is its meaning."

### 5.
"That which is certain serves for six purposes: house-posts and house-fittings, the ornamentation of tools, the carving of images, offering-platters and beds for sweet repose—and to be burnt as the sixth. The radiance of 'six'—its meaning is the tongue's season—indeed the six tastes: sweet and spicy in meaning, and sour and bitter in meaning."

### 6.
"'Tikta' means bitter, 'kyasa' means savory, 'dura' means salty, 'sarkara' means sweet—thus character 'six' is precisely these. Now character 'seven' is described. 'Ardi' means mountain, that which stretches along the coast—therefore it becomes character 'seven', for the mountain contains seven kinds."

### 7.
"Stones, caves, ravines, cliffs, waterfalls, slopes, and vegetation—'turangga' means 'horse': its gear is seven: first the reins, then the rope and breast-strap, the crupper, the girth, and the whip—these seven. 'Giri' means great mountain, therefore it becomes the dwelling-place of sound."

### 8.
"'Rêsi' means a pure sage, he who practices holiness—counted as seven matters: first, aversion to evil; aversion to deceit; aversion to bribery; and truly, aversion to flattery. The seventh is aversion to all filth—aversion to every kind of ostentation."

### 9.
"'Angsa' means 'goose'—therefore it becomes character 'seven', gifted with rich sound. 'Biksuka' means 'cow'—therefore it becomes character 'seven', for in ancient times it served as the sage's mount. 'Aldaka' means the foot of the mountain—it has seven names indeed."

### 10.
"'Lambung', 'tepong', 'sisik'—the foot of the mountain. 'Himawan' means 'cloud', whose place is the mountain peak—therefore its character is seven. From vapor there are seven kinds: the vapor of cooling earth, the vapor of growing plants, the vapor of burning trees, the vapor of falling drizzle, and the vapor of streaming water."

### 11.
"The vapor of the censer and the vapor of incense—complete as seven. The seven sages are its meaning: the regalia of the sages—seven: the beard, the dagger, the headcloth, the robe, the shoulder-cloth, the sandals, and the staff. 'Gora' means 'thunderous voice'—indeed seven matters."

### 12.
"From the sounds of water, earth, fire, wind, thunder, and rain—from these actions come the seven. 'Swa kuda' means 'horse'—a horse moves in seven ways: prancing, spreading its forelegs, trotting, bearing its rider, sidestepping, pulling—these are its seven."

### 13.
"The side-saddled horse, the Palangki ox, the cart, the palanquin, the kisruki, and the bojadhi—complete as seven. And the sage, also seven—he who practices the seven sacred knowledges: first, the knowledge of victory; second, the knowledge of love-magic; third, the powerful knowledge of invisibility; fourth, the knowledge of etiquette."

### 14.
"Fifth, the knowledge of supervision; sixth, the knowledge of battle-calculations; seventh, the knowledge of protection—thus character 'seven' is complete. Now character 'eight' is described. The coiling of the serpent—'naga' means great snake—therefore it becomes character 'eight', possessing eight powers: spitting, biting, moving."

### 15.
"Coiling, strong, living in two places—the eight can live, from oil in its box. Its naming count—there are eight places: the year-naga and the true-naga of essence, the month-naga and the day-naga, the market-naga, the forbidden-naga, and also the naga of the place of origin."

### 16.
"'Naga dina' and 'naga pasaran' complete it. 'Salira' means 'the body incarnates'—with eight powers: it can move through water, it can move across land, it can climb and descend, toward sorrow or desire, lengthening and rejuvenating—the eighth is yielding to its own kind."

### 17.
"The gecko is its origin—character 'eight' has power. These are its eight: the clinging of its feet, feared by its kind; a patient and gentle spirit, calm in its movement; its heart's means are effective, used as medicine for one afflicted by the gecko's curse—it possesses an antidote for poison."

### 18.
"The eighth power: it can stretch itself. Now 'tanu' means 'chameleon'—it can blend eight colors: red, black, yellow, pale, green, blue, dun, and purple—complete as eight. The gecko has the power of eight colors of attachment since ancient times—it can climb walls."

### 19.
"Longevity, means, and skill—it can wag and lengthen its member. Now 'kunjara' means 'prison'—the hall where judgment is given. In ancient times, one who received punishment was sentenced for only eight days; if the offense was severe, eight months. Now 'gajah' means 'elephant' in the forest."

### 20.
"Ridden by the king—its trappings are of eight kinds: ornament, necklace, breast-strap, girth, chain, and tether—the eighth is the rider. 'Èsthi' means the elephant that is brought; 'samadya' is the elephant on the road; 'manggala' is the elephant leading the battle; 'dwipara' also means 'elephant'."

### 21.
"Carried in the king's pleasure-procession—'bujangga' means 'great serpent', equal to the naga in power. 'Bramana' means 'eight'—in ancient times in the land of Java, it was reached by the Brahmins. These eight were the ordinances of Ajisaka, commanded to teach all forms of power according to the Asthabrata."

### 22.
"'Liman' truly means 'the elephant in the enclosure', and 'ula' is the same as 'naga'—the serpent with eight powers. All those elephants also have eight as their character: first 'têbih' (distant), 'sanggama' (union), 'wangi' (fragrant), 'kopèk' (its sheath), 'lwan' (toward), 'sarosa' (strong passion), 'pramana' (vital force), the eighth is 'sugih mangrêti' (rich in understanding)."

### 23.
"Now character 'nine' is described. 'Trustha' means the notch of a weapon; 'trusthi' means the notch of a blowpipe; 'muka' means the face; 'gapura' means the king's gate; 'wiwara' means its opening; 'dwara' means its door—these doors are measured equally, they have nine divisions."

### 24.
"'Nanda' means the frog's hole; 'wilasita' means the bee's hole; 'guwa' means 'cave', a hole in the wilderness; 'rago' means 'empty hollow'; 'gatra' means the cricket's burrow; 'lèng' means the snake's hole; 'rong' means the crab's hole; 'song' means the porcupine's hole; and 'têrusan' means a tunnel—these are the meaning of the number."

### 25.
"The count of nine is complete. 'Babahan' means the gap of a thief; 'hawa sasanga' (nine winds) is its meaning—the nine breath-passages of the body indeed. Nine are manifested—thus they become its source. Character 'nine' comes only from the openings, because the body's channels—indeed there are nine."

### 26.
"The nose is like the bore of a gun, that which guides to the face. The mouth is the likeness—the gate of the king. The ears are likened to the gate of a fortress—their passage, like the door of a house. The throat is likened to the door of a cave. The anus is the jackal's gate."

### 27.
"And all the holes of animals merely follow their instinct from the season. The nine dwell in their holes. The count of that number is only nine—ten, in the same group, is zero. Its form is nothingness. As for the exposed openings—'lèng durjana' means the hole of man—the redemption of the nine winds."

### 28.
"Now character 'ten' concludes. 'Boma' means dead grass; 'sonya' means emptiness; 'gagana' means the void; 'barakan' is its meaning—that which is barely visible, far, the true interpretation. 'Windu' from ancient times—'there is and is not' is its meaning."

### 29.
"There are ten windu-cycles enumerated, called the windu of Sangkala. Ten are the colors of their ages: Tirta and Maruta, Pranala and Pratala, Baya, Byantara, Manta, Kartala—the ninth windu is Suribta, and the tenth that completes them is called the windu Gurita."

### 30.
"In the firmament is the sky's existence—all the sun and moon, the stars and the clouds, the lightning, the thunderclouds, the thunder, the wind, the rain, and the rainbow complete it. That is the existence of that realm—the form of what I perceive, that which is seen and heard."

### 31.
"'Malêtik mancurat' is its meaning—'sirna' means 'vanished', indeed gone. In the sky is its form. And all of the ten—that which is lost is its meaning. 'Walang' means 'to revert'; 'kos' means 'empty'. Character 'ten' has been interpreted: 'distant', 'indistinct', 'empty', 'barely visible'."

### 32.
"Lost is the height that was first perceived—from within the depth of the mind—truly it has no form—becoming truly dissolved—for its intent is empty. The Candrageni is finished, and one must understand the secret of the Sangkala—for it is different from the family of Kawi words and from the words of poetry."

### 33.
"'Rupa' (form) and 'warna' (color) share the same meaning. In Sangkala, 'rupa' also has its character—'warna' has four as its character—as I understand. In Kawi, their meanings are the same—but Kawi-Sangkala—its perception has two characters."

### 34.
"In Kawi, both mean 'child'—in Sangkala, they are different: 'suta' has one character, and 'yoga' has four characters. That indeed is the difference between Kawi-Sangkala and plain Kawi words. That is all my pride can accomplish—following the feelings of days long past—that which has been given words, I reverently bow."

### 55.
## Asmaradana  1.

### 55.
"The prince was entranced hearing the exposition of Kawi-sangkala. Ajar Suganda spoke softly: 'Child, I will explain the dates, from the first date up to the thirtieth—which are favorable and which are unfavorable.'"

### 2.
"The nights together with their day-names: the wuku calendar is merely a tool. From the first date, called Day Horse—good for all endeavors, because Allah Most High made it thus."

### 3.
"He created Lord Adam and Lord Muhammad on that day. If there is war, to attack is fortunate; to be attacked is most unfortunate. If there is news—"

### 4.
"Good news is truly false; bad news is true. A child born is fortunate. One who is ill recovers quickly. Hunting is quickly rewarded. The second date is called Day Deer."

### 5.
"Favorable for all endeavors, because Allah Most High created Eve. Marriage is fortunate. The third date, Day Tiger—unfavorable for all endeavors, because of Lord Adam."

### 6.
"And (Eve) revealed the secret, sent down from heaven. The fourth date, Day Cat—all endeavors are favorable, because God created Abel. The fifth date is called Day Cow—unfavorable for all endeavors, because the All-Seeing created Cain. The sixth date is called Day Buffalo—all endeavors are favorable."

### 8.
"If someone is ill, truly he will quickly recover. The seventh date is called Day Rat—all endeavors are favorable. The eighth date is called Day Spear."

### 9.
"All endeavors are favorable, because Allah Most High created Lord Noah the blessed on that day. But travel is unfavorable; if one joins battle, truly one will suffer defeat."

### 10.
"The ninth date is called Day Dog—unfavorable for all endeavors. The tenth date is called Day Serpent—favorable for all endeavors: planting and trading."

### 11.
"Marriage is also favorable. The eleventh date is called Day Flower—all endeavors are favorable. The twelfth date is called Day Blossom—buying and selling is favorable."

### 12.
"The thirteenth date is called Day Elephant—no endeavor is favorable. The fourteenth date is called Day Lion—favorable for all endeavors, because God the Great created heaven."

### 13.
"Preserved Tablet, Throne, and Footstool. The fifteenth date is called Day Fish—favorable for all endeavors, because the Most Glorious created Lord Joseph. The sixteenth date—"

### 14.
"called Night—all endeavors are unfavorable, because the All-Seeing destroyed all the people of Lord Lot at that time. The seventeenth date is called Day Eagle."

### 15.
"All endeavors are favorable; hunting soon rewards. Because the All-Seeing created Lord Moses and Lord Jacob. The eighteenth date is called Day Coconut."

### 16.
"All endeavors are favorable, because Allah Most High created the moon and sun, and the tongue of Lord Jesus, and made meet Lord Jacob and his son Lord Joseph the blessed."

### 17.
"The nineteenth date is called Day Ox—endeavors are favorable. If the news is bad, it is true; if the news is good, it is surely false. In war, one who is attacked wins; one who attacks loses."

### 18.
"The twentieth date is called Day Ghost—endeavors are favorable; in war—neither wins nor loses. If the news is good, it is true. The twenty-first date is called Day Charcoal—unfavorable for all actions."

### 19.
"Unfavorable for all actions. The twenty-second date is called Day Shrimp—favorable for all endeavors; only long travel is not favorable—one does not reach the goal. The twenty-third date—"

### 20.
"called Day Sea-Serpent—all endeavors are favorable. The twenty-fourth date is called Day Parrot—exceedingly unfavorable, because God the Most High created King Pharaoh and King Nimrod."

### 21.
"If a child is born on this day, he will be very disobedient to father and mother. The twenty-fifth date is called Day Blacksmith—unfavorable for all endeavors."

### 22.
"Because of great ill-omen. The twenty-sixth date is unfavorable for all endeavors, because the prophet's community died in the river. The twenty-seventh date is called Day Snake."

### 23.
"Favorable for all endeavors, because God Most High sends down great mercy to all His servants. A child born is favorable—devoted to God the Most High. The twenty-eighth date—"

### 24.
"called Day Rice—favorable for all endeavors. The twenty-ninth date is called Day Caterpillar—unfavorable. Finally the thirtieth date is called Day Cowry."

### 25.
"Travel is not favorable; planting is also unfavorable. Only buying and selling is favorable. Thus ends the character of dates—following the teachings of the ancients. True or not, it is as you wish."

### 26.
The prince spoke softly: "Since it is set down in the volume truly, certainly much is to be trusted. To slip at an obstacle—to attack is unfavorable, and defeat only becomes accusation."

### 27.
The story tells how the prince was long at Mandalawangi, that is Mount Gede—great was his love for Suganda. His knowledge became apparent, outer and inner with nothing left, all received by the prince.

### 28.
On a certain day the prince prepared to depart, intending to continue his journey to see the ancient palace ruins of the kingdom of Pajajaran—now called Bogor."

### 29.
"At the foot of Mount Salak," Ajar Suganda spoke, "This date, the seventeenth, is called Kapasang-yoga—all endeavors are favorable. The divination of arrival is auspicious; there is no difficulty."

### 30.
"Come, child, while it is morning—I will show the way." The prince knelt and spoke softly: "Exceeding thanks—that I am permitted to depart, shown a favorable way." All was ready, then he set out.

### 31.
Without obstacle on the road, pleasant was his journey while conversing on esoteric knowledge. Unnarrated is the time on the road—at last arriving at Gandhok village. Ajar Suganda spoke:

### 32.
"Child, allow me to return to my hermitage. Here is Gandhok—already close to Pajajaran. You may go north or west." "As you say," replied the prince, "only your blessing."

### 33.
"Yes, child, I give my consent to all your intent," following his words. Then they separated in their journey: the prince with Gathuk and Gathak passed through Gandhok village and arrived at the ruins of Bogor.

### 34.
His heart stirred at the sight of the planted trees all flourishing—many bearing ripe fruit, green and lush. None were untended; everything planted grew well—fruit trees and flowering plants.

### 35.
The air was cool and pleasant, bringing peace to the body. Rain surely fell every afternoon from noon to late afternoon, continuing through the night. Many springs and fountains flowed, adding to the heart's emotion.

### 36.
At noon the prince arrived at the middle of the marketplace, glancing about. The prince said to Gathak: "Since it will rain, it is best we take shelter in that hut."

## Dhandhanggula 1.

### 1.
Arriving at the hut, Prince Jayengresmi approached—Gathak and Gathuk behind, not far. The one inside the hut, the village head of Bogor—aged, bald, smooth-skinned—a goatherd with white-spotted skin, a neatly trimmed beard. He wore blue trousers wrapped in a sarong, a Bugis sarong, a batik headcloth of the country style—Bandung in the land of Pasundan.

### 2.
His staff long—banner below and above—shirtless, he was startled at the sight of the prince's arrival. Hastily he greeted the newcomer: "Uncle greets you—where from, young sir? And whom do you call? What is sought in your heart?" The prince spoke gently.

### 3.
"I am from the kingdom of Giri, traveling the land of Surapringga—the eastern tip of Java. My name is Jayengresmi, a true mystic, with two companions: Gathak and Gathuk. My purpose: to go to Karang. I stop at Bogor first, wishing to see the ruins of the ancient palace."

### 4.
Nodding, the seeker of news heard. Jayengresmi gently asked whom he met—still young. "What is your name?" The reply came softly: "I am Ki Wargapati, headman of the village. Indeed, Bogor is very far from Giri—coming here by land."

### 5.
"By what route did you travel to reach my dwelling? Most perilous all the way—the obstacles: many forbidden mountains, deep ravines, caves—many dangers: giant snakes, forest spirits, tangled thorny trees, many spines—tigers, rhinos, wild oxen."

### 6.
"Those who block the path are all cruel predators, with steep ravines on either side, slippery rocks jutting out at an angle—unfit to step on, rolling stones, hollows full of snakes, the cliffsides spewing forth, the snakes ferocious."

### 7.
"From where did you come walking? Hardly any level ground—the leaves are always thick, blocking the way—various and multicolored, the edges of ravines slippery. If you touch the trees—their branches jut with spines."

### 7.
"With venom."

### 8.
"The trees at the edge of the jagged cliffs—if you grab them, only bark comes away—sharp rocks follow, falling into the ravine. Snakes and tigers receive you. Apart from that, young lord—the midpoint is impassable. Hard to reach here—I have explored those mountains and am daunted, unable to go."

### 9.
Thus spoke Ki Wargapati. The prince smiled and said gently: "O uncle—truly, ease and difficulty are as the One who makes them. If inclined to ease, fear difficulty—nothing is attained. But with resolve, ease and difficulty become one—in the beginning there was nothing."

### 10.
Ki Wargapati shook his head, speaking within his heart: "Truly this is an extraordinary man." His face was cheerful, overflowing—his speech sweet and precise. His words were gentle and graceful. Softly he offered: "O you who are granted grace—please stop at my home in Bogor that you see."

### 11.
"How great the joy of my heart and all the family—as if finding gems by the handful. Whatever your desire, child—I will follow." The prince did not refuse. He descended from the hut. Ki Warga walked ahead, his staff in hand, a hand-rattle carried. When they arrived at the house:</pag>

### 12.
The hand-rattle was struck in signal—family members, male and female, came streaming without end, knowing there was a guest. They bustled about preparing offerings. The prince was invited to sit on a bamboo mat spread over sand—a Palembang style seat.

### 13.
Gathak and Gathuk were also provided for. Ki Warga spoke to the prince: "Make yourself comfortable, child." Having spoken, he withdrew and ordered his household: "Serve the guests." They came forward. Then came refreshments and coffee, snacks of palm sugar and brown sugar. Ki Warga spoke softly.

### 14.
"Forgive me, father, for offering what there is—please partake." Gathak and Gathuk set to, drinking, eating, snacking—content in heart. Then they were urged to eat the main meal: upland rice—plain or with slightly fishy-smelling tambra fish—salted fish relish with tambra.

### 15.
Tambra fried in coconut, salty fried tambra, sweet-tamarind tambra stew, pickled tambra eggs, tambra with pickled chili, salty tambra sauce.

### 15.
Tambra fried in batter, tambra floss, tambra braised in coconut milk—all dishes of tambra fish—fried sambal, raw sambal with vegetables.

### 16.
Row upon row arranged before them—ready. Ki Warga said: "Father offers this meal as it is." The prince bowed, then began: "Bismillah"—started eating. Five mouthfuls with salted tambra—about finished, just a bit left. He ate again and again.

### 17.
Ki Wargapati kept glancing, thinking in his heart: "He's been a long time—the noble one hasn't touched the food and drink. Not from difficulty—his manner seems restrained, perhaps from shyness with me." Then the meal was cleared and arranged before his two servants. Then he was urged.

### 18.
"Come, children, eat heartily—don't hesitate in your hearts—as if at your own home." Gathak and Gathuk thanked him: "Yes, we've been holding back—we're not shy—we've eaten our fill." Gathuk asked softly: "The dishes—none other than tambra fish?"

### 19.
"I really like this fish," said Wargapati. "Yes, child—that's the only kind I raise. All my ponds contain nothing but tambra—flavorful, tender, soft." Gathak and Gathuk ate until finished, then quickly cleared away as Asr prayer time approached.

### 20.
Ki Warga ordered his servants to clean the prayer house and the washing basin. Everything ready, they informed the noble one: "If you wish to pray Asr, the prayer house is prepared." The prince went quickly. They took water for ablution. Gathuk gave the call to prayer, performed the sunna prayers, then they observed the Asr prayer.

### 21.
Finished with the Asr prayer, Jayengresmi spoke gently: "Uncle, if you allow—I will not return home—I am comfortable staying here." Ki Warga replied: "Most welcome—no objections." He ordered his servants to bring food and drink.

### 22.
A white cloth spread over sand was laid out with food. Ki Warga sat before them, near Gathak and Gathuk. Gathuk asked Wargapati: "O Kyai, forgive all offense—I wish to ask: how old are you, Kyai, that you are still so strong?"

### 23.
"I am ninety-two years old, child." Gathak jumped—truly so old, yet looking still young! "What is the secret?" Ki Warga spoke kindly: "No special secret—just every day performing prayer-devotions. To this day it flows like water—truly great is its reward."

### 24.
Gathak and Gathuk said together: "Thank you, Kyai—we seek blessing, to be like you—granted long life, happy, peaceful, a full life, supported by well-being all one's days." Ki Warga spoke gently: "Yes, child—such are the prayers and your own conduct."

### 25.
Gathak and Gathuk drew near as he explained the daily prayers—seven in number, each different. The first: Friday—the practice of the Prophet, blessings and peace be upon him—he did not eat rice for a day and a night. That was his devotion. The manner thereof:</pag>

### 26.
"The recitation: 'Ya Patah ya Rajak' read 103 times nightly—the reward is God's grace. The second: Saturday—the practice of Sayidina Usman—sleepless in devotion day and night. The prayer: 'Ya Patah ya Rajak' read 103 times nightly—the reward is safety from all creatures."

### 27.
"The third: Sunday—whose practice is that of Sayidina Umar—day and night without betel chew—the prayer: 'Ya Kayu ya Kayuma'—103 times nightly—the reward is well-being all one's life. The fourth: Monday."

### 28.
"The practice of Prophet Jesus (peace be upon him)—not eating meat for a day and night. The prayer: 'Ya Rahman ya Rahim'—103 times nightly—the reward is granted by Allah for one's desires. The fifth: Tuesday—the practice of Abu Bakr."

### 29.
"Not eating nor drinking for a day and night. The prayer: 'Subkana Malikal Kudus'—103 times nightly—the reward: beloved of Allah. The sixth: Wednesday—the practice of Prophet Abraham (peace be upon him)—not eating salt."

### 30.
"For a day and night. The prayer: 'Subkana Maliki Khahar'—103 times nightly—the reward: sins forgiven by Allah Most High. The seventh: Thursday—the practice of Sayid Ali—not drinking water by night or day."

### 31.
"The prayer: 'Ya Kabiru ya Mutangal'—103 times nightly—the reward: strength for all endeavors from Allah Most High. Such are the daily prayers. Whoever performs them, their deeds are accepted by God—safe in this world and the hereafter."

### 32.
Gathak and Gathuk praised it greatly. At Maghrib, sunset, all assembled—students young and old—took water for prayer. At the prayer house they recited blessings, voices resounding. Soon the call to prayer was made. They performed the sunna prayers, then the iqama, and all prayed Maghrib.

### 33.
Jayengresmi led the prayer—his voice sweet, clear, precise—from Maghrib to Isha. When the time was complete, the hymns were sung in turns, prayers heaped upon prayers, led in harmony. After the Isha prayer, the students left the mosque. Only those who served remained—and Wargapati.

### 34.
He did not go home—pleasantly seated in the mosque with his guest. Wargapati spoke: "O child, if you permit—I wish to know: what is called the 'End Times'—has it drawn near or is it still far off?"

### 35.
Jayengresmi spoke sweetly: "That which is called the End Times—God knows best—my estimate: not yet does it show its signs in the condition of people, kingdoms, villages, coasts, or mountains—all are still without its mark. Listen, and be moved."

## Asmaradana 1.

### 1.
The words of the Prophet to his companions: "O all my companions—in the time after me, there will come a season—those who experience it will be in the End Times."

### 2.
"Its sign is the destruction of religion among all my people." Sayidina Ali asked reverently: "Lord, will I witness the End Times?" The Prophet replied:

### 3.
"None among my companions will witness it." Sayid Ali spoke softly: "I take refuge in you, Lord." The Prophet continued: "O all my companions, listen:

### 4.
"When the End Times come—of all people, only a small portion are destined for heaven, divided into seventy parts destined for hell—and they will be accursed."

### 5.
"Those who go to heaven are those who follow the religion of Islam—the five pillars: the confession of faith, prayer, fasting, alms, and the pilgrimage—that is the perfect path."

### 6.
"Those who do not follow my path are not my people. There are many signs in the End Times: there will be those in the garb of priests, dressed like true priests."

### 7.
"The words of prophets and saints are used as shields—without truth—mere gossip. They do not worship—they claim madness in knowledge—claim perfection in gnosis."

### 8.
"There are those who claim to be saints, opposing the Messenger—speaking confusedly. The sign: they are accursed by the Most Glorious God—they are mindless, filling hell."

### 9.
"Prayers are useless, the eye's guidance is lost—their speech is chaotic. Women are made companions, claiming to be trustees of the entire world—heed my teaching."

### 10.
"'Eat and sleep, this is the same as praying—there is no difference. All actions, good and evil, are no different—one has become the embodiment of the Spirit.'"

### 11.
"Those who eat without choosing—lawful and forbidden are both consumed—liquor is their drink—the belly is their god. And those who commit adultery—there is no danger for them—they claim to have clear sight."

### 12.
"'The pure and the impure are no different—earth and dog are the same.' You do not see—you are of one origin. The dog is not to be shunned—you have become the embodiment of the ocean."

### 13.
"All those who die 'saints' who claim union with the Spirit—there is no difference—you may eat anything. This destroys religion. All of them are accursed—you should avoid them."

### 14.
"Some behave like forced madmen, destroying their own bodies. Their clothing is patched—their manner like madmen, their conduct like the deranged—wearing tiger-skin garments."

### 15.
"All their actions are false—imitating those mad with love for God—like the teachings they claim are the perfection of gnosis. This is what they teach—not knowing they have become infidels—this destroys religion."

### 16.
"It happens in this age: the wicked appear as priests, the learned appear as fools, truth becomes error, the lowly become exalted, the exalted become lowly, the rich become pitied."

### 17.
"The pitied become like the rich. The king is regarded as a subject, the subject regarded as a king. Great villages become hamlets, hamlets become cities—much confusion in the End Times."

### 18.
"In this present age many commit degeneracy—men and women are forced into it—debate is what they practice, used for contention, forcing shame upon knowledge—claiming love for God."

### 19.
"What is not yet known is made a subject of debate—a cover for questioning—ashamed to study—always arrogant—nothing is believed—the knowledge that is discussed."

### 20.
"Half-baked knowledge is only visited and clung to—matched with intellect—they feel they have been opened—matched and discussed—nothing is believed—only gossip."

### 21.
"Some force clairvoyance—like one who lives in asceticism, perfecting their posture to appear calm—walking to be praised—boasting of their deeds before many."

### 22.
"In private they do not choose—lawful and forbidden are both eaten—pure and impure are both worn—they do not guard their prayers. In gatherings they appear truly devout—because of disputes."

### 23.
"Some keep their knowledge hidden—it should all be known—they take pleasure in its sweetness—what they constantly practice—therefore they hide knowledge—so its secret may be known."

### 24.
"There are those who dispute—pretending inability—to invite others' pity, then acting arrogantly. When they speak, they are truly accused—they only look away."

### 25.
"Others do not choose—lawful and forbidden are both eaten—like madmen in behavior—sitting in garbage heaps—by the roadside—this is their method—to become famous among many."

### 26.
"Much false knowledge in the End Times—people take up knowledge used as a tool. When the heart is troubled—like one truly distressed—when joyful, nothing matters."

### 27.
"Believers remain in name only—and all who are Muslim—only the sign remains—and those who recite the Quran—many are absorbed in deeds—their thoughts fixed on other things."

### 28.
"They walk praised on all sides—which serves as their weapon—money and coins—the belly becomes their guide—absorbed in pleasure—overwhelmed by desire—unceasingly fixed on the world."

### 29.
"Not one remains who chooses goodness—and those who sin are not restrained. The king is like a tiger, the judge like a lion—the hypocrite like a wild dog."

### 30.
"The poor and wretched are like goats—unable to make friends—like a bird in a cage. For the tiger and lion and wild dog surround the goat—it cannot go out to eat."

### 31.
"Thus ends, uncle, the account of the End Times." Ki Wargapati spoke softly: "Greatly do I praise your words, prince." Then came the time of Dawn prayer—all took water for ablution.

### 32.
Gathak gave the call to prayer in turns, then they performed the Dawn prayer—the prince acted as imam. Afterward, hymns and prayers heaped upon prayers—those saying 'Amin' in full accord. The sun began to rise.

### 33.
Lighting the contents of the earth, the prince spoke gently: "Forgive me greatly—I wish to continue my journey while it is morning. My heart is eager to see the ruins of the ancient palace."

### 34.
"In the time of Pajajaran of old," Ki Warga spoke softly: "Aunt would like to prepare breakfast early." The prince replied gently: "Greatly do I thank you—do not trouble yourself."

### 35.
"Continue the journey while morning," Ki Warga bowed: "As you wish—I shall follow." They left the prayer house, Ki Warga going ahead. Unnarrated the time on the road—they arrived at the ruins.

### 36.
The palace ruins of the kingdom of Pajajaran. The prince saw a pool—wide, its water clear—edged with black stone carved with flowers. The noble youth was greatly amazed. Gathak and Gathuk did not hesitate.

### 37.
The two immediately jumped in, splashing in the pool. Ki Wargapati said: "This is the relic that remains—only this pool. It is now in Comas village—a great pool of benefit."

### 38.
"People here say: if a woman is childless, both husband and wife bathe here. Each day they come—if within three days at the right time—within seven days—they will surely have a child."

### 39.
"The stone at the edge of the pool with carved flower patterns—that is the 'pupunden'—greatly revered by many. If someone has a wish they make—whatever their desire—"

### 40.
"their prayer is granted—truly fulfilled. Therefore it is famous among many. People from all lands come together, vying to make offerings to Kyai Sela Gilang."

### 41.
Now then the story tells: Prince Jayengresmi, aided by Ki Wargapati, built a hermitage on Mount Salak, so that he became famous for his charitable distribution of food. It is told that Kiageng Karang, whose name was Seh Ibrahim, was grieving because his son had left without destination. He received a vision to take Jayengresmi as a replacement for his son. Prince Jayengresmi was brought by Kiageng Karang and taught the 'ocean of mercy.' Later Gathuk and Gathak followed to Karang.

### 42.
The prince smiled and spoke softly: "Yes, uncle—it is not as it seems. The relics here of the great ones of the Buddhist age, even to this day, their power does not diminish—because it is accepted by God."

### 43.
"Since I have come and seen the ruins—uncle, allow me to continue to Mount Salak." Ki Warga replied: "O noble one—if it pleases your will—I wish to follow you to Mount Salak. Later, when you are settled in your hermitage, allow me to return home to the village."

### 44.
Jayengresmi spoke gently: "Uncle, that would be most fitting." Ki Warga then set out, preparing his children and grandchildren and family members—those skilled in the work—to make everything pleasing to the eye.

## Dhandhanggula 1.

### 1.
Ki Wargapati walked ahead with his children, grandchildren, and family—carrying their tools and gear. Arriving at the mountain, they began work building the hermitage on the western slope. The location was favorable—they chose the middle area on level ground, where water flowed down for use.

### 2.
Prince Jayengresmi with his two servants followed from the bathing place behind, accompanying the journey. Along the way they were eager, as if anxious to reach the mountain. Unnarrated is the journey—they arrived at the mountain's flank. They saw those working. Unnarrated is the time of labor—now it was complete.

### 3.
Wargapati, children, grandchildren, and family all took their leave, all permitted. Not one remained—only the prince and his two servants in deep contemplation of God Most High—unceasing night and day. Gathak and Gathuk were happy seeing the beauty of the mountain—the hermitage of the noble one.

### 4.
"Exceedingly lovely were the adornments of the mountain—firmly and earnestly constructed, complete in its perfection. Flanking two fine lakes, whose waters spread clear and pure, gleaming in the sunlight. The rounded stones were carved with flower patterns, decorated with small enchanting waterfalls. The trees bore pale yellow blossoms."

### 5.
"Arranged from below to above, opening and closing as the water washed over them, revealing the beauty of the moss. The stones were layered, carved without flaw, and true fish swam there. The hermitage was surrounded by water, small waterfalls cascading into channels of black stone."

### 6.
"The falling water scattered, spraying to the right and left of the lake, all beautifully adorned. The sedges gathered, flowers encircled them, drawn in groups, arranged and fitted—some on trellises and some in the water, truly not disappointing, bringing delight to the heart."

### 7.
"Andong, argulo of many colors, adaliyah, andul, moon orchid, moja and nagasari, cepiring, fragrant cempaka, claket, cengger, ragaina, rukem and rejasa, krandhang, sweet kalak, kanigara and kalurak, kalilika, kamuning and kanikir, kasturi and kananga."

### 8.
"Dlima, dangan, druju, dilêm, tluki, tunjung, trate, tlutur, tungkèng sungsang, soka, srigadhing, slastri, sruni, sumarsanèku, widuri and wungu, warsiki, wora-wari red, landêp, larawudhu, liandêr and larangêndhat, patragala, pudhak, pulu, purbanagri, prabu-sêt, pancasuda."

### 9.
"Pacar banyu red, yellow, white; sungsun, lamba, puthut, pacar cina; pacarkuku sweetly fragrant; sruni, sumarsanèku, widuri and wudhu, warsiki; mênur, sungsun and lamban; mêlathi, malêtuk; mandhakaki, gandapura; gambir red, dhawung and gambir white; buntut kucing red and yellow."

### 10.
"Bakung, bangah, bêbêg dwelling at a distance; têlêng, gubêt stealing fragrance; sinambêtan, angge-angge; ulêt-ulêt and inggu; adas, cêngkèh, cêguk, cabèi; rasukngin, rasamala; kêtumbar, kêmukus; kayu abang, kapulaga; klêmbak, klabêt, kadhawung and kayu lêgi; daging têgari, trawas."

### 11.
"Kayu tai, sintasari murni, sarikurung, sumba, sidawayah, sêprantu, suksmadi luwèh."
### 11.
"Widara putih, laut, waron, pala, pupulasari, pucuk pacar, jongrahap, jintên, mrica, makmur, mêsoyi, manis, maênyan, mungsi, ganthi, gêlan, garu, botor, and all the flowering plants."

### 12.
"Below, further surrounded and arranged, were trellised fruit trees of many kinds: asam, êlo and êprèh; nam-ênam, nangka, madu; crême, rukêm, randhu, kuwèni; kokosan, kêtos, kleca; kalayu, kapundhung; kacapi, kawis, kêmlaka; kopi, kluwih, dlima, durèn, tanjung, tangkil; sawo, sêmak, srikaya."

### 13.
"Sêntul, soklat, sukun and sulastri; wuni, waringin, langsêp, pijêtan; pêlêm, plênisan and pakèl; pohoncung, dhuwêt, dhuku; jêruk, jirak, jambu, mêpêki; nyamplung, manggis, malowa; mlinjo, maja, mundhu; gowok, kêdhondhong and gayam; balong, blimbing, blimbing wuluh, besaran, brit; bogêman, babanjaran."

### 14.
"Completing the fruits above, surrounding the hermitage, arranged in their places—interspersed large and small: aren, klapa, siwalan, and also jambe, jêmbar, banjaran, dhawe among them. Plantings of banana and flowers, and medicinal roots: kêncur, kunci, jae, sunthi, kunir red and white."

### 15.
"Temu lawak, epoh, temu lathi; lempuyang, kapur, emprit, dipangga; dalingo, pacing, laos; puspanyidranya, munggul; candhana fragrant, wijen glossy. The dry fields had vegetables: kacang, kara, timun; bestru, ceme, pare, ula; bligo, waluh, labu, pare, dhempel, markis—all planted on the trellises."

### 16.
"Those cultivated with delight, one after another: kapri, buncis, lobak, bit, ramenas; wortel, brambang and bawang; sledri, madinah arum; kobis, sawi, selat, kucai; andewi, patraselya—all in the garden. Lombok, terong and semangka; krai, timun, wuku, kenthang, kacang, kucir—interplanted beneath the salam tree."

### 17.
"Larasetu, sele, nalisiri; pala kesimpar, juwawut, boga; canthèl, otek, jepen, wijen; kemangi, gagang wulung. The root vegetables that followed: gadhung, walur, suweg and pohung, kimpul, bentul; uwi, gembili, gembela; linjik, tela, gembolo, nampu—ending the plantings, continuing through the clearings."

### 18.
"Beautiful to behold, the mountain's delight—the sacred power of the Truly Exalted One. All the fruits were ripe, never ceasing. It was known far and wide that a holy presence dwelt on Mount Salak, named Jayengresmi, together with his two companions, the young santri Gathuk and Gathak."

### 19.
"He distributed the bounty of the mountain without restriction—whoever wished could pick the produce. Each day people came in turn ascending the mountain to partake of the gifts, never ceasing. What was taken was never depleted; indeed it increased, spreading to other regions—yet none took without gratitude."

### 20.
"Joyful in heart, grateful to God, Jayengresmi dwelt long on Salak. Here the tale turns to one who had a hermitage on Mount Karang in Banten—Sheikh Ibrahim, called Kyageng Karang, who led a great religious school, already famous throughout the land, clear in knowledge, learned in scripture and in the various paths of religion."

### 21.
"Clear in thought without need of written page—whatever questions people asked, none were turned away. Knowledge both outward and subtle, outer and inner, perfect and true. Those who studied with him could not be counted; his santri were without number. Many who had completed their studies chose not to return home—so many remained settled at Karang."

### 22.
"Kyageng Karang was then enduring a profound sorrow. His son had gone to Java and vanished without any news. One day he heard a voice, exceedingly distinct, yet not with his ears. The voice said: 'You must accept God's will—it is ordained that your son cannot meet with you.'"

### 23.
"'But now you shall be given one who is like the image of your son. Go quickly to Mount Salak—there dwells an exceptional knight, the descendant of Susunan of Giri Prapen, whose holy power is already manifest. His name is Jayengresmi, with two santri, Gathuk and Gathak.'"

### 24.
"'You should carry him in your heart as the replacement for your son. In time you will know—your lost son is united with Jayengresmi, entered as a student, reckoned as a true sibling. They are like children of one mother and father; their spiritual attainment is nearly equal, differing only by a finger's breadth.'"

### 25.
"The voice faded. Kyageng Kawis prayed, giving thanks to God Most High, his heart overflowing with joy. After the dawn prayer, without any servant, Kyageng Kawis focused his intention on Mount Salak. Before long he had arrived—he stopped at the edge of the clearing, astonished to behold the mountain's beauty, with all its planted crops in season."

### 26.
"And the multitude of people, men and women, who were receiving the bounty, coming in turn without end, carrying it on their backs and shoulders. Kyageng Karang thought in his heart: 'The voice spoke true.' Then the noble young man, his heart without hesitation, descended at once, his two santri following behind, bowing and touching the elder's feet."

### 27.
"Kyageng Karang understood in his heart. He embraced him and said: 'O fruit of my heart, my son—how many months have you been on Mount Salak until now, that it has become so prosperous?' The prince replied softly: 'Thank you, only two months, Father. If it pleases you, I invite you to my hermitage.'"

### 28.
"Smiling, Kyageng Kawis was escorted to the hermitage. The elder spoke sweetly: 'Ah, my boy—my coming here, if it is acceptable to you, my grandson, now I will take you to my kingdom, established by God's grace, to be the replacement for your brother who has gone.' The prince bowed in homage."

### 29.
"'O exalted one, it is my duty to follow. Life or death, I submit to your will, to follow wherever you lead, with gladness of heart.' Kyageng Karang spoke again: 'Come, let us depart, my boy. As for those who remain—Gathuk and Gathak—they should not be left behind; let them come with me to Kawis.' The prince did not refuse."

### 30.
"He at once departed for Kawis and was received at the Karang court, never parted from his santri. On Mount Salak, after Jayengresmi's departure, those who had been receiving the bounty found no difference—they still met Gathuk and Gathak as if the prince were still on the mountain. Thus continued those at Karang."

### 31.
"Jayengresmi and his two servants were now gathered among all the students. Not long after, on a certain day after the midday prayer, Kyageng Kawis sat on the veranda with the noble prince. The elder spoke sweetly: 'While sitting here, my boy, I wish to hear about your journey.'"

### 32.
"'From Giri to Mount Salak—how did you travel, my boy? Tell me.' The prince replied: 'As you command,' and related everything without omission, from beginning to middle to end, to the great delight of the elder. Kyageng spoke sweetly: 'That is most excellent, and it is necessary and fitting to follow the path that leads to your well-being.'"

### 33.
"'As for all your knowledge, my son—the practices and the observances, the days and the dates, and all the measures, the good and the bad, the unfavorable and the dire, the causal links, and also the cycles of human life, the physical frame, the pulse prognostication, the kudhu-ma-bèki [divination system], and all such things—'"

### 34.
"'Good days and bad days—if you are able to choose, select the good. But if you are pressed in your journey, and you must proceed, then whether good or bad, you must go forward. Do not be anxious in your heart—surrender to the Lord. Death and life, sickness and health, good fortune and misfortune—these are not of your doing.'"

### 35.
"'That is not the work of human beings—it rests with the Ruler of the Universe, who ordains all things. Do not let your heart be troubled even a little. Float upon the power of God. Humanity is but the servant; every movement is from the help of the Lord. There is no strength or power of one's own—only the assistance of the Divine Essence.'"

### 36.
"'Float upon the power of God. The human body is like a raft upon the ocean—wherever it goes, it follows the waves of the sea. That is a simile. But as for humans, there is a saying: one who is overwhelmed by sakarat [death-agony]—that is truly every human being.'"

### 37.
"'During life in this world, each day is already sakarat, and each night too, continuously in sakarat. Awake or asleep, it is also sakarat. Therefore the name sakarat applies to people in this world. For throughout their lives, people in this world are ceaselessly sailing on the ocean of mercy.'"

### 38.
"'Sailing north, south, east, west—yet one never reaches the shore. Because the ocean is without end, no one finds land. Yet those who truly know sail but a moment and reach the shore of the boundless sea—even so, it exists.'"

### 39.
"'Consider the shore: although it is called the ocean without end, yet it seems to have a shore. This language is truly perplexing. How can this be? One may guess it in the heart, estimate approximately, but it cannot be reached by imagination—only by those who already know.'"

### 40.
"'The shore of that ocean—the ocean of mercy without waves, yet its swell rumbles. It appears calm when seen, its aspect clean and clear. Therefore those who sail without a boat think there is no danger. But if they venture into its depths, there are many sunken rocks and reefs. Hence few reach the shore.'"

### 41.
"'The shore of that ocean—because they sail without a boat, how rarely they reach the shore. Many sink, struck by reefs. Therefore be ever watchful of the dangers of the ocean of mercy. For there are many perils within that sea—hence few arrive.'"

### 42.
"'Rare indeed is the one who knows the true nature of the ocean of mercy. Yet every day and every night, people in this world are playing in that sea, within the ocean of mercy—yet few recognize it, because their sight is veiled, obscured by the many perils of life. Hence few are aware.'"

### 43.
"'If you wish to sail upon the ocean of mercy, you must have a boat, together with its rudder, and the sail must be in good condition, and the provisions for the journey must not be lacking. If not, you will not reach the shore of the ocean of mercy—instead you will encounter great difficulty.'"

### 44.
"'The boat is tranquility of heart; the rudder is firmness of resolve; the sail is purity of soul; the compass is the guidance of the true teacher; the provision is the knowledge of inner feeling; the movement of the boat is by divine grace. Throughout life in this world, one never ceases sailing on the ocean of mercy.'"

### 45.
"'Therefore a person is ignorant—the reason one is ignorant is that one does not know oneself. The true nature is emptiness. It is those who already understand, who know the shore of the ocean—truly marvelous. The station of Unity [Akadiyat], when sailing in Oneness [Wahdat], its station indeed, its departure in Singularity [Wakidiyat].'"

### 46.
"'Know further: there are four oceans. Here they are, one by one. The first is called the Ocean of Essence [Dat]. The second is called the Ocean of Attributes [Sifat]. The third is the Ocean of Actions [Af'al]. The fourth is called the Ocean of Names [Asma].'"

### 47.
"'If a person does not yet know the location of these four oceans, the place of their seeds, then that person is not yet perfect in his station—still lacking a little. Therefore seek, ask those of exalted knowledge. Do not speak idly of matters—for it is fitting for a living person to strive with effort.'"

### 48.
"'For the burden of human life is heavy. To reject or to choose—that is one's right. If one chooses evil, one becomes a person without merit. If one only chooses good, one becomes a person without feeling. One must be good—yet one shrinks from evil. If one chooses evil, one shrinks from doing good—such a person wanders astray.'"

### 49.
"'Let all strive in wisdom—that is the symbol of the ancients, so that each may understand for himself. Seek knowledge step by step, not by the command of others. Strive with reason gathered in the heart. Do not rely only on the teaching—the teaching of the personal teacher who strives—the [inner] meaning will be found.'"

## Pocung 1.

### 1.
"That, my boy, is the meaning behind all the calculations." The prince bowed. The time of midday prayer came. After the prayer, the elder went in to eat.

### 2.
"Jayengresmi's heart was content at Karang." Here the tale turns to the journey of Prince Jayengsari and his younger sister.

### 3.
The young lady, Niken Rancangkapti, still young, with the servant Buras—they escaped from Giri and came to the River Mas, boarding a boat.

### 4.
They descended from the boat and quickly continued, traveling through the forest, intending to stop at the hamlet and Islamic school at Sidacrema.

### 5.
"When they arrived at Sidacrema, the noble young man sent Ki Buras ahead. He met with Kyai Amat Sungèb, and after exchanging salutations,"

### 6.
"Amat Sungèb asked about the purpose of the journey. Ki Buras answered simply. When he heard, he was greatly startled and quickly came before the prince. Having bowed, Ki Sungèb clasped the prince's feet, weeping like a maiden, his lamentation pitiful: 'O my lord, this servant did not expect...'"

### 7.
"Jayengsari spoke sweetly: 'What is troubling you? I am merely carrying out my duty—every movement is by the power of God.'"

### 8.
"He was then invited to proceed to the dwelling. Many santri looked on, amazed at his noble appearance, and they all followed as he entered the house."

### 9.
"Jayengsari was offered a place to sit. Niken Rancangkapti went into the inner house and met Nyai Sungèb, who was overjoyed and waited on her devotedly."

### 10.
"He was entertained and honored greatly. Ki Sungèb spoke his request: 'This servant begs that you please rest here for a while.'"

### 11.
"'If any messenger comes from Surabaya seeking you, I will handle it and resist the noble one's pursuers.' The prince smiled and spoke gently:"

### 12.
"'Uncle, I am most grateful for your willingness to stand in my defense, as a return of the kindness and grace of my father toward you.'"

### 13.
"'I will follow whatever you wish.' Hearing this, Ki Amad was greatly astonished, not expecting that his words would be accepted."

### 14.
"At sunset, in the mosque, the santri were crowded, reciting blessings. The prince wished to go to the mosque with Ki Buras, while the young lady went out from the house."

### 15.
"'Brother, where are you going? I don't want to be left behind!' 'Little sister, I want to pray the Maghrib prayer at the mosque. After Isha I will return to the house.'"

### 16.
"'Just a moment, little sister, don't come along.' 'Yes, yes, brother—but make me a wedding first!' 

### 16.
'Where is my bride that you carry, Buras?'"

### 17.
"'Buras!' he shouted. 'Oh no! It was left behind when we stopped at the ravine—I forgot to bring it. What now, Buras? Go back quickly!'"

### 18.
"'My gameboard and dhakon—where are they? I demand them!' 'Oh, disaster! The dhakon and gameboard have been taken—the crabs and crayfish in the River Mas have them!'"

### 19.
"Rancangkapti stamped her feet and thrashed about. Buras was scolded, puffing and nearly crying. 'Tomorrow morning I'll replace everything.'"

### 20.
"The prince was at a loss, unable to pray at the appointed time, trying to calm his sibling, tears welling in his eyes. The young lady finally grew calm and slept in her brother's lap."

### 21.
"After Isha, Ki Sungèb came before the prince, offering refreshments. The women served, reporting that the young lady, the prince's sister like a jewel, was asleep."

### 22.
"'I have prepared a place for her when she wakes.' The prince replied: 'I am most grateful.' After eating, he was led away by Ki Buras."

### 23.
"Buras said: 'I'm still full, ma'am—I ate lunch, but because of all the hardship my stomach still feels queasy.'"

### 24.
"Ki Buras planned that at dawn they would slip away without taking leave, provisions for the journey, lest the young lady insist on bringing rice."

### 25.
"Nyai Sungèb had provided fish and rice—a cone-shaped mound of rice topped with dried fish. The women then retired into the house."

### 26.
"Buras hastily stole some fish and rice, then pretended to sleep. The prince sat watching over his younger sister as Ki Sungèb ate."

### 27.
"The woman came before him, speaking softly: 'My lord, your younger sister should sleep in the inner room, held by her aunt as a mother.'"

### 28.
"'Uncle, let her sleep here first—the child might startle, and if she wakes up cranky, it could trouble the heart.'"

### 29.
"Kyai Sungèb gave orders: 'Let there be no commotion.' All fell silent without a sound. Kyai and nyai rested and soon fell asleep."

### 30.
"As if struck by drowsiness, everyone slept—only Buras remained awake. He rose and quickly approached the prince."

### 31.
"He spoke softly: 'What is your wish, Buras? As for myself, here we have found quiet—it is not fitting to delay long.'"

### 32.
"'We will surely be discovered by the people from Surabaya.' Buras replied: 'You are right, noble sir. My heart trembles with foreboding.'"

### 33.
"'If we ask permission to leave this house, surely they will not be happy—the loss of face, everyone would follow along, and in the end they would only hinder our journey.'"

### 34.
"'Yes, that is correct, that is my thought. Buras, it is my wish—come, let us depart tonight. Now carry your burden who is already asleep.'"

### 35.
"Everything was ready. The prince departed hastily, reaching the outer gate, and succeeded without anyone knowing. Amat Sungèb and all his household slept on."

### 36.
"In the morning they looked for their guests—everyone was frantic, waking each other, searching for the visitors."

### 37.
"Utter confusion—nowhere could they be found. Let us leave Sidacrema. The journey of Prince Jayengsari continued southeast; the young man had entered the forest."

## Sinom  1.

### 1.
"The sun peeked over the mountain, and their journey paused beneath a sacred banyan tree, at the edge of a clear spring. The young lady Rancangkapti, who had been carried, now awoke: 'Buras, put me down! Where are we going? Last night we were in a village, now we're in the forest!'"

### 2.
"She got down from being carried and sat in the noble prince's lap. 'Brother, I'm not comfortable here—there's no one around, and we haven't met the elder brother we're searching for. Let's just go home! Tonight where will we sleep, and where can we buy snacks?'"

### 3.
"The prince's heart was moved. He spoke softly: 'You said you wanted to search for your brother, but now you want to go home! Later we will meet him, and then the three of us will return.' The young lady seemed reassured. 'Brother, I want to bathe first—after I've bathed, Buras can go buy snacks.'"

### 4.
"'Rice with beef satay, shrimp paste crackers!' Buras replied: 'Yes, ma'am!' He went off quickly. Under the banyan tree he finished his business, washed up, and hurried back. The young lady was still in the water. 'Buras, where are the snacks you went to buy? Did you get them, good sir?' 'Yes, miss—here, I already got them.'"

### 5.
"The prince asked softly: 'Where did you buy snacks that you returned so quickly?' Buras whispered: 'The rice was from last night—since I was still full, I didn't eat my share. The rice and dried fish—now I've made them into snacks.'"

### 6.
"The young lady had dressed and was now held in the prince's lap. Buras unwrapped the package: rice, satay, crackers, shrimp paste. 'Brother, where did you buy the snacks? Is there a warung nearby?' 'Little sister, the warung is far—yet Buras was quick.' Buras said: 'I entrusted it to Ki Maruta.' 'Maruta—what kind of person is that?' 'Oh, a wind-person.' The young lady laughed: 'The name sounds like a fabric pattern! Here, eat quickly. Buras, here's my leftover—eat, don't let it go to waste.' He opened wide, showing his teeth, and the rice and fish disappeared in one gulp."

### 7.
"(Buras pretended to hide,) the young lady hurried to get water, her heart joyful. The prince spoke gently: 'Come, let us go, my dear, while it is still morning.' They left the banyan tree. His younger sister was carried in his arms as they traveled southeast, entering the region of Pasuruan."

### 8.
"At night they rested in villages; at dawn they traveled on. At midday when the heat was intense, they paused to rest, picking wild fruits for the little one. Thus their journey continued until they reached the edge of a lake called Ranu Grati. The water was clear; crocodiles and alligators floated on the surface."

### 9.
"They drifted in rows, the lake's channels like a river with many ducks, all laying eggs on the shore. When the young lady saw this, her sadness lifted. She asked her brother: 'Brother, what animals are these? They're huge with mouths like rice-steamers!'"

### 10.
"'Look, there are small ones too!' The prince answered sweetly: 'My dear, those are called baya [crocodiles]—the slightly smaller ones are bajul, and the even smaller ones are nrete.' 'Brother, what do they want? They're all coming toward us! Do they want money, or rice, or food?'"

### 11.
"'If they could talk, what would they say?' The prince smiled: 'Yes, little sister, they cannot talk. If they could speak like humans, they would come near asking for their prey.' 'Brother, I want to know—how do they eat? What do crocodiles like to eat?'"

### 12.
"'That, my dear, is what they prey on—birds and animals that come to the water. Sometimes they also snatch people.' The young lady said to the servant: 'Buras! Don't you want to be eaten by a crocodile?' Ki Buras trembled, ran away rolling, and pretended to cry desperately."

### 13.
"The young lady laughed heartily and said: 'Buras, come back—the crocodile doesn't want you, your face looks like shrimp paste!' Ki Buras quickly returned, bringing four duck eggs which he presented to the young lady. 'Where did you get those?' 'Brother, let's go look for eggs!'"

### 14.
"The prince then walked on; the young lady happily collected the floating duck eggs along the riverbank. The sun blazed down, its heat intense. The young lady fidgeted: 'Hey, Buras, carry me! I can't stand it—up here or down there, it's all hot!'"

### 15.
"'My body feels terrible—come on, brother, let's rest first!' So they continued on until they reached a clean, sandy spot with a great tree offering shade. Monkeys were hanging everywhere, large ones in great numbers. Noticing people approaching, they gathered together, chattering noisily."

### 16.
"In the middle of this shaded spot was a spring, its water so clear it appeared blue to the eye. The young lady asked softly: 'Brother, where is this? The pool's water is blue—it comes from below. Who pays for it, brother? How great must be the water-bearer's wages!'"

### 18.
Radyan gumujêng ngandika|kagawa rupaning warih|Banyubiru ingaranan|kakang aku wêdi kuwi|sing padha ting paringis|anèng ngêpang mangkruk-mangkruk|gêdhene padha janma|nora nana nganggo tapih|ting caruwèt basane nora karuwan||

### 17.
"'Brother, are those animals or people?' The prince answered softly: 'Those are called monkeys—the one in front is the largest, called the leader, respected by all the monkeys.' 'Brother, will they eat offerings?' The prince then threw them the duck eggs, which the monkeys caught."

### 18.
"After catching the eggs, they vanished in an instant. As the sun shifted westward, they left the blue-green water. Niken was carried, circling the spring, and saw a black stone standing like a square building. On the north side was carved a Buddhist inscription."

### 19.
"After examining it, they continued their journey and entered a forest filled with flowering trees, their fruits ripe and abundant. The young lady happily picked flowers as she pleased. Buras gathered fruits: fragrant klampok, white dhuwet, kepel, banana, mangosteen, duku, golden banana."

### 20.
"The gathered fruits were wrapped in a cloth and presented to the princess and the prince. They ate together, satisfied, not feeling the passage of time. When finished, the prince spoke gently: 'Little sister, let's go now—it's getting late on the road. Will you walk, or be carried by Buras?'"

### 21.
"'Brother, I'll just walk—I'm not tired yet,' she said, picking flowers along the way. The three of them continued their journey. After an unspoken time, they arrived in the Malang region, at the foot of Mount Tengger. They heard a loud sound like the howling of a dog."

### 22.
"The prince stopped to look. From a distance he saw a waterfall cascading from the mountain into a deep ravine. The place was called Baung. The water was white like kapok being rinsed. The sound was so loud they could not hear each other speak."

### 23.
"After seeing the waterfall, they continued on. At night they slept in a village. After dawn prayer they departed again, arriving at Singasari. The prince paused his journey to see the ancient candi—a rectangular temple of black stone shaped like a mosque, beautifully carved with intricate patterns."

### 24.
"The temple was surrounded by a stone wall. The gate was like a crab-claw, with two giant guardian statues on either side, huge and holding clubs, sitting as if guarding, with crossed legs, snarling fangs bared, tongues protruding over their teeth in fierce grimaces."

### 25.
"All of solid stone without joins. Niken Rancangkapti was startled when she saw them. She asked her brother: 'Brother, why are they so frightening? Are they people or not? And what kind of house is this—all stone like a mosque?' Jayengsari smiled and answered:"

### 26.
"'Those, little sister, are stone statues. The building like a mosque is called a candi.' 'Oh, brother, let's go see! I love looking at them—it's like a beautiful wedding display! Who made them?' After viewing, they left the temple enclosure and continued on, the road very difficult."

### 27.
"The young lady was carried by Ki Buras. They arrived at the area of Sisir village. The prince stopped to look at a spring called Sanggariti. Water emerged from a stone chamber, beautifully carved. On one side flowed fresh water, cool and refreshing beyond measure; on the other side came warm water."

### 28.
"The young lady bathed, alternating between the cool and warm waters. After bathing, they continued on. Night overtook them, so they stopped at a hamlet to sleep. At dawn they departed again, and soon arrived at Tumpang village, where they saw a small, beautiful candi."

### 29.
"It was like a tomb-shrine structure, of moderate height. Below it was dry and sheltered; inside, the floor was clean sand—the remains of a hermit's ashram. Its fragrance was sweet and aromatic. The young lady asked her brother: 'Brother, what house is that? It smells like perfume, sweetly spreading.'"

### 30.
"'Is it a palace for storing fine fabrics?' 'That, little sister, is also a candi—I think it was once a hermit's meditation place, which is why it smells so sweet, from the incense smoke when they worshipped in meditation.' The young lady said: 'Brother, send someone quickly!'"

### 31.
"'Buras, take some money—three coins and a bit more, add three more gobang—tell him to buy fragrant perfume. Later when we reach home, I'll use it as gifts for the baby and for our father.' Hearing this, the prince's heart was deeply touched."

### 32.
"He was reminded of their mother and father, and of the brother who had gone away. Tears streamed down his face. Niken Rancangkapti wiped them and said: 'Brother, why are you crying? Is your stomach hungry?' 'Oh, dear sister, I'm not hungry—a jati leaf has gotten in my eye. Both eyes need wiping.'"

### 33.
"'Quick, wipe both eyes! Brother, just stay up here—Father rides an elephant, his dagger is a thin wooden sliver. Now it's over—it's clear, your tears have stopped. Buras, go inside!' He entered under the candi, and before long came out with a bubutulan [a cicada/lizard creature]."

### 34.
"'What, have you got something, Buras? That was quick!' 'Oh, miss, you should know—inside I found only emptiness. What was there was just a little creature, about the size of a finger joint.' The young lady asked: 'Doesn't it bite?' 'No, miss—the ones that bite are only nine kinds.'"

### 35.
"'Enough, Buras, don't go back.' 'Yes, my lady, I'm scared.' After examining everything, they continued their journey, stopping at the village of Kidhal. There they saw a small, beautiful candi, carved with images of various monkey wayang figures. Rancangkapti was captivated, examining them endlessly."

### 1.
"'Brother, what's that picture of the crouching figure, with branches from long ago, and what is its name? Why are their faces all the same, like a perforated cloth?'"

### 2.
"'This one has steps going up—come on, brother, let me climb up! I want to see the top!' She climbed up, held by her brother, while she kept up a stream of chatter."

### 3.
"'That candi over there has monkey images.' When they reached the top, it was a place of worship. After resting a while, they descended, the young lady carried."

### 4.
"On Mount Tengger, in the village of Tasari, they met Buyut Sudarga. Prince Jayengsari ascended Mount Tengger accompanied by Ki Buyut, viewing the crater and the sea of sand. Arriving at Ngadisari village, they met Resi Satmaka, the leader of the Buddhist religion, who explained the practices of his faith and the existence of the deities: Sambo, Brahma, Wisnu, Indra, Bayu and Kala, together with their rituals."

### 5.
"After descending, they stopped at the village. The prince slept one night; at dawn they set out again, his intention firmly fixed in his heart—the young hero's wish."

### 6.
"To Mount Tengger, hoping to meet his elder brother Jayengresmi—the length of the journey is not told. His younger sister was always held [kinanthi], and when tired, carried."

### 7.
"Northward then eastward, they arrived at the village of Pasrepan without stopping, climbing again—steep and difficult the terrain. They arrived at the village of Tasari, on the north side of Mount Tengger."

### 8.
