"'I do not yet truly know—I only follow along with the crowd. I beg you, tell me the truth of it.' Amongbudi spoke gently: 'Yes, my lad, it is far better to discuss the matter of religion, what is valid and what is invalid, than to gossip about others. Use it as a prop for sitting awhile. The things that break wudu are five:'"

### 10.
"'First: the emission from two passages—urinating, passing wind, defecating, and the like. Second: sleeping for a long time, such that one's sitting posture is not steady. Third: insanity, unconsciousness, or lying down. Fourth: touching a person of the opposite sex who is not one's mahram, whether accidentally or deliberately, making it permissible. Fifth: touching the anus or the genitals. The sunnah acts of wudu are five:'"

### 11.
"'First: washing both hands. Second: rinsing the mouth. Third: washing the nostrils. Fourth: wiping the head and the back of the neck. The obligatory acts of wudu are six matters. First: intention. Second: washing the face as is customary. Third: washing the arms—'"

### 12.
"'—including the right elbow first, then the left. Fourth: wiping part of the head. Fifth: washing the feet, including both heels—the right one first, then the left. These six must be done in order, step by step as has been explained. Do not mix up the sequence. Now, the things that break prayer are eleven matters:'"

### 13.
"'First: the breaking of one's wudu. Second: being struck by impurity. Third: uncovering one's private parts. Fourth: drinking. Fifth: eating, even a little. Sixth: speaking. Seventh: performing many extraneous actions. Eighth: turning away repeatedly from the qibla. Ninth: laughing aloud joyfully—'"

### 14.
"'Tenth: adding an extra pillar, like performing a pillar twice intentionally. Eleventh: remembering a previous bowing or i'tidal. As for the conditions for the validity of prayer, there are eight. First: knowing that the time for prayer has come. Second: being able to distinguish the obligatory from the supererogatory—'"

### 15.
"'Third: being able to differentiate the obligatory acts. Fourth: knowing the boundaries of the prayer times. Fifth: covering one's private parts. Sixth: facing the qibla. Seventh: the body being pure. Eighth: being pure also in one's clothing, place, and oneself. The pillars of prayer are eighteen in number. First: intention—'"

### 16.
"'Second: Takbiratul-ihram. Third: standing. Fourth: reciting the Fatihah. Fifth: bowing (ruku'). Sixth: tranquility (tuma'ninah) in ruku'. Seventh: i'tidal (standing up from ruku'). Eighth: tranquility in i'tidal after completion. Ninth: prostration (sujud). Tenth: tranquility in prostration.'"

### 17.
"'Eleventh: sitting between the two prostrations. Twelfth: sitting for the first tahiyyat. Thirteenth: tranquility between the two prostrations. Fourteenth: sitting for the final tahiyyat. Fifteenth: reciting blessings upon the Prophet while in the final tahiyyat. Sixteenth: order—step by step, in proper sequence.'"

### 18.
"'Seventeenth: muwalat (continuity). Eighteenth: giving the salam. Those are the valid and invalid aspects of prayer and ablution.' Buras said: 'I greatly treasure this, but I have not yet memorized it. I will recall it later and write it down.' When the night was deep, the beautiful lady went to sleep behind her elder brother."

### 19.
"Buras tossed and turned, mumbling, trying to memorize the requirements of prayer, but did not finish before he began to snore—snorting and muttering in his sleep, gurgling but it did not break his wudu. His limbs were splayed everywhere, his legs and feet twitching, his snores flickering. The prince smiled. Ki Among said gently: 'It would be best to sleep.'"

### 20.
"'Yes, uncle, for a while. You alone should rest in the house.' When Amongbudi had withdrawn, the prince sat in contemplation until the time of dawn. The lady awoke and asked: 'Brother, are you still sitting? Did you not sleep at all?' 'I only just woke up, dear sister. Brother, wake up Buras.'"

### 21.
"When he had woken, they all went to the bathing place to purify themselves, taking water for ablution. Ki Among approached and said: 'Come, young sir, it is time—let us pray Subuh together.' Sheikh Among led as imam. After the prayer was completed, they recited praises and the Subuh supplication. The leader of the prayers finished. The prince spoke softly to Sheikh Amongbudi: 'Uncle, I ask your permission—'"

### 22.
"'—to continue my journey while it is morning. I pray that you will forgive any shortcomings toward us all.' Sheikh Among replied: 'I shall accompany you with prayers for safety,' and presented provisions—fish and rice, pickled windu fish and Chinese-style peanuts—to be used as supplies should the journey be long and difficult, since the destination was uncertain."

### 23.
"A flask of coffee was also prepared. The prince said: 'I am most grateful. Uncle Among, farewell—I entrust everything to you.' Ki Buras had already taken charge. When all was ready, they quickly shook hands. The prince then departed with his sister, while Buras followed behind carrying four flasks."

### 24.
"He said: 'Truly it is a burden to carry these. If I meet anyone, I will just carry them; for sure my arms will be sore.' The lady quickly said: 'Here, just sling them over your shoulder. Let down your carrying pole.' The four flasks were then carried on Buras's back. Off they went, traveling from Bangil eastward."

### 25.
"Exceedingly difficult was the journey. The time spent on the road is not told. They had already climbed the slopes of Mount Argapura, stretching north and east. Many forest creatures were there, all tame—deer, barking deer, wild oxen—on the path, seeming to gaze with compassion upon those who journeyed."

### 26.
"Peacocks danced as if showing the way. Forest animals walked before them—monitor lizards and their young. The lady was delighted to see them, forgetting her weariness. She was entranced, watching the beauty of the trees and the multitude of forest creatures. Birds sang at nightfall. They rested in a village."

### 27.
"When they arrived at the mountain's flank, a beautiful wide view appeared. Jayengsari said gently: 'Sister, if it seems good, let us stop at that village visible yonder, to rest our bodies, and if perchance we should meet—perhaps our elder brother might be there, or if not, we can ask news of people who have attained spiritual perfection.'"

### 28.
"'Brother, that is supremely fitting.' Now the story tells of the hermit of Argapura, named Sheikh Wahdat, whose sight was not unclear—he knew that a prince and princess intended to approach the mountain. He sensed they had already arrived before him. 'Ah, welcome, Jayengsari and Rancangkapti, in my presence.'"

### 29.
"Jayengsari and Lady Rancangkapti were startled in their hearts that the sage already knew about them. They fell prostrate and spoke: 'Your command, O great yogi.' Sheikh Wahdat said gently: 'Do not be anxious in your hearts. Truly, I possess only the power of God.'"

### 30.
"'What is your purpose, my grandson, that you and your sister, a young woman, have wandered here astray? What is it that you seek?' The prince bowed and answered softly: 'Truly nothing is hidden from you regarding my person.' The sage smiled and said: 'Your words to me are correct. If it were not so—'"

### 31.
"'—there would be no need to dwell in silent seclusion. You, my young lord, and you, young lady, both rest here. When the time is right, you may depart from here. Since you have no companion, I offer you lodging according to the prompting of your hearts. By the grace of God, perhaps you will soon receive true favor.'"

### 32.
"The prince and his sister felt great longing to eat—polished rice, roast partridge, mung bean soup with dhere vegetables, thick deer jerky, celery and bean sprout salad with basil, carabikang cakes, sweet-potato fritters and timus. Buras craved plain mountain rice, white blenyik, offal pècèl with sêmanggi greens, and thick buffalo jerky."

### 33.
"The hermit said: 'My grandson and granddaughter, go into the house—eat whatever is available.' Buras stood gaping, grumbling: 'Oh dear, Kyai, what a miserly offer, when the place is so quiet, with no sign of other people—suddenly offering, but what is there to eat, the wind?' Sheikh Wahdat smiled and said:"

### 34.
"'Buras, go quickly to the kitchen. See if it is ready. Report to the prince, your master.' Ki Buras bowed and withdrew. When he entered the kitchen, rice, fish, and various dishes were already laid out beautifully arranged—the containers and the setting astonished him, since he had been longing to eat mountain rice."

### 35.
"Whatever his heart desired was fully provided. He was utterly amazed, gaping left and right, suddenly overcome with awe. He scrambled back and knelt, reporting to the prince: 'Master, your command—the young lady and your lordship are summoned to eat. In the kitchen it is already prepared. It is I who arranged it.'"

### 36.
"'Young lord and young lady, eat first. Let Buras alone serve you.' When the three had withdrawn and arrived at the house, they were greatly amazed at the wondrous power of the perfected one. Ki Buras chattered: 'I am utterly astounded. There was mountain rice, white blenyik, jerky, offal pècèl with sêmanggi—just as I had imagined.'"

### 37.
"Exceedingly delicious and beneficial was the food. Buras cleared away everything—not a scrap remained. All were satisfied. They returned to attend the yogi. During their stay at Argapura, they experienced only delight and benefit—the heart stilled, facing the remembrance of God, becoming enraptured in the Spirit."

### 1.
## Asmaradana 1.

### 1.
"Jayengsari and Rancangkapti—before long it was as if they had become his own children. Sheikh Wahdat said to the lady: 'O daughter, my child, know the Great God—that is the obligation of every servant.'"

### 2.
"'Know the Essence of God, His Attributes and His Names, and also His Actions. The existence of God is One. There is none who created Him. No color, no form can be seen. And the One is the Acting of the Spirit.'"

### 3.
Nganakakên bumi langit|sabda Kun sampun gumêlar|saisining jagad kabèh|ni rara matur ing rama|ing pundi (ng)gèn uninga|kang manah dèrèng dumunung|pae ananing kawula||

### 4.
Amba sampun ngudanèni|mênggah Dat Sipating Allah|Asma lawan Apêngale|ing pundi margane wikan|suka sang maha tapa|myarsa ature kang sunu|aris wijiling wacana||

### 5.
"'It is the obligation of every living person, once one has reached adulthood, to know the All-Seeing God: what is obligatory for Him and what is impossible, and also what is permissible—that is, the Twenty Attributes. That is the path by which you may see clearly.'"

### 6.
"'The first attribute is Wujud (Existence). The meaning of Wujud is that His existence is certain. The existence of the Lord—this is the Sifat Nafsiyah, only one, called Wujud. The second is the attribute Qidam (Eternity without beginning).'"

### 7.
"'The meaning of Qidam is pre-eternity. There is nothing that precedes Him. There is no before or beginning. Before the creation of heaven and earth, the void—the Preserved Tablet and the Pen did not yet exist—'"

### 8.
"'—nor the Throne nor the Footstool. Allah Ta'ala already existed. That is Qidam in its essence. These are called the Sifat Salbiyah, five in number. Baqa' (Eternity) means everlasting: the existence of God without end.'"

### 9.
"'Though heaven and earth perish, the existence of God does not change. That is the third of the Salbiyah: Mukhalafah (Difference), meaning unlike anything created—the existence of God Most High is different from all that is new.'"

### 10.
"'The existence of created things comes from non-existence into being. If everything is destroyed, they all return to nothing. All created things are distinct from God the Most High, whose existence has no end.'"

### 11.
"'Wa Kiyamuhu Binafsihi (Self-Subsistence): the meaning is that the existence of Allah stands by His own existence, without comparison to any created thing. The existence and the act—the fifth of the Salbiyah is called Wahdaniyah (Oneness).'"

### 12.
"'The meaning of Wahdaniyah: the Essence of Allah is One. His Attributes and His Actions—the Lord is One. The meaning of the Oneness of Essence: God is not compounded. By the Oneness of His Actions—'"

### 13.
"'—He creates the earth and heaven and all their contents. His creation is not double; it is impossible that it could be doubled. His existence has no direction, no place below or above, and cannot be encompassed by the mind.'"

### 14.
"'Complete are the five Salbiyah attributes, together with the Nafsiyah. There are more attributes of God: the Sifat Ma'ani, seven in number. The first is Qudrat (Power).'"

### 15.
"'The Qudrat of God the All-Wise—the meaning of Qudrat is Power: to bring into being what does not exist, to annihilate what exists. And the attribute Iradah (Will): the meaning is the Will of God Most High, to bring into being all created things.'"

### 16.
"'All creatures are determined, great and small, narrow and wide, by the Iradah of the All-Seeing. There is another attribute of God: Ilmu (Knowledge) is the name of the attribute, knowing the meaning of all knowledge, seeing without obscurity.'"

### 17.
"'However many veils there may be, the innermost thoughts of creatures are not hidden—Allah knows all. And yet another attribute: Hayat (Life). Its meaning is life: the life of God Most High, life without a soul.'"

### 18.
"'All created things—their life together with a soul—are all subject to death. Allah cannot die. And the attribute Samak (Hearing): its meaning is the Hearing of the Lord.'"

### 19.
"'He hears without obscurity within Himself, without a veil, He hears. The attribute Basar (Sight): in its meaning, Allah sees all the universe, not with eyes—nothing in creation is hidden from Him. That is the Basar of the Lord.'"

### 20.
"'Those are the Sifat Ma'ani, complete at seven. And the attribute Kalam (Speech): its meaning is Allah Ta'ala speaks, without the instrument of lips. The twenty attributes are now complete. The next are called Sifat Maknawiyah.'"

### 21.
"'These correspond to the Sifat Ma'ani, seven in number, fixed and certain: the attribute of being Qadiran (All-Powerful) is the first; Muridan (All-Willing) the third; 'Aliman (All-Knowing) the fourth; the attribute of being Hayyan (Ever-Living) the fifth; Sami'an (All-Hearing) the sixth; Basiran (All-Seeing) the seventh—'"

### 22.
"'—and Mutakalliman (All-Speaking) completes the set, completing the twenty. Only the attribute of being Hayyan (Living) has no opposite. It is not subject to anything else, nor can it be a cause of anything. All the other attributes depend upon the attribute of Life.'"

### 23.
"'Hayyun (Living) means the Living One—that is the Sifat Maknawiyah. Thus the twenty are complete. All the attributes that have been mentioned are the very being of Allah. These are the Twenty Attributes, my daughter—know them well.'"

### 24.
"'And know also the opposite of each of the twenty attributes—the opposite of each. What is obligatory and what is impossible for the existence of Allah. Most Holy is His name, without beginning and without end.'"

### 25.
"'The opposite of each of the twenty attributes is the impossibility of Allah: that He should be non-existent—that is impossible—or that He should be like created things. The sign of Allah the Most High is the display of the universe.'"

### 26.
"'The opposite of the Sifat Ma'ani is clearly seen in the Maknawiyah. Be watchful with the tasbih as your proof. All the attributes mentioned must not be questioned in their certainty. This is called Taqlid (imitative belief)—knowledge of the Lord.'"

### 27.
"'The meaning of the word Taqlid is imitating the words of others, not distinguishing for oneself, merely following reports. Its certainty is not firm. Such taqlid is called superficial—the understanding is ruined.'"

### 28.
"'Be meticulous, my daughter. Do not tread the path of the Qadariyah, nor the Jabariyah. Those two paths are both obscure, flanking you on both sides. The Sunni path—you must avoid both extremes.'"

### 29.
"'The Qadariyah when they speak say: "My existence is from the Lord, created by the All-Seeing God, and so is my power—all my actions are my own doing; I have no power of my own, only the Lord who acts."'"

### 30.
"'When the Sunni speaks, he says: "My existence is from the Lord—He created me—and so is my power. Yet my strength is also granted, linked together with the will of Allah."'"

### 31.
"'The attribute that enters into the Nafsiyah category is only one: Wujud. Those that enter into the Salbiyah are five: the attribute of Qidam is the first, the second is Baqa'—'"

### 32.
"'—third is Mukhalafatu lil-kawadis, fourth is Qiyamuhu binafsihi, and Wahdaniyah completes the five. Those that enter into the Ma'ani are seven: Qudrat and Iradah—'"

### 33.
"'—third is Ilmu, fourth is Hayat, Samak, Basar, and Kalam. Likewise those that enter into the Maknawiyah are also seven: being Qadiran is the second, Muridan is third, 'Aliman fourth—'"

### 34.
"'—being Hayyan is the fourth, being Sami'an is the fifth, being Basiran is the sixth, being Mutakalliman is the seventh.' The sage was pleased and finally said gently: 'Yes, that is correct, my daughter.'"

### 35.
"After being taught knowledge by her spiritual father, the beautiful lady went out to amuse herself as her occupation, wandering about being praised by her father. The one and only princess—thus the jewel of beauty—spoke again to her father:"

### 36.
"Lady Rancangkapti approached her father, presenting flowers with grace, skilled in her manners, sitting before her father, the great hermit of Argapura. His speech was gentle: 'My child, daughter of your father—'"

### 37.
"'Where have you been? Three days you have come and gone, returning each time. Each day you obtained flowers: red argulo, sweet-scented white blossoms, lotus, tutur flowers, jalamprang, and naga puspita.'"

### 38.
"The lady bowed and answered softly: 'I have been wandering in the forest, to the hillocks, caves, and lakes—my heart delighting in seeing the flowers, their sweet fragrance wafting. Some were still buds, awaiting the release of their scent.'"

### 39.
"Now the story tells of the young prince: day and night he remained in the presence of the sage, eating, sleeping, until he forgot himself. The hermit was pleased in his heart: the young lord's knowledge had taken root, cutting off all passion for anything but the Real Lord."

### 40.
"Jayengsari and Rancangkapti knelt before the sage, their eyes appearing as if they wept, like the moon eclipsed. When the sage saw the expression of those who knelt before him, sweet words issued from his lips:"

### 41.
"'Ah, my two children, at dawn you must depart for Mount Rawun. After some days there on Mount Rawun, then proceed to Banyuarum. Later you will meet a ship captain—'"

### 42.
"'—from the city of Pekalongan. If he takes you aboard, just follow him. When you arrive at Pekalongan, the captain will die. Then give away all his possessions in charity—'"

### 43.
"'—to the poor, the destitute, and orphans. Keep some for yourselves as sufficient provisions. Then go to Mount Parahu. Continue on to Mount Bisma. There is a great kingdom there, named Sokayasa.'"

### 44.
"'The lord of Sokayasa, my grandson, is already called a Waliyullah, clear and penetrating his sight; his title is Sheikh Akhadiyat. He has a child, only one, a son, handsome in appearance, radiant like Narayana.'"

### 45.
"'He is called Ki Mas Cebolang. He is now wandering, destined for greatness in truth, currently experiencing humble trials. When he is opened, granted remembrance of God Most High, Cebolang will return to himself.'"

### 46.
"'Serve him, my grandson, and let your sister accompany you. Follow his words. When the time draws near, you will meet your elder brother. When you have settled, at Wanataka you will find him.'"

### 47.
"'Young lord, the time has come. The two of you, depart. I pray for your safety, my child—safety in your origin and destination. May you find happiness and glory.' They both gave thanks, bowed, and withdrew from his presence."

### 74.
## Maskumambang  1.

### 1.
"Immediately, like gold emerging from the water, their journey was released—there was no danger on the path, free from hunger, weariness, or fatigue."

### 2.
"The hearts of both were firm, trusting in God. There was no anxiety at all; they surrendered wholly to God, their bodies acting as if resigned."

### 3.
"On the sea they followed the waves of the ocean, so that they did not feel as if their own power was at work—all was the power of Allah."

### 4.
"And accompanied by the blessing of the revered sage, they traveled swiftly, soon arriving at Mount Rawun, halting at the edge of a lake."

### 4.
"Section 5: On Mount Rawun they met a female hermit named Retna Tan Timbangsih, who explained the Hadith Markum, which describes the depiction of the four passions at war, finally the Mutmainah passion emerging victorious... pages 265-281."

### 5.
"Astonished, they gazed at the clear water—the Supitan Sea, with Bali clearly visible, sailing ships flickering in the distance."

### 6.
"Kingdoms, villages, settlements, and rice fields—dry fields and gardens, graveyards at the river's edge—were visible from the lakeshore."

### 7.
"Canals and streams appeared clearly, with water descending in channels from the lake, like jeweled serpents gliding."

### 8.
"Ahead, it became a wide river dividing the rice fields and dry fields; there was no shortage of water, so all the crops—"

### 9.
"—grew lush, green leaves tender and glistening. Flowers were all blooming. Fruit trees were laden with fruit, giving a charming appearance."

### 10.
"From the summit of Mount Rawun, the settlements were visible, surrounded by lovely trees—white champaka and sandalwood."

### 11.
"The winding path was like a serpent coiled around the trees. Along both sides of the road, the left and right, were planted flowers of many colors."

### 12.
"Beside the flowers ran small streams lined with stones—black, white, and red—running alongside the path."

### 13.
"Birds of all kinds mingled and perched: salindhitan, bethèt, rang-urangan of five colors, drêkuku, dêruk, sêpahan—"

### 14.
"—mynas, starlings, turtle-doves, grey-white pigeons, perukut doves on branches—the penduduk tree drooped, glistening—the manyura danced with tail feathers fanned out."

### 15.
"Rancangkapti said to her handsome elder brother: 'Brother, look at what is visible—the peak of this mountain—its shape is like a settlement.'"

### 16.
"'Surely this is the path going upward, winding around the mountain, with a flat area in the middle, clean and clear, adorned with flowers.'"

### 17.
"'Little streams lined with stones of three colors, their water like a mirage. Come, brother, let us climb up—there must be a village above.'"

### 18.
"They all climbed the slopes of the mountain, Buras in the rear, grumbling with a happy heart, a sign of his clear intent."

### 19.
"When they arrived at the peak, they stopped outside a fence of flowers—red, yellow, and white argulo, green, purple, and pink."

### 20.
"Interspersed were white kenanga and green flowers. Outside the enclosure was a fence of black stone. The gateway was shaped like a four-way junction."

### 21.
"The courtyard was paved with glossy black stone tiles, bordered all around with star-shaped stones the size of kemiri nuts, evenly arranged."

### 22.
"A floating pavilion stood in the middle, shaped like a shrine. The path led straight to the gateway, stretching as a pebbled path."

### 23.
"Woven bamboo fences were entwined with limarkatangi creepers, their tips fixed in the earth, leaves sprouting and spreading."

### 24.
"The path traversed a ridge with no vegetation—only on the left and right were there shoots appearing, about a cubit high."

### 25.
"A pool surrounded it like a shrine, filled with fish—guramèh and red tambra—and monitor lizards, as if delightfully tame."

### 26.
"Now is told the dweller of the hermitage in the middle of the pool—a widow, her face clean and radiant, her glow merging with the moonlight."

### 27.
"Lady Sukci Wahdat without ceremony, a female saint, the beauty of the world, Lady Tan Timbangsih—she knew of their arrival."

### 28.
"She emerged from the floating pavilion, her skirt flowing softly, her shoulder-cloth of green jasmine, her hair-ribbon yellow-based silk."

### 29.
"She waved and spoke sweetly: 'Ah, my children, do not linger outside—come inside at once.'"

### 30.
"Those addressed were startled and quickly entered. Upon arriving before her, they kissed her feet one after another. After kissing her feet—"

### 31.
"Rancangkapti was led by the hand by the great female yogi. The prince followed behind with Ki Buras the santri. They entered the hermitage."

### 32.
"Eight low buildings and four storehouses were there, with mats spread on the verandas—prangwadani cloth in four colors covering half the veranda."

### 33.
"Stone seats of candhani stone were carved with flower tendrils, the lower part hollow like a chair, round, divided into four sections."

### 34.
"Facing the gate that led outside, there were four side-buildings, carved with fern scrollwork, with armrests of polished, glossy concaves."

### 35.
"Above, the ceiling was decorated with leaves and flowers, on the rafters crawling with carvings, at the peak a dhandhang bird with fanned tail."

### 1.
## Dhandhanggula 1.

### 1.
"Inside the hall were four buildings: the northeast one used red stone, all its ornaments red; the northwest one used white stone, all its ornaments white; the southwest one had yellow as its base, all ornaments yellow; the southeast one used black stone, all ornaments black."

### 2.
"The four buildings shared a single partition wall. In the middle, the openings formed a cross, all the doors shifted toward the front veranda. Each one had a back wall, carved with scrollwork and beautiful flowers. The doorjambs left and right were shaped like a dragon coiled around a tree, reaching for a bird amid fruit-bearing branches."

### 3.
"Unceasing was the ornamental carving. The hermitage of the female hermit Sukci... the prince and his sister and the three of them with Buras were speechless, greatly astonished in their hearts, shaking their heads in wonder. Lady Sukci had already sat down, facing east in the middle of the lovely veranda. The three stood on the mat."

### 4.
"Tan Timbangsih spoke sweetly: 'My young lord and lady, I make your acquaintance—I welcome you both. From where did you start? Arriving here, what do you seek? The place is lonely, silent, desolate, and exceedingly dangerous. On the road there are many perils, and the terrain is rough, full of bandits. Who guided you?'"

### 5.
"The prince answered with reverence: 'O my lord, beloved of the Spirit, I speak truly: my name is Jayengsari, my younger sister is Rancangkapti, our companion is named Buras. We are from Surengkewuh, the realm of Giri palace, seeking our elder brother who is lost, named Jayengresmi.'"

### 6.
"'We wandered until we reached Argapura, where we met Sheikh Wahdat—he who gave guidance to his servant. The words of that revered one brought us easily to you here.' The female hermit said: 'Sheikh Wahdat—he is truly my elder brother. In knowledge, we are of one milk and one faith, united. Lady Sukci and Sheikh Wahdat—'"

### 7.
"'Young lord and lady, consider yourselves as if you were at Argapura.' Hearing this, the prince affirmed with deep gratitude the words of the great lady. Now the story tells that every day the lady felt great joy, and the female hermit sat by the edge of the floating pavilion—"

### 8.
"'—providing food: guramèh fish and red tambra.' Jayengsari and Rancangkapti knelt before her. The female hermit said gently: 'Young lord and lady, I will tell you the story of my own path—how I withdrew into solitude, not looking back at family or relatives. From the beginning, I read and pondered the hadith called Kadis Markus Baslam.'"

### 9.
"'I read it over and over, pondering its meaning, until I was overcome with emotion—like a blossoming flower that has reached full maturity in ceremony. In the end, my understanding led me to nothing but solitude.' The prince bowed and asked: 'What is the story of that hadith? It must be deeply meaningful.'"

### 10.
"Rancangkapti spoke with reverence: 'If it pleases you, may it be permitted to my heart—please tell the whole story, everything that is mentioned in that hadith.' The female hermit smiled and said: 'Daughter, as you request—the story of Markum Baslam I shall begin from the very origin of the writing. Listen well.'"

### 11.
"'Hadha bayanul bihainati, ya ayu halladina amana, wan sikhan waman bae, kayat luamanhu, wabil umaratu khayati, waman supiya tukha, waina kayatu, wawimutmaina tukha, sapinati khamil saratu albahri, kharijul pilbansukha.'"

### 12.
"'Now, there is a story narrated, the words of the Hadith Markum Baslam, as a charm to become a reminder, knowledge of signs and examples: the passions (nafs) that are spoken of—the blaming nafs (Luamah), the life of the angry nafs (Amarah), the life of the lustful nafs (Supiyah), and the peaceful nafs (Mutmainah).'"

### 13.
"'King Mutmainah is the one described, titled Lord Dyatmika, named Suksma Mulya. So states the revelation: his kingdom is Lali-muslimin (Forgetfulness of the Faithful). He has not a single soldier—yet his supernatural power is supreme; he has the power to rule the world. None can oppose him—all the world surrenders in submission.'"

### 14.
"'Only by thought does he have armies—in an instant, troops without number. Such is the power of Mutmainah. When he has no desire, he possesses not a single soldier—he grants only guidance. Thus is it told. Now let the story turn: there were three exceedingly mighty kings, all of fierce power.'"

### 15.
"'The first king was called Maharaja Luamah. King Amarah was the second. The youngest was Queen Supiyah, a woman—valiant, supernaturally powerful, subtle in bringing about error. All had countless armies, gathered from the three corners of the earth and the edges of the sea.'"

### 16.
"'The three kings emerged, holding court, and all their mighty troops marched forth, arrogant and threatening—glaring, mumbling, grumbling. The prime minister was Patih Mangkubilai, with Tumenggung Kamandaka and Arya Pangridhu. Panji Wirajail paid homage, Rangga Wirakemat, Rangga Waningisin, and Rangga Tisnacidra.'"

### 17.
"'Angabei Surawirang attended court; Angabei Kiyanat paid homage; Angabei Plenya-plenye, Angabei Tebel-ngempluk, Angabei Rucibesiwit. Ki Demang Doyanmangan (Loves-to-eat), Demang Siptaturu (Instant-sleep), Kyai Demang Antukan (Bump-into), Ki Demang Trocoh (Dribble), Demang Sregep-ngisin (Eager-to-embarrass), Ki Lurah Dhososugal (Malicious-gossip).'"

### 18.
"'Lurah Ras-arasen-ngebluk (Half-hearted-thickhead) attended court; Lurah Tan-graita (Mindless) paid homage; Kyai Lurah Kalalen (Forgetful). All were before the king, in full assembly paying homage. Sri Maharaja Luamah spoke to his younger brother King Amarah and to all the assembled lords: "What is your intention—'"

### 19.
"'—regarding the plan to wage war and destroy King Mutmainah? It must be crushed, made to vanish. Let there be none who see him—no one who can challenge my bravery." The younger brother replied: "If it is the king's will, let us attack immediately, strike with courage, and cut off the root completely."'"

### 20.
"'All counsel was complete—King Luamah had reached a decision. "Since the deliberation is thus, let it be swiftly carried out—do not let our intention become known. Younger brother King Amarah, with all your troops, and also your younger sister Queen Supiyah, do not stay behind—march out your armies."'"

### 21.
"'Then King Amarah spoke to his prime minister Angkaramurka (Evil-oppression), ordering him to depart with all his troops. They emerged from their kingdom—his dwelling is in the liver. In truth, the liver means the spleen. His power is anger in every action—that is the nature of Amarah.'"

### 22.
"'Queen Supiyah gave orders to her prime minister Nyai Jajawatan (Department-lady), Nyai Tumenggung Nyalereng (Slanted), Nyai Arya Guyu-guyu (Laughter), Nyai Kinincangalis (Sweet-faced), Nyai Rangga Eseman (Charming), Nyai Adusemu (Facial-wash), Nyai Parikan (Jest-maker), Nyai Dhepel (Clinging), Nyai Weweh-wangi-wangi (Scent-giver), Nyai Gregeten-nekat (Irritable-persistent).'"

### 23.
"'They issued from the inner heart—that is the true spleen. And its power is desire for anything one wishes, longing, yearning with intense craving. Then King Luamah quickly summoned his prime minister Mengkucilaka with his troops, tumenggung, arya, ngabe—the army swarmed countless.'"

### 24.
"'Now they emerged from the literal heart. The true meaning of the spleen is the heart itself. Its power concerns the desire to eat—whatever is eaten, the intensity of appetite, forgetfulness in every action. The appearance of Luamah—its radiance—and that of the nafs Amarah—'"

### 25.
"'—its radiance is extremely red. And the nafs Supiyah—its radiance is yellow in color. These three passions defile the pure consciousness. As for purity—that is the good passion, the nafs Mutmainah. Its dwelling is in the inner heart—that is the perfect heart.'"

### 26.
"'Its radiance is brilliantly white. Its power is patience and acceptance, tranquility, calmness, trust in God, beautiful faith. This is the one that would be attacked by King Luamah and all his armies. Roaring, filling the world, they advanced by three paths, the march of King Luamah's forces.'"

### 27.
"'They emerged from the mouth, flowing forth—full and loud was their sound. King Amarah's throat roared in the ears, pouring out, surging, the troops gasping and pressing forward—swarming recklessly, charging without listening. As for Queen Supiyah—she emerged from the eyes, with a drooping, captivating gaze, suddenly striking.'"

### 28.
"'Swiftly they arrived at the inner heart, immediately besieging it, surrounding it open-mouthed, pressing in until all was contained. Those who became the champions were the three female prime ministers, valiant and supernaturally powerful, deadly in battle—their nature is to destroy religion, capable of erasing good deeds, undermining ascetic practice.'"

### 29.
"'They cause good works to be forgotten. Therefore many people fall into evil, into their own bad behavior—because of passion, which becomes the cause of calamity. The power of Luamah and Amarah and the supernatural might of Supiyah—every day they obstruct good deeds, allowing no safety.'"

### 30.
"'Now the forces were arrayed for the contest of mounts. King Mutmainah saw the enemy approaching—he stretched and arched his back. Then he summoned a vast army: lords and officials presented themselves before him, arranged in ranks. Kyana Patih Sabar-maklum (Patient-understanding) stood at the front, with Tumenggung Tumaninah (Tranquility).'"

### 31.
"'Arya Prasaja (Simple), Arya Berbudi (Virtuous), Ngabei Mantep-temen-ingkarsa (Firm-sincere-in-intent), Ngabei Liladanane (Generous), Rangga Eklas (Sincere) in front, Rangga Tawakal (Trust-in-God) and Demang Sidik (Honest), Demang Musawarat (Consultation), Demang Tapakur (Contemplation)—all were complete before him. King Mutmainah spoke to his troops: "Hey—'""

### 32.
"'"—what do you think of this? There is an enemy without number, destroying the righteous heart." Ki Patih Sabar-maklum answered: "Yes, if it is to be—what is offered, let it be accepted. It would be best to meet them in battle." The king said: "Yes, well then—fight against the infidels."'"

### 33.
"'Then the army marched forth gently from the inner heart, emerging through the nose as their passage—white in radiance. They arrived to meet the opposing forces. They faced each other, and then the battle was joined—fighting in matched pairs: lords against lords, troops against troops.'"

### 34.
"'Mangkublai (Disaster-bringer) against Rahayu-budi (Well-being-wisdom); Angkaramurka (Evil-oppression) against Tumaninah (Tranquility); Siptaturu (Instant-sleep) against Betah-mlek (Wakeful-persistent);'"

## rame pupuh pinupuh |

### 34.
Doyan mangan lan Bêtah-ngêlih|pun Jail lan Prasaja|pan pêluk-pinêluk|Ki Srèi mungsuhan Sabar|ki Jubriakibir lan ki Sarèhjurit|rame abandhayuda||

### 35.
### 35.
"The battle alternated back and forth, raging day after day—neither side prevailed. King Luamah saw his troops fighting a battle without end, with no victory or defeat. Swiftly King Luamah summoned the shock troops of war: giant demon warriors."

### 36.
"Countless demons without number: the great Drembadursila, Gugur-tingkah (Fall-behavior), Berung-bregudung (Snorting-bellowing), Deksura (Fierce-voice), Tobat-ngelih (Repent-hungry), Bingung (Confused) attacking wildly, Geragas-nguthuh (Scratching-bald), Kalana Gas-egasan (Restless-hasty), Besono (Poisonous), Denggleng-ora-eling (Staggering-unmindful), Ngluka ngawut wuta (Snatching blindly)."

### 37.
"Swiftly they were ordered into battle by King Luamah's command. 'Attack them one by one! Besiege them with two, three, four at once so they die quickly! Let them all vanish—let none remain! That beast called Safety—it is not right that it should act arrogantly, obstructing and causing harm.'"

### 38.
"Quickly they pressed forward in battle. The giant passions rushed in with fierce, cruel onslaughts—attacking by sixes, sevens, eights—crowding, filling, overflowing, swelling, swaggering. The world shrank, devoured by the demons. The army of purity was routed—wiped out entirely."

### 39.
"The army of purity was devoured, becoming food for the demons. The demons were satisfied, cheering with relief, nodding contentedly—not a single soldier of purity was to be seen. Only their king remained, still standing alone. King Mutmainah, seeing his army utterly destroyed, took refuge in silent contemplation."

### 1.
## Sinom 1.

### 1.
"There is a saying of the scholars, the authoritative ones among the teachers: the seed of passion is what is called budi (inner nature). Budi comes into being from the martabat Akhadiyah in the unseen realm of Divinity—the Latakayun in the Nukat Gaib. Budi serves as the veil of God."

### 2.
"Therefore it is clear: Wujudullah, Dzatullah—in the duality-within-unity of human and budi. Budi is the Kalamullah (God's Speech); the human body is the messenger (rasul). Whoever understands this knows that human and budi are one—there is no difference in power and authority over creation."

### 3.
### 3.
"'Struggling within, struggling without—human and budi are like gold and copper mixed and hammered in a crucible; both names disappear, becoming suwasa (an alloy). Therefore human and budi are the same. Budi is the form of the four passions.'"

### 4.
"'These four passions are the true form of humanity. Look carefully, human, at budi. If you are mistaken, budi of Luamah will envelop you—it can no longer be remedied, filling you with evil character, certainly not improving, becoming depraved.'"

### 5.
### 5.
"'If your perception is sharp, you will attain the excellence of budi—budi that envelops Mutmainah. That is being near to God, attaining well-being, a body of goodness that cannot be harmed—that is the realm of God. Yet knowledge alone is not yet simple.'"

### 6.
"'Therefore it is constantly adorned, so that knowledge may shine forth—faith that has been accepted, in speech kept secret—honor, benefit, respect in knowledge, so that understanding may be released, the depth of budi realized, continually in muka wahdat, answering and teaching.'"

### 7.
"'According to the saying, "Wallahu a'lam" (God knows best)—let us return to the story. King Mutmainah, after the destruction of his army, made a vow: "If humanity must be good, may it be strengthened to face enemies as numerous as the sky. If humanity must be brought to ruin—'""

### 8.
"'—surely it will lose the battle, unable to withstand what is destined. Heaven is certain for those whose budi is sharp, who receive true grace—they will surely be able to face the enemy, not counting pain or death. A person dies while still alive—this cannot be changed, it was destined from the beginning.'"

### 9.
### 9.
"'God creates evil fortune and good fortune—which do you choose? Yet all is already determined: who shall win in battle and who shall lose. That belongs to God—the reward of humanity. It is simply so—do not let humanity be ignorant.'"

### 10.
"'Thus the prayer was received by God: "Strengthen me for battle—do not let me fear death. Feel the battle! Be steadfast as king—king of the Imam leaders—strong in the true battle!" King Mutmainah swiftly joined the fray.'"

### 11.
### 11.
"'He donned noble garments: the robe of Utama-ing-ilmu (Highest-knowledge), the headdress of Sharia-path, the belt of Eneng-ing-puji (Love-of-praise), the kris Sempana-sidik (Accurate-omen), the spear Takbir-ikram (Magnification-honor), the sash Percaya-ing-rat (Trust-in-the-world), the trousers Tapakur-kapti (Contemplative-intent), the shoes Andoning-cermin-paesan (Mirror-of-countenance).'"

### 12.
"'He mounted the steed of Breath—its gait was breath-and-not-breath. His whip was Tingal-tunggal (Single-glance). The horse was named Eling-eling (Mindful). The reins were Iman-tauhid (Faith-in-Unity). The crupper was the Pillars of Islam. The stirrups were Makrifat (Gnosis). The saddle was Hakikat-jati (Essential-truth). The breastplate was Tetep-pikukuhing-iman (Steadfast-pillar-of-faith).'"

### 13.
"'King Mutmainah charged with his whip, exulting like a great hero, fierce and fearless. He faced enemies by the hundreds of thousands—challenged, he did not retreat. Attacked by millions, he did not turn aside from pain or death—he would surely find glory in the end.'"

### 14.
"'Accurate was the lash of his whip—surely the troops of King Luamah died and vanished, struck by his whip: Patih Mangkubliai, Ujubriya-kibir (Arrogance-pride) fell; Sumungah (Haughtiness) and Kiyanat (Treachery); Sungkan (Reluctance) and Doyan-jail (Enjoy-mischief) died; Berung-bregundung (Snorting-bellowing) retreated and fell.'"

### 15.
"'All were scattered, destroyed, wiped out—not one remained. King Luamah alone was left after his army's destruction. He spoke to his two siblings: "Now what of your supernatural power?" King Amarah and Queen Supiyah replied: "If it must be—let us three attack him together in battle."'"

### 16.
"'They consulted and made plans, then prepared for war. Amarah donned his Kamandaka (Greed) attire—headdress of Karam-najis (Filth-impurity), belt of Ora-eling (Unmindful), sash of Maido-kukum (Deny-the-law), trousers of Sia-sia (Futile), shoes of Tanpa-ngilmu (Without-knowledge), spear Dorawaca (Harsh-speech), kris Jarumman (Needle-like).'"

### 17.
"'He mounted his steed—a horse with a hide of geseng-ati (Scorched-heart), named Paracidra (Full-of-deceit). The reins were Emoh-ing-becik (Unwilling-to-do-good), the crupper Paksa-lumuwih (Force-excessive), the neck-strap Ngabul-abul (Arbitrary), the tail-strap Mursal (Apostate), the saddle Tinggal-bekti (Abandon-devotion), the breastplate Drengki-ambeg-salah-tampa (Envy-selfish-wrong-reception).'"

### 18.
"'His whip was Doyan-ujar (Likes-to-speak), its lash Kumingsun-sumugih (Boastful-rich). His movements were crooked and wrong—he boasted, forgetful of God. He advanced, pointing, striking at the stomach, saying: "I am all-powerful, skilled at bringing disaster! I am a master-craftsman of evil!"'"

### 19.
"'They faced each other for battle—kings fighting kings. Fierce was the combat. In the battle he was attacked by three—from front, right, and left—trying to confuse Mutmainah. But the three kings could not withstand his fury in battle, attacked by three.'"

### 20.
"'King Mutmainah was stabbed again and again, mocked and whipped, but could not be penetrated. They struck him repeatedly—King Mutmainah fell together with his steed, slipping, unable to rise—yet still he remained steadfast in his intent.'"

### 21.
"'They stabbed and thrust without stopping, shouting "Die! Now your life is over! You cannot possibly live!"—"Come on, hurry and end it!" King Mutmainah said: "Come then, finish it—do not hold back your tune! I do not fear pain or death!"'"

### 22.
"'Instantly Mutmainah made a vow within his heart, praying for a sign: "If humanity is meant to be good, may I be strengthened for battle—may I repay the law, eradicate Luamah, Amarah, and Supiyah!" Then he received supernatural power—swiftly granted.'"

### 23.
"'Accurate was his strike—Luamah was whipped squarely on the cheek, struck flat, falling head over heels. Amarah came to help—whipped on the face, he pitched backward to the ground. Queen Supiyah came to help—whipped on the face, she too fell.'"

### 24.
"'They were thrust into the abyss—the three kings slipping, lying prostrate for a long time, unable to speak. After a while they revived. King Mutmainah said: "Hey—what do you intend? Do you want to die? If you want to die, I will kill you!"'"

### 25.
"'The three kings answered: "Whatever you will, let it be—live or die—if possible, we beg to live." The Holy King smiled: "What a pity, your stubbornness, since you are not worthy—all your life you begged to live!" Swiftly he whipped their heads again.'"

### 26.
"'Kings Luamah, Amarah, and Supiyah all died—their corpses lay gleaming. The troops and relatives of the three kings—after that, King Mutmainah intended to storm the city, to breach the three palaces—his might appeared exceedingly strong.'"

### 27.
"'The city of King Luamah had walls of iron mixed with copper. The city of King Amarah had walls of red copper. The city of Supiyah had walls of bronze. King Mutmainah entered the palace gates, delighting in the structure of the City of Love.'"

### 4.
### 4.
"The youngest, Princess Rifangi, after her father's death, was exceedingly troubled in heart—weeping, beating her breast, lamenting pitifully: 'O father, let me die too—whom shall I worship now?'"

### 5.
"Before long, King Mutmainah arrived, entering the palace. He saw the princesses weeping in the palace, their crying roaring, lamenting their king."

### 6.
"He met the two princesses; both were weeping. The Holy King said: 'Stop your crying—pity your swollen eyes. Your voice may grow hoarse and your radiance may fade.'"

### 7.
"'Look at me with your eyes—do not be so overwhelmed with emotion. Ah, be still, both of you, who are like reflected moons.' The two beautiful princesses opened their eyes and looked—and saw a supreme knight."

### 8.
"His radiance shone brilliantly, his gaze darting and sparkling. Their grief was defeated, vanished. Their weeping subsided. Their thoughts cleared: 'Could it be that this is the one who killed our father?'"

### 9.
"Princess Rifangi was nudged by the beautiful Salasiyah: 'Come, withdraw—this is no place for us. Even if I become a captive, I will not be so easily taken.' The beautiful princess withdrew gracefully, like a full moon passing by."

### 10.
"Still sobbing as she retreated, she could only lament her dead father. Then King Mutmainah saw the princess and followed after her, speaking sweetly:"

### 11.
"'Stop, O queen of prayer, O flower of grace who embodies all blessings. Help your heart—if it is the will of the Spirit, you are granted both anger and beauty. What do you resist?'"

### 12.
"'Therefore I stake my life, sworn to the jewel of maidens. If I am mistaken, let me die joyfully—it must be the will of the Spirit. The flower and I—the mingling of grace and revelation—meet in divine guidance.'"

### 13.
"The princess ceased her weeping and finally spoke softly, adjusting her seat: 'Hey, King Mutmainah—do not act so presumptuously, even if I become your captive, a prize of war.'"

### 14.
"'But I have already resolved firmly: I have a request of you, O king. If you will agree to my wish and take me as your wife, even if you make me suffer, beat me as a captive—'"

### 15.
"'—if not, I will not consent.' King Mutmainah said: 'What do you desire, my jewel? Truly I will fulfill all your wishes.' The beautiful princess answered softly concerning her request:"

### 16.
"'Revive my father—revive all three of them, my fathers who lie dead. I will take it as a sign that you are truly powerful—to revive the dead—truly a bestower of grace.'"

### 17.
"King Mutmainah heard the princess's request. The king was perplexed in his heart. Finally he said softly: 'What is this, my jewel? Such is the way of great warriors—to die serving the kingdom.'"

### 18.
"'It is already the will of God. Moreover, if your fathers who are dead were revived, they would create chaos, destroy religion, and spread wicked passion.' The two princesses said quietly: 'You cannot yet control things.'"

### 19.
"'Furthermore, if my fathers remain dead, this whole world will be destroyed. You would be sinning against God if you do not revive them. And I will not consent to be taken as your wife.'"

### 20.
"King Mutmainah said: 'Even so, my precious one, I have no power. If there is love from the Lord, I will be merely the instrument. If the Most High God permits, I will revive your fathers.'"

### 21.
"'Come, let us go quickly. You shall witness your fathers' revival.' Then they went to the place where the three kings' corpses lay. The beautiful princess wept over them. Then King Mutmainah—"

### 22.
"—prayed to God: 'May it be made easy—let the dead live again.' His wish was granted. King Mutmainah created wind and mist—the wind of Hayatullah (God's Life)."

### 23.
"The mist of sustenance—his wish was fulfilled. The mist felt like steam from rice. Then their noses brightened and warmth spread. Then they stirred, twitched—then moved and showed signs of life."

### 24.
"The dead were revived—Kings Aluamah (Luamah), Amarah, and Supiyah. They all sat properly before the king. King Mutmainah spoke softly to the three kings:"

### 25.
"'Hey, Aluamah, Amarah, and Supiyah—now that you are alive again, will you still rebel, or will you follow?' King Luamah answered: 'I will not disobey.'"

### 26.
"'Whatever my lord wills—whether to suffer or die—my body is surrendered. I have no will of my own.' King Mutmainah said: 'Since you submit wholly to my will—'"

### 27.
"'—if you follow my desire, I will simply imprison you in your own kingdoms. I will grant you food but not freedom. I will keep you confined forever, I will shackle your necks.'"

### 28.
"It was done swiftly: Luamah was imprisoned. Amarah and Supiyah likewise. The two princesses were then escorted as the spoils of war, brought to his kingdom, rejoicing at the victory."

### 29.
"According to a saying within the Hadith: there are four kinds of good deeds. First: the person who persists in wakefulness. Second: the one who persists in ascetic practice, refraining from eating greedily. Third: the one who persists in patience and generosity."

### 30.
"And the fourth: the one who persists in teaching knowledge, with conduct consistent with his knowledge. That person is marked by the ability to control, to confine, and to imprison the passions—truly attaining abundant grace."

### 31.
### 31.
"'That much of the story is told—the meeting of man and woman is not described further. The tale would be improper, with its flirtatious tone—it would undermine the trusting heart of one who can maintain faith.'"

### 32.
"'Thus ends the story of the Hadith Markum Baslam.' Rancangkapti said softly: 'I beg your blessing that I may practice it as you have, O mother, without violating proper conduct.'"

### 33.
"'It is difficult for a woman with a husband to attain Mutmainah.' The hermit spoke gently: 'You, my daughter, are not permitted—if you were like me... In the future you will undertake the holy bond of marriage.'"

### 34.
"'As for God's reward for those who enter marriage, who fulfill its proper form—the conduct of a woman under the protection of a man—truly it is sevenfold greater than for those who do not marry.'"

### 35.
"'What do you think, Jayengsari? Is it indeed so?' The prince bowed and said: 'What you have spoken is correct.' The female hermit looked at the young lord and said within her heart:"

### 36.
"'This person is holy at his core—sharp in the weaving of his understanding, simple, firm, calm—the seed of a saint. His nobility is apparent. He is indifferent to worldly things, drawn to gnosis.'"

### 78.
Mêgatruh 1.

### 78.
"Finally the female hermit said: 'You, young lord, and your sister Rancangkapti—continue your journey to the port of Banyuwangi, and carry out the command that was given.'"

### 2.
"'It is not at all that I am driving you away, leaving you stranded here. My heart is full of joy—truly I love you both.'"

### 3.
"'But your journey would be cut short, my child, and you would never meet the one you seek. Therefore, I entrust your journey to the All-Seeing God.'"

### 4.
"'May your purpose be safely accomplished, guarded by God.' Both bowed and said: 'Your command, O yogi—our deepest gratitude.'"

### 5.
"'Permit us now to withdraw, proceeding to Toyawangi (Banyuwangi). We ask only your blessing, O lord.' The female hermit nodded, and both kissed her feet quickly."

### 6.
"Both heads were stroked: 'Mind you, have no fear—day and night I watch over you.' They withdrew, the sister led by the hand, Ki Buras hurrying ahead."

### 7.
"'Forgive me, but I too have a request—I ask for your blessing for safety, and also for provisions—the kind that cannot be used up, the kind that returns to the pocket.'"

### 8.
"The female hermit laughed and said: 'Strange is your request—my thoughts are quite puzzled. Provisions that cannot be used up—just the loose change, please!'"

### 9.
"'It is not knowledge, it is just plain cash.' The hermit then gave some: 'Here, take this quickly—only two gobog coins, no more.' Buras was startled, his eyes bulging."

### 10.
"All the more pleased, the hermit said: 'As for these two gobog coins—use them for snacks on your journey. Your pockets will still be full—these two gobog will never be spent.'"

### 11.
"Snatched from her hand in an instant—Buras withdrew without taking leave, grumbling that he had been left out. When he reached the back of the princess, she turned and said gently:"

### 12.
"'Buras, why are you so late in following?' 'I was left behind for a while. The truth is, I was delayed because I asked the yogi for provisions—she gave me two gobog coins.'"

### 13.
"'Later, when we reach a food stall, I will treat you. There is something special about these gobog coins—for now they cannot be used, lest they prove worthless without effect.'"

"Section 21: In Banyuwangi they saw the Sela Candhani temple which, according to stories from ancient times, was the meditation place of King Menakjingga of Blambangan, formerly called the White Tiger Temple. Then they went to the home of Ki Menak Luhung, the caretaker of the said temple. There they met Ki Hartati, a merchant from Pekalongan who had just arrived from Bali. Ki Hartati had no children, so Prince Jayengsari and Lady Rancangkapti were adopted as his children and taken to Pekalongan. The two children were received by Nyi Hartati with joyful hearts and taught various kinds of knowledge, such as the calculation of salvation for the deceased and the meaning of the five fingers. Nyi Hartati passed away. Exactly a thousand days later she was followed by Ki Hartati... pages 281-300."

### 14.
"'Buras, tell me now—about your journey down from the mountain.' Jayengsari and Rancangkapti—the duration is not described."

### 15.
"They arrived at the plain of Toyaarum (Banyuwangi), traversing a vast, dangerous forest on the seashore—the strait of the island of Bali. Ki Buras was extremely thirsty."

### 16.
"He sought fresh water and found it—a great spring, exceedingly clear, near huge boulders piled up from the mountain. Buras drank until full, gulping noisily."

### 17.
"He quickly returned to report to his master: 'There is clear fresh water emerging from between the boulders piled up from the mountain, beneath a cool banyan tree.'"

### 18.
"'Come, brother, I am hot and wish to bathe.' They continued on their way and arrived at the well. The beautiful lady bathed. After bathing, she dried off beneath the cool banyan tree."

### 19.
### 19.
"A man descended from a boat, seeking sea fish—he came to the well carrying a water jar. After drawing water, he was startled to see the young lord and lady."

### 20.
"Ki Pandaya said gently: 'O lord, whom I see for the first time, I pray do not stay long—this well is a resting place for evil folk.'"

### 21.
"'Sea-pirates are their business.' The prince answered sweetly: 'My great thanks, friend, for your warning—may your journey be blessed.'"

### 22.
"Buras urged hastily: 'Come, let us depart quickly!' The two of them set off, the lady walking ahead, noticing large footprints on the path."

### 23.
"'Brother—what footprints are these, so large? They look like cat prints.' 'Sister, those are tiger prints.' 'But where is the tiger? Not in sight.' 'Sister, it has long gone far away.'"

### 24.
"They continued on and arrived at Toyaarum (Banyuwangi), delighted to see the beauty of the kingdom, the villages and settlements, springs and wells, countless in number."

### 25.
"Small streams lined with white stones, pleasant trees growing lushly, shaded and fertile—the soil was so good, all the people appeared peaceful and content."

### 26.
"So beautiful was the kingdom at night—no one closed their doors, their wealth lay spread out, nothing was locked away, and no one attempted to steal."

### 27.
"Banyuwangi was holding its market—it was market time. The sound of the market buzzed. They entered together, strolling under the cool kanari trees."

### 28.
"Buras, ever ready, quickly went shopping with the two gobog coins, coming and going. Brought before the beautiful lady were: skewers of wilegi snacks and boiled corn."

### 29.
"He reached into his pocket and took out the two gobog coins, showing them to the princess. Then he quickly bought wilegi—a thief's head's worth. When he reached into his pocket again—"

### 30.
"—the two coins were still whole! He bought corn—given twelve ears. Buras slapped his thigh excitedly, amazed at the two gobog again—he received fourteen ears of corn."

### 31.
"Nearly a cubit long were the ears, the contents full and tight. The corn kernels were large, fragrant, sweet, and tender—the husks thin and fine."

### 32.
"The lady asked: 'Buras, do you still have the gobog?' He felt his pocket—the two gobog coins were still whole. Truly, as the female hermit had said—the gobog could never be spent."

### 33.
"From the market they journeyed south, at the hour when children drive the cattle home. They arrived at Nglicin village on the peak of Mount Ijen—both their hearts were joyful."

### 34.
"They saw the kingdoms, villages, and settlements, and the strait of Bali—ships at anchor, the island and mountains of Bali, and the Southern Sea in view."

### 35.
"All the mountains of Banyuwangi were visible, delighting the first-time viewers. Clearly seen: the sun descending into the mountains, replaced by the full moon."

### 36.
"Illuminating the contents of the whole world—light-hearted were the two. So they did not stop in the village; all night they did not sleep. At dawn the roosters crowed."

### 37.
"They departed from Nglicin, journeying south—pleasant was their walking. The path was shaded by trees, the sunlight blocked, with nothing piercing through."

### 38.
"Clear streams flowed between the elephant grass, lined with black stones. Their journey brought them to the Sela Candhani temple—a charming complex to behold."

### 79.
## Kinanthi  1.

### 79.
"The prince and his beautiful sister halted beneath a nagasari tree. Buras presented food. They ate a little, gazing in wonder at the white temple, carved with intricate floral patterns."

### 2.
"The lady said to the young lord: 'Brother, what temple is that, and why is it so white? What stone is used?' 'It is made of candhani stone, sister. But this is the first time I have seen one.'"

### 3.
"'A temple of white stone—a beautiful shape with intricate carvings. Who could have built it? It seems fit for a king.' They paused, talking together, when an old man approached."

### 4.
"His hair was gleaming white down to the nape, brushed smooth. His mustache was full and white, his great beard all white. His body was tall and dark, with a yellow waist-cloth and a jacket."

### 5.
Sarigak amundhuk-mundhuk|kadya sata amèmèti|nyakêti kang samya lênggah|makidhupuh praptèng ngarsi|amung silanira tumpang|umatur marang sang pêkik||

### 6.
Kula nama Menakluhung|kang minangka jurukunci|ing candhi pêthak punika|rawuh paduka ing ngriki|atur basuki pambagya|sintên sinambating kang sih||

### 7.
"'Grandfather, you ask me? My name is Jayengsari. This is my younger sister, Lady Rancangkapti.' Buras added: 'I am Buras, servant to both master and mistress.'"

### 8.
"Ki Menak Luhung asked further: 'Do you intend to do ascetic practice, or do you merely wish to see?' The prince answered: 'I only wish to see—I am amazed at the white temple.'"

### 9.
"Ki Menak Luhung said: 'The story from ancient times, in the era of the Majapahit kingdom—this place was part of the palace of the kingdom of Blambangan, where the reigning king was—'"

### 10.
"'King Menakjingga, the monarch, also called Prabu Urubisma, who possessed a heirloom: a yellow-metal weapon shaped like a mace. Its magical power was incredible—whoever was struck by it would have their life-force drained.'"

### 11.
"'Even the strongest and most enduring would lose their power if struck by that yellow metal. As for this white-stone temple, it was the meditation place of King Urubisma. The temple is called the White Tiger Temple.'"

### 12.
"'Come, if you wish to see, I will guide you.' The three of them set out from beneath the nagasari tree, walking around the white temple, gazing at it with wonder."

### 13.
"The young lord was amazed to see the broad, level grounds and the fertile soil. Menak said: 'In former times, this was a prosperous kingdom, in the saying: "blessed with fertile land, sea, and mountains."'"

### 14.
"'Peaceful, prosperous, and abundant—the kingdom had its back to the mountains and faced the harbor—to the right a port, to the left rice fields. Clear water was plentiful—no danger threatened the kingdom.'"

### 15.
"'Now in recent times, there is a saying that often ships' captains stop here, lodging in my village. Even now there is still one—'"

### 16.
"'—staying at my house—just arrived from Bali—a ship's captain from Pangangsalan (Pekalongan), named Ki Hartati. If you are agreeable, please come to my home.'"

### 17.
"The young lord felt in his heart the words of the great sage Sheikh Wahdat of Argapura: 'By the grace of God, could this be the one who will take me in?' Finally he spoke gently:"

### 18.
"'Grandfather, this is most fortunate.' Ki Menak was exceedingly glad and said: 'Come, let us go.' They all had already departed. When they arrived at the house, Ki Menak informed the captain."

### 19.
"'Ki Hartati, great captain, my brother—I have brought guests: a young man named Jayengsari, and his younger sister, named Lady Rancangkapti. They are most pitiable.'"

### 20.
"'Here they are, sitting outside the house.' When Ki Hartati saw the prince and princess—their radiance was gentle and lovely—he could scarcely stop staring."

### 21.
Martrênyuh tyas arawat luh|asêrêt wijiling angling|anggèr di kaparèng ngarsa|lungguha cakêt lan mami|sakaliyan wus alênggah|cakêt lawan Ki Hartati||

### 22.
"He spoke to the young lord: 'My request, I pray—do not let it trouble your heart—I may be forward, but in my embrace, you are as my own child.'"

### 23.
"'I have no children—my heart is sorrowful. Now my time is passing; my wife can no longer bear children. How is it that you could come to be my children?'"

### 24.
"'By the grace of God the Most High—last night I dreamed I received a pigeon and a dove, and then I brought them home to the country of Pekalongan. It was a lasting omen.'"

### 25.
"'It draws toward great glory, as the saying goes, it brings blessing. From the moment I lay down to sleep, I could not rest again—what was seen in the dream—what is its interpretation?'"

### 26.
"As for your arrival, my child—surely it is the fulfillment of the dream. This meeting is thus. But my elder brother, what is the meaning of the dream?" Menak Luhung spoke softly:

### 27.
"Younger brother, that dream—its meaning is a meeting. I recall four stages. The first meeting is through something glimpsed from the side. The second meeting is through following a trail. The third meeting is through an exchange of words. The fourth meeting is through a resemblance. Now, as for what is met—"

### 29.
Saking piridan puniku|kadi ta supêna uning|ing urubing kang dahana|wahananipun aruntik|latu dadya pipiridan|ing duka sasaminèki||
